Near-critical Ising, sine-Gordon at the free fermion point, and bosonization
S. C. Park, Tuomas Virtanen, Christian Webb

Abstract
. In this article, we study the continuous correlations of the near-critical Ising model in two dimensions with plus boundary conditions, and prove that doubled correlation functions of primary fields (spin, disorder, fermions, energy) in the Ising model are given by correlation functions of the sine-Gordon model at the free fermion point. This is an instance of bosonization. The main ideas involve analyticity of correlation functions in a mass parameter in finite volume and proving that in a perturbative regime, the Taylor coefficients of the correlation functions match due to known bosonization results for the critical Ising model in terms of the Gaussian free field. The main techniques on the Ising side involve construction and precise estimates of certain massive holomorphic functions while on the sine-Gordon side, we control an iterated Mayer expansion with techniques going back to Brydges and Kennedy.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 82B20, 81T40, 60G60; Secondary 30E25, 81T08

1.
Introduction and main results

1.1.
Background

Scaling limits of two-dimensional models of statistical physics have long been understood by physicists to have a very rich structure. For many models, the scaling limit at precisely the critical point of the model is conjectured to be described by a
conformal field theory
, and many aspects of the scaling limit become exactly solvable (see e.g.
[
10
,
26
]
). Mathematically, such statements remain conjectures in general, but there has been great progress in understanding them in the setting of the
critical Ising model

[
20
,
21
,
35
]
and the
Liouville model

[
40
]
.

Physicists also predict that for very special perturbations of certain critical models (and conformal field theories), the scaling limits still remain exactly solvable (see e.g.
[
51
]
). Some of the prime examples of such models are the
near-critical Ising model
(either perturbed in the thermal or magnetic direction) and the
sine-Gordon model
. Again, mathematically rigorous results about such statements are somewhat more limited, but for the near-critical Ising model (perturbed in the thermal direction) see
[
64
]
and more recently e.g.
[
54
,
22
]
and for the sine-Gordon model (at the free fermion point) see e.g.
[
7
,
8
]
.

Another fascinating phenomenon physicists have predicted to occur in two-dimensional models is
bosonization/fermionization
. This is a duality that relates the correlation functions of certain very special bosonic models to certain equally special fermionic models. Perhaps the original mathematically rigorous work on such a phenomenon goes back to
[
48
]
which was done in a Hilbert space setting. Recently, some results of this flavor have been given rigorous mathematical proofs in a probabilistic setting: in
[
9
]
, it was proven that correlation functions of primary fields in the critical double Ising model can be expressed as Gaussian free field correlation functions, while in
[
7
,
8
]
, it was proven that correlation functions related to massive free fermions can be expressed in terms of sine-Gordon correlation functions at the free fermion point. In
[
47
]
, this was used to show that the scaling limit of the height function in a massive perturbation of the full plane
dimer model
is the sine-Gordon field at the free fermion point, and in
[
12
]
, such a result was proven for suitable domains with boundaries. See also
[
11
]
for an instance of bosonization that relates the sine-Gordon model away from the free fermion point to an interacting fermionic model known as the
massive Thirring model
– this duality is sometimes known as the Coleman correspondence due to it being conjectured by Coleman in
[
24
]
. In fact, the main results of this article can be seen as an extension of the Coleman correspondence.

The goal of this article is to extend the bosonization results of
[
9
]
from the critical Ising model to the near-critical Ising model (perturbed in the thermal direction). The relevant bosonic model is now the sine-Gordon model at the free fermion point instead of the Gaussian free field. Conceptually, this may not be too surprising to the reader, as the Ising model is in a sense a model of massive Majorana fermions; the double Ising model is then a model of two massive Majorana fermions. This is equivalent to one massive Dirac fermion which by
[
8
]
is known to be equivalent to the sine-Gordon model at the free fermion point. Indeed, this bosonization result was already predicted (for spin correlation functions) in
[
39
]
.

1.2.
The Ising model and the double Ising model

The Ising model
[
43
,
38
]
is one of the most fundamental models of statistical mechanics. It is complicated enough to describe interesting phenomena, namely ferromagnetic phase transitions, but tractable enough to be studied in a mathematically rigorous way.

In this article, we are concerned with the two-dimensional nearest neighbor Ising model with zero external field and plus boundary conditions. While we will mainly focus on the continuum model, we briefly review as motivation the discrete model. For this purpose, let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be a nice domain (say bounded and simply connected with smooth boundary),
\delta>0
, and
\Omega_{\delta}=\Omega\cap\delta\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. The set of
spin configurations
on
\Omega_{\delta}
is
\{-1,1\}^{\Omega_{\delta}}
and the Ising model is a family of probability measures on
\{-1,1\}^{\Omega_{\delta}}
. More precisely, for each
inverse temperature

\beta>0
, the probability of a spin configuration
\sigma^{\delta}\in\{-1,1\}^{\Omega_{\delta}}
is

(1.2.1)

\mathbb{P}_{\beta}(\sigma^{\delta})\propto e^{\beta\sum_{x\sim y}\sigma_{x}^{\delta}\sigma_{y}^{\delta}},

where
x\sim y
means that
x
and
y
are nearest neighbors in
\Omega_{\delta}
(each pair summed over only once), and we allow also
x
(or
y
) to be on the boundary (one lattice step outside) of
\Omega_{\delta}
, in which case the boundary condition is that
\sigma_{x}^{\delta}=1
(or
\sigma_{y}^{\delta}=1
).

Since
[
53
]
, it has been understood that this model has a phase transition at
\beta=\beta_{c}=\frac{1}{2}\log(1+\sqrt{2})
. This exact solution of the model spurred a number of celebrated rigorous computations of various statistics of the model, see
[
49
]
for a comprehensive treatment. More recently, in
[
20
]
(see also
[
49
,
58
,
28
]
for infinite volume results), it was proven that
spin correlation functions
have scaling limits that enjoy conformal covariance properties, as predicted in
[
10
,
17
]
. More precisely, there exist limiting correlation functions
\langle\sigma_{x_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{x_{n}}\rangle_{0;\Omega}
, which transform covariantly under conformal transformations of
\Omega
(see Remark
6.5
) such that as
\delta\to 0
,

(1.2.2)

\delta^{-n/8}\sum_{\sigma\in\{-1,1\}^{\Omega_{\delta}}}\sigma_{x_{1}}^{\delta}\cdots\sigma_{x_{n}}^{\delta}\mathbb{P}_{\beta_{c}}(\sigma)=\mathcal{C}_{\sigma}^{n}\langle\sigma_{x_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{x_{n}}\rangle_{0;\Omega}+o(1),

where
\mathcal{C}_{\sigma}=2^{\frac{5}{48}}e^{\frac{3}{2}\zeta^{\prime}(-1)}
, see e.g.
[
21
, (1.8)]
(note they work on the rotated square lattice). The subscript
0
refers here to the fact that we are exactly at the critical, or massless, point.

To see the full conformal field theory structure predicted by physicists, one needs to also consider other correlation functions in addition to pure spin correlation functions. The relevant
primary
fields in the model are the
disorder field

\mu
,
fermion fields

\psi,\psi^{*}
, and the
energy field

\varepsilon
. These all have lattice level definitions that need to be renormalized by a suitable power of
\delta
to lead to meaningful correlation functions in the scaling limit. We refer the reader to
[
21
, Section 1 and Section 2]
for details of the lattice level definitions of these fields and conformal invariance results on their critical scaling limit. It also bears mentioning that conformal invariance of the critical model has been seen in the limit in the form of a
Schramm-Loewner Evolution
, or SLE of its interface curves
[
60
,
18
]
.

At the heart of the aforementioned demonstrations of the conformal invariance of the discrete model has been a robust theory of discrete complex analysis which passes to the continuum limit, following the approach of
[
60
]
(see also
[
28
,
57
]
for related discrete context). Indeed, the key insight for our analysis is the characterization of these limiting correlation functions in terms of objects arising from boundary value problems of complex analysis. We now briefly outline this (purely continuous) characterization of these functions, in order to generalize it later.

The starting point is that if we define for
\eta\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
,
\psi^{[\eta]}=\frac{1}{2}(\bar{\eta}\psi+\eta\psi^{*})
, then
spin-weighted fermionic correlation functions
of the form

(1.2.3)

f(z)=\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\psi_{z}\psi^{[\eta]}_{w}\rangle_{0;\Omega}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{0;\Omega}}

as functions of
z
, are meromorphic on the double cover of
\Omega\setminus\{a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\}
and uniquely characterized by suitable boundary values on
\partial\Omega
and
\{a_{1},\dots,a_{n},w\}
(see
[
21
, Section 3]
). General spin-weighted fermionic correlation functions (with more insertions of
\psi
and/or
\psi^{[\eta]}
) can be recovered from two insertions due to a Pfaffian identity fermionic correlation functions satisfy (see
[
21
, Proposition 2.24]
). General correlation functions can then be extracted from the operator product expansions (OPEs) the fields satisfy, proven in
[
21
, Section 6]
, and decorrelation estimates for pure spin correlations (see
[
20
, Section 2.9]
or alternatively
[
21
, Proposition 5.3]
).

We now turn to the near-critical Ising model. Near-critical here refers to the fact that instead of choosing that
\beta
is exactly
\beta_{c}
, we fix some
m\in\mathbb{R}
, nonzero if not exactly at criticality, and take
\beta=\beta_{c}-m\delta/2
. It turns out that this is exactly the correct scaling in
\delta
for perturbing the inverse temperature to see a non-trivial (and different from the critical case, being
massive
) regime in the scaling limit
\delta\to 0
. More precisely, in
[
54
]
, it is proven that there exists a family of non-critical limiting correlation functions
\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{m;\Omega}
for
m<0
such that instead of (
1.2.2
) we have

(1.2.4)

\delta^{-n/8}\sum_{\sigma\in\{-1,1\}^{\Omega_{\delta}}}\sigma_{a_{1}}^{\delta}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}^{\delta}\mathbb{P}_{\beta_{c}-m\delta/2}(\sigma)=\mathcal{C}_{\sigma}^{n}\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{m;\Omega}+o(1).

The proof again goes through analyzing spin-weighted correlation functions similar to (
1.2.3
), which also for
m\neq 0
satisfy the same boundary conditions as in the critical case, but instead of meromorphicity (
\bar{\partial}f=0
)
f
is
m
-
massive holomorphic
, in the sense that it satisfies the equation
\bar{\partial}f=-im\bar{f}
, an instance of the
Vekua equation

[
62
,
15
]
.

For our proof of bosonization of the near-critical Ising model, it turns out to be convenient to generalize this setting slightly. Instead of
m
a constant (as above), we replace it by a non-constant deterministic function
\alpha
. We construct an
\alpha
-massive counterpart to fermion-fermion correlations (
1.2.3
) (and disorder-fermion correlations, for technical reasons) directly in the continuum as the unique solutions to
\bar{\partial}f=-i\alpha\bar{f}
with analogous branching and boundary conditions as in the critical case. More general correlation functions can then be constructed from Pfaffian identities, operator product expansions, and decorrelation assumptions on pure spin correlations. To simplify notation slightly, we will from now on write e.g.
\sigma_{V}:=\prod_{i\in V}\sigma_{v_{i}}
,
1
1

1

While for spin and energy observables, the order of the product does not matter, for fermions and disorder observables it does. We will always assume that our index sets are ordered, say
Z=\{1,...,n\}
, in which case we understand that
\psi_{Z}=\prod_{i\in Z}\psi_{z_{i}}=\psi_{z_{1}}\cdots\psi_{z_{n}}
. We use the same convention for
\psi_{W}^{*}
and
\mu_{U}
.
within correlation functions, so that a general primary field correlation can be written compactly as

\langle\sigma_{V}\mu_{U}\psi_{Z}\psi_{W}^{*}\varepsilon_{A}\rangle_{\alpha;\Omega},

with the convention that the insertion points are all distinct unless otherwise stated. We will do this construction in detail in Sections
6
and
7.3
.

To our knowledge, this general setup with non-constant mass does not yet have full rigorous scaling limit results available, and as we work purely in the continuum, we do not claim here that continuous correlations constructed in this paper arise as a limit of discrete correlations (though see the closely related setting of the dimer model with a spatially varying mass studied in
[
12
]
). Nonetheless, when
\alpha
is a negative constant
m
, the pure spin correlations constructed in this paper coincide with the scaling limit established in
[
54
]
(see Proposition
6.44
), which may be generalized to any primary field correlation with any
m\in\mathbb{R}

[
23
]
. In addition, the core assumptions in the continuous construction (Vekua equation, boundary condition, decorrelation near the boundary) have been shown to appear and be valid in far more general settings than in the constant mass case, see
[
19
,
46
]
.

The final fact related to the Ising model we need to discuss is the
double Ising model
. On the lattice level, this simply amounts to taking two independent copies of the probabilistic model and looking at suitable observables constructed from these two independent copies. While we do not go into it further, it is known that there is an exact combinatorial correspondence between the (critical) dimer model and the critical double Ising model
[
28
]
, which is expected to generalize to massive versions of both models and should be the discrete counterpart for our continuous correspondence (see e.g.
[
13
]
).

Working in the continuum, we also define the double Ising model correlation functions directly as deterministic functions, but one should view this definition as being motivated by
(\sigma,\mu,\psi,\psi^{*},\varepsilon)
and
(\widetilde{\sigma},\widetilde{\mu},\widetilde{\psi},\widetilde{\psi}^{*},\widetilde{\varepsilon})
being independent random objects so that correlation functions factorize suitably. The definition is as follows

(1.2.5)

\displaystyle\left\langle(\sigma\widetilde{\sigma})_{V}(\mu\widetilde{\mu})_{U}(\psi\widetilde{\psi})_{Z}(\psi^{*}\widetilde{\psi}^{*})_{W}(\varepsilon+\widetilde{\varepsilon})_{A}\right\rangle_{\alpha;\Omega}

\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{S\subset A}\left\langle\sigma_{V}\mu_{U}\psi_{Z}\psi_{W}^{*}\varepsilon_{S}\right\rangle_{\alpha;\Omega}\left\langle\sigma_{V}\mu_{U}\psi_{Z}\psi_{W}^{*}\varepsilon_{A\setminus S}\right\rangle_{\alpha;\Omega}.

Here we interpret
(\sigma\widetilde{\sigma})_{V}=\prod_{i\in V}\sigma_{v_{i}}\widetilde{\sigma}_{v_{i}}
and
(\varepsilon+\widetilde{\varepsilon})_{A}=\prod_{m\in A}(\varepsilon_{a_{m}}+\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{a_{m}})
.

Note that for the energy field, we are considering
\varepsilon+\widetilde{\varepsilon}
, while for the other fields we consider products. One could also consider the product
\varepsilon\widetilde{\varepsilon}
, but the sum turns out to be slightly more convenient when discussing the sine-Gordon model. Note that, apart from a choice of branches of square roots, one can recover inductively the Ising correlation functions from the double Ising correlation functions.

1.3.
The sine-Gordon model

The (Euclidean massless) sine-Gordon model is a fascinating model of two-dimensional quantum field theory. It is predicted (and in some cases proven) to have connections to various types of interesting phenomena: bosonization
[
24
,
8
,
11
]
, solitons
[
25
, Chapter 6]
, spontaneous symmetry breaking and dynamical mass generation, connections to the classical 2d Coulomb gas
[
33
,
42
]
, coupling dependent structure of renormalization counter terms
[
52
]
, and the Kosterlitz-Thouless transition
[
31
]
. As already mentioned, it is a fundamental example of a near-critical model where there are many conjectures about exact solvability of certain central quantities – see e.g.
[
45
]
and
[
51
, Part IV]
. The model has been studied mathematically rigorously in various ways – in addition to the references above, see e.g.
[
3
,
27
]
.

While some of the properties described above arise only in the infinite volume limit of the model, we will mainly be focused on the model in finite volume (though see Corollary
1.3
for results about the infinite volume model). Formally, given a nice domain
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
and parameters
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
and
\beta\in(0,8\pi)
, the finite volume model on
\Omega
is for us a family of probability measures on a suitable space of generalized functions (e.g.
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega)
, but this will not be important in what follows) of the form

(1.3.1)

\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{sG}(\beta,\mu|\Omega)}(d\varphi)=\frac{1}{Z_{\mathrm{sG}(\beta,\mu|\Omega)}}e^{\mu\int_{\Omega}\mathop{:}\cos(\sqrt{\beta}\varphi)\mathop{:}}\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}}(d\varphi),

where
\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}}(d\varphi)
is the law of the Gaussian free field on
\Omega
with zero Dirichlet boundary conditions on
\partial\Omega
,
\mathop{:}\cdot\mathop{:}
denotes Wick ordering, and
Z_{\mathrm{sG}(\beta,\mu|\Omega)}
is a normalization constant (the partition function of the model). One could also study other types of boundary conditions (for example, the ones in
[
8
,
7
]
are different), but for the connection to the Ising model (in a simply connected domain with plus boundary conditions), these turn out to be the relevant ones.

The most interesting objects in the model (that have meaningful infinite volume limits, connections to the Coulomb gas, and which are related to exact solvability and bosonization) are correlation functions of the form

(1.3.2)

\displaystyle\left\langle\prod_{i\in Z}\partial\varphi(z_{i})\prod_{j\in W}\bar{\partial}\varphi(w_{j})\prod_{k\in U}\mathop{:}e^{i\gamma_{k}\varphi(u_{k})}\mathop{:}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{sG}(\beta,\mu|\Omega)},

where
\gamma_{k}\in\mathbb{R}
for each
k
.

We again use similar shorthand notation as we did for the Ising model and write this as

(1.3.3)

\displaystyle\left\langle(\partial\varphi)_{Z}(\bar{\partial}\varphi)_{W}(\mathop{:}e^{i\gamma\varphi}\mathop{:})_{U}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{sG}(\beta,\mu|\Omega)}.

Note that we are abusing notation here as
\gamma
also depends on the index in the product over
U
.

In fact, for the connection to the Ising model, we will consider Wick ordered trigonometric functions of the field rather than the Wick ordered complex exponentials. These are related as follows:

\displaystyle\mathop{:}\cos(\gamma\varphi(x))\mathop{:}=\frac{1}{2}(\mathop{:}e^{i\gamma\varphi(x)}\mathop{:}+\mathop{:}e^{-i\gamma\varphi(x)}\mathop{:})\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathop{:}\sin(\gamma\varphi(x))\mathop{:}=\frac{1}{2i}(\mathop{:}e^{i\gamma\varphi(x)}\mathop{:}-\mathop{:}e^{-i\gamma\varphi(x)}\mathop{:}).

For
\beta\geq 4\pi
, it turns out that such a direct definition of the model or its correlation functions is not possible, but one has to define the model through a limit of models where the Gaussian free field has been regularized. The particular choice of regularization should not matter too much, but the one we use is detailed in Section
2
. Also to avoid dealing with complications coming from the boundary of the domain, we find it convenient to replace the constant
\mu
with a spatially varying function
\rho\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
. Moreover, while the sine-Gordon model is an interesting model for all values of
\beta\in(0,8\pi)
(
8\pi
is the point where the Kosterlitz-Thouless transition happens), the connection to the Ising model occurs only at
\beta=4\pi
, so we focus on this case. We will also drop the
\beta
from the notation
\langle\cdot\rangle_{\mathrm{sG}(\beta,\mu|\Omega)}
and simply write
\langle\cdot\rangle_{\mathrm{sG}(\mu|\Omega)}=\langle\cdot\rangle_{\mathrm{sG}(4\pi,\mu|\Omega)}
. Similarly not all observables of the form
\mathop{:}\cos(\gamma\varphi)\mathop{:}
or
\mathop{:}\sin(\gamma\varphi)\mathop{:}
correspond to Ising primary fields – we only study those with
\gamma\in\{\sqrt{\pi},\sqrt{4\pi}\}
(though as discussed in
[
7
]
, also other values of
\gamma
have meaningful fermionic interpretations for the complex exponentials). As a final comment about these correlation functions, we mention that for
\beta=4\pi
, the correlation functions of
\mathop{:}\cos(\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi)\mathop{:}
are not well defined, but require further renormalization in addition to Wick ordering. To summarize this discussion, the correlation functions we study in this article are

(1.3.4)

\left\langle(\mathop{:}\cos(\sqrt{\pi}\varphi)\mathop{:})_{V}(\mathop{:}\sin(\sqrt{\pi}\varphi)\mathop{:})_{U}(\partial\varphi)_{Z}(\bar{\partial}\varphi)_{W}(\mathop{\typecolon}\cos(\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi)\mathop{\typecolon})_{A}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{sG}(\rho|\Omega)}\\
\quad=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\left\langle(\mathop{:}\cos(\sqrt{\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon})\mathop{:})_{V}(\mathop{:}\sin(\sqrt{\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon})\mathop{:})_{U}(\partial\varphi_{\varepsilon})_{Z}(\bar{\partial}\varphi_{\varepsilon})_{W}(\mathop{\typecolon}\cos(\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon})\mathop{\typecolon})_{A}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{sG}(\rho|\varepsilon,\Omega)}\\
\quad:=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{\left\langle e^{\int_{\Omega}d^{2}v\rho(v)\mathop{:}\cos(\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(v))\mathop{:}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}(\varepsilon)}}\Big\langle(\mathop{:}\cos(\sqrt{\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon})\mathop{:})_{V}(\mathop{:}\sin(\sqrt{\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon})\mathop{:})_{U}\\
\times(\partial\varphi_{\varepsilon})_{Z}(\bar{\partial}\varphi_{\varepsilon})_{W}(\mathop{\typecolon}\cos(\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon})\mathop{\typecolon})_{A}e^{\int_{\Omega}d^{2}v\rho(v)\mathop{:}\cos(\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(v))\mathop{:}}\Big\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}(\varepsilon)},

where
\varphi_{\varepsilon}
is the regularized Gaussian free field,
\langle\cdot\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}(\varepsilon)}
denotes integration with respect to its law (see Section
2
),

\mathop{:}\cos(\alpha\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x))\mathop{:}=c_{\alpha}\varepsilon^{-\frac{\alpha^{2}}{4\pi}}\cos(\alpha\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x))\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathop{:}\sin(\alpha\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x))\mathop{:}=c_{\alpha}\varepsilon^{-\frac{\alpha^{2}}{4\pi}}\sin(\alpha\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x)),

where
c_{\alpha}
is defined in (
2.2.5
), and

(1.3.5)

\displaystyle\mathop{\typecolon}\cos(\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x))\mathop{\typecolon}=\mathop{:}\cos(\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x))\mathop{:}-\int_{\Omega}d^{2}y\,\rho(y)\mathcal{A}(x,y|\varepsilon),

where

(1.3.6)

\mathcal{A}(x,y|\varepsilon)=\left\langle\mathop{:}\cos(\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x))\mathop{:}\mathop{:}\cos(\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(y))\mathop{:}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}(\varepsilon)}\\
-\left\langle\mathop{:}\cos(\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x))\mathop{:}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}(\varepsilon)}\left\langle\mathop{:}\cos(\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(y))\mathop{:}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}(\varepsilon)}.

As we will later argue,
\mathcal{A}(x,y|\varepsilon)
is not integrable in the
\varepsilon\to 0
limit, so
\mathop{\typecolon}\cdot\mathop{\typecolon}
amounts to subtracting an “infinite constant” when compared to the usual Wick ordering
\mathop{:}\cdot\mathop{:}
, and this is necessary to construct non-trivial correlation functions.

Existence of such limiting correlation functions is one of the main results of our analysis of the sine-Gordon model in this article as presented in Theorem
1.5
, though note that there we prove existence of a limit in the sense of generalized functions. In the proof of Theorem
1.1
, we then argue that these limiting correlation functions are actually functions and not just generalized functions.

1.4.
Main results

Having given an informal definition of the correlation functions in both models (with precise definitions in Section
5
and Section
6
), we can now state our main bosonization result. Briefly put, it states that the correlation functions of the two models agree when we use the dictionary given in Table
1
.

Double Ising

Sine-Gordon

\alpha

\rho=-\frac{2}{\pi}\alpha

\sigma\tilde{\sigma}

\sqrt{2}\mathop{:}\cos(\sqrt{\pi}\varphi)\mathop{:}

\mu\tilde{\mu}

\sqrt{2}\mathop{:}\sin(\sqrt{\pi}\varphi)\mathop{:}

\varepsilon+\tilde{\varepsilon}

2\mathop{\typecolon}\cos(\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi)\mathop{\typecolon}

\psi\tilde{\psi}

4i\sqrt{\pi}\partial\varphi

\psi^{\star}\tilde{\psi}^{\star}

-4i\sqrt{\pi}\bar{\partial}\varphi

Table 1.
The double Ising – sine-Gordon correspondence.

The precise statement is as follows.

Theorem 1.1
.

For
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
a bounded, open, simply connected set with smooth boundary and
\alpha\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
, the correlation functions of the double Ising model (
1.2.5
) and sine-Gordon model (
1.3.4
) exist and satisfy

\displaystyle\left\langle(\sigma\widetilde{\sigma})_{V}(\mu\widetilde{\mu})_{U}(\psi\widetilde{\psi})_{Z}(\psi^{*}\widetilde{\psi}^{*})_{W}(\varepsilon+\widetilde{\varepsilon})_{A}\right\rangle_{\alpha;\Omega}

\displaystyle=\left\langle(\sqrt{2}\mathop{:}\cos\sqrt{\pi}\varphi\mathop{:})_{V}(\sqrt{2}\mathop{:}\sin\sqrt{\pi}\varphi\mathop{:})_{U}(4i\sqrt{\pi}\partial\varphi)_{Z}(-4i\sqrt{\pi}\bar{\partial}\varphi)_{W}(2\mathop{\typecolon}\cos\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi\mathop{\typecolon})_{A}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{sG}(-\frac{2\alpha}{\pi}|\Omega)}.

The proof of this theorem relies on two key results: one concerning (double) Ising correlation functions and one sine-Gordon correlation functions. We will state these shortly, and then, using them, give a brief proof of Theorem
1.1
. First however, we state two corollaries of Theorem
1.1
.

Our first corollary concerns the situation where
\alpha
tends to a constant. In this situation, we can identify our (double) Ising correlation functions with scaling limits of correlation functions of the discrete model (constructed in
[
54
]
).

Corollary 1.2
.

For
m\in\mathbb{R}
, the following holds:

\langle\sigma_{V}\mu_{U}\psi_{Z}\psi^{*}_{W}\varepsilon_{A}\rangle_{m;\Omega}=\lim_{\alpha\to m}\langle\sigma_{V}\mu_{U}\psi_{Z}\psi^{*}_{W}\varepsilon_{A}\rangle_{\alpha;\Omega},

where
\alpha\to m
means pointwise convergence of functions
\alpha
to
m
, with the condition that
\alpha\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
such that
|\alpha|\leq\kappa_{\infty}
and
\operatorname{dist}(z,\partial\Omega)^{2}|\nabla\alpha(z)|\leq\kappa_{b}
for some fixed
\kappa_{\infty},\kappa_{b}>0
.

Consequently, under the same assumptions on
\Omega
as in Theorem
1.1
, and the above assumptions on
\alpha
, the limit

\displaystyle\left\langle(\sqrt{2}\mathop{:}\cos\sqrt{\pi}\varphi\mathop{:})_{V}(\sqrt{2}\mathop{:}\sin\sqrt{\pi}\varphi\mathop{:})_{U}(4i\sqrt{\pi}\partial\varphi)_{Z}(-4i\sqrt{\pi}\bar{\partial}\varphi)_{W}(2\mathop{\typecolon}\cos\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi\mathop{\typecolon})_{A}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{sG}(-\frac{2m}{\pi}|\Omega)}

\displaystyle=\!\lim_{\alpha\to m}\!\Big\langle(\sqrt{2}\mathop{:}\cos\sqrt{\pi}\varphi\mathop{:})_{V}(\sqrt{2}\mathop{:}\sin\sqrt{\pi}\varphi\mathop{:})_{U}(4i\sqrt{\pi}\partial\varphi)_{Z}(-4i\sqrt{\pi}\bar{\partial}\varphi)_{W}(2\mathop{\typecolon}\cos\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi\mathop{\typecolon})_{A}\Big\rangle_{\mathrm{sG}(-\frac{2\alpha}{\pi}|\Omega)}

exists, and in the specific case
m<0
:

\displaystyle\left\langle\big(\sqrt{2}\mathop{:}\cos\sqrt{\pi}\varphi\mathop{:}\big)_{V}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{sG}(-\frac{2m}{\pi}|\Omega)}=\left\langle\sigma_{V}\right\rangle_{m;\Omega}^{2},

with the spin correlation functions agreeing with those constructed as scaling limits in
[
54
,
22
]
.

Proof.

The convergence under
\alpha\to m
is essentially identical to the proof of Lemma
6.40
(basically, using dominated convergence on the summands involving
\alpha
), after series expansion of general correlations have been justified (see also Remark
7.1
and Section
8.3
, that the radius of convergence is uniform as long as the assumptions on
\alpha
are kept). For the constant mass case, see Proposition
6.44
.
∎

Our second result concerns the infinite volume limit
\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
. While there should be a similar closed system of equations obeyed by sine-Gordon correlations corresponding to
n
-point pure spin or disorder correlations at any
m
(see
[
56
]
and references therein), for the sake of complete yet concise presentation, we focus on the two-point pure spin or disorder correlation functions and their connection to Painlevé equations.

Corollary 1.3
.

Suppose
m<0
and let
\{u_{1},u_{2}\}\subset B_{R}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:|x|<R\}
for large enough
R
with
U=\{1,2\}
. The following limits

\displaystyle\mathfrak{C}(|u_{1}-u_{2}|):=\left\langle(\sqrt{2}\mathop{:}\cos(\sqrt{\pi}\varphi)\mathop{:})_{U}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{sG}(-\frac{2m}{\pi}|\mathbb{C})}=\lim_{R\to\infty}\left\langle(\sqrt{2}\mathop{:}\cos(\sqrt{\pi}\varphi)\mathop{:})_{U}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{sG}(-\frac{2m}{\pi}|B_{R})},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{S}(|u_{1}-u_{2}|):=\left\langle(\sqrt{2}\mathop{:}\sin(\sqrt{\pi}\varphi)\mathop{:})_{U}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{sG}(-\frac{2m}{\pi}|\mathbb{C})}=\lim_{R\to\infty}\left\langle(\sqrt{2}\mathop{:}\sin(\sqrt{\pi}\varphi)\mathop{:})_{U}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{sG}(-\frac{2m}{\pi}|B_{R})},

exist, and may be written

\displaystyle\mathfrak{C}(r)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{C}_{\sigma}^{-4}(8|m|)^{1/2}\cosh^{2}h_{0}(|m|r)\exp\left(2\int_{\infty}^{|m|r}\left(\frac{(h^{\prime}_{0}(s))^{2}}{4}-4\sinh^{2}h_{0}(s)\right)ds\right),

\displaystyle\mathfrak{S}(r)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{C}_{\sigma}^{-4}(8|m|)^{1/2}\sinh^{2}h_{0}(|m|r)\exp\left(2\int_{\infty}^{|m|r}\left(\frac{(h^{\prime}_{0}(s))^{2}}{4}-4\sinh^{2}h_{0}(s)\right)ds\right),

where
\eta_{0}=\exp(-2h_{0})
satisfies the Painlevé III equation
\eta_{0}^{\prime\prime}=\eta_{0}^{-1}(\eta^{\prime}_{0})^{2}-r^{-1}\eta^{\prime}_{0}+\eta_{0}^{3}-\eta_{0}^{-1}
and has the asymptotic
\eta_{0}(\theta)\sim 1-\frac{2}{\pi}K_{0}(2\theta)
as
\theta\to\infty
, where
K_{\nu}
is the modified Bessel function of the second kind.

Proof.

For the infinite volume limit, see e.g.
[
22
, the proof of Corollary 4.21]
for the Ising correlation, and the sine-Gordon correlation is simply its square. Although in
[
22
]
the result is stated in terms of the spin correlation for
m>0
with free boundary conditions, it is elementary to state it in the
m<0
setting with plus boundary conditions (which is the case for our continuous correlations model) using Kramers-Wannier duality, e.g.
[
22
, Remark 4.10, Theorem 4.20]
. Then the expression in terms of the Painlevé III transcendent is a celebrated result from
[
64
]
, see also
[
54
, Section 4.2]
or
[
56
]
for a recent exposition.
∎

One may wish to compare these results to the main results of
[
7
]
, where sine-Gordon correlation functions of
\mathop{:}e^{i\sqrt{4\pi}\gamma\varphi}\mathop{:}
with
|\gamma|<1/2
were constructed, proven to be related to those of so-called twisted fermions, and to satisfy a differential equation related to Painlevé equations as well. A significant difference is that our correlation functions correspond to the boundary case
\gamma=\pm 1/2
for which the fermionic analysis in
[
7
]
breaks down. Also our analysis on the Ising side relies on entirely different ideas than the fermionic analysis in
[
7
]
.

We now turn to stating the main tools we need for proving Theorem
1.1
. We begin with the main statement on the Ising side. Note that this result is valid for more general
\alpha
than in Theorem
1.1
.

Theorem 1.4
.

(1)

For a smooth, bounded, simply connected domain
\Omega\subset\mathbb{C}
and
\alpha:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
smooth up to the boundary, the Ising correlation functions

\left\langle\sigma_{V}\mu_{U}\psi_{Z}\psi^{*}_{W}\varepsilon_{A}\right\rangle_{\alpha;\Omega}

exist and are locally uniformly bounded, in the sense that their values are bounded by a constant only depending on
\Omega
,
\alpha
, the number of insertion points, and the minimum distance among them and the boundary. Consequently, the double Ising model correlation functions

\left\langle(\sigma\widetilde{\sigma})_{V}(\mu\widetilde{\mu})_{U}(\psi\widetilde{\psi})_{Z}(\psi^{*}\widetilde{\psi}^{*})_{W}(\varepsilon+\widetilde{\varepsilon})_{A}\right\rangle_{\alpha;\Omega}

exist and are locally uniformly bounded as well.

(2)

The correlations
\left\langle\sigma_{V}\mu_{U}\psi_{Z}\psi^{*}_{W}\varepsilon_{A}\right\rangle_{m\alpha;\Omega}
are analytic as functions of
m
in a neighborhood of
\mathbb{R}
(for each
m_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
, there exists a convergent Taylor series expansion for
m
in some neighborhood of
m_{0}
) and are continuous functions of the insertion points indexed by
U,V,Z,W,A
, away from the diagonal and the boundary.
Again, this translates immediately to double Ising correlation functions.

(3)

Around
m=0
, we have the Taylor expansions

(1.4.1)

\langle\sigma_{V}\widetilde{\sigma}_{V}\mu_{U}\widetilde{\mu}_{U}(\psi\widetilde{\psi})_{Z}(\psi^{*}\widetilde{\psi}^{*})_{W}(\varepsilon+\widetilde{\varepsilon})_{A}\rangle_{m\alpha;\Omega}\\
=\sum_{r=0}^{\infty}\left(-\frac{m}{\pi}\right)^{r}\frac{1}{r!}\int_{\Omega^{r}}d^{2r}x\prod_{j=1}^{r}\alpha(x_{j})\sum_{I\subset A}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}K\subset[r]:\\
|K|=|I|\end{subarray}}\sum_{\nu:I\leftrightarrow K}\prod_{i\in I}\left(-\langle\varepsilon_{a_{i}}+\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{a_{i}};\varepsilon_{x_{\nu(i)}}+\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{x_{\nu(i)}}\rangle_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)\\
\times\left\langle\sigma_{V}\widetilde{\sigma}_{V}\mu_{U}\widetilde{\mu}_{U}(\psi\widetilde{\psi})_{Z}(\psi^{*}\widetilde{\psi}^{*})_{W}(\varepsilon+\widetilde{\varepsilon})_{A\setminus I};(\varepsilon+\widetilde{\varepsilon})_{x_{b(1)}};\dots;(\varepsilon+\widetilde{\varepsilon})_{x_{b(r-|K|)}}\right\rangle_{0;\Omega}^{\mathsf{T}}

where
\nu:I\leftrightarrow K
is a bijection from
I
to
K
,
b(1),\dots,b(r-|K|)
is an enumeration of
\{1,\dots,r\}\setminus K
, and
\langle\cdots\rangle_{0;\Omega}^{\mathsf{T}}
denotes a “cumulant” in that it is defined through the moments to cumulants map from the Ising correlation functions:

(1.4.2)

\langle X_{1};\dots;X_{n}\rangle^{\mathsf{T}}=\sum_{\lambda\in\Pi_{n}}(-1)^{|\lambda|-1}(|\lambda|-1)!\prod_{B\in\lambda}\bigg\langle\prod_{b\in B}X_{b}\bigg\rangle,

where
\Pi_{n}
denotes the set of partitions of
[n]=\{1,\dots,n\}
, and
|\lambda|
the number of parts of the partition
\lambda\in\Pi_{n}
.

A useful way of writing the cumulant that we use in our analysis of these correlation functions (and is readily checked from (
1.4.2
)) is

(1.4.3)

\displaystyle\left\langle\sigma_{V}\widetilde{\sigma}_{V}\mu_{U}\widetilde{\mu}_{U}\Psi_{Z}\widetilde{\Psi}_{Z}(\varepsilon+\widetilde{\varepsilon})_{W\setminus I};(\varepsilon+\widetilde{\varepsilon})_{x_{b(1)}};\dots;(\varepsilon+\widetilde{\varepsilon})_{x_{b(r-|K|)}}\right\rangle_{0;\Omega}^{\mathsf{T}}

\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{C\subset[r]\setminus K}\bigg\langle\sigma_{V}\widetilde{\sigma}_{V}\mu_{U}\widetilde{\mu}_{U}\Psi_{Z}\widetilde{\Psi}_{Z}(\varepsilon+\widetilde{\varepsilon})_{W\setminus I}\prod_{j\in C}(\varepsilon+\widetilde{\varepsilon})_{x_{j}}\bigg\rangle_{0;\Omega}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\sum_{\lambda\in\Pi_{[r]\setminus(K\cup C)}}(-1)^{|\lambda|}|\lambda|!\prod_{B\in\lambda}\bigg\langle\prod_{k\in B}(\varepsilon+\widetilde{\varepsilon})_{x_{k}}\bigg\rangle_{0;\Omega},

where for a set
A
,
\Pi_{A}
denotes the set of partitions of
A
and
\Psi_{Z}=\prod_{i\in Z}\Psi_{z_{i}}
, where
\Psi_{z_{i}}
stands for
\psi_{z_{i}}
or
\psi_{z_{i}}^{*}
.

We choose to state the explicit expansion only for the doubled correlation, since it is the one used directly in the proof of bosonization; similar techniques as in the proof of Theorem
1.4
(namely, a combination of Proposition
7.2
and Lemma
7.12
) should also yield an analogous explicit expansion for the correlation
\left\langle\sigma_{V}\mu_{U}\psi_{Z}\psi^{*}_{W}\varepsilon_{A}\right\rangle_{m\alpha;\Omega}
.

On the sine-Gordon side, the corresponding result is of the following form.

Theorem 1.5
.

Let
\Omega
be as in Theorem
1.1
and
f_{1},\dots,f_{p}
,
f_{1}^{\prime},\dots,f_{p^{\prime}}^{\prime}
,
g_{1},\dots,g_{q}
,
g_{1}^{\prime},\dots,g_{q^{\prime}}^{\prime}
,
h_{1},\dots,h_{r}

\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
with disjoint supports.

(1)

For
\rho\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
, the smeared correlation functions

\left\langle\prod_{j=1}^{p}\partial\varphi(f_{j})\prod_{j^{\prime}=1}^{p^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}\varphi(f_{j^{\prime}}^{\prime})\prod_{k=1}^{q}\!\mathop{:}\cos(\sqrt{\pi}\varphi)\mathop{:}(g_{k})\prod_{k^{\prime}=1}^{q^{\prime}}\!\mathop{:}\sin(\sqrt{\pi}\varphi)\mathop{:}(g_{k^{\prime}}^{\prime})\prod_{l=1}^{r}\mathop{\typecolon}\cos(\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi)\mathop{\typecolon}(h_{l})\right\rangle_{\mathrm{sG}(\rho|\Omega)}\\
\quad=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\Omega^{p+p^{\prime}+q+q^{\prime}+r}}d^{2p}zd^{2p^{\prime}}wd^{2q}ud^{2q^{\prime}}vd^{2r}a\prod_{j=1}^{p}(-\partial f_{j}(z_{j}))\prod_{j^{\prime}=1}^{p^{\prime}}(-\bar{\partial}f_{j^{\prime}}^{\prime}(w_{j^{\prime}}))\prod_{k=1}^{q}g_{k}(u_{k})\\
\qquad\times\prod_{k^{\prime}=1}^{q^{\prime}}g_{k^{\prime}}^{\prime}(v_{k^{\prime}})\prod_{l=1}^{r}h_{l}(a_{l})\Bigg\langle\prod_{j=1}^{p}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(z_{j})\prod_{j^{\prime}=1}^{p^{\prime}}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(w_{j^{\prime}})\prod_{k=1}^{q}\mathop{:}\cos(\sqrt{\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(u_{k}))\mathop{:}\\
\times\prod_{k^{\prime}=1}^{q^{\prime}}\mathop{:}\sin(\sqrt{\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(v_{k^{\prime}}))\mathop{:}\prod_{l=1}^{r}\mathop{\typecolon}\cos(\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(a_{l}))\mathop{\typecolon}\Bigg\rangle_{\mathrm{sG}(\rho|\varepsilon,\Omega)}

exist.

(2)

For each
\rho\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
, there exists a complex neighborhood of the real axis into which the function

\mu\!\mapsto\!\Bigg\langle\!\prod_{j=1}^{p}\!\partial\varphi(f_{j})\!\prod_{j^{\prime}=1}^{p^{\prime}}\!\bar{\partial}\varphi(f_{j^{\prime}}^{\prime})\!\prod_{k=1}^{q}\!\mathop{:}\cos(\sqrt{\pi}\varphi)\mathop{:}(g_{k})\!\prod_{k^{\prime}=1}^{q^{\prime}}\!\mathop{:}\sin(\sqrt{\pi}\varphi)\mathop{:}(g_{k^{\prime}}^{\prime})\!\prod_{l=1}^{r}\!\mathop{\typecolon}\cos(\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi)\mathop{\typecolon}(h_{l})\!\Bigg\rangle_{\mathrm{sG}(\mu\rho|\Omega)}

has an analytic continuation.

(3)

The Taylor coefficients at zero of this analytic function are given by

\frac{\partial^{n}}{\partial\mu^{n}}\bigg|_{\mu=0}\Bigg\langle\prod_{j=1}^{p}\partial\varphi(f_{j})\prod_{j^{\prime}=1}^{p^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}\varphi(f_{j^{\prime}}^{\prime})\prod_{k=1}^{q}\mathop{:}\cos(\sqrt{\pi}\varphi)\mathop{:}(g_{k})\\
\times\prod_{k^{\prime}=1}^{q^{\prime}}\mathop{:}\sin(\sqrt{\pi}\varphi)\mathop{:}(g_{k^{\prime}}^{\prime})\prod_{l=1}^{r}\mathop{\typecolon}\cos(\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi)\mathop{\typecolon}(h_{l})\Bigg\rangle_{\mathrm{sG}(\mu\rho|\Omega)}\\
=\int_{\Omega^{p+p^{\prime}+q+q^{\prime}+r+n}}d^{2p}zd^{2p^{\prime}}wd^{2q}ud^{2q^{\prime}}vd^{2r}ad^{2n}b\,\prod_{j=1}^{p}(-\partial f_{j}(z_{j}))\prod_{j^{\prime}=1}^{p^{\prime}}(-\bar{\partial}f_{j^{\prime}}^{\prime}(w_{j^{\prime}}))\prod_{k=1}^{q}g_{k}(u_{k})\\
\qquad\times\prod_{k^{\prime}=1}^{q^{\prime}}g_{k^{\prime}}^{\prime}(v_{k^{\prime}})\prod_{l=1}^{r}h_{l}(a_{l})\prod_{m=1}^{n}\rho(b_{m})\sum_{S\subset[r]}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S^{\prime}\subset[n]:\\
|S|=|S^{\prime}|\end{subarray}}\sum_{\pi:S\leftrightarrow S^{\prime}}\prod_{s\in S}(-\mathcal{A}(a_{s},b_{\pi(s)}))\\
\qquad\times\Bigg\langle\prod_{j=1}^{p}\varphi(z_{j})\prod_{j^{\prime}=1}^{p^{\prime}}\varphi(w_{j^{\prime}})\prod_{k=1}^{q}\mathop{:}\cos(\sqrt{\pi}\varphi(u_{k}))\mathop{:}\prod_{k^{\prime}=1}^{q^{\prime}}\mathop{:}\sin(\sqrt{\pi}\varphi(v_{k^{\prime}}))\mathop{:}\\
\prod_{l\notin S}\mathop{:}\cos(\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi(a_{l}))\mathop{:};\mathop{:}\cos(\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi(b_{\alpha_{1}}))\mathop{:};\dots;\mathop{:}\cos(\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi(b_{\alpha_{n-|S^{\prime}|}}))\mathop{:}\Bigg\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}}^{\mathsf{T}},

where
\{1,\dots,n\}\setminus S^{\prime}=\{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n-|S^{\prime}|}\}
,
\mathcal{A}(x,y)=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathcal{A}(x,y|\varepsilon)
(existence of this limit is justified by Lemma
2.6
), the sum over
\pi
is over bijections between
S
and
S^{\prime}
, and
\langle A_{1};\dots;A_{n}\rangle^{\mathsf{T}}
denotes a cumulant, which in this statement is defined through the usual moments to cumulants map and Lemma
2.6
. Part of the statement is that the integrand is an integrable function.

Using these two results, we can now prove Theorem
1.1
. A key fact that we use here is the bosonization dictionary of Table
1
(with
\mathop{\typecolon}\cos(\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi)\mathop{\typecolon}
replaced by
\mathop{:}\cos(\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi)\mathop{:}
) holds for the critical Ising model due to
[
9
]
. The setup there allows for multiply connected domains with mixed boundary conditions; see
[
9
, Proposition 2.15, Remark 2.16]
for an explanation that the main theorem is valid for our case, i.e. simply connected domain with plus boundary conditions. The precise fact we need is as follows.
2
2

2

There is a minor typo in
[
9
, Remark 2.11]
. It is stated there that
\frac{1}{2}(\varepsilon+\widetilde{\varepsilon})
corresponds to
2\mathop{:}\cos(\sqrt{2}\Phi)\mathop{:}
, when it should be just
\mathop{:}\cos(\sqrt{2}\Phi)\mathop{:}
, as stated in Proposition
1.6
. Note that
\Phi
in
[
9
]
is our
\sqrt{2\pi}\varphi
. To check that this is indeed a typo, we offer here a brief argument (essentially the one of
[
9
, Remark 2.11]
) – the reader can readily make this precise by viewing these identities as holding within appropriate correlation functions. For
\gamma=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}
,
[
9
, (2.20)]
yields

\mathop{:}\cos(\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\Phi(z))\mathop{:}\mathop{:}\cos(\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\Phi(w))\mathop{:}=\tfrac{1}{2}|z-w|^{-1/2}(1+\mathop{:}\cos(\sqrt{2}\Phi(w))\mathop{:}|z-w|+O(|z-w|^{2}))

On the other hand, the Ising OPE
[
21
, (6.12)]
implies that

\sigma_{z}\sigma_{w}=|z-w|^{-1/4}(1+\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{w}|z-w|+o(|z-w|))

so using the bosonization identity for spin-observables,
\sigma\widetilde{\sigma}=\sqrt{2}\mathop{:}\cos(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\Phi)\mathop{:}
, we have on the one hand

\sigma_{z}\widetilde{\sigma}_{z}\sigma_{w}\widetilde{\sigma}_{w}=|z-w|^{-1/2}(1+\tfrac{1}{2}(\varepsilon_{w}+\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{w})|z-w|+o(|z-w|))

and on the other hand

\sigma_{z}\widetilde{\sigma}_{z}\sigma_{w}\widetilde{\sigma}_{w}=\sqrt{2}\mathop{:}\cos(\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\Phi(z))\mathop{:}\sqrt{2}\mathop{:}\cos(\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\Phi(w))\mathop{:}=|z-w|^{-1/2}(1+\mathop{:}\cos(\sqrt{2}\Phi(w))\mathop{:}|z-w|+O(|z-w|^{2})).

Equating coefficients gives the correct identity.

Proposition 1.6

(
[
9
, Theorem 1.1, Remark 2.11]
)
.

We have

\displaystyle\left\langle\sigma_{V}\widetilde{\sigma}_{V}\mu_{U}\widetilde{\mu}_{U}\psi_{Z}\widetilde{\psi}_{Z}\psi_{W}^{*}\widetilde{\psi}_{W}^{*}(\varepsilon+\widetilde{\varepsilon})_{X}\right\rangle_{0;\Omega}

\displaystyle=\left\langle(\sqrt{2}\mathop{:}\cos(\sqrt{\pi}\varphi)\mathop{:})_{V}(\sqrt{2}\mathop{:}\sin(\sqrt{\pi}\varphi)\mathop{:})_{U}(4i\sqrt{\pi}\partial\varphi)_{Z}(-4i\sqrt{\pi}\bar{\partial}\varphi)_{W}(2\mathop{:}\cos(\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi)\mathop{:})_{X}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}}.

Proof of Theorem
1.1
.

Existence of the Ising correlation functions follows from item (1) of Theorem
1.4
while the existence of the sine-Gordon correlation functions (in the sense of distributions) follows from item (2) of Theorem
1.5
.

By items (1) and (2) of Theorem
1.4
, for
f_{1},\dots,f_{p}
,
f_{1}^{\prime},\dots,f_{p^{\prime}}^{\prime}
,
g_{1},\dots,g_{q}
,
g_{1}^{\prime},\dots,g_{q^{\prime}}^{\prime}
,
h_{1},\dots,h_{r}\allowbreak\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
with disjoint supports and
\alpha\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
(whose support need not be disjoint from the other ones) the functions

m\mapsto\int_{\Omega^{p+p^{\prime}+q+q^{\prime}+r}}d^{2p}zd^{2p^{\prime}}wd^{2q}vd^{2q^{\prime}}ud^{2r}a\prod_{j=1}^{p}f_{j}(z_{j})\prod_{j^{\prime}=1}^{p^{\prime}}f^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}}(w_{j^{\prime}})\prod_{k=1}^{q}g_{k}(v_{k})\prod_{k^{\prime}=1}^{q^{\prime}}g_{k^{\prime}}^{\prime}(u_{k^{\prime}})\\
\times\prod_{l=1}^{r}h_{l}(a_{l})\left\langle(\sigma\widetilde{\sigma})_{V}(\mu\widetilde{\mu})_{U}(\psi\widetilde{\psi})_{Z}(\psi^{*}\widetilde{\psi}^{*})_{W}(\varepsilon+\widetilde{\varepsilon})_{A}\right\rangle_{m\alpha;\Omega}.

are well defined for
m\in\mathbb{R}
and analytic in
m
in a neighborhood of
\mathbb{R}
. Moreover, by item (3) of Theorem
1.4
, the
n
th Taylor coefficient of this analytic function at zero is

\left(-\frac{1}{\pi}\right)^{n}\int_{\Omega^{p+p^{\prime}+q+q^{\prime}+r+n}}d^{2p}zd^{2p^{\prime}}wd^{2q}vd^{2q^{\prime}}ud^{2r}ad^{2n}b\prod_{j=1}^{p}f_{j}(z_{j})\prod_{j^{\prime}=1}^{p^{\prime}}f^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}}(w_{j^{\prime}})\prod_{k=1}^{q}g_{k}(v_{k})\\
\times\prod_{k^{\prime}=1}^{q^{\prime}}g_{k^{\prime}}^{\prime}(u_{k^{\prime}})\prod_{l=1}^{r}h_{l}(a_{l})\prod_{m=1}^{n}\alpha(b_{m})\sum_{S\subset[r]}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S^{\prime}\subset[n]:\\
|S|=|S^{\prime}|\end{subarray}}\sum_{\pi:S\leftrightarrow S^{\prime}}\prod_{s\in S}\left(-\left\langle\varepsilon_{a_{s}}+\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{a_{s}};\varepsilon_{b_{\pi(s)}}+\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{b_{\pi(s)}}\right\rangle_{0;\Omega}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)\\
\times\left\langle(\sigma\widetilde{\sigma})_{V}(\mu\widetilde{\mu})_{U}(\psi\widetilde{\psi})_{Z}(\psi^{*}\widetilde{\psi}^{*})_{W}(\varepsilon+\widetilde{\varepsilon})_{A\setminus S};\varepsilon_{b_{\alpha_{1}}}+\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{b_{\alpha_{1}}};\dots;\varepsilon_{b_{\alpha_{n-|S^{\prime}|}}}+\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{b_{\alpha_{n-|S^{\prime}|}}}\right\rangle_{0;\Omega}^{\mathsf{T}}.

We now make use of Proposition
1.6
which says that the critical Ising correlation functions appearing in this series expansion can be expressed in terms of GFF correlation functions using Table
1
(with
\mathop{\typecolon}\cos(\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi)\mathop{\typecolon}
replaced by
\mathop{:}\cos(\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi)\mathop{:}
). A couple of remarks are in order here. First of all,
[
9
]
deals with usual correlation functions (or moments) instead of cumulants, but as cumulants can be expressed in terms of moments (by the usual moments to cumulants map (
1.4.2
)), we can also use the bosonization dictionary for cumulants. Secondly, the GFF correlation functions defined in
[
9
, Section 2]
are a priori defined differently from ours (in Section
2
), but in Lemma
2.9
, we argue that they are indeed the same functions.

This means that for
m
in our neighborhood of the origin, we can write this Taylor coefficient as

\displaystyle\left(-\frac{1}{\pi}\right)^{n}\!\int_{\Omega^{p+p^{\prime}+q+q^{\prime}+r+n}}d^{2p}zd^{2p^{\prime}}wd^{2q}vd^{2q^{\prime}}ud^{2r}ad^{2n}b

\displaystyle\qquad\prod_{j=1}^{p}f_{j}(z_{j})\prod_{j^{\prime}=1}^{p^{\prime}}f^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}}(w_{j^{\prime}})\prod_{k=1}^{q}g_{k}(v_{k})\prod_{k^{\prime}=1}^{q^{\prime}}g_{k^{\prime}}^{\prime}(u_{k^{\prime}})\prod_{l=1}^{r}h_{l}(a_{l})\prod_{m=1}^{n}\alpha(b_{m})

\displaystyle\qquad\times\sum_{S\subset[r]}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S^{\prime}\subset[n]:\\
|S|=|S^{\prime}|\end{subarray}}\sum_{\pi:S\leftrightarrow S^{\prime}}\prod_{s\in S}\left(-\left\langle\big(2\mathop{:}\cos\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi(a_{s})\mathop{:}\big);\big(2\mathop{:}\cos\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi(b_{\pi(s)})\mathop{:}\big)\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\Big\langle\big(\sqrt{2}\mathop{:}\cos\sqrt{\pi}\varphi\mathop{:}\big)_{V}\big(\sqrt{2}\mathop{:}\sin\sqrt{\pi}\varphi\mathop{:}\big)_{U}(4i\sqrt{\pi}\partial\varphi)_{Z}(-4i\sqrt{\pi}\bar{\partial}\varphi)_{W}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\big(2\mathop{:}\cos\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi\mathop{:}\big)_{A\setminus S};\big(2\mathop{:}\cos\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi(b_{\alpha_{1}})\mathop{:}\big);\dots;\big(2\mathop{:}\cos\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi(b_{\alpha_{n-|S^{\prime}|}})\mathop{:}\big)\Big\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}}^{\mathsf{T}}.

By Lemma
2.9
, we can write

\displaystyle\Big\langle\big(\sqrt{2}\mathop{:}\cos(\sqrt{\pi}\varphi)\mathop{:}\big)_{V}\big(\sqrt{2}\mathop{:}\sin(\sqrt{\pi}\varphi)\mathop{:}\big)_{U}(4i\sqrt{\pi}\partial\varphi)_{Z}(-4i\sqrt{\pi}\bar{\partial}\varphi)_{W}

\displaystyle\qquad\big(2\mathop{:}\cos(\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi)\mathop{:}\big)_{A\setminus S};\big(2\mathop{:}\cos(\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi(b_{\alpha_{1}}))\mathop{:}\big);\dots;\big(2\mathop{:}\cos(\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi(b_{\alpha_{n-|S^{\prime}|}}))\mathop{:}\big)\Big\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}}^{\mathsf{T}}

\displaystyle=\prod_{j=1}^{p}(4i\sqrt{\pi}\partial_{z_{j}})\prod_{j^{\prime}=1}^{p^{\prime}}(-4i\sqrt{\pi}\bar{\partial}_{w_{j^{\prime}}})\Big\langle\big(\sqrt{2}\mathop{:}\cos(\sqrt{\pi}\varphi)\mathop{:}\big)_{V}\big(\sqrt{2}\mathop{:}\sin(\sqrt{\pi}\varphi)\mathop{:}\big)_{U}\varphi_{Z}\varphi_{W}

\displaystyle\qquad\big(2\mathop{:}\cos(\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi)\mathop{:}\big)_{A\setminus S};\big(\big(2\mathop{:}\cos(\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi(b_{\alpha_{1}}))\mathop{:}\big);\dots;\big(2\mathop{:}\cos(\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi(b_{\alpha_{n-|S^{\prime}|}}))\mathop{:}\big)\Big\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}}^{\mathsf{T}}.

Integrating by parts the
z
and
w
derivatives (which is allowed by the integrability of both functions due to Theorem
1.4
and Theorem
1.5
), we see by Theorem
1.5
that this is precisely the same as the
n
th Taylor coefficient at zero of the analytic function

m\mapsto\bigg\langle\prod_{j=1}^{p}\big(4i\sqrt{\pi}\partial\varphi(f_{j})\big)\prod_{j^{\prime}=1}^{p^{\prime}}\big(-4i\sqrt{\pi}\bar{\partial}\varphi(f_{j^{\prime}}^{\prime})\big)\prod_{k=1}^{q}\big(\sqrt{2}\mathop{:}\cos(\sqrt{\pi}\varphi)\mathop{:}(g_{k})\big)\\
\times\prod_{k^{\prime}=1}^{q^{\prime}}\big(\sqrt{2}\mathop{:}\sin(\sqrt{\pi}\varphi)\mathop{:}(g_{k^{\prime}}^{\prime})\big)\prod_{l=1}^{r}\big(2\mathop{\typecolon}\cos(\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi)\mathop{\typecolon}(h_{l})\big)\bigg\rangle_{\mathrm{sG}(-\frac{2}{\pi}m\alpha|\Omega)}

Since both this sine-Gordon correlation function and the double Ising correlation function we started with are analytic in a neighborhood of
\mathbb{R}
and have the same Taylor expansions at the origin, they must agree everywhere on
\mathbb{R}
.

To conclude, we point out that since we know from Theorem
1.4
that the double Ising correlation functions are in fact continuous functions of the insertion points (away from the diagonal and the boundary) instead of just generalized functions and since the sine-Gordon correlation functions agree with these when integrated against test functions with disjoint supports, we conclude that the sine-Gordon correlation functions can also be understood as being continuous functions instead of just generalized functions.
∎

1.5.
Structure of the article

The rest of the article focuses on proving Theorem
1.4
and Theorem
1.5
. The organization is as follows. In Section
2
, we review the definition and some basic properties of our regularization of the GFF needed in the construction of the sine-Gordon model. In Section
3
, we introduce and study the renormalized potential of the sine-Gordon model with an iterated Mayer expansion following closely ideas introduced in
[
16
]
and further developed in
[
5
,
6
,
7
,
8
]
. In Section
4
, we introduce a renormalized version of the partition function of the sine-Gordon model, and use the results of Section
3
to control analyticity properties of the renormalized partition function (this follows the analysis of
[
8
]
closely). In Section
5
, we finally use our analysis on the renormalized partition function to construct the sine-Gordon correlation functions and prove Theorem
1.5
.

In Section
6
, we turn to analyzing the Ising model and show existence of general correlation functions from the ‘basic correlation functions’, namely spin-weighted two-point fermion-fermion and disorder-fermion correlations. We prove their existence, uniqueness, and various quantitative bounds on them. In Section
7
, we prove analyticity properties and explicit series expansion in a neighborhood of the origin for the relevant correlation functions. In Section
8
, we study the series expansions of the double Ising correlation functions and finally prove Theorem
1.4
in Section
8.3
.

from the case of a disk to a more general domain. While the approach suggested by AI was flawed, some parts of the discussion have influenced our approach to the proof.

2.
The Gaussian Free Field and its Heat Kernel Regularization

In this section, we review the regularization for the Gaussian free field that we use in our construction of the correlation functions of the sine-Gordon model. There are various regularizations one could use, but for the analysis of the sine-Gordon model through the renormalized potential, we find it convenient to mimic the construction of
[
8
]
and consider a regularization using the heat kernel of the domain
\Omega
(in
[
8
]
, the massive heat kernel of the full plane was used instead).

Much of the material in this section is likely to be familiar to experts at least in some form. We however rely heavily on the heat kernel regularization for our sine-Gordon analysis and to our knowledge, many of the facts we present here do not exist as such in the literature (while very similar results for other regularizations certainly do).

We begin with a review of some basic estimates involving the heat kernel of a nice domain. We then review the construction of the regularized GFF in terms of this heat kernel as well as some estimates for GFF correlation functions.

2.1.
The heat kernel

Let us write
\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}
for the transition density of (a temporally rescaled) Brownian motion on
\Omega
killed at the boundary of
\Omega
. This transition density is of course also the heat kernel on
\Omega
(with zero boundary conditions). More precisely, if we write
T_{\Omega}
for the first exit time from
\Omega
for (standard planar) Brownian motion,
3
3

3

If necessary, we will write
T_{\Omega}^{x}
to emphasize that the Brownian motion has been started at
x\in\Omega
.
then there exists a non-negative function
\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}:(0,\infty)\times\Omega\times\Omega\to[0,\infty)
such that if
(B(t))_{t\geq 0}
denotes our Brownian motion and
\mathbb{P}_{x}
its law when started at
x
, then for each Borel
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
, each
t>0
and
x\in\Omega
,

(2.1.1)

\mathbb{P}_{x}\big(\{B(2t)\in A\}\cap\{2t\leq T_{\Omega}\}\big)=\int_{A}d^{2}y\,\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(t,x,y).

As discussed in
[
50
, Chapter 3.3]
, a basic fact about
\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}
is that it can be related to the full plane transition density of Brownian motion. More precisely, if we write

\mathsf{p}(t,x,y)=\mathsf{p}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(t,x,y)=\frac{1}{4\pi t}e^{-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4t}}

and let

(2.1.2)

R_{t}(x,y):=\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\mathsf{p}(t-\tfrac{1}{2}T_{\Omega},B(T_{\Omega}),y)\mathbf{1}_{\{T_{\Omega}<2t\}}\right],

then

(2.1.3)

\displaystyle\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(t,x,y)=\mathsf{p}(t,x,y)-R_{t}(x,y).

A simple but useful consequence is that we have the following estimate

0\leq\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(t,x,y)\leq\mathsf{p}(t,x,y).

One can readily check from (
2.1.2
) and (
2.1.3
) the standard fact that
\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}
is really the heat kernel (namely the fundamental solution to the heat equation) of
\Omega
with zero Dirichlet boundary conditions:

(2.1.4)

\begin{cases}(\partial_{t}-\Delta_{x})\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(t,x,y)&=0,\,\qquad\qquad t>0,x,y\in\Omega\\
\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(t,x,y)&=0,\,\qquad\qquad t>0,x\in\partial\Omega,y\in\Omega\\
\lim_{t\to 0}\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(t,x,y)&=\delta(x,y),\qquad x,y\in\Omega\end{cases}.

We will want to use
\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}
to construct a continuous Gaussian process and for this purpose, we will need some regularity estimates for
\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}
. For some of these, we find it convenient to express
\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}
in terms of Laplacian eigenfunctions, but later on, we will also need some regularity estimates for the function
R_{t}
that are derived from the probabilistic representation instead of the basis expansion.

We now discuss how to express
\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}
in terms of Laplacian eigenfunctions. First of all, it is a basic fact that for
\Omega
as in Theorem
1.1
, there exists an orthonormal basis
(e_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
of
L^{2}(\Omega)
such that,
e_{n}
is real, smooth up to the boundary,
-\Delta e_{n}=\lambda_{n}e_{n}
, where
0<\lambda_{1}\leq\lambda_{2}\leq\dots
and
e_{n}
vanishes on the boundary. We record here some standard estimates for
\lambda_{n}
and
e_{n}
.

Lemma 2.1
.

For
\Omega
as in Theorem
1.1
, there exists a universal
C_{\Omega}>0
such that for
n\geq 1
we have the following estimates

\lambda_{n}\geq\frac{2\pi}{|\Omega|}n,

where
|\Omega|
is the Lebesgue measure of
\Omega
,
as well as

\|e_{n}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\leq C_{\Omega}\lambda_{n}^{1/4},\qquad\|\nabla e_{n}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\leq C_{\Omega}\lambda_{n}^{3/4},\qquad\text{and}\qquad\|\partial_{i}\partial_{j}e_{n}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\leq C_{\Omega}\lambda_{n}^{5/4},

for
i,j\in\{1,2\}
.

Proof.

The bound on
\lambda_{n}
is
[
44
, Corollary 1]
.
4
4

4

Note that part of
[
44
]
is in dimension three or more, but the proof of Theorem 1 and Corollary 1 does not require this.
The estimate for
\|e_{n}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}
is
[
34
, Theorem 1]
, the estimate for
\|\nabla e_{n}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}
is
[
59
, Corollary 1.1]
, and the estimate for
\|\partial_{i}\partial_{j}e_{n}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}
is
[
61
, Theorem 2]

∎

In terms of the eigenfunctions and eigenvalues, we can write the heat kernel
\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}
as

(2.1.5)

\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(t,x,y)=e^{t\Delta}(x,y)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}e^{-t\lambda_{n}}e_{n}(x)e_{n}(y).

While it is slightly circular reasoning (some of the estimates of Lemma
2.1
are derived from heat kernel estimates), we point out that Lemma
2.1
implies that the series above converges (uniformly) and it can be differentiated termwise twice. From this fact, one readily checks that the series truly equals
\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}
by noting that, if we write
u(t,x,y)
for this series, then
u
is a smooth function on
(0,\infty)\times\Omega^{2}
which satisfies the heat equation:
(\partial_{t}-\Delta_{x})u(t,x,y)=0
(with zero boundary conditions) and
\lim_{t\to 0}u(t,x,y)=\delta(x,y)
, and since
\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}
is also a solution to the same problem, we must have
u=\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}
by standard uniqueness results for the heat equation.

We now turn to more detailed estimates for the heat kernel that we will need later in our analysis of the GFF and the renormalized potential for the sine-Gordon model. The relevant results are recorded in the following lemma.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
\delta>0
,
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4}\in\Omega
be such that
d(x_{i},\partial\Omega)\geq\delta
. Moreover, let
t_{0}>0
be arbitrary and
R_{t}
be as in (
2.1.2
). Then there exists a
C=C(t_{0},\delta,\Omega)
such that for
t<t_{0}

(2.1.6)

|R_{t}(x_{1},x_{2})|\leq Ce^{-\frac{\delta^{2}}{32t}},

(2.1.7)

\big|R_{t}(x_{1},x_{3})-R_{t}(x_{2},x_{3})\big|\leq C|x_{1}-x_{2}|e^{-\frac{\delta^{2}}{32t}}

and

(2.1.8)

\big|R_{t}(x_{1},x_{3})-R_{t}(x_{1},x_{4})-R_{t}(x_{2},x_{3})+R_{t}(x_{2},x_{4})\big|\leq C|x_{1}-x_{2}||x_{3}-x_{4}|e^{-\frac{\delta^{2}}{200t}}.

Proof.

Before turning to the actual proof, we record the following well known fact: if
d(x,\partial\Omega)\geq\delta
, and
0<t<t_{0}
, then for a constant
C=C(\delta,t_{0})>0
, we have

(2.1.9)

\mathbb{P}_{x}(T_{\Omega}<2t)\leq Ce^{-\frac{\delta^{2}}{32t}}.

For the sake of completeness, we give a short proof. Let
S(x,\delta)
be the square (aligned with the coordinate axes) of side length
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\delta
with midpoint
x
. Then (since
S(x,\delta)\subset\overline{\Omega}
)

\mathbb{P}_{x}(T_{\Omega}<2t)\leq\mathbb{P}_{x}(T_{S(x,\delta)}<2t)=\mathbb{P}_{0}(T_{S(0,\delta)}<2t)\leq 2\mathbb{P}_{0}\left(\sup_{0<s<2t}|W_{s}|>\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}\delta\right),

where
W
denotes one-dimensional standard Brownian motion and for the last bound, the reasoning is that for 2d-Brownian motion to leave the square, at least one of its coordinates has to leave the interval
\left[-\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}\delta,\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}\delta\right]
. Next note that since
W\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}-W
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{0}\left(\sup_{0<s<2t}|W_{s}|>\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}\delta\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{0}\left(\left\{\sup_{0<s<2t}W_{s}>\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}\delta\right\}\bigcup\left\{\sup_{0<s<2t}(-W_{s})>\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}\delta\right\}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathbb{P}_{0}\left(\sup_{0<s<2t}W_{s}>\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}\delta\right).

Now by the reflection principle, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{0}\left(\sup_{0<s<2t}W_{s}>\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}\delta\right)=2\mathbb{P}_{0}\left(W_{2t}>\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}\delta\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\sqrt{2\pi 2t}}\int_{\frac{\delta}{2\sqrt{2}}}^{\infty}e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{4t}}du

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi t}}\int_{\frac{\delta}{2\sqrt{2}}}^{\infty}\frac{u}{\delta/(2\sqrt{2})}e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{4t}}du

\displaystyle=\frac{4\sqrt{2t}}{\delta\sqrt{\pi}}\int_{\frac{\delta}{2\sqrt{2}}}^{\infty}\frac{u}{2t}e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{4t}}du

\displaystyle=\frac{4\sqrt{2t}}{\delta\sqrt{\pi}}e^{-\frac{\delta^{2}}{32t}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4\sqrt{2t_{0}}}{\delta\sqrt{\pi}}e^{-\frac{\delta^{2}}{32t}}.

This shows (
2.1.9
).

Let us now turn to the actual proof. For (
2.1.6
), we recall from (
2.1.2
) that

\displaystyle R_{t}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\frac{1}{4\pi(t-\frac{1}{2}T_{\Omega})}e^{-\frac{|y-B(T_{\Omega})|^{2}}{4(t-\frac{1}{2}T_{\Omega})}}\mathbf{1}_{\{T_{\Omega}<2t\}}\right]

We then note that since
x\mapsto xe^{-x}
is bounded on
(0,\infty)
, there exists a universal constant
c>0
such that

\frac{1}{4\pi(t-\frac{1}{2}T_{\Omega})}e^{-\frac{|y-B(T_{\Omega})|^{2}}{4(t-\frac{1}{2}T_{\Omega})}}\leq\frac{c}{|y-B(T_{\Omega})|^{2}}\leq\frac{C}{\delta^{2}}.

Thus by (
2.1.9
), there exists some
C=C(\delta,t_{0})
such that

R_{t}(x,y)\leq\frac{c}{\delta^{2}}\mathbb{P}_{x}(T_{\Omega}<2t)\leq\frac{c}{\delta^{2}}C(\delta,t_{0})e^{-\frac{\delta^{2}}{32t}},

which yields (
2.1.6
).

For (
2.1.7
), we first of all note that e.g. by (
2.1.5
) and (
2.1.3
),
R_{t}(x,y)=R_{t}(y,x)
. Next we note that by the simple inequality

|e^{-a}-e^{-b}|\leq|a-b|\max(e^{-a},e^{-b})

for
a,b\geq 0
, we have

\displaystyle|\mathsf{p}(s,x,y)-\mathsf{p}(s,x,z)|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{16\pi s^{2}}\big||x-y|^{2}-|x-z|^{2}\big|\max\left(e^{-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4s}},e^{-\frac{|x-z|^{2}}{4s}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathrm{diam}(\Omega)}{8\pi s^{2}}|y-z|\max\left(e^{-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4s}},e^{-\frac{|x-z|^{2}}{4s}}\right).

Thus, under our assumption that
d(x_{i},\partial\Omega)\geq\delta
(so that
|x_{i}-B(T_{\Omega})|\geq\delta
)

\displaystyle|R_{t}(x_{1},x_{3})-R_{t}(x_{2},x_{3})|

\displaystyle=|R_{t}(x_{3},x_{1})-R_{t}(x_{3},x_{2})|

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{x_{3}}\left[|\mathsf{p}(t-\tfrac{1}{2}T_{\Omega},B(T_{\Omega}),x_{1})-\mathsf{p}(t-\tfrac{1}{2}T_{\Omega},B(T_{\Omega}),x_{2})|\mathbf{1}_{\{T_{\Omega}<2t\}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathrm{diam}(\Omega)}{8\pi}|x_{1}-x_{2}|\mathbb{E}_{x_{3}}\left[\frac{1}{(t-\frac{1}{2}T_{\Omega})^{2}}e^{-\frac{\delta^{2}}{4(t-\frac{1}{2}T_{\Omega})}}\mathbf{1}_{\{T_{\Omega}<2t\}}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{2\mathrm{diam}(\Omega)}{\pi\delta^{4}}|x_{1}-x_{2}|\mathbb{E}_{x_{3}}\left[\frac{\delta^{4}}{(4(t-\frac{1}{2}T_{\Omega}))^{2}}e^{-\frac{\delta^{2}}{4(t-\frac{1}{2}T_{\Omega})}}\mathbf{1}_{\{T_{\Omega}<2t\}}\right]

Since
x\mapsto x^{2}e^{-x}
is bounded on
(0,\infty)
, we see that for some universal constant
c>0
,

|R_{t}(x_{1},x_{3})-R_{t}(x_{2},x_{3})|\leq c\frac{\mathrm{diam}(\Omega)}{\delta^{4}}|x_{1}-x_{2}|\mathbb{P}_{x_{3}}(T_{\Omega}<2t)

from which (
2.1.9
) concludes the proof of (
2.1.7
).

For (
2.1.8
), we take a PDE approach instead of a probabilistic one – more precisely, we use local interior gradient estimates for solutions of the heat equation.

Let us write
u(t,x)=R_{t}(x,x_{3})-R_{t}(x,x_{4})
. Our goal is to show that
|u(t,x_{1})-u(t,x_{2})|\leq C|x_{1}-x_{2}||x_{3}-x_{4}|e^{-\frac{\delta^{2}}{200t}}
. For this we note that
u
satisfies the heat equation in
(0,\infty)\times\Omega
(since
(t,x)\mapsto R_{t}(x,x_{i})
does as it is given by the difference of two fundamental solutions to the heat equation). Note though that we are not specifying the boundary conditions of
u
on
\partial\Omega
.

The local interior gradient estimate we use is a special case of
[
30
, Theorem 2.3.9]
, which we recall here. First of all, we introduce cylinders: for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
t>0
, and
0<r<\sqrt{t}
, let

(2.1.10)

C(x,t;r)=\{(y,s)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:|x-y|\leq r,t-r^{2}\leq s\leq t\}.

We also write for any
T>0
,
\Omega_{T}=(0,T]\times\Omega
. Then a special case of
[
30
, Theorem 2.3.9]
states that for any
u
that solves the heat equation in
\Omega_{T}
(with any reasonable boundary conditions) and any cylinders
C(x,t;\frac{r}{2})\subset C(x,t;r)\subset\Omega_{T}
, we have

(2.1.11)

\|\nabla u\|_{L^{\infty}(C(x,t;\frac{r}{2}))}\leq\frac{C}{r^{5}}\|u\|_{L^{1}(C(x,t;r))}

for some universal constant
C
.

We apply this to our
u(t,x)=R_{t}(x,x_{3})-R_{t}(x,x_{4})
with
r=\min(\sqrt{\frac{t}{2}},\frac{\delta}{2})
. We find from (
2.1.7
) (applied with
\delta
replaced with
\frac{\delta}{2}
) that

\displaystyle|\nabla u(t,x)|

\displaystyle\leq\|\nabla u\|_{L^{\infty}(C(x,t;\frac{r}{2}))}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{r^{5}}\|u\|_{L^{1}(C(x,t;r))}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\tilde{C}}{r^{5}}|x_{3}-x_{4}|\int_{t-r^{2}}^{t}\int_{B(x,r)}e^{-\frac{\delta^{2}}{128s}}dyds

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\pi\tilde{C}}{r}|x_{3}-x_{4}|e^{-\frac{\delta^{2}}{128t}}

\displaystyle\leq C(\delta,t_{0},\Omega)|x_{3}-x_{4}|e^{-\frac{\delta^{2}}{200t}}

for suitable constants (
C
being universal, while
\tilde{C},C(\delta,t_{0},\Omega)
depending on
\delta,t_{0},\Omega
). Thus
u
is locally Lipschitz in the spatial variable and by compactness, we recover the claim.
∎

We now turn to the GFF.

2.2.
The heat kernel regularization of the GFF

We can now use the heat kernel to construct our regularized GFF.

Lemma 2.3
.

For any
\varepsilon>0
,

(2.2.1)

C_{\varepsilon}(x,y)=\int_{\varepsilon^{2}}^{\infty}ds\,\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x,y)

is the covariance of a continuous Gaussian process
\varphi_{\varepsilon}
on
\Omega
.

Proof.

As this result is rather standard, we will be a bit brief on details and refer to the literature on various facts.

Let us first prove that
C_{\varepsilon}
is symmetric and positive definite. Symmetry, namely that
\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x,y)\allowbreak=\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,y,x)
is proven in
[
50
, Theorem 3.30]
. For positive definiteness, we use the fact that
\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}
is a transition density (also see
[
50
, Theorem 3.30]
) and argue that by symmetry, we have for any
s>0
,
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\in\Omega
and
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}\in\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{i},x_{j})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}\int_{\Omega}d^{2}z\,\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(\tfrac{s}{2},x_{i},z)\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(\tfrac{s}{2},z,x_{j})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}\int_{\Omega}d^{2}z\,\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(\tfrac{s}{2},x_{i},z)\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(\tfrac{s}{2},x_{j},z)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}d^{2}z\,\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(\tfrac{s}{2},x_{i},z)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\geq 0.

Thus for each
s>0
,
\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,\cdot,\cdot)
is symmetric and positive definite, which implies that also
C_{\varepsilon}
is as well. Thus there is some Gaussian process
\varphi_{\varepsilon}
with this covariance.

Using standard regularity theory of Gaussian processes (see e.g.
[
41
, Appendix B]
), we can choose
\varphi_{\varepsilon}
to be continuous if

\left|C_{\varepsilon}(x,x)+C_{\varepsilon}(y,y)-2C_{\varepsilon}(x,y)\right|\leq A|x-y|^{\gamma}

for some
A,\gamma>0
. This follows readily from (
2.1.5
) and Lemma
2.1
.
∎

Next we record some basic facts about GFF-correlation functions in the
\varepsilon\to 0
limit.

We will find it convenient to write
\langle\cdot\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}(\varepsilon)}
for expectation with respect to the law of
\varphi_{\varepsilon}
. The reader may be familiar with the notion that the GFF on a domain
\Omega
is a Gaussian process whose covariance is the Green’s function of
\Omega
. The following lemma connects our definition of
\varphi_{\varepsilon}
as the regularized GFF to this notion.

Lemma 2.4
.

The function
G_{\Omega}:\Omega\times\Omega\to(-\infty,\infty]
,

(2.2.2)

G_{\Omega}(x,y)=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}C_{\varepsilon}(x,y)=\int_{0}^{\infty}ds\,\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x,y)

is the Green’s function of
\Omega
:

(2.2.3)

G_{\Omega}(x,y)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\log|x-y|^{-1}+g_{\Omega}(x,y),

where
g
is harmonic in
x\in\Omega
(and in
y
), symmetric, smooth up to the boundary, and
g(x,y)=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\log|x-y|^{-1}
for
x\in\partial\Omega
and
y\in\Omega
.

Proof.

This is standard – see for example
[
50
, Theorem 3.35]
.
∎

The previous lemma can also be viewed as a statement about correlation functions of the regularized GFF: it states that for fixed
x,y\in\Omega
with
x\neq y

(2.2.4)

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\langle\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x)\varphi_{\varepsilon}(y)\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}(\varepsilon)}=\langle\varphi(x)\varphi(y)\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}}:=G_{\Omega}(x,y).

We will need some further information about
\varepsilon\to 0
limits of GFF correlation functions. We begin with uniform covariance estimates.

Lemma 2.5
.

Uniformly on compact subsets of
\big\{(x,y)\in\Omega\times\Omega:x\neq y\big\}
, as
\varepsilon\to 0
,

\displaystyle\left\langle\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x)\varphi_{\varepsilon}(y)\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}(\varepsilon)}

\displaystyle\to G_{\Omega}(x,y)

and uniformly on compact subsets of
\Omega
, as
\varepsilon\to 0
,

\displaystyle\Big\langle\big(\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x)\big)^{2}\Big\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}(\varepsilon)}+\frac{1}{2\pi}\log(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\to\frac{1}{4\pi}\big(\gamma-\log 4\big)+g_{\Omega}(x,x),

where
\gamma
is the Euler-Mascheroni constant.

Proof.

We begin by noting that

\displaystyle\left\langle\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x)\varphi_{\varepsilon}(y)\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}(\varepsilon)}

\displaystyle=\int_{\varepsilon^{2}}^{\infty}ds\left(\frac{1}{4\pi s}e^{-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4s}}-R_{s}(x,y)\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{1}^{\infty}ds\left(\frac{1}{4\pi s}e^{-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4s}}-R_{s}(x,y)\right)+\int_{\varepsilon^{2}}^{1}ds\left(\frac{1}{4\pi s}e^{-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4s}}-R_{s}(x,y)\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{1}^{\infty}ds\left(\frac{1}{4\pi s}e^{-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4s}}-R_{s}(x,y)\right)-\int_{\varepsilon^{2}}^{1}ds\,R_{s}(x,y)+\int_{\varepsilon^{2}}^{1}\frac{ds}{4\pi s}e^{-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4s}},

where the first two integrals are convergent in the
\varepsilon\to 0
limit – the first one since it is independent of
\varepsilon
and converges (with the appropriate uniformity) by Lemma
2.3
, while the second one converges (with the appropriate uniformity) due to the estimate (
2.1.6
).

For the last integral, we note that a change of variables (and an application of dominated convergence) yields

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\varepsilon^{2}}^{1}\frac{ds}{4\pi s}e^{-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4s}}

\displaystyle=\int_{|x-y|^{2}}^{\infty}\frac{ds}{4\pi s}e^{-\frac{s}{4}}

\displaystyle=\int_{|x-y|^{2}}^{1}\frac{ds}{4\pi s}-\int_{|x-y|^{2}}^{1}ds\frac{1-e^{-\frac{s}{4}}}{4\pi s}+\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{ds}{4\pi s}e^{-\frac{s}{4}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\log|x-y|^{-1}-\int_{|x-y|^{2}}^{1}ds\frac{1-e^{-\frac{s}{4}}}{4\pi s}+\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{ds}{4\pi s}e^{-\frac{s}{4}}.

The integrals converge and we have proved the first part of the lemma (one readily checks also here the required uniformity in the convergence).

For the second part, we begin by noting that what we have just computed implies that

g_{\Omega}(x,y)=\int_{1}^{\infty}ds\bigg(\frac{1}{4\pi s}e^{-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4s}}-R_{s}(x,y)\bigg)-\int_{0}^{1}ds\,R_{s}(x,y)-\int_{|x-y|^{2}}^{1}ds\frac{1-e^{-\frac{s}{4}}}{4\pi s}+\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{ds}{4\pi s}e^{-\frac{s}{4}}.

By continuity (and the estimates we have used), this also holds for
x=y
.

This remark allows us to write as
\varepsilon\to 0
(with the required uniformity)

\displaystyle\Big\langle\big(\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x)\big)^{2}\Big\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}(\varepsilon)}+\frac{1}{2\pi}\log(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=\int_{\varepsilon^{2}}^{\infty}ds\bigg(\frac{1}{4\pi s}-R_{s}(x,x)\bigg)-\int_{\varepsilon^{2}}^{1}\frac{ds}{4\pi s}

\displaystyle=\int_{1}^{\infty}ds\bigg(\frac{1}{4\pi s}-R_{s}(x,x)\bigg)-\int_{\varepsilon^{2}}^{1}ds\,R_{s}(x,x)

\displaystyle=g_{\Omega}(x,x)+\int_{0}^{1}ds\frac{1-e^{-\frac{s}{4}}}{4\pi s}-\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{ds}{4\pi s}e^{-\frac{s}{4}}+o(1).

Finally, integration by parts yields

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}ds\frac{1-e^{-\frac{s}{4}}}{4\pi s}-\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{ds}{4\pi s}e^{-\frac{s}{4}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{a\to 0}\bigg[\int_{a}^{1}\frac{ds}{4\pi s}-\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{a/4}^{\infty}\frac{ds}{s}e^{-s}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\lim_{a\to 0}\bigg[-\frac{1}{4\pi}\log a-\frac{1}{4\pi}\bigg(-\frac{\log\frac{a}{4}}{e^{a/4}}+\int_{a/4}^{\infty}ds\,\frac{\log s}{e^{s}}\bigg)\bigg]

\displaystyle=\lim_{a\to 0}\frac{1}{4\pi}\bigg[-\log a\Big(1-e^{-a/4}\Big)-\frac{\log 4}{e^{a/4}}-\int_{a/4}^{\infty}ds\,\frac{\log s}{e^{s}}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\pi}\big(\gamma-\log 4\big),

which concludes the proof.
∎

For the sine-Gordon model, we will also need Wick ordered exponentials of the GFF and estimates for correlation functions involving them. We define the Wick ordered exponentials of the GFF as follows: for each
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
, let

(2.2.5)

\mathop{:}e^{i\alpha\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x)}\mathop{:}=e^{\frac{\alpha^{2}}{8\pi}(\gamma-\log 4)}\varepsilon^{-\frac{\alpha^{2}}{4\pi}}e^{i\alpha\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x)}=:c_{\alpha}\varepsilon^{-\frac{\alpha^{2}}{4\pi}}e^{i\alpha\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x)},

where again
\gamma
denotes the Euler-Mascheroni constant. The reason for including
e^{\frac{\alpha^{2}}{8\pi}(\gamma-\log 4)}
in our definition of normal order is that correlation functions become cleaner looking (and consistent with the definition of
[
9
]
) with this choice. For example, one readily checks using Lemma
2.5
that with this convention,

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\left\langle\mathop{:}e^{i\alpha\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x)}\mathop{:}\mathop{:}e^{i\alpha^{\prime}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(y)}\mathop{:}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}(\varepsilon)}=e^{-\alpha\alpha^{\prime}G_{\Omega}(x,y)-\frac{\alpha^{2}}{2}g_{\Omega}(x,x)-\frac{(\alpha^{\prime})^{2}}{2}g_{\Omega}(y,y)}.

Without adding this constant to our normal order convention, this would get multiplied by
c_{\alpha}^{-1}c_{\alpha^{\prime}}^{-1}
.

Using this normal order notation, we can also define Wick ordered trigonometric functions as expected,

(2.2.6)

\mathop{:}\cos(\alpha\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x))\mathop{:}=\frac{1}{2}\Big(\mathop{:}e^{i\alpha\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x)}\mathop{:}+\mathop{:}e^{-i\alpha\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x)}\mathop{:}\Big)

and

(2.2.7)

\mathop{:}\sin(\alpha\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x))\mathop{:}=\frac{1}{2i}\Big(\mathop{:}e^{i\alpha\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x)}\mathop{:}-\mathop{:}e^{-i\alpha\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x)}\mathop{:}\Big).

Estimates for the correlation functions involving Wick ordered exponentials are recorded in the following lemma.

Lemma 2.6
.

For
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n}\in\mathbb{R}
and distinct points
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
,
y_{1},\dots,y_{m}

\in\Omega
, the limit

\displaystyle\Bigg\langle\prod_{j=1}^{n}\mathop{:}e^{i\gamma_{j}\varphi(x_{j})}\mathop{:}\prod_{k=1}^{m}\varphi(y_{k})\Bigg\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}}:=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\Bigg\langle\prod_{j=1}^{n}\mathop{:}e^{i\gamma_{j}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x_{j})}\mathop{:}\prod_{k=1}^{m}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(y_{k})\Bigg\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}(\varepsilon)}

exists.

Moreover, the convergence is uniform on compact subsets of
\big\{u\in\Omega^{n+m}:u_{i}\neq u_{j}\ \text{for}\ i\neq j\big\}
.

Proof.

We begin by noting that by the Leibniz integral rule,

\displaystyle\Bigg\langle\prod_{j=1}^{n}\mathop{:}e^{i\gamma_{j}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x_{j})}\mathop{:}\prod_{k=1}^{m}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(y_{k})\Bigg\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}(\varepsilon)}=\prod_{k=1}^{m}\frac{\partial}{\partial t_{k}}\Bigg|_{t=0}\Bigg\langle\prod_{j=1}^{n}\mathop{:}e^{i\gamma_{j}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x_{j})}\mathop{:}e^{\sum_{k=1}^{m}t_{k}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(y_{k})}\Bigg\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}(\varepsilon)}.

By Girsanov’s theorem and a routine Gaussian calculation, we find that

\Bigg\langle\prod_{j=1}^{n}\mathop{:}e^{i\gamma_{j}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x_{j})}\mathop{:}e^{\sum_{k=1}^{m}t_{k}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(y_{k})}\Bigg\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}(\varepsilon)}\\
\quad=e^{\frac{1}{2}\langle(\sum_{k=1}^{m}t_{k}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(y_{k}))^{2}\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}(\varepsilon)}}\prod_{j=1}^{n}e^{i\gamma_{j}\sum_{k=1}^{m}t_{k}\langle\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x_{j})\varphi_{\varepsilon}(y_{k})\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}(\varepsilon)}}\Bigg\langle\prod_{j=1}^{n}\mathop{:}e^{i\gamma_{j}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x_{j})}\mathop{:}\Bigg\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}(\varepsilon)}\\
\quad=e^{\frac{1}{2}\langle(\sum_{k=1}^{m}t_{k}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(y_{k}))^{2}\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}(\varepsilon)}}\prod_{j=1}^{n}e^{i\gamma_{j}\sum_{k=1}^{m}t_{k}\langle\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x_{j})\varphi_{\varepsilon}(y_{k})\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}(\varepsilon)}}\\
\times e^{-\sum_{1\leq j<j^{\prime}\leq n}\gamma_{j}\gamma_{j^{\prime}}\langle\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x_{j})\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x_{j^{\prime}})\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}(\varepsilon)}}e^{-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{\gamma_{j}^{2}}{2}\big(\langle(\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x_{j}))^{2}\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}(\varepsilon)}+\frac{1}{2\pi}\log(\varepsilon)-\frac{1}{4\pi}(\gamma-\log 4)\big)}.

Carrying out the
t
-differentiations and setting
t=0
, we see that the question of (uniform) convergence boils down to convergence of terms handled by Lemma
2.5
(note that since we differentiate only once with respect to each
t_{k}
and then set
t=0
, we never get terms of the type
\langle(\varphi_{\varepsilon}(y_{k}))^{2}\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}(\varepsilon)}
).
∎

We record a further GFF estimate we shall need in the proof of Theorem
1.5
.

Lemma 2.7
.

For
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
and
x\in\Omega
, we have

(2.2.8)

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\left\langle\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x)\varphi_{\varepsilon}(f)\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}(\varepsilon)}=\left\langle\varphi(x)\varphi(f)\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}}:=\int_{\Omega}d^{2}y\,f(y)G_{\Omega}(x,y)

where the convergence is uniform on compact subsets of
\Omega
and the limiting function is a continuously differentiable function of
x\in\Omega
. Moreover, for any compact
K\subset\Omega
, there exists
C_{K,f}>0
such that

\sup_{0<\varepsilon<c_{K}}\left|\left\langle\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x)\varphi_{\varepsilon}(f)\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}(\varepsilon)}-\left\langle\varphi_{\varepsilon}(y)\varphi_{\varepsilon}(f)\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}(\varepsilon)}\right|<C_{K,f}|x-y|

for
x,y\in K
.

Proof.
