We begin by noting that by Fubini, we can write

\left\langle\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x)\varphi_{\varepsilon}(f)\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}(\varepsilon)}=\int_{\Omega}d^{2}y\,f(y)\left\langle\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x)\varphi_{\varepsilon}(y)\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}(\varepsilon)}=\int_{\Omega}d^{2}y\,f(y)\int_{\varepsilon^{2}}^{\infty}ds\,\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x,y).

Pointwise convergence of this follows readily from the dominated convergence theorem and noting that

0\leq\int_{\varepsilon^{2}}^{\infty}ds\,\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x,y)\leq G_{\Omega}(x,y)

which is integrable in
y
for each
x\in\Omega
.

For local uniform convergence, it thus suffices to show that for each compact
K\subset\Omega
,

\sup_{x\in K}\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{K}d^{2}y\int_{0}^{\varepsilon^{2}}ds\,\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x,y)=0.

For this, we recall that
\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x,y)\leq\mathsf{p}(s,x,y)=\frac{1}{4\pi s}e^{-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4s}}
, so (by shifting our integration variable) it is sufficient to prove that for each
R>0

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{B(0,R)}d^{2}u\,\int_{0}^{\varepsilon^{2}}\frac{ds}{4\pi s}e^{-\frac{|u|^{2}}{4s}}=0.

Using Fubini and writing
u=\sqrt{s}v
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{B(0,R)}d^{2}u\,\int_{0}^{\varepsilon^{2}}\frac{ds}{4\pi s}e^{-\frac{|u|^{2}}{4s}}\leq\int_{0}^{\varepsilon^{2}}ds\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{d^{2}v}{4\pi}e^{-\frac{|v|^{2}}{4}}=\varepsilon^{2},

which concludes the proof of local uniform convergence.

For the differentiability claim, recall that
G_{\Omega}(x,y)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\log|x-y|^{-1}+g_{\Omega}(x,y)
, where
g_{\Omega}(x,y)
is smooth in
\Omega\times\Omega
. Thus we need to prove that

x\mapsto\int_{\Omega}d^{2}y\,f(y)\log|x-y|^{-1}

is continuously differentiable for
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
. This follows readily from the Leibniz integral rule since
\big|\nabla_{x}\log|x-y|^{-1}\big|=\frac{1}{|x-y|}
is integrable in
2d
.

For the final claim, we use (
2.1.3
) and Lemma
2.3
to write

\displaystyle\left|\left\langle\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x)\varphi_{\varepsilon}(f)\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}(\varepsilon)}-\left\langle\varphi_{\varepsilon}(y)\varphi_{\varepsilon}(f)\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}(\varepsilon)}\right|

\displaystyle\quad\leq\int_{\Omega}d^{2}u\,f(u)\int_{1}^{\infty}ds\,|\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x,u)-\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,y,u)|

\displaystyle\qquad+\left|\int_{\Omega}d^{2}u\,f(u)\int_{\varepsilon^{2}}^{1}ds\,(\mathsf{p}(s,x,u)-\mathsf{p}(s,y,u))\right|

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{\Omega}d^{2}u\,f(u)\int_{\varepsilon^{2}}^{1}ds\,|R_{s}(x,u)-R_{s}(y,u)|.

Using (
2.1.5
) and Lemma
2.1
, one can perform a gradient estimate and readily show that

\int_{\Omega}d^{2}u\,f(u)\int_{1}^{\infty}ds\,|\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x,u)-\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,y,u)|\leq C_{K,f}|x-y|.

For the last term, a simple application of (
2.1.7
) of Lemma
2.2
readily shows the existence of a
C_{K,f}>0
(possibly different from above) such that

\int_{\Omega}d^{2}u\,f(u)\int_{\varepsilon^{2}}^{1}ds\,|R_{s}(x,u)-R_{s}(y,u)|\leq C_{K,f}|x-y|.

Finally for the term involving the full plane heat kernel, we write

\displaystyle\Bigg|\int_{\Omega}d^{2}u\,f(u)\int_{\varepsilon^{2}}^{1}ds\,(\mathsf{p}(s,x,u)-\mathsf{p}(s,y,u))\Bigg|

\displaystyle\quad\leq\int_{\varepsilon^{2}}^{1}ds\Bigg|\int_{\Omega}d^{2}u\,f(u)\frac{e^{-\frac{|x-u|^{2}}{4s}}}{4\pi s}-\int_{\Omega}d^{2}u\,f(u)\frac{e^{-\frac{|y-u|^{2}}{4s}}}{4\pi s}\Bigg|

\displaystyle\quad\leq\int_{0}^{1}ds\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{d^{2}v}{4\pi}|f(x+\sqrt{s}v)-f(y+\sqrt{s}v)|e^{-\frac{|v|^{2}}{4}}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\|\nabla f\|_{\infty}|x-y|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{d^{2}v}{4\pi}e^{-\frac{|v|^{2}}{4}}

\displaystyle\quad=\|\nabla f\|_{\infty}|x-y|.

Putting these estimates together concludes the proof.
∎

We will also need limiting correlation functions involving derivatives of the fields. Presumably one could construct these as limits of correlation functions involving derivatives of the regularized fields, but to avoid doing further estimates, we define these correlation functions directly in the
\varepsilon=0
limit.

For this purpose, we first give an explicit formula for functions involving the Wick ordered exponentials and the free field itself.

Lemma 2.8
.

For
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n}\in\mathbb{R}
and distinct points
x_{1},\dots,x_{n},y_{1},\dots,y_{m}\in\Omega
, we have

(2.2.9)

\Bigg\langle\prod_{j=1}^{n}\mathop{:}e^{i\gamma_{j}\varphi(x_{j})}\mathop{:}\prod_{k=1}^{m}\varphi(y_{k})\Bigg\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}}=e^{-\sum_{1\leq j<j^{\prime}\leq n}\gamma_{j}\gamma_{j^{\prime}}G_{\Omega}(x_{j},x_{j^{\prime}})-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\gamma_{j}^{2}g_{\Omega}(x_{j},x_{j})}\\
\times\left\langle\prod_{k=1}^{m}\bigg(\varphi(y_{k})+i\sum_{j=1}^{n}\gamma_{j}\langle\varphi(y_{k})\varphi(x_{j})\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}}\bigg)\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}},

where the correlation function on the right is reduced to correlation functions involving only
\varphi(y_{k})
by expanding the product and using linearity of the correlation function.

Proof.

This is basically Girsanov’s theorem, but as the limiting objects are not real valued Gaussian random variables and we have purely imaginary exponents (instead of real as one usually has in Girsanov’s theorem), there is some justification needed.

We start by considering for
\varepsilon>0
the function

(2.2.10)

\displaystyle F_{\varepsilon}(z_{1},\dots,z_{n})=\Bigg\langle\prod_{j=1}^{n}\mathop{:}e^{z_{j}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x_{j})}\mathop{:}\prod_{k=1}^{m}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(y_{k})\Bigg\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}(\varepsilon)}

For
z_{j}=i\gamma_{j}
, we know that as
\varepsilon\to 0
,
F_{\varepsilon}
converges to our desired object. We also note that for general
z_{1},\dots,z_{n}\in\mathbb{C}
,
F_{\varepsilon}(z_{1},\dots,z_{n})
(where
\mathop{:}e^{z\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x)}\mathop{:}=e^{-\frac{z^{2}}{8\pi}(\gamma-\log 4)}\varepsilon^{\frac{z^{2}}{4\pi}}e^{z\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x)}
) is well defined by standard Gaussian estimates. Moreover, combining Morera’s theorem with Fubini, one readily checks that
F_{\varepsilon}
is an entire function of
z_{1},\dots,z_{n}
.

Let us then compute
F_{\varepsilon}(z_{1},\dots,z_{n})
for
z_{1},\dots,z_{n}\in\mathbb{R}
. For this, we can make use of a standard Girsanov (“complete-the-square”) argument and find

\displaystyle F_{\varepsilon}(z_{1},\dots,z_{n})

\displaystyle\qquad=\prod_{j=1}^{n}e^{-\frac{z_{j}^{2}}{8\pi}(\gamma-\log 4)}\varepsilon^{\frac{z_{j}^{2}}{4\pi}}\left\langle\prod_{j=1}^{n}e^{z_{j}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x_{j})}\prod_{k=1}^{m}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(y_{k})\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}(\varepsilon)}

\displaystyle\qquad=\prod_{j=1}^{n}e^{-\frac{z_{j}^{2}}{8\pi}(\gamma-\log 4)}\varepsilon^{\frac{z_{j}^{2}}{4\pi}}\left\langle\prod_{j=1}^{n}e^{z_{j}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x_{j})}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}(\varepsilon)}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\left\langle\prod_{k=1}^{m}\bigg(\varphi_{\varepsilon}(y_{k})+\sum_{j=1}^{n}z_{j}\langle\varphi_{\varepsilon}(y_{k})\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x_{j})\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}(\varepsilon)}\bigg)\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}(\varepsilon)}

\displaystyle\qquad=\left\langle\prod_{j=1}^{n}\mathop{:}e^{z_{j}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x_{j})}\mathop{:}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}(\varepsilon)}\left\langle\prod_{k=1}^{m}\bigg(\varphi_{\varepsilon}(y_{k})+\sum_{j=1}^{n}z_{j}\langle\varphi_{\varepsilon}(y_{k})\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x_{j})\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}(\varepsilon)}\bigg)\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}(\varepsilon)}.

Since the right hand side makes perfect sense for complex values of
z_{j}
as well, and is also an entire function of
z_{1},\dots,z_{n}
, this identity must in fact hold for arbitrary
z_{1},\dots,z_{n}\in\mathbb{C}
– in particular, for
z_{j}=i\gamma_{j}
.

We also know from Lemma
2.5
and Lemma
2.6
, that for
z_{j}=i\gamma_{j}
this has a limit as
\varepsilon\to 0
:

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}F_{\varepsilon}(i\gamma_{1},\dots,i\gamma_{n})=\left\langle\prod_{j=1}^{n}\mathop{:}e^{i\gamma_{j}\varphi(x_{j})}\mathop{:}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}}\left\langle\prod_{k=1}^{m}\bigg(\varphi(y_{k})+i\sum_{j=1}^{n}\gamma_{j}\langle\varphi(y_{k})\varphi(x_{j})\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}}\bigg)\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}}.

The second factor is already the same as in the statement of the lemma, so we only need to compute the first factor on the right. This was nearly done in Lemma
2.6
. From the routine Gaussian calculation there (with
t_{1},\dots,t_{m}=0
), we have

\left\langle\prod_{j=1}^{n}\mathop{:}e^{i\gamma_{j}\varphi(x_{j})}\mathop{:}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}}=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\bigg(e^{-\sum_{1\leq j<j^{\prime}\leq n}\gamma_{j}\gamma_{j^{\prime}}\langle\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x_{j})\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x_{j^{\prime}})\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}(\varepsilon)}}\\
\times e^{-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{\gamma_{j}^{2}}{2}\big(\langle(\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x_{j}))^{2}\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}(\varepsilon)}+\frac{1}{2\pi}\log(\varepsilon)-\frac{1}{4\pi}(\gamma-\log 4)\big)}\bigg).

The convergence of this to the first factor in the statement of the lemma follows from Lemma
2.5
. This concludes the proof.
∎

Note that by expanding the product and using Gaussianity, the right hand side of Lemma
2.8
can be written entirely in terms of
G_{\Omega}
and
g_{\Omega}
. In particular, the right hand side is a smooth function of
y_{1},\dots,y_{m}
(as long as none of the
y_{k}
coincide with another
y
point or an
x
point). This allows us to make the following definition: if
D_{k}
is either
\partial_{y_{k}}
or
\bar{\partial}_{y_{k}}
(holomorphic or antiholomorphic derivative with respect to
y_{k}
), then

(2.2.11)

\displaystyle\Bigg\langle\prod_{j=1}^{n}\mathop{:}e^{i\gamma_{j}\varphi(x_{j})}\mathop{:}\prod_{k=1}^{m}D_{k}\varphi(y_{k})\Bigg\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}}

\displaystyle:=\prod_{k=1}^{m}D_{k}\Bigg\langle\prod_{j=1}^{n}\mathop{:}e^{i\gamma_{j}\varphi(x_{j})}\mathop{:}\prod_{k=1}^{m}\varphi(y_{k})\Bigg\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}}.

In addition to defining these correlation functions, we will eventually need to identify them with certain critical Ising correlation functions using the results of
[
9
]
. The corresponding GFF correlation functions were defined there slightly differently, so we need to show that the definition there (
[
9
, Definition 2.3]
) is equivalent to our definition. This is done in the following lemma.

Lemma 2.9
.

We have

(2.2.12)

\Bigg\langle\prod_{j=1}^{n}\mathop{:}e^{i\gamma_{j}\varphi(x_{j})}\mathop{:}\prod_{k=1}^{m}D_{k}\varphi(y_{k})\Bigg\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}}\\
\quad=\prod_{k=1}^{m}D_{k}\partial_{z_{k}}|_{z_{k}=0}\Big[e^{-\sum_{1\leq j<j^{\prime}\leq n}\gamma_{j}\gamma_{j^{\prime}}G_{\Omega}(x_{j},x_{j^{\prime}})-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\gamma_{j}^{2}g_{\Omega}(x_{j},x_{j})}\\
\times e^{i\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{m}\gamma_{j}z_{k}G_{\Omega}(x_{j},y_{k})+\sum_{1\leq k<k^{\prime}\leq m}z_{k}z_{k^{\prime}}G_{\Omega}(y_{k},y_{k^{\prime}})+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{m}z_{k}^{2}g_{\Omega}(y_{k},y_{k})}\Big].

Proof.

Note first of all that on the right hand side of the claim, the terms involving only
x_{j}
and
x_{j^{\prime}}
can be commuted past the differential operators. Also the
z_{k}^{2}
terms do not matter since once we differentiate with respect to
z_{k}
and set
z_{k}=0
, these terms disappear. So by our definition of the correlation functions involved and Lemma
2.8
, it is enough for us to prove that

\displaystyle\prod_{k=1}^{m}\partial_{z_{k}}|_{z_{k}=0}e^{i\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{m}\gamma_{j}z_{k}G_{\Omega}(x_{j},y_{k})+\sum_{1\leq k<k^{\prime}\leq m}z_{k}z_{k^{\prime}}G_{\Omega}(y_{k},y_{k^{\prime}})}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=\left\langle\prod_{k=1}^{m}\bigg(\varphi(y_{k})+i\sum_{j=1}^{n}\gamma_{j}\langle\varphi(y_{k})\varphi(x_{j})\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}}\bigg)\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}}.

This is of course a rather standard Gaussian fact one could prove with standard Gaussian combinatorial arguments, but let us give a direct analytic proof. Using Lemma
2.5
, the Leibniz integral rule, and the formula for the moment generating function of a multivariate Gaussian random variable, we find that

\displaystyle\left\langle\prod_{k=1}^{m}\bigg(\varphi(y_{k})+i\sum_{j=1}^{n}\gamma_{j}\langle\varphi(y_{k})\varphi(x_{j})\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}}\bigg)\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}}

\displaystyle\quad=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\left\langle\prod_{k=1}^{m}\bigg(\varphi_{\varepsilon}(y_{k})+i\sum_{j=1}^{n}\gamma_{j}\langle\varphi_{\varepsilon}(y_{k})\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x_{j})\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}(\varepsilon)}\bigg)\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}(\varepsilon)}

\displaystyle\quad=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\prod_{k=1}^{m}\partial_{z_{k}}|_{z_{k}=0}\left\langle e^{\sum_{k=1}^{m}z_{k}(\varphi_{\varepsilon}(y_{k})+i\sum_{j=1}^{n}\gamma_{j}\langle\varphi_{\varepsilon}(y_{k})\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x_{j})\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}(\varepsilon)})}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}(\varepsilon)}

\displaystyle\quad=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\prod_{k=1}^{m}\partial_{z_{k}}|_{z_{k}=0}e^{i\sum_{k=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\gamma_{j}z_{k}\langle\varphi_{\varepsilon}(y_{k})\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x_{j})\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}(\varepsilon)}}e^{\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k,k^{\prime}=1}^{m}z_{k}z_{k^{\prime}}\langle\varphi_{\varepsilon}(y_{k})\varphi_{\varepsilon}(y_{k^{\prime}})\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}(\varepsilon)}}.

Since the diagonal terms in the last sum do not contribute to the derivative (since we differentiate only once with respect to
z_{k}
and then set it to be zero), we can replace the last exponential by
e^{\sum_{1\leq k<k^{\prime}\leq m}z_{k}z_{k^{\prime}}\langle\varphi_{\varepsilon}(y_{k})\varphi_{\varepsilon}(y_{k^{\prime}})\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}(\varepsilon)}}
. Thus by Lemma
2.5
, it only remains to justify swapping the order of the
\varepsilon\to 0
limit and the derivatives. This is clear again by Lemma
2.5
since the derivatives (after setting
z_{k}=0
for all
k
) simply produce a polynomial in the covariances of which we can take a limit by Lemma
2.5
and then rewrite as the derivatives acting on the correct quantity. This concludes the proof.
∎

3.
The Renormalized Potential of the sine-Gordon Model

In this section, we begin our analysis of the sine-Gordon model. This will be done through the so-called renormalized potential of the model. The renormalized potential can be viewed as a generating function of the correlation functions featuring in Theorem
1.5
. As already mentioned, our approach follows very closely similar analysis in
[
5
,
6
,
7
,
8
,
16
]
– the main difference to previous works is that we use the zero boundary GFF and thus cannot use directly known results (which use different variants of the GFF). While the renormalized potential gives access to the sine-Gordon correlation functions we are interested in, it is a quantity that is computed from the GFF. As our domain is fixed, we will simply write
\langle\cdot\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}
instead of
\langle\cdot\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}_{\Omega}(\varepsilon)}
when convenient.

Let us begin by introducing some notation: first of all we introduce the functional

(3.0.1)

\phi\mapsto v_{\varepsilon^{2}}(\eta,\phi|\varepsilon):=\int d\xi\,\eta(\xi)c_{\sigma\sqrt{\pi}}\varepsilon^{-\frac{\sigma^{2}}{4}}e^{i\sqrt{\pi}\sigma\phi(x)},

where we have written
\xi=(x,\sigma)
and
\int d\xi=\sum_{\sigma\in\{\pm 1,\pm 2\}}\int_{\Omega}d^{2}x
. The constant
c_{\sigma\sqrt{\pi}}
is defined in (
2.2.5
). Also here
\eta\in C_{c}(\Omega\times\{\pm 1,\pm 2\})
(meaning that for each
\sigma\in\{\pm 1\pm 2\}
,
x\mapsto\eta(x,\sigma)\in C_{c}(\Omega)
– we find it convenient to allow for less smooth functions here than in Theorem
1.5
) and
\phi\in C(\Omega)
. Further, we define a kind of generalized partition function (which will serve as a generating function for the correlation functions appearing in Theorem
1.5
) as

(3.0.2)

Z(\eta|\varepsilon)=\left\langle e^{-v_{\varepsilon^{2}}(\eta,\varphi_{\varepsilon}|\varepsilon)}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}.

Now, noting that since for each
t>\varepsilon^{2}

(x,y)\mapsto\int_{t}^{\infty}ds\,\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x,y)\qquad\text{and}\qquad(x,y)\mapsto\int_{\varepsilon^{2}}^{t}ds\,\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x,y)

are covariances (see the proof of Lemma
2.3
) and they sum up to
\left\langle\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x)\varphi_{\varepsilon}(y)\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}
, we can decompose
\varphi_{\varepsilon}=\varphi_{\sqrt{t}}+\psi_{\varepsilon,t}
, where
\varphi_{\sqrt{t}}
and
\psi_{\varepsilon,t}
are independent continuous Gaussian fields (again, see the proof of Lemma
2.3
) where the covariance of
\varphi_{\sqrt{t}}
is
\int_{t}^{\infty}ds\,\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x,y)
and that of
\psi_{\varepsilon,t}
is
\int_{\varepsilon^{2}}^{t}ds\,\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x,y)
. This allows introducing the renormalized potential:

(3.0.3)

\phi\mapsto v_{t}(\eta,\phi|\varepsilon):=-\log\mathbb{E}_{\psi_{\varepsilon,t}}\Big(e^{-v_{\varepsilon^{2}}(\eta,\phi+\psi_{\varepsilon,t}|\varepsilon)}\Big),

so that our partition function can be written as

(3.0.4)

Z(\eta|\varepsilon)=\Big\langle e^{-v_{t}(\eta,\varphi_{\sqrt{t}}|\varepsilon)}\Big\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\sqrt{t})}.

While it seems hard to compute the renormalized potential
v_{t}
explicitly, we will show that for small enough
t
, it can be expressed in terms of a suitable series. More precisely, let us introduce for
\eta\in C_{c}(\Omega\times\{\pm 1,\pm 2\})
and
\phi\in C(\Omega)
(real valued) the series

(3.0.5)

u_{t}(\eta,\phi|\varepsilon):=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\int_{(\Omega\times\{\pm 1,\pm 2\})^{n}}d\xi_{1}\dots d\xi_{n}\,\eta(\xi_{1})\dots\eta(\xi_{n})\tilde{v}_{t}^{n}(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n}|\varepsilon)e^{i\sqrt{\pi}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sigma_{j}\phi(x_{j})}.

with the coefficients
\tilde{v}_{t}^{n}
defined recursively by

(3.0.6)

\tilde{v}_{t}^{1}(\xi_{j}|\varepsilon):=c_{\sigma_{j}\sqrt{\pi}}e^{-\frac{\pi}{2}\sigma_{j}^{2}\big(\frac{1}{4\pi}\log\varepsilon^{2}+\int_{\varepsilon^{2}}^{t}ds\,\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{j},x_{j})\big)}

and for
I\subset\{1,\dots,n\}
such that
|I|\geq 2
, let

(3.0.7)

\tilde{v}_{t}^{|I|}(\xi_{I}|\varepsilon):=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\varepsilon^{2}}^{t}ds\,\sum_{I_{1}\dot{\cup}I_{2}=I}\sum_{i\in I_{1},j\in I_{2}}\pi\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{i},x_{j})\tilde{v}_{s}^{|I_{1}|}(\xi_{I_{1}}|\varepsilon)\tilde{v}_{s}^{|I_{2}|}(\xi_{I_{2}}|\varepsilon)\\
\times\exp\bigg\{-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k,l\in I}\pi\sigma_{k}\sigma_{l}\int_{s}^{t}du\,\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(u,x_{k},x_{l})\bigg\},

where
\xi_{I}=(\xi_{i})_{i\in I}
denotes a vector of elements of
\Omega\times\{\pm 1,\pm 2\}
and
I_{1}\dot{\cup}I_{2}=I
means that
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
are disjoint (non-empty) subsets of
I
so that their union is
I
. We will show that for small enough
t>\varepsilon^{2}
(“small enough” depending only on
\eta
– not
\varepsilon
) this series converges and agrees with the renormalized potential
v_{t}
.

To state the required estimate, we introduce the following norm

(3.0.8)

\|f\|_{n}(\sigma):=\begin{cases}\sup_{x\in\Omega}|f(x,\sigma)|,&\text{ if }\quad n=1,\\
\sup_{x_{1}\in\Omega}\int_{\Omega^{n-1}}dx_{2}\dots dx_{n}|f((x_{1},\sigma_{1}),\dots,(x_{n},\sigma_{n}))|,&\text{ if }\quad n\geq 2.\end{cases}

The main content of this section is the proof of the following proposition.

Proposition 3.1
.

For any
t>0
and
n\geq 1
there exists a function
h_{t}^{n}:(\Omega\times\{\pm 1,\pm 2\})^{n}\to[0,\infty]
independent of
\varepsilon
, that is symmetric in its arguments and such that for a fixed
t_{0}>0
and
\varepsilon^{2}<t<t_{0}
and
n\neq 2

|\tilde{v}_{t}^{n}(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n}|\varepsilon)|\leq h_{t}^{n}(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n}).

Moreover for each
K\subset\Omega
compact and
M\geq 1
, there exists a constant
C_{K,M,t_{0}}
such that if
\eta\in C_{c}(\Omega\times\{\pm 1,\pm 2\})
with
\mathrm{supp}(\eta(\cdot,\sigma))\subset K
and
\|\eta\|_{\infty}\leq M
, then the functions
h_{t}^{n}
(with
n\neq 2
) satisfy

(3.0.9)

\|\eta^{\otimes n}h_{t}^{n}\|_{n}(\sigma)\leq n^{n-2}t^{-1}C_{K,M,t_{0}}^{n}t^{\frac{1}{8}\big(8n-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sigma_{j}^{2}\big)}.

Here
\eta^{\otimes n}:(\Omega\times\{\pm 1,\pm 2\})^{n}\to\mathbb{C}
denotes the function
\eta^{\otimes n}(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n})=\prod_{j=1}^{n}\eta(\xi_{j})
.

Furthermore, for
n=2
and
\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}>-4
, there exists a function
h_{t}^{2}
such that
|\tilde{v}_{t}^{2}|\leq h_{t}^{2}
and (
3.0.9
) holds, that is

\|\eta^{\otimes 2}h_{t}^{2}\|_{2}(\sigma)\leq C_{K,M,t_{0}}^{2}t^{1-\frac{1}{8}(\sigma_{1}^{2}+\sigma_{2}^{2})}

for some
C_{K,M,t_{0}}>0
and all
\eta\in C_{c}(\Omega\times\{\pm 1,\pm 2\})
with
\mathrm{supp}(\eta(\cdot,\sigma))\subset K
and
\|\eta\|_{\infty}\leq M
.

This proposition gives us our main tool for analyzing the renormalized partition function, in that it allows us to control the series expansion (
3.0.5
). This is recorded in the following corollary.

Corollary 3.2
.

For any
K\subset\Omega
compact,
M\geq 1
,
\eta\in C_{c}(\Omega\times\{\pm 1,\pm 2\})
with
\mathrm{supp}(\eta(\cdot,\sigma))\subset K
,
t_{0}
as in Proposition
3.1
,
\|\eta\|_{\infty}\leq M
,
t_{0}>0
, and
\mu\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
, let

(3.0.10)

T_{\mu}=T_{\mu}(t_{0},\eta)=(4eC_{K,M,t_{0}}|\mu|)^{-1}\wedge(4eC_{K,M,t_{0}}|\mu|)^{-2},

where
C_{K,M,t_{0}}
is the constant from Proposition
3.1
. Then for
0<\varepsilon^{2}<t<T_{\mu}
, the series defining
u_{t}(\mu\eta,\phi|\varepsilon)
converges absolutely, and equals
v_{t}(\mu\eta,\phi|\varepsilon)
for each
\phi\in C(\Omega)
.

Proof.

The proof here is very similar to that of
[
8
, Corollary 4.3]
and we direct the reader there for further details. We begin by pointing out that by Proposition
3.1
, for say
n\geq 3
and
\varepsilon^{2}<t<t_{0}
and writing for simplicity
C=C_{K,M,t_{0}}
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n!}\bigg|\mu^{n}\int_{\Omega^{n}}d^{2}x_{1}\dots d^{2}x_{n}\,\eta(\xi_{1})\dots\eta(\xi_{n})\tilde{v}_{t}^{n}(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n}|\varepsilon)e^{i\sqrt{\pi}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sigma_{j}\phi(x_{j})}\bigg|

\displaystyle\quad\leq\frac{|\mu|^{n}}{n!}|\Omega|\|\eta^{\otimes n}h_{t}^{n}\|_{n}(\sigma)

\displaystyle\quad\leq|\Omega|\frac{|\mu|^{n}}{n!}n^{n-2}t^{-1}C^{n}t^{\frac{1}{8}(8n-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sigma_{j}^{2})}

\displaystyle\quad\leq|\Omega|n^{-2}t^{-1}\left(eC|\mu|t^{1-\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sigma_{j}^{2}}{8n}}\right)^{n},

where we used the fact that
\frac{n^{n}}{n!}\leq e^{n}
.

If we now have
t<1
(and
t<T_{\mu}
) then

t^{1-\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sigma_{j}^{2}}{8n}}\leq t^{1/2}<T_{\mu}^{1/2}\leq(4eC|\mu|)^{-1},

so

\displaystyle\sum_{n\geq 3}\frac{1}{n!}\bigg|\mu^{n}\int_{(\Omega\times\{\pm 1,\pm 2\})^{n}}d\xi_{1}\dots d\xi_{n}\,\eta(\xi_{1})\dots\eta(\xi_{n})\tilde{v}_{t}^{n}(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n}|\varepsilon)e^{i\sqrt{\pi}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sigma_{j}\phi(x_{j})}\bigg|

\displaystyle\quad\leq\sum_{n\geq 3}|\Omega|n^{-2}t^{-1}\left(4eC|\mu|t^{1/2}\right)^{n},

which converges geometrically under our assumption on
t
(the factor four came from the
\sigma
-sums). If
1<t<T_{\mu}
then

t^{1-\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sigma_{j}^{2}}{8n}}\leq t<T_{\mu}\leq(4eC|\mu|)^{-1},

so

\displaystyle\sum_{n\geq 3}\frac{1}{n!}\bigg|\mu^{n}\int_{(\Omega\times\{\pm 1,\pm 2\})^{n}}d\xi_{1}\dots d\xi_{n}\,\eta(\xi_{1})\dots\eta(\xi_{n})\tilde{v}_{t}^{n}(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n}|\varepsilon)e^{i\sqrt{\pi}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sigma_{j}\phi(x_{j})}\bigg|

\displaystyle\quad\leq\sum_{n\geq 3}|\Omega|n^{-2}t^{-1}\left(4eC|\mu|t\right)^{n},

which also converges geometrically under our assumption on
t
.

The
n=1
and
n=2
terms do not influence convergence and we conclude that the series defining
u_{t}(\mu\eta,\phi|\varepsilon)
converges geometrically, uniformly in
\phi\in C(\Omega)
for (fixed)
\varepsilon^{2}<t<T_{\mu}
. It remains to show that this series agrees with the renormalized potential.

To simplify notation, let us from now on write
u_{t}(\phi)=u_{t}(\mu\eta,\phi|\varepsilon)
. Arguing exactly as in
[
8
, Proof of Corollary 4.3]
, one can show that
h_{t}(\phi)=e^{-u_{t}(\phi)}
satisfies the infinite dimensional heat equation

(3.0.11)

\partial_{t}h_{t}(\phi)=\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(t,\cdot,\cdot)}h_{t}(\phi),

where

(3.0.12)

\Delta_{\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(t,\cdot,\cdot)}h_{t}(\phi)=\int_{\Omega^{2}}d^{2}x\,d^{2}y\,\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(t,x,y)\mathrm{Hess}\,h_{t}(\phi,x,y),

and
\mathrm{Hess}\,h_{t}
is the Hessian of
h_{t}
: if
D^{2}
denotes the second Fréchet derivative on
C(\Omega)
,
5
5

5

Note that while this is not a Banach space – something one typically assumes for Fréchet analysis – we can for example restrict to the support of
\eta
or work on the Banach space of bounded continuous functions on
\Omega
, so this is not an issue.
then for twice Fréchet differentiable functions
F:C(\Omega)\to\mathbb{C}
(such as
h_{t}
)

D^{2}F(\phi,f_{1},f_{2})=\int_{\Omega^{2}}d^{2}x\,d^{2}y\,f_{1}(x)f_{2}(y)\mathrm{Hess}\,F(\phi,x,y).

We will argue that
g_{t}=e^{-v_{t}}
satisfies this same equation and argue that the two solutions must agree, thus
u_{t}=v_{t}
. The idea here is to make use of the representation (
2.1.5
) to transform the question into a finite dimensional heat equation. Lemma
2.1
ensures that we then have for any nice functional
g

(3.0.13)

\Delta_{\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(t,\cdot,\cdot)}g(\phi)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}e^{-t\lambda_{n}}D^{2}g(\phi,e_{n},e_{n}).

For each
N\geq 1
, we then introduce the
L^{2}(\Omega)
-projection
\Pi_{N}:L^{2}(\Omega)\to L^{2}(\Omega)
which truncates the eigenfunction expansion: if
f=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}f_{n}e_{n}
, then

\Pi_{N}f=\sum_{n=1}^{N}f_{n}e_{n}.

We define
h_{t}^{N}(\phi)=h_{t}(\Pi_{N}\phi)
, and note that since
h_{t}
satisfies the heat equation, (
3.0.13
) implies that
h_{t}^{N}
satisfies the finite dimensional heat equation

(3.0.14)

\partial_{t}h_{t}^{N}(\phi)=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n=1}^{N}e^{-t\lambda_{n}}D^{2}h_{t}^{N}(\phi,e_{n},e_{n})

with initial data
h_{\varepsilon^{2}}^{N}(\phi)=h_{\varepsilon^{2}}(\Pi_{N}\phi)
.
6
6

6

This really is a finite dimensional heat equation in the sense that if we write
\Pi_{N}\phi=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\phi_{n}e_{n}
, then

\frac{1}{2}\!\sum_{n=1}^{N}\!e^{-t\lambda_{n}}D^{2}h_{t}^{N}(\phi,e_{n},e_{n})=\frac{1}{2}\!\sum_{n=1}^{N}\!e^{-t\lambda_{n}}\partial_{\phi_{n}}^{2}h_{t}^{N}(\phi),

which is simply a Laplacian operator with a time-dependent rescaling in the spatial coordinates.

Moreover, by our controlled series expansion, this solution is bounded in the “spatial”
\phi
-coordinates.

Analogously to
[
8
]
, we can realize the field
\psi_{\varepsilon,t}
as the following series

\psi_{\varepsilon,t}(x)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\bigg(\int_{\varepsilon^{2}}^{t}ds\,e^{-s\lambda_{n}}\bigg)^{1/2}V_{n}e_{n}(x),

where
(V_{n})_{n\geq 1}
are independent standard Gaussians. Using this, one can check that
g_{t}=e^{-v_{t}}
satisfies the heat equation (
3.0.11
) and in particular,
g_{t}^{N}(\phi)=g_{t}(\Pi_{N}\phi)
satisfies the finite dimensional heat equation (
3.0.14
) with initial data
g_{\varepsilon^{2}}(\Pi_{N}\phi)=e^{-v_{\varepsilon^{2}}(\mu\eta,\Pi_{N}\phi|\varepsilon)}
and by definition, the solution is bounded in
\phi
.

Thus to argue that
g_{t}^{N}=h_{t}^{N}
, we must check that the initial data match. For
h_{t}^{N}
, the initial data is

h_{\varepsilon^{2}}(\Pi_{N}\phi)=e^{-u_{\varepsilon^{2}}(\mu\eta,\Pi_{N}\phi|\varepsilon)}

and since

\tilde{v}_{t}^{n}(\xi|\varepsilon)=\begin{cases}c_{\sigma\sqrt{\pi}}\varepsilon^{-\frac{\sigma^{2}}{4}},&n=1\\
0,&\text{else}\end{cases},

we see that

u_{\varepsilon^{2}}(\mu\eta,\Pi_{N}\phi|\varepsilon)=\int_{\Omega\times\{\pm 1,\pm 2\}}d\xi\,\mu\eta(\xi)c_{\sigma\sqrt{\pi}}\varepsilon^{-\frac{\sigma^{2}}{4}}e^{i\sqrt{\pi}\sigma\Pi_{N}\phi(x)}=v_{\varepsilon^{2}}(\mu\eta,\Pi_{N}\phi|\varepsilon)

so

h_{\varepsilon^{2}}^{N}(\phi)=g_{\varepsilon^{2}}^{N}(\phi)

and we must have
h_{t}^{N}(\phi)=g_{t}^{N}(\phi)
since we have bounded solutions to finite dimensional heat equation with the same initial data.

To conclude the proof, it is enough to show that
\lim_{N\to\infty}h_{t}^{N}(\phi)=h_{t}(\phi)
and
\lim_{N\to\infty}g_{t}^{N}(\phi)=g_{t}(\phi)
for smooth
\phi
. For this, we note that the uniform convergence of the series defining
u_{t}
implies that
u_{t}
is continuous in
\phi
and similarly, one can use the definition of
v_{t}
to show that
g_{t}
is continuous in
\phi
. It thus remains to prove that
\lim_{N\to\infty}\Pi_{N}\phi=\phi
uniformly for each compactly supported smooth function (compact support is sufficient since everything is happening in the support of
\eta
).

For uniform convergence, note that we have (a priori with convergence only in
L^{2}(\Omega)
),
\phi=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\phi_{n}e_{n}
where

\phi_{n}=\int_{\Omega}d^{2}x\,\phi(x)e_{n}(x)=\big(\lambda_{n}\big)^{-l}\int_{\Omega}d^{2}x(-\Delta)^{l}\phi(x)e_{n}(x)

for each
l\geq 0
. Taking
l=2
, we see by Cauchy-Schwarz that

|\phi_{n}|\leq C_{\phi}\lambda_{n}^{-2}.

Thus using Lemma
2.1
, we have

|(\Pi_{N}\phi)(x)-\phi(x)|\leq C_{\phi}\sum_{n=N+1}^{\infty}\lambda_{n}^{-\frac{7}{4}}\leq C_{\phi,\Omega}\sum_{n=N+1}^{\infty}n^{-7/4}

for a suitable constant
C_{\phi,\Omega}
.
This tends to zero as
N\to\infty
, so we conclude that
h_{t}(\phi)=g_{t}(\phi)
, implying that
u_{t}(\phi)=v_{t}(\phi)
for all smooth
\phi
. Since
u_{t}
and
v_{t}
are continuous, we see that
u_{t}(\phi)=v_{t}(\phi)
for all
\phi\in C(\Omega)
by density.
∎

Before going into the proof of Proposition
3.1
, we record some estimates that will be important for handling the cases
n=3
and
n=4
. We first look at an
n=3
estimate.

Lemma 3.3
.

For
0<\varepsilon^{2}<t<t_{0}
and
\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3}\in\Omega\times\{\pm 1,\pm 2\}
, we have

\big|\pi\sigma_{1}\sigma_{3}\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(t,x_{1},x_{3})+\pi\sigma_{2}\sigma_{3}\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(t,x_{2},x_{3})\big|\left|1-e^{-\pi\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\int_{\varepsilon^{2}}^{t}ds\,\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{1},x_{2})}\right|\leq F_{t}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3})

for some function
F_{t}
independent of
\varepsilon
and symmetric in the arguments. Moreover for any
K\subset\Omega
compact and
M\geq 1
, we have for each
\eta\in C_{c}(\Omega\times\{\pm 1,\pm 2\})
with
\mathrm{supp}(\eta(\cdot,\sigma))\subset K
and
\|\eta\|_{\infty}\leq M
, and
\sigma\in\{\pm 1,\pm 2\}^{3}
the estimate

\|\eta^{\otimes 3}F_{t}\|_{3}(\sigma)\leq Ct

for some constant
C=C_{K,M,t_{0}}
.

Proof.

Our first remark is that we always have

\left|1-e^{-\pi\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\int_{\varepsilon^{2}}^{t}ds\,\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{1},x_{2})}\right|\leq\left|1-e^{-\pi\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\int_{0}^{t}ds\,\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{1},x_{2})}\right|.

Thus it is enough to prove the claim for
\varepsilon=0
.

We also note that since (by (
2.1.3
)) we have
\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x,y)=\mathsf{p}(s,x,y)-R_{s}(x,y)
with
0\leq R_{s}(x,y)\leq\mathsf{p}(s,x,y)
, we have

\left|1-e^{-\pi\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\int_{0}^{t}ds\,\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{1},x_{2})}\right|\leq\left|1-e^{-\pi\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\int_{0}^{t}ds\,\mathsf{p}(s,x_{1},x_{2})}\right|.

Thus (using the triangle inequality), we see that we have

\displaystyle\big|\pi\sigma_{1}\sigma_{3}\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(t,x_{1},x_{3})+\pi\sigma_{2}\sigma_{3}\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(t,x_{2},x_{3})\big|\left|1-e^{-\pi\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\int_{\varepsilon^{2}}^{t}ds\,\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{1},x_{2})}\right|

\displaystyle\quad\leq\big|\pi\sigma_{1}\sigma_{3}\mathsf{p}(t,x_{1},x_{3})+\pi\sigma_{2}\sigma_{3}\mathsf{p}(t,x_{2},x_{3})\big|\left|1-e^{-\pi\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\int_{0}^{t}ds\,\mathsf{p}(s,x_{1},x_{2})}\right|

\displaystyle\qquad+\big|\pi\sigma_{1}\sigma_{3}R_{t}(x_{1},x_{3})+\pi\sigma_{2}\sigma_{3}R_{t}(x_{2},x_{3})\big|\left|1-e^{-\pi\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\int_{0}^{t}ds\,\mathsf{p}(s,x_{1},x_{2})}\right|.

The first term above is proven to satisfy the required estimate in
[
8
, Lemma 4.5]
(in the notation of
[
8
]
, one has to first take the
m\to 0
limit and choose
\beta
appropriately to get our claim). It remains to show that the
R_{t}
-term satisfies the required estimate. We consider separately
\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}<0
and
\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}>0
.

The case
\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}<0
: Here our task is to show that

|R_{t}(x_{1},x_{3})-R_{t}(x_{2},x_{3})|(e^{\pi|\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}|\int_{0}^{t}ds\,\mathsf{p}(s,x_{1},x_{2})}-1)

satisfies the desired estimate. For the exponential term, we rely on
[
8
, equation (4.41)]
, where it is shown that

(3.0.15)

e^{\pi|\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}|\int_{0}^{t}ds\,\mathsf{p}(s,x_{1},x_{2})}-1\leq C\int_{0}^{t}\frac{dr}{r}e^{-\frac{|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{2}}{4r}}e^{-\frac{|\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}|}{2}\log\big(\frac{|x_{1}-x_{2}|}{\sqrt{r}}\wedge 1\big)}.

for a universal constant
C>0
. For the
R
-term, we recall from (
2.1.7
) that if
\delta=d(\mathrm{supp}(\eta(\cdot,\sigma)),\allowbreak\partial\Omega)>0
(since we assumed that
\eta
has compact support), then for
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}\in\mathrm{supp}(\eta(\cdot,\sigma))

|R_{t}(x_{1},x_{3})-R_{t}(x_{2},x_{3})|\leq C|x_{1}-x_{2}|e^{-\frac{\delta^{2}}{32t}}

for
C=C_{K,M,t_{0}}
.

Thus the relevant estimate is

\displaystyle\sup_{x_{1}\in\Omega}\int_{\Omega^{2}}\!\!d^{2}x_{2}\,d^{2}x_{3}|\eta(x_{1},\sigma_{1})\eta(x_{2},\sigma_{2})\eta(x_{3},\sigma_{3})|C_{K,t_{0}}|x_{1}-x_{2}|e^{-\frac{\delta^{2}}{32t}}\!\!\int_{0}^{t}\!\frac{dr}{r}e^{-\frac{|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{2}}{4r}}\!e^{-\log\!\big(\frac{|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{2}}{r}\wedge 1\big)}

\displaystyle\quad\leq C_{K,M,t_{0}}e^{-\frac{\delta^{2}}{32t}}\int_{0}^{t}dr\,r^{1/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}du(|u|+|u|^{-1})e^{-\frac{|u|^{2}}{4}}

\displaystyle\quad=C_{K,M,t_{0}}t^{3/2}e^{-\frac{\delta^{2}}{32t}}

\displaystyle\quad\leq C_{K,M,t_{0}}t

for
t<t_{0}
. Here we made some basic shifts and scalings of the integration variables and the constant
C_{K,M,t_{0}}
may change from line to line. By symmetrizing, we find the contribution to
F_{t}
from the
R_{t}
-terms.

The case
\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}>0
: Here the argument is even simpler. We can simply make use of the bound
\Big|1-e^{-\pi\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\int_{0}^{t}ds\,\mathsf{p}(s,x_{1},x_{2})}\Big|\leq 1
and use (
2.1.6
) to find that the relevant estimate is

\displaystyle\sup_{x_{1}\in\Omega}\int_{\Omega^{2}}d^{2}x_{2}\,d^{2}x_{3}\,|\eta(x_{1},\sigma_{1})\eta(x_{2},\sigma_{2})\eta(x_{3},\sigma_{3})|R_{t}(x_{1},x_{2})

\displaystyle\leq C_{K,M,t_{0}}e^{-\frac{\delta^{2}}{32t}}\leq C_{K,M,t_{0}}t

for
t<t_{0}
. Again,
C_{K,M,t_{0}}
may change from one instance to another.
∎

For
n=4
, we have the following estimate which is of a very similar flavor.

Lemma 3.4
.

For
0<\varepsilon^{2}<t<t_{0}
and
\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3},\xi_{4}\in\Omega\times\{\pm 1,\pm 2\}
, we have

\displaystyle\Bigg|\sum_{i\in\{1,2\},j\in\{3,4\}}\pi\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(t,x_{i},x_{j})\Bigg|\left|1-e^{-\pi\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\int_{\varepsilon^{2}}^{t}ds\,\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{1},x_{2})}\right|\left|1-e^{-\pi\sigma_{3}\sigma_{4}\int_{\varepsilon^{2}}^{t}ds\,\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{3},x_{4})}\right|

\displaystyle\quad\leq G_{t}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3},\xi_{4}),

for some function
G_{t}
independent of
\varepsilon
and symmetric in its arguments. Moreover, for
K\subset\Omega
compact,
M\geq 1
,
\eta\in C_{c}(\Omega\times\{\pm 1,\pm 2\})
with
\mathrm{supp}(\eta(\cdot,\sigma))\subset K
and
\|\eta\|_{\infty}\leq M
, and
\sigma\in\{\pm 1,\pm 2\}^{4}
we have

\|\eta^{\otimes 4}G_{t}\|_{4}(\sigma)\leq C_{K,M,t_{0}}t^{2}

for some constant
C=C_{K,M,t_{0}}
.

Proof.

The proof is very similar to that of Lemma
3.3
and we only sketch the argument leaving the details to the reader. Again, one can use the bound

\left|1-e^{-\pi\sigma_{p}\sigma_{q}\int_{\varepsilon^{2}}^{t}ds\,\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{p},x_{q})}\right|\leq\left|1-e^{-\pi\sigma_{p}\sigma_{q}\int_{0}^{t}ds\,\mathsf{p}(s,x_{p},x_{q})}\right|

as well as write

\displaystyle\Bigg|\sum_{i\in\{1,2\},j\in\{3,4\}}\pi\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(t,x_{i},x_{j})\Bigg|

\displaystyle\quad\leq\Bigg|\sum_{i\in\{1,2\},j\in\{3,4\}}\pi\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\mathsf{p}(t,x_{i},x_{j})\Bigg|+\Bigg|\sum_{i\in\{1,2\},j\in\{3,4\}}\pi\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}R_{t}(x_{i},x_{j})\Bigg|.

The bound for the term involving only
\mathsf{p}
was obtained in
[
8
, Lemma 4.6]
. For the term involving
R_{t}
, one can, as in the proof of Lemma
3.3
, make use of (
2.1.6
), (
2.1.7
), and (
2.1.8
) to find the required estimate, and the final function
G_{t}
is obtained by symmetrizing. We leave further details to the interested reader.
∎

We now turn to the proof of the main result in this section.

Proof of Proposition
3.1
.

The proof is by induction, though we treat the
n=1,2,3,4
-cases explicitly.

The case of
n=1
: Here we begin by recalling from (
3.0.6
) that

\displaystyle\tilde{v}_{t}^{1}(\xi|\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=c_{\sigma\sqrt{\pi}}\exp\left\{-\frac{\pi}{2}\sigma^{2}\left[\frac{1}{4\pi}\log\varepsilon^{2}+\int_{\varepsilon^{2}}^{t}ds\left(\frac{1}{4\pi s}-R_{s}(x,x)\right)\right]\right\}

\displaystyle=c_{\sigma\sqrt{\pi}}\exp\left\{-\frac{1}{8}\sigma^{2}\log t+\frac{\pi}{2}\sigma^{2}\int_{\varepsilon^{2}}^{t}ds\,R_{s}(x,x)\right\}

Since
0\leq R_{s}(x,y)
for all
s,x,y
, (
2.1.6
) implies that for any fixed
t_{0}>0
there exists a
C=C_{t_{0},K}>0
such that for
x\in K
,
\int_{0}^{t_{0}}dsR_{s}(x,x)\leq C
. For this
C
, we have for
t<t_{0}
and
(x,\sigma)\in K\times\{\pm 1,\pm 2\}

(3.0.16)

0\leq\tilde{v}_{t}^{1}(\xi|\varepsilon)\leq c_{\sigma\sqrt{\pi}}e^{2\pi C}t^{-\frac{\sigma^{2}}{8}}=:h_{t}^{1}(\xi).

This clearly satisfies the required estimate for Proposition
3.1
, and we can move on.

The case of
n=2
: Let us begin the
n=2
case with an explicit expression for
\tilde{v}_{t}^{2}
. From (
3.0.7
) we get

(3.0.17)

\tilde{v}_{t}^{2}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}|\varepsilon)=\tilde{v}_{t}^{1}(\xi_{1}|\varepsilon)\tilde{v}_{t}^{1}(\xi_{2}|\varepsilon)\left(1-e^{-\pi\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\int_{\varepsilon^{2}}^{t}ds\,\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{1},x_{2})}\right).

Recalling that
0\leq\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}\leq\mathsf{p}
, using the bound from the
n=1
-case,

(3.0.18)

|\tilde{v}_{t}^{2}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}|\varepsilon)|\leq h_{t}^{1}(\xi_{1})h_{t}^{1}(\xi_{2})\left|1-e^{-\pi\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\int_{0}^{t}ds\,\mathsf{p}(s,x_{1},x_{2})}\right|=:h_{t}^{2}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}).

To see that this satisfies the conditions of Proposition
3.1
(where the relevant situation was
\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\neq-4
), we make use of (
3.0.15
) (and a similar estimate for
\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}>0
– see
[
8
, equation (4.47)]
) and the
n=1
-case to find

\displaystyle\|\eta^{\otimes 2}h_{t}^{2}\|_{2}(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\sup_{x_{1}\in\Omega}\int_{\Omega}d^{2}x_{2}\,e^{4\pi C}t^{-\frac{\sigma_{1}^{2}}{8}-\frac{\sigma_{2}^{2}}{8}}|\eta(x_{1},\sigma_{1})||\eta(x_{2},\sigma_{2})|\Big(e^{\pi\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\int_{0}^{t}ds\,\mathsf{p}(s,x_{1},x_{2})}-1\Big)

\displaystyle\leq C\sup_{x_{1}\in\Omega}\int_{\Omega}d^{2}x_{2}\,t^{-\frac{\sigma_{1}^{2}}{8}-\frac{\sigma_{2}^{2}}{8}}|\eta(x_{1},\sigma_{1})||\eta(x_{2},\sigma_{2})|\int_{0}^{t}\frac{dr}{r}e^{-\frac{|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{2}}{4r}}e^{\frac{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}{2}\log\big(\frac{|x_{1}-x_{2}|}{\sqrt{r}}\wedge 1\big)}

\displaystyle\leq Ct^{-\frac{\sigma_{1}^{2}}{8}-\frac{\sigma_{2}^{2}}{8}}\int_{0}^{t}dr\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}d^{2}u\,e^{-\frac{|u|^{2}}{4}}e^{\frac{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}{2}\log(|u|\wedge 1)},

where
C=C_{K,M,t_{0}}
can change from line to line. For
\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\neq-4
, this
\mathbb{R}^{2}
-integral is convergent and we find that for
t<t_{0}

\|\eta^{\otimes 2}h_{t}^{2}\|_{2}(\sigma)\leq Ct^{1-\frac{\sigma_{1}^{2}}{8}-\frac{\sigma_{2}^{2}}{8}}

for some constant
C=C_{K,M,t_{0}}
– namely the claim of Proposition
3.1
for
n=2
.

The case of
n=3
: From the recursion (
3.0.7
) and the expression (
3.0.17
) for
\tilde{v}_{t}^{2}
we can calculate the following expression for
\tilde{v}_{t}^{3}

\tilde{v}_{t}^{3}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3}|\varepsilon)\\
\quad=\pi\int_{\varepsilon^{2}}^{t}ds\prod_{j=1}^{3}\tilde{v}_{s}^{1}(\xi_{j}|\varepsilon)\bigg[\left(\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{1},x_{2})+\sigma_{1}\sigma_{3}\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{1},x_{3})\right)\left(1-e^{-\pi\sigma_{2}\sigma_{3}\int_{\varepsilon^{2}}^{s}dr\,\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(r,x_{2},x_{3})}\right)\\
\qquad\quad+\left(\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1}\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{2},x_{1})+\sigma_{2}\sigma_{3}\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{2},x_{3})\right)\left(1-e^{-\pi\sigma_{1}\sigma_{3}\int_{\varepsilon^{2}}^{s}dr\,\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(r,x_{1},x_{3})}\right)\\
\qquad\quad+\left(\sigma_{3}\sigma_{1}\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{3},x_{1})+\sigma_{3}\sigma_{2}\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{3},x_{2})\right)\left(1-e^{-\pi\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\int_{\varepsilon^{2}}^{s}dr\,\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(r,x_{1},x_{2})}\right)\bigg]\\
\times\exp\bigg\{-\frac{\pi}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{3}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\int_{s}^{t}dr\,\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(r,x_{i},x_{j})\bigg\}.

The last exponential term is bounded by one (since
\int_{s}^{t}dr\,\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(r,x,y)
is a covariance), so by Lemma
3.3
and the estimate for
\tilde{v}_{s}^{1}
we find

(3.0.19)

|\tilde{v}_{t}^{3}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3})|\leq C\int_{0}^{t}ds\,h_{s}^{1}(\xi_{1})h_{s}^{1}(\xi_{2})h_{s}^{1}(\xi_{3})F_{s}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3})=:h_{t}^{3}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3})

and the norm can be bounded by

\|\eta^{\otimes 3}h_{t}^{3}\|_{3}(\sigma)\leq C\int_{0}^{t}ds\,s^{-\frac{1}{8}\sigma_{1}^{2}-\frac{1}{8}\sigma_{2}^{2}-\frac{1}{8}\sigma_{3}^{2}}s\leq Ct^{2-\frac{1}{8}(\sigma_{1}^{2}+\sigma_{2}^{2}+\sigma_{3}^{2})},

as claimed in Proposition
3.1
(again,
C=C_{K,M,t_{0}}
may change from one instance to another). Note that here the
s
-integral is convergent since
\frac{1}{8}(\sigma_{1}^{2}+\sigma_{2}^{2}+\sigma_{3}^{2})\leq\frac{3}{2}
(if we allowed for slightly larger values of
\sigma
, the argument would need to be modified following
[
8
, Lemma 4.7]
).

The case of
n=4
: From the definition (
3.0.7
) we can write the following expression for
\tilde{v}_{t}^{4}

\tilde{v}_{t}^{4}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3},\xi_{4}|\varepsilon)\\
\quad=\int_{\varepsilon^{2}}^{t}ds\Bigg[\sum_{i=1}^{4}\sum_{j\in[4]\setminus\{i\}}\pi\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{i},x_{j})\tilde{v}_{s}^{1}(\xi_{i}|\varepsilon)\tilde{v}_{s}^{3}(\xi_{[4]\setminus\{i\}}|\varepsilon)\\
\qquad+\sum_{i\in\{1,2\},j\in\{3,4\}}\pi\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{i},x_{j})\tilde{v}_{s}^{2}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}|\varepsilon)\tilde{v}_{s}^{2}(\xi_{3},\xi_{4}|\varepsilon)\\
\qquad+\sum_{i\in\{1,3\},j\in\{2,4\}}\pi\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{i},x_{j})\tilde{v}_{s}^{2}(\xi_{1},\xi_{3}|\varepsilon)\tilde{v}_{s}^{2}(\xi_{2},\xi_{4}|\varepsilon)\\
+\sum_{i\in\{1,4\},j\in\{2,3\}}\pi\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{i},x_{j})\tilde{v}_{s}^{2}(\xi_{1},\xi_{4}|\varepsilon)\tilde{v}_{s}^{2}(\xi_{2},\xi_{3}|\varepsilon)\Bigg]e^{-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j\in[4]}\pi\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\int_{s}^{t}du\,\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(u,x_{i},x_{j})}.

We have written here
[4]=\{1,2,3,4\}
. We look separately at the two different kinds of terms, ones with
\tilde{v}_{s}^{1}\tilde{v}_{s}^{3}
and ones with
\tilde{v}_{s}^{2}\tilde{v}_{s}^{2}
.

Let us first consider the
1,3
-case. First of all, we note that again by positive definiteness, we can drop the last exponential term
\big(\sum_{i,j\in[4]}\pi\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\int_{s}^{t}du\,\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(u,x_{i},x_{j})\geq 0\big)
. Next we note that since
0\leq\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}\leq\mathsf{p}
, we have from the
n=1
and
n=3
cases

\Bigg|\sum_{i=1}^{4}\sum_{j\in[4]\setminus\{i\}}\pi\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{i},x_{j})\tilde{v}_{s}^{1}(\xi_{i}|\varepsilon)\tilde{v}_{s}^{3}(\xi_{[4]\setminus\{i\}}|\varepsilon)\Bigg|\leq 4\pi\sum_{i=1}^{4}\sum_{j\in[4]\setminus\{i\}}\mathsf{p}(s,x_{i},x_{j})h_{s}^{1}(\xi_{i})h_{s}^{3}(\xi_{[4]\setminus\{i\}}).

Using the bounds from the
n=1
and
n=3
-cases (along with the translation invariance of
\mathsf{p}
and the fact that
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}du\,\mathsf{p}(s,u,0)=1
for all
s>0
) we see that the
\|\cdot\|_{4}
-norm of this can be bounded by

4\pi\cdot 4\cdot 3\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}d^{2}u\,\mathsf{p}(s,u,0)\|\eta h_{s}^{1}\|_{1}\|\eta^{\otimes 3}h_{s}^{3}\|_{3}\leq Cs^{2-\frac{1}{8}\sum_{j=1}^{4}\sigma_{j}^{2}}.

Since
\sigma_{j}^{2}\leq 4
, this is integrable in
s
, so when we integrate from
0
to
t
, we get a bound of the form
Ct^{3-\frac{1}{8}\sum_{j=1}^{4}\sigma_{j}^{2}}
– precisely of the form needed for Proposition
3.1
.

It remains to understand the
\tilde{v}_{2}\tilde{v}_{2}
-terms, and by symmetry, we can focus on the term

\sum_{i\in\{1,2\},j\in\{3,4\}}\pi\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{i},x_{j})\tilde{v}_{s}^{2}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}|\varepsilon)\tilde{v}_{s}^{2}(\xi_{3},\xi_{4}|\varepsilon)\\
=\sum_{i\in\{1,2\},j\in\{3,4\}}\pi\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{i},x_{j})\tilde{v}_{s}^{1}(\xi_{1}|\varepsilon)\tilde{v}_{s}^{1}(\xi_{2}|\varepsilon)\left(1-e^{-\pi\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\int_{\varepsilon^{2}}^{s}du\,\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(u,x_{1},x_{2})}\right)\\
\times\tilde{v}_{s}^{1}(\xi_{3}|\varepsilon)\tilde{v}_{s}^{1}(\xi_{4}|\varepsilon)\left(1-e^{-\pi\sigma_{3}\sigma_{4}\int_{\varepsilon^{2}}^{s}dr\,\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(r,x_{3},x_{4})}\right).

By the estimate (
3.0.16
) and Lemma
3.4
we have

\displaystyle\Bigg|\sum_{i\in\{1,2\},j\in\{3,4\}}\pi\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{i},x_{j})\tilde{v}_{s}^{2}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}|\varepsilon)\tilde{v}_{s}^{2}(\xi_{3},\xi_{4}|\varepsilon)\Bigg|

\displaystyle\quad\leq h_{s}^{1}(\xi_{1})h_{s}^{1}(\xi_{2})h_{s}^{1}(\xi_{3})h_{s}^{1}(\xi_{4})G_{s}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3},\xi_{4}).

So the terms in
\tilde{v}_{t}^{4}
containing
\tilde{v}_{s}^{2}\tilde{v}_{s}^{2}
we want to estimate the norm of are

\displaystyle\Bigg|\int_{\varepsilon^{2}}^{t}ds\sum_{i\in\{1,2\},j\in\{3,4\}}\pi\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{i},x_{j})\tilde{v}_{s}^{2}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}|\varepsilon)\tilde{v}_{s}^{2}(\xi_{3},\xi_{4}|\varepsilon)e^{-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j\in[4]}\pi\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\int_{s}^{t}du\,\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(u,x_{i},x_{j})}\Bigg|

\displaystyle\quad\leq\int_{0}^{t}ds\,h_{s}^{1}(\xi_{1})h_{s}^{1}(\xi_{2})h_{s}^{1}(\xi_{3})h_{s}^{1}(\xi_{4})G_{s}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3},\xi_{4}),

where again we dropped the last exponential factor.

Calculating the norm, we find by (
3.0.16
) and Lemma
3.4
that

\displaystyle\sup_{x_{1}\in\Omega}\int_{\Omega^{3}}d^{2}x_{2}\,d^{2}x_{3}\,d^{2}x_{4}\,\int_{0}^{t}ds\,h_{s}^{1}(\xi_{1})h_{s}^{1}(\xi_{2})h_{s}^{1}(\xi_{3})h_{s}^{1}(\xi_{4})G_{s}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3},\xi_{4})

\displaystyle\quad\leq C\int_{0}^{t}ds\,s^{-\frac{1}{8}\sigma_{1}^{2}}s^{-\frac{1}{8}\sigma_{2}^{2}}s^{-\frac{1}{8}\sigma_{3}^{2}}s^{-\frac{1}{8}\sigma_{4}^{2}}\|G_{s}\|_{4}

\displaystyle\quad\leq C\int_{0}^{t}ds\,s^{-\frac{1}{8}(\sigma_{1}^{2}+\sigma_{2}^{2}+\sigma_{3}^{2}+\sigma_{4}^{2})}s^{2}

\displaystyle\quad\leq Ct^{3-\frac{1}{8}(\sigma_{1}^{2}+\sigma_{2}^{2}+\sigma_{3}^{2}+\sigma_{4}^{2})},

where again
C=C_{K,M,t_{0}}
may vary from line to line. Also, again, since
\sigma_{j}^{2}\leq 4
, the integral is convergent.

Combining the two cases, we find a
h_{t}^{4}
which satisfies the claim of Proposition
3.1
in the
n=4
case.

We can finally move on to the induction step.

The general case
: Above we have showed the existence of
h_{t}^{n}
for
n=1,3,4
(as well as for
n=2
with
\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\neq-4
) and the bounds for the norms agree with the proposition. For
n\geq 5
we follow the induction proof from
[
5
,
8
,
16
]
.

Assume that for all
k<n
and
k\neq 2
, it holds that for
t<t_{0}
,
|\tilde{v}_{t}^{|I|}(\xi_{I})|\leq h_{t}^{k}(\xi_{I})
and

(3.0.20)

\|\eta^{\otimes k}h_{t}^{k}\|_{k}(\sigma)\leq k^{k-2}t^{-1}C^{k}t^{\frac{1}{8}\big(8k-\sum_{j=1}^{k}\sigma_{j}^{2}\big)}

for some
C=C_{K,M,t_{0}}
.

We can split the definition of
\tilde{v}_{t}^{n}
from (
3.0.7
) in two parts, one function that does not include factors of
\tilde{v}_{t}^{2}
and another function does. We look at these separately and begin with the one with no factors of
\tilde{v}_{t}^{2}
. Using the induction hypothesis, the fact that
\int_{s}^{t}du\,\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(u,\cdot,\cdot)
is a covariance, and that
\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}\leq\mathsf{p}
, we find that

\displaystyle\Bigg|\frac{1}{2}\int_{\varepsilon^{2}}^{t}ds\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I_{1}\dot{\cup}I_{2}=[n]\\
|I_{1}|,|I_{2}|\neq 2\end{subarray}}\sum_{i\in I_{1},j\in I_{2}}\pi\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{i},x_{j})\tilde{v}_{s}^{|I_{1}|}(\xi_{I_{1}}|\varepsilon)\tilde{v}_{s}^{|I_{2}|}(\xi_{I_{2}}|\varepsilon)e^{-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{l,m\in I}\pi\sigma_{l}\sigma_{m}\int_{s}^{t}du\,\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(u,x_{l},x_{m})}\Bigg|

\displaystyle\quad\leq 2\pi\int_{0}^{t}ds\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I_{1}\dot{\cup}I_{2}=[n]\\
|I_{1}|,|I_{2}|\neq 2\end{subarray}}\sum_{i\in I_{1},j\in I_{2}}\mathsf{p}(s,x_{i},x_{j})h_{s}^{|I_{1}|}(\xi_{I_{1}})h_{s}^{|I_{2}|}(\xi_{I_{2}})

\displaystyle\qquad=:h_{t}^{n,1}(\xi_{I}).

Here we have used the notation
[n]=\{1,\dots,n\}
.

Estimating the norm using symmetry, the induction hypothesis (
3.0.20
), and the fact that
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}d^{2}x\,\mathsf{p}(s,x,0)=1
, we find

\displaystyle\|\eta^{\otimes n}h_{t}^{n,1}\|_{n}

\displaystyle\leq 2\pi\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I_{1}\dot{\cup}I_{2}=[n]\\
|I_{1}|,|I_{2}|\neq 2\end{subarray}}|I_{1}||I_{2}|\int_{0}^{t}ds\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}d^{2}x\,\mathsf{p}(s,x,0)\|\eta^{\otimes|I_{1}|}h_{s}^{|I_{1}|}\|_{|I_{1}|}\|\eta^{\otimes|I_{2}|}h_{s}^{|I_{2}|}\|_{|I_{2}|}

\displaystyle\leq 2\pi\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I_{1}\dot{\cup}I_{2}=[n]\\
|I_{1}|,|I_{2}|\neq 2\end{subarray}}|I_{1}||I_{2}|\int_{0}^{t}ds\,|I_{1}|^{|I_{1}|-2}s^{-1}C^{|I_{1}|}s^{\frac{1}{8}\big(8|I_{1}|-\sum_{j\in I_{1}}\sigma_{j}^{2}\big)}

\displaystyle\quad\times|I_{2}|^{|I_{2}|-2}s^{-1}C^{|I_{2}|}s^{\frac{1}{8}\big(8|I_{2}|-\sum_{k\in I_{2}}\sigma_{k}^{2}\big)}

\displaystyle=2\pi C^{n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I_{1}\dot{\cup}I_{2}=[n]\\
|I_{1}|,|I_{2}|\neq 2\end{subarray}}|I_{1}|^{|I_{1}|-1}|I_{2}|^{|I_{2}|-1}\int_{0}^{t}ds\,s^{-2+\frac{1}{8}\big(8n-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sigma_{j}^{2}\big)}

\displaystyle\leq 2\pi C^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}\binom{n}{k}k^{k-1}(n-k)^{n-k-1}\frac{1}{-1+\frac{1}{8}\big(8n-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sigma_{j}^{2}\big)}t^{-1+\frac{1}{8}\big(8n-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sigma_{j}^{2}\big)}

\displaystyle\leq 2\pi C^{n}n^{n-2}\frac{2(n-1)}{-1+\frac{1}{8}(8n-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sigma_{j}^{2})}t^{-1}t^{\frac{1}{8}\big(8n-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sigma_{j}^{2}\big)}.

Here we made use of the identity
\sum_{k=1}^{n}\binom{n}{k}k^{k-1}(n-k)^{n-k-1}=2(n-1)n^{n-2}
(see
[
8
, Proof of Proposition 4.1]
for an explanation of this identity) and noted that since
\sigma_{j}^{2}\leq 4
and
n\geq 5
, the
s
–integral is convergent. The point now is that for
n\geq 5
,
\frac{2(n-1)}{-1+\frac{1}{8}(8n-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sigma_{j}^{2})}
is bounded by a universal constant. Thus by possibly increasing
C
, we find the correct bound for terms not involving
\tilde{v}_{2}
.

Now for the terms with
|I_{1}|=2
or
|I_{2}|=2
, recalling (
3.0.17
), Lemma
3.3
and the induction hypothesis, and dropping the exponential term as before, we find

\displaystyle\Bigg|\frac{1}{2}\int_{\varepsilon^{2}}^{t}ds\hskip-9.0pt\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I_{1}\dot{\cup}I_{2}=[n]\\
|I_{1}|=2\vee|I_{2}|=2\end{subarray}}\sum_{i\in I_{1},j\in I_{2}}\hskip-3.0pt\pi\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{i},x_{j})\tilde{v}_{s}^{|I_{1}|}(\xi_{I_{1}}|\varepsilon)\tilde{v}_{s}^{|I_{2}|}(\xi_{I_{2}}|\varepsilon)e^{-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{l,m\in I}\pi\sigma_{l}\sigma_{m}\int_{s}^{t}du\,\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(u,x_{l},x_{m})}\Bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{t}ds\!\sum_{1\leq a<b\leq n}\,\sum_{j\in[n]\setminus\{a,b\}}|\pi\sigma_{a}\sigma_{j}\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{a},x_{j})+\pi\sigma_{b}\sigma_{j}\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{b},x_{j})|

\displaystyle\quad\times|\tilde{v}_{s}^{2}(\xi_{a},\xi_{b}|\varepsilon)||\tilde{v}_{s}^{n-2}(\xi_{[n]\setminus\{a,b\}}|\varepsilon)|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{1\leq a<b\leq n}\,\sum_{j\in[n]\setminus\{a,b\}}\int_{0}^{t}ds|\pi\sigma_{a}\sigma_{j}\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{a},x_{j})+\pi\sigma_{b}\sigma_{j}\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{b},x_{j})|\left|1-e^{-\pi\sigma_{a}\sigma_{b}\int_{\varepsilon^{2}}^{s}du\,\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(u,x_{a},x_{b})}\right|

\displaystyle\quad\times\tilde{v}_{s}^{1}(\xi_{a}|\varepsilon)\tilde{v}_{s}^{1}(\xi_{b}|\varepsilon)|\tilde{v}_{s}^{n-2}(\xi_{[n]\setminus\{a,b\}}|\varepsilon)|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{1\leq a<b\leq n}\,\sum_{j\in[n]\setminus\{a,b\}}\int_{0}^{t}ds\,F_{s}(\xi_{a},\xi_{b},\xi_{j})h_{s}^{1}(\xi_{a})h_{s}^{2}(\xi_{b})h_{s}^{n-2}(\xi_{[n]\setminus\{a,b\}})

\displaystyle\quad=:h_{t}^{n,2}(\xi).

Using the induction hypothesis (
3.0.20
), Lemma
3.3
, and symmetry, we get

\displaystyle\|\eta^{\otimes n}h_{t}^{n,2}\|_{n}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{n(n-1)}{2}(n-2)C^{n}(n-2)^{n-4}\int_{0}^{t}ds\,s^{-2}s^{\frac{1}{8}\big(8n-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sigma_{j}^{2}\big)}

\displaystyle\leq\widetilde{C}\frac{n(n-1)}{2}(n-2)^{n-3}C^{n}\frac{2}{n-2}t^{-1+\frac{1}{8}\big(8n-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sigma_{j}^{2}\big)}

\displaystyle\leq\widetilde{C}n^{n-2}t^{-1}C^{n}t^{\frac{1}{8}\big(8n-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sigma_{j}^{2}\big)},

where
\widetilde{C}
is the constant from Lemma
3.3
, and again, we note that the integral is convergent as
n\geq 5
. Just as in the case of no
\tilde{v}_{t}^{2}
-terms, we can again possibly increase
C
to find the correct bound.

Thus, choosing
h_{t}^{n}=h_{t}^{n,1}+h_{t}^{n,2}
(and symmetrizing) it holds that
|\tilde{v}_{t}^{n}(\xi|\varepsilon)|\leq h_{t}^{n}(\xi)
and that

\|\eta^{\otimes n}h_{t}^{n}\|_{n}\leq n^{n-2}t^{-1}C^{n}t^{\frac{1}{8}\big(8n-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sigma_{j}^{2}\big)},

as required.
∎

4.
The Renormalized Partition Function

In this section, we will study the
\varepsilon\to 0
asymptotics of the partition function (
3.0.2
) through our estimates for the renormalized potential from Proposition
3.1
and Corollary
3.2
. We will see that to have a non-trivial
\varepsilon\to 0
limit, we must renormalize the partition function with an explicit multiplicative counter term. After understanding these
\varepsilon\to 0
asymptotics, we will be able to analyze the relevant sine-Gordon correlation functions (in Section
5
). The discussion here is again very similar to that in
[
8
, Section 5]
.

The idea in our approach is to use (
3.0.4
), namely
Z(\eta|\varepsilon)=\langle e^{-v_{t}(\eta,\varphi_{\sqrt{t}}|\varepsilon)}\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\sqrt{t})}
, and control this with the expansion provided by Corollary
3.2
. The issue with this is the
n=2
-term in the expansion of
v_{t}
, namely

(4.0.1)

\frac{1}{2}\int_{(\Omega\times\{\pm 1,\pm 2\})^{2}}d\xi_{1}\,d\xi_{2}\,\eta(\xi_{1})\eta(\xi_{2})\tilde{v}_{t}^{2}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}|\varepsilon)e^{i\sqrt{\pi}\sum_{j=1}^{2}\sigma_{j}\varphi_{\sqrt{t}}(x_{j})}.

Recalling from (
3.0.17
) that
\tilde{v}_{t}^{2}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}|\varepsilon)=\tilde{v}_{t}^{1}(\xi_{1}|\varepsilon)\tilde{v}_{t}^{1}(\xi_{2}|\varepsilon)\left(1-e^{-\pi\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\int_{\varepsilon^{2}}^{t}ds\,\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{1},x_{2})}\right)
, one can show that if
\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}<0
, then in the
\varepsilon\to 0
limit, this behaves like
|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{-\frac{|\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}|}{2}}
as
x_{1}\to x_{2}
. In particular, if
\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}=-4
, then this is not an integrable singularity. It follows that the
n=2
-term is divergent. This divergence can be cured with a single counter term and the
n\neq 2
terms can all be controlled with Proposition
3.1
.

Let us begin by defining our counter term and renormalized partition function. There could be various choices here (in particular, compared to
[
8
]
, we add a non-singular term to the counter term as this is more convenient for identifying with Ising). For
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\Omega\times\{-2,2\}
(note that there is no counter term needed for
\sigma=\pm 1
), let

(4.0.2)

A(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}|\varepsilon):=\left\langle\mathop{:}e^{i\sqrt{\pi}\sigma_{1}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x_{1})}\mathop{:}\mathop{:}e^{i\sqrt{\pi}\sigma_{2}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x_{2})}\mathop{:}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}\\
-\left\langle\mathop{:}e^{i\sqrt{\pi}\sigma_{1}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x_{1})}\mathop{:}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}\left\langle\mathop{:}e^{i\sqrt{\pi}\sigma_{2}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x_{2})}\mathop{:}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}.

We can then define (for
\eta\in C_{c}(\Omega\times\{\pm 1,\pm 2\})
) the renormalized partition function as

(4.0.3)

\mathcal{Z}(\eta|\varepsilon)=Z(\eta|\varepsilon)\exp\bigg\{-\frac{1}{2}\int_{(\Omega\times\{\pm 2\})^{2}}d\xi_{1}\,d\xi_{2}\,\eta(\xi_{1})\eta(\xi_{2})A(x_{1},x_{2}|\varepsilon)\bigg\}.

The goal of this section is to prove the following theorem.

Theorem 4.1
.

For
\eta\in C_{c}(\Omega\times\{\pm 1,\pm 2\})
the following claims hold

(i)

The limit

\mathcal{Z}(\eta):=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathcal{Z}(\eta|\varepsilon),

exists and is finite.

(ii)

The function
\mu\to\mathcal{Z}(\mu\eta)
is an entire function of
\mu\in\mathbb{C}
.

(iii)

If
\eta(x,\sigma)=\overline{\eta(x,-\sigma)}
for
\sigma\in\{1,2\}
and
x\in\Omega
, then
\mathcal{Z}(\eta)>0
.

(iv)

Let
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}^{N}
be some complex parameters and
K\subset\mathbb{C}^{N}
compact. If
\eta_{\alpha}(\cdot),\eta_{\alpha}(\cdot|\varepsilon)\in C_{c}(\Omega\times\{\pm 1,\pm 2\})
depend on
\alpha
so that

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\sup_{\alpha\in K}\|\eta_{\alpha}(\cdot|\varepsilon)-\eta_{\alpha}(\cdot)\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega\times\{\pm 1,\pm 2\})}=0,

(4.0.4)

\sup_{\alpha\in K}\|\eta_{\alpha}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega\times\{\pm 1,\pm 2\})}<\infty\qquad\text{and}\qquad\overline{\bigcup_{\alpha\in K}\bigcup_{\sigma\in\{\pm 1,\pm 2\}}\mathrm{supp}(\eta_{\alpha}(\cdot,\sigma))}\subset\Omega\qquad\text{is compact},

then

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}|\mathcal{Z}(\eta_{\alpha}(\cdot|\varepsilon)|\varepsilon)-\mathcal{Z}(\eta_{\alpha}(\cdot))|=0

and the convergence is uniform in
\alpha\in K
.

The main step in proving the convergence and analyticity statements of Theorem
4.1
, is to prove suitable bounds. More precisely, we prove the following proposition.

Proposition 4.2
.

Fix a compact set
K\subset\Omega
. Then

(i)

For any fixed
M>0

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\varepsilon\in(0,1),\eta\in C_{c}(\Omega\times\{\pm 1,\pm 2\}):\\
\|\eta\|_{L^{\infty}(K\times\{\pm 1,\pm 2\})}\leq M,\\
\mathrm{supp}(\eta(\cdot,\sigma))\subset K\end{subarray}}|\mathcal{Z}(\eta|\varepsilon)|<\infty.

(ii)

There exists a
\delta>0
independent of
\varepsilon
such that

\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\varepsilon\in(0,1),\eta\in C_{c}(\Omega\times\{\pm 1,\pm 2\}):\\
\|\eta\|_{L^{\infty}(K\times\{\pm 1,\pm 2\})}\leq\delta,\\
\mathrm{supp}(\eta(\cdot,\sigma))\subset K\end{subarray}}|\mathcal{Z}(\eta|\varepsilon)|>0.

(iii)

For any fixed
M>0
, if
\eta(\cdot,\sigma)=\overline{\eta(\cdot,-\sigma)}
for
\sigma\in\{1,2\}
,

\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\varepsilon\in(0,1),\eta\in C_{c}(\Omega\times\{\pm 1,\pm 2\})\\
\|\eta\|_{L^{\infty}(K\times\{\pm 1,\pm 2\})}\leq M\\
\mathrm{supp}(\eta(\cdot,\sigma))\subset K\\
\eta(\cdot,\sigma)=\overline{\eta(\cdot,-\sigma)}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{Z}(\eta|\varepsilon)>0.

The proof is rather lengthy. We begin with some facts that are needed to control the
n=2
-term: first we recall some basic estimates for Gaussian processes and then some estimates for the counter term.

Lemma 4.3
.

For any fixed
K\subset\Omega
compact,
M>0
,
t>0
there exists a constant
C_{K,M,t}
such that if
\eta\in C_{c}(\Omega)
satisfies
\mathrm{supp}(\eta(\cdot,\sigma))\subset K
and
\|\eta\|_{\infty}\leq M
, then

\left\langle e^{\|\eta\nabla\varphi_{\sqrt{t}}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\sqrt{t})}\leq C_{K,M,t}.

For the proof (which is very similar to the proof of
[
8
, Lemma 5.2]
), we will need a general fact about extrema of Gaussian processes known as the Borell-TIS inequality
[
1
, Theorem 2.1.1.]
. To state the theorem, let us assume that
X
is an almost surely bounded Gaussian process on a topological space
T
and define

(4.0.5)

\sigma_{T}^{2}:=\sup_{x\in T}\mathbb{E}\Big[X(x)^{2}\Big].

Then

\mathbb{E}\bigg[\sup_{x\in T}X(x)\bigg]<\infty

and for all
u>0
,

(4.0.6)

\mathbb{P}\bigg(\sup_{x\in T}X(x)-\mathbb{E}\bigg[\sup_{x\in T}X(x)\bigg]>u\bigg)\leq e^{-u^{2}/(2\sigma_{T}^{2})}.

We can now turn to the proof of the Gaussian estimate.

Proof of Lemma
4.3
.

We begin by pointing out that by symmetry,
\partial_{1}\varphi_{\sqrt{t}}
and
\partial_{2}\varphi_{\sqrt{t}}
have the same law, so by Cauchy-Schwarz it is sufficient to prove the statement for
\nabla\varphi_{\sqrt{t}}
replaced by say
\partial_{1}\varphi_{\sqrt{t}}
. Let us write
K
for the support of
\eta
. Noting that since
\varphi_{\sqrt{t}}
is centered,

\displaystyle\left\langle e^{\|\eta\partial_{1}\varphi_{\sqrt{t}}\|_{\infty}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\sqrt{t})}

\displaystyle\leq\left\langle e^{\|\eta\|_{\infty}\sup_{x\in K}|\partial_{1}\varphi_{\sqrt{t}}|}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\sqrt{t})}

\displaystyle\leq\left\langle e^{M\sup_{x\in K}\partial_{1}\varphi_{\sqrt{t}}(x)}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\sqrt{t})}+\left\langle e^{M\sup_{x\in K}(-\partial_{1}\varphi_{\sqrt{t}}(x))}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\sqrt{t})}

\displaystyle=2\left\langle e^{M\sup_{x\in K}\partial_{1}\varphi_{\sqrt{t}}(x)}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\sqrt{t})}.

We will control this by estimating the tail of the distribution of
\partial_{1}\varphi_{\sqrt{t}}
with Borell-TIS.

To apply Borell-TIS, let us compute
\sigma_{t}^{2}=\sup_{x\in K}\mathbb{E}\Big[\big(\partial_{1}\varphi_{\sqrt{t}}(x)\big)^{2}\Big]
. Using Lemma
2.1
, we have for
x\in K

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\big(\partial_{1}\varphi_{\sqrt{t}}(x)\big)^{2}\Big]=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}}e^{-t\lambda_{n}}|\partial_{1}e_{n}(x)|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C_{t,K}

for a suitable constant
C_{t,K}>0
.

If we now let
\tilde{u}=u-\mathbb{E}\big[\sup_{x\in K}\partial_{1}\varphi_{\sqrt{t}}(x)\big]>0
we get from (
4.0.6
)

(4.0.7)

\mathbb{P}\bigg(\sup_{x\in\Omega}\partial_{1}\varphi_{\sqrt{t}}(x)>u\bigg)\leq\exp\left\{-\frac{\tilde{u}^{2}}{2\sigma_{t}^{2}}\right\}\leq\exp\bigg\{-C_{t,K}\bigg(u-\mathbb{E}\bigg[\sup_{x\in K}\partial_{1}\varphi_{\sqrt{t}}(x)\bigg]\bigg)^{2}\bigg\}

for some (possibly different from earlier) constant
C_{t,K}>0
.

Writing

\displaystyle\left\langle e^{M\sup_{x\in K}\partial_{1}\varphi_{\sqrt{t}}(x)}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\sqrt{t})}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\int_{\mathbb{R}}du\,Me^{Mu}\mathbf{1}_{\{\sup_{x\in K}\partial_{1}\varphi_{\sqrt{t}}(x)>u\}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\sqrt{t})}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}du\,Me^{Mu}\mathbb{P}\bigg(\sup_{x\in K}\partial_{1}\varphi_{\sqrt{t}}(x)>u\bigg),

and letting
\mu_{t,K}=\mathbb{E}\big[\sup_{x\in K}\partial_{1}\varphi_{\sqrt{t}}(x)\big]
(which is finite by Borell-TIS), by (
4.0.7
)

\displaystyle\left\langle e^{M\sup_{x\in K}\partial_{1}\varphi_{\sqrt{t}}(x)}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\sqrt{t})}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{-\infty}^{\mu_{t,K}}du\,Me^{Mu}+\int^{\infty}_{\mu_{t,K}}du\,Me^{Mu}\mathbb{P}\bigg(\sup_{x\in K}\partial_{1}\varphi_{\sqrt{t}}(x)>u\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{-\infty}^{\mu_{t,K}}du\,Me^{Mu}+\int^{\infty}_{\mu_{t,K}}du\,Me^{Mu}e^{-C_{t,K}(u-\mu_{t,K})^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{-\infty}^{\mu_{t,K}}du\,Me^{Mu}+e^{M\mu_{t,K}}\int^{\infty}_{0}dv\,Me^{Mv-C_{t,K}v^{2}}.

This is a finite constant depending only on
M,t,K
. Thus

\left\langle e^{\sup_{x\in\Omega}|\eta(x)\nabla\varphi_{\sqrt{t}}(x)|}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\sqrt{t})}\leq\tilde{C}_{K,M,t}<\infty.

∎

We now turn to estimating the counter term. The following lemma says basically that in the renormalized partition function, we can replace
A
by
\tilde{v}_{t}^{2}
for any fixed
t>0
without influencing convergence.

Lemma 4.4
.

For each
t>0
, there exists a function
g_{t}\in L_{loc}^{1}((\Omega\times\{-2,2\})^{2})

(
namely
g_{t}((\cdot,\sigma),(\cdot,\sigma^{\prime}))\in L^{1}(K)
for any
K\subset\Omega^{2}
compact and
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\in\{\pm 2\}
\,)
which is independent of
\varepsilon
such that for all
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\Omega\times\{-2,2\}
and
t>\varepsilon^{2}

\big|A(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}|\varepsilon)+\tilde{v}_{t}^{2}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}|\varepsilon)\big|\leq g_{t}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}),

with
A(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}|\varepsilon)
from (
4.0.2
) and
\tilde{v}_{t}^{2}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}|\varepsilon)
from (
3.0.17
).

Proof.

First of all, note that for
\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}=4
, the left hand side is bounded and there is nothing to prove. We can thus focus on the
\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}=-4
-case.

From (
3.0.17
) and (
3.0.6
) we get that

\displaystyle\tilde{v}_{t}^{2}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}|\varepsilon)\mathbf{1}_{\{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}=-4\}}

\displaystyle=\tilde{v}_{t}^{1}(\xi_{1}|\varepsilon)\tilde{v}_{t}^{1}(\xi_{2}|\varepsilon)\left(1-e^{-\pi\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\int_{\varepsilon^{2}}^{t}ds\,\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{1},x_{2})}\right)\mathbf{1}_{\{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}=-4\}}

\displaystyle=c_{\sigma_{1}\sqrt{\pi}}c_{\sigma_{2}\sqrt{\pi}}\varepsilon^{-\frac{\sigma_{1}^{2}+\sigma_{2}^{2}}{4}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\pi\sigma_{1}^{2}\int_{\varepsilon^{2}}^{t}ds\,\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{1},x_{1})}

\displaystyle\qquad\times e^{-\frac{1}{2}\pi\sigma_{2}^{2}\int_{\varepsilon^{2}}^{t}ds\,\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{2},x_{2})}\left(1-e^{-\pi\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\int_{\varepsilon^{2}}^{t}ds\,\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{1},x_{2})}\right)\mathbf{1}_{\{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}=-4\}}

\displaystyle=c_{\sqrt{4\pi}}^{2}\varepsilon^{-2}e^{-2\pi\int_{\varepsilon^{2}}^{t}ds\,\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{1},x_{1})}e^{-2\pi\int_{\varepsilon^{2}}^{t}ds\,\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{2},x_{2})}\!\left(\!1-e^{4\pi\int_{\varepsilon^{2}}^{t}ds\,\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{1},x_{2})}\right).

Calculating
A
, we have (by symmetry we can choose
\sigma_{1}=2
,
\sigma_{2}=-2
)

\displaystyle A(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}|\varepsilon)\mathbf{1}_{\{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}=-4\}}

\displaystyle\quad=\left\langle\mathop{:}e^{i\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x_{1})}\mathop{:}\mathop{:}e^{-i\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x_{2})}\mathop{:}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}-\left\langle\mathop{:}e^{i\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x_{1})}\mathop{:}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}\left\langle\mathop{:}e^{-i\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x_{2})}\mathop{:}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}

\displaystyle\quad=c_{\sqrt{4\pi}}^{2}\varepsilon^{-2}\left\langle e^{i\sqrt{4\pi}\big(\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x_{1})-\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x_{2})\big)}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}-\left\langle\mathop{:}e^{i\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x_{1})}\mathop{:}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}\left\langle\mathop{:}e^{-i\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x_{2})}\mathop{:}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}

\displaystyle\quad=c_{\sqrt{4\pi}}^{2}\varepsilon^{-2}e^{-2\pi\int_{\varepsilon^{2}}^{\infty}ds\,\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{1},x_{1})}e^{-2\pi\int_{\varepsilon^{2}}^{\infty}ds\,\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{2},x_{2})}\left(e^{4\pi\int_{\varepsilon^{2}}^{\infty}ds\,\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{1},x_{2})}-1\right).

Thus

\big|A(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}|\varepsilon)+\tilde{v}_{t}^{2}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}|\varepsilon)\big|\mathbf{1}_{\{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}=-4\}}\\
\quad\leq c_{\sqrt{4\pi}}^{2}\varepsilon^{-2}e^{-2\pi\int_{\varepsilon^{2}}^{t}ds\,(\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{1},x_{1})+\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{2},x_{2}))}e^{4\pi\int_{\varepsilon^{2}}^{t}ds\,\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{1},x_{2})}\\
\qquad\times\Big|e^{-2\pi\int_{t}^{\infty}ds\,(\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{1},x_{1})+\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{2},x_{2}))}e^{4\pi\int_{t}^{\infty}ds\,\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{1},x_{2})}-1\Big|\\
+c_{\sqrt{4\pi}}^{2}\varepsilon^{-2}e^{-2\pi\int_{\varepsilon^{2}}^{t}ds\,(\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{1},x_{1})+\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{2},x_{2}))}\Big|e^{-2\pi\int_{t}^{\infty}ds\,(\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{1},x_{1})+\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{2},x_{2}))}-1\Big|.

Let us estimate the various quantities here for fixed a compact set
K\subset{\Omega}
.

First of all, from (
3.0.16
), we have for each
t>0
a constant
C_{t,K}>0
such that for
x_{1},x_{2}\in K

c_{\sqrt{4\pi}}^{2}\varepsilon^{-2}e^{-2\pi\int_{\varepsilon^{2}}^{t}ds\,(\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{1},x_{1})+\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{2},x_{2}))}\leq C_{t,K}.

Next, we note since
\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}\leq\mathsf{p}
,
[
8
, Lemma 4.4]
implies that for each
t>0
there exists a constant
C_{t,K}>0
such that for
x_{1},x_{2}\in K

e^{4\pi\int_{\varepsilon^{2}}^{t}ds\,\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{1},x_{2})}\leq e^{4\pi\int_{0}^{t}ds\,\mathsf{p}(s,x_{1},x_{2})}\leq C_{t,K}|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{-2}.

Next, we note that Lemma
2.1
implies that for some
C_{t,K}>0
we have for
x_{1},x_{2}\in K

\displaystyle\bigg|\int_{t}^{\infty}ds(2\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{1},x_{2})-\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{1},x_{1})-\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{2},x_{2}))\bigg|

\displaystyle\quad\leq\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\int_{t}^{\infty}ds\,e^{-s\lambda_{n}}\Big|e_{n}(x_{1})(e_{n}(x_{2})-e_{n}(x_{1}))-(e_{n}(x_{2})-e_{n}(x_{1}))e_{n}(x_{2})\Big|

\displaystyle\quad\leq C_{t,K}|x_{1}-x_{2}|.

Thus for some (possibly different)
C_{t,K}>0
,

\left|e^{-2\pi\int_{t}^{\infty}ds\,(\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{1},x_{1})+\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{2},x_{2}))}e^{4\pi\int_{t}^{\infty}ds\,\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{1},x_{2})}-1\right|\leq C_{t,K}|x_{1}-x_{2}|.

Finally, noting that since
\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}
is non-negative,

\left|e^{-2\pi\int_{t}^{\infty}ds\,(\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{1},x_{1})+\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{2},x_{2}))}-1\right|\leq 1.

Combining all these estimates, we find that for some constant
C_{t,K}>0
,

\big|A(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}|\varepsilon)+\tilde{v}_{t}^{2}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}|\varepsilon)\big|\mathbf{1}_{\{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}=-4\}}\\
\leq C_{t,K}|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{-1},

which concludes the proof.
∎

The previous lemma says that we can swap the counter term
A
for
\tilde{v}_{t}^{2}
. Let us now argue that the
\tilde{v}_{t}^{2}
-counter term is sufficient to cure the divergence in the
n=2
term.

Lemma 4.5
.

For each
t>0
and
K\subset\Omega
compact, there exists a function
\widehat{g}_{t}\in L^{1}(K^{2})
which is independent of
\varepsilon
such that for all
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\Omega\times\{-2,2\}

\left|\tilde{v}_{t}^{2}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}|\varepsilon)\Big(e^{i\sqrt{\pi}\big(\sigma_{1}\varphi_{\sqrt{t}}(x_{1})+\sigma_{2}\varphi_{\sqrt{t}}(x_{2})\big)}-1\Big)\right|\leq\widehat{g}_{t}(x_{1},x_{2})\left(1+\|\nabla\varphi_{\sqrt{t}}\|_{L^{\infty}(K)}\right),

with
\tilde{v}_{t}^{2}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}|\varepsilon)
from (
3.0.17
).

Proof.

First of all, if
\sigma_{1}=\sigma_{2}
, then the left hand side is bounded, and there is nothing to prove. We thus focus on
\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}=-4
. Let us fix a compact set
K\subset\Omega
. Using (
3.0.17
) and (
3.0.16
), there exists a constant
C_{t,K}
so that we can write

\displaystyle\left|\tilde{v}_{t}^{2}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}|\varepsilon)\Big(e^{i\sqrt{\pi}\big(\sigma_{1}\varphi_{\sqrt{t}}(x_{1})+\sigma_{2}\varphi_{\sqrt{t}}(x_{2})\big)}-1\Big)\right|\mathbf{1}_{\{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}=-4\}}

\displaystyle\quad\leq C_{t,K}\left|1-e^{4\pi\int_{\varepsilon^{2}}^{t}ds\,\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{1},x_{2})}\right|\left|e^{i\sqrt{4\pi}\big(\varphi_{\sqrt{t}}(x_{1})-\varphi_{\sqrt{t}}(x_{2})\big)}-1\right|.

Since
x\mapsto e^{ix}
is
1
-Lipschitz, we see that the last term can be bounded by the quantity
\sqrt{4\pi}\big\|\nabla\varphi_{\sqrt{t}}\big\|_{L^{\infty}(K)}|x_{1}-x_{2}|
. Since
\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}\leq\mathsf{p}
, it is sufficient to prove local integrability of

|x_{1}-x_{2}|e^{4\pi\int_{0}^{t}ds\,\mathsf{p}(s,x_{1},x_{2})}.

It follows from
[
8
, Lemma 4.4]
(note that the statement there is stated for the “massive heat kernel”, but the estimate is uniform in the mass, so it holds also for our
\mathsf{p}
) that there exists a constant
C_{t,K}
such that for
x_{1},x_{2}\in K
, this can be bounded by
C_{t,K}|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{-1}
, which is integrable, and the proof is complete.
∎

We can now turn to the proof of Proposition
4.2
.

Proof of Proposition
4.2
.

(i)
Using Corollary
3.2
, we can choose
t
small enough (independent of
\varepsilon
) that for each of the relevant
\eta
, the renormalized partition function can be written as

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(\eta|\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=\bigg\langle e^{-\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\int_{(\Omega\times\{\pm 1,\pm 2\})^{n}}d\xi_{1}\dots d\xi_{n}\,\eta(\xi_{1})\dots\eta(\xi_{n})\tilde{v}_{t}^{n}(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n}|\varepsilon)e^{i\sqrt{\pi}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sigma_{j}\varphi_{\sqrt{t}}(x_{j})}}\bigg\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\sqrt{t})}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\exp\bigg\{-\frac{1}{2}\int_{(\Omega\times\{\pm 2\})^{2}}d\xi_{1}\,d\xi_{2}\,\eta(\xi_{1})\eta(\xi_{2})A(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}|\varepsilon)\bigg\}

\displaystyle=\bigg\langle\exp\bigg\{-\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\int_{(\Omega\times\{\pm 1,\pm 2\})^{n}}d\xi_{1}\dots d\xi_{n}\,\eta(\xi_{1})\dots\eta(\xi_{n})\tilde{v}_{t}^{n}(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n}|\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\Big(e^{i\sqrt{\pi}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sigma_{j}\varphi_{\sqrt{t}}(x_{j})}-\mathbf{1}_{\{n=2\}}\mathbf{1}_{\{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}=\pm 4\}}\Big)\bigg\}\bigg\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\sqrt{t})}

\displaystyle\quad\qquad\times\exp\bigg\{-\frac{1}{2}\int_{(\Omega\times\{\pm 2\})^{2}}d\xi_{1}\,d\xi_{2}\,\eta(\xi_{1})\eta(\xi_{2})\Big(A(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}|\varepsilon)+\tilde{v}_{t}^{2}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}|\varepsilon)\Big)\bigg\}.

For the second factor on the right hand side we have by Lemma
4.4
and by our assumption of
\mathrm{supp}(\eta(\cdot,\sigma))\subset K
and that
\|\eta\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega\times\{\pm 1,\pm 2\})}\leq M
that there exists a constant
C_{t,K,M}>0
such that

\displaystyle\exp\bigg\{-\frac{1}{2}\int_{(\Omega\times\{\pm 2\})^{2}}d\xi_{1}\,d\xi_{2}\,\eta(\xi_{1})\eta(\xi_{2})\Big(A(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}|\varepsilon)+\tilde{v}_{t}^{2}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}|\varepsilon)\Big)\bigg\}\leq C_{t,K,M}.

For the first factor on the right hand side we use Proposition
3.1
(we also fix some small enough
t_{0}
for the proposition and consider
t<t_{0}
), so

\displaystyle\bigg|\exp\bigg\{-\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\int_{(\Omega\times\{\pm 1,\pm 2\})^{n}}d\xi_{1}\dots d\xi_{n}\,\eta(\xi_{1})\dots\eta(\xi_{n})\tilde{v}_{t}^{n}(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n}|\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\Big(e^{i\sqrt{\pi}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sigma_{j}\varphi_{\sqrt{t}}(x_{j})}-\mathbf{1}_{\{n=2\}}\mathbf{1}_{\{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}=\pm 4\}}\Big)\bigg\}\bigg|

\displaystyle\quad\leq e^{\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{n^{n-2}}{n!}|K|t^{-1}C_{K,M,t_{0}}^{n}\sum_{\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{n}\in\{\pm 1,\pm 2\}}t^{\frac{1}{8}\big(8n-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sigma_{j}^{2}\big)}}

\displaystyle\qquad\times e^{\frac{1}{2}\int_{(\Omega\times\{\pm 1,\pm 2\})^{2}}d\xi_{1}\,d\xi_{2}\,|\eta(\xi_{1})||\eta(\xi_{2})||\tilde{v}_{t}^{2}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}|\varepsilon)|\big|e^{i\sqrt{\pi}\sum_{j=1}^{2}\sigma_{j}\varphi_{\sqrt{t}}(x_{j})}-1\big|\mathbf{1}_{\{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}=\pm 4\}}}

By Stirling, we have for some
\tau=\tau(K,M,t_{0})
: if
t<\tau
, then the above series converges geometrically, and

\displaystyle e^{\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{n^{n-2}}{n!}|K|t^{-1}C_{K,M,t_{0}}^{n}\sum_{\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{n}\in\{\pm 1,\pm 2\}}t^{\frac{1}{8}\big(8n-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sigma_{j}^{2}\big)}}\leq e^{C_{K,M,t_{0}}t^{-1}}

again for a possibly different
C_{K,M,t_{0}}
. For the
n=2
-term, we note that by the assumption of
\|\eta\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega\times\{\pm 1,\pm 2\})}\leq M
,
\mathrm{supp}(\eta(\cdot,\sigma))\subset K
, and Lemma
4.5

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{(\Omega\times\{\pm 1,\pm 2\})^{2}}d\xi_{1}\,d\xi_{2}\,|\eta(\xi_{1})\eta(\xi_{2})\tilde{v}_{t}^{2}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}|\varepsilon)|\left|e^{i\sqrt{\pi}(\sigma_{1}\varphi_{\sqrt{t}}(x_{1})+\sigma_{2}\varphi_{\sqrt{t}}(x_{2}))}-1\right|\mathbf{1}_{\{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}=\pm 4\}}

\displaystyle\quad\leq M^{2}\int_{K^{2}}dx_{1}\,dx_{2}\,\widehat{g}_{t}(x_{1},x_{2})\left(1+\big\|\nabla\varphi_{\sqrt{t}}\big\|_{L^{\infty}(K)}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\leq C_{K,M,t}\left(1+\big\|\nabla\varphi_{\sqrt{t}}\big\|_{L^{\infty}(K)}\right).

Therefore, for the relevant values of
t
, there exists a (possibly different) constant
C_{K,M,t}
such that

\displaystyle|\mathcal{Z}(\eta|\varepsilon)|

\displaystyle\leq\left\langle e^{C_{K,M,t}\big(1+\|\nabla\varphi_{\sqrt{t}}\|_{L^{\infty}(K)}\big)}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\sqrt{t})}

Using Lemma
4.3
(in the notation of Lemma
4.3
, we find some compactly supported
\eta
such that
\eta=C_{K,M,t}
on
K
), we see that this can be bounded by some constant depending only on
K,M,t
. We can then fix
t
suitably depending only on
K,M
to find that uniformly in
\varepsilon
, this quantity is bounded by some constant depending only on
K,M
. This concludes the proof of the first claim.

The arguments for the proofs of the claims (ii) and (iii) are identical to the corresponding claims of
[
8
, Proposition 5.3]
, and we omit the details.
∎

We now turn to analyticity properties of the renormalized partition function. These will allow us to deduce convergence of the renormalized partition function from Proposition
4.2
.

We begin with an estimate that will allow controlling derivatives of the renormalized partition function – eventually we will use it to prove that the (limiting) renormalized partition function is an entire function as claimed in Theorem
4.1
.

Lemma 4.6
.

For fixed
\varepsilon>0
and
\eta\in C_{c}(\Omega\times\{\pm 1,\pm 2\})
, the function
\mu\to\mathcal{Z}(\mu\eta|\varepsilon)
is entire and we have

(4.0.8)

\mathcal{Z}(\mu\eta|\varepsilon)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\mu^{n}}{n!}\mathcal{M}_{n}(\eta|\varepsilon),

where

(4.0.9)

\mathcal{M}_{n}(\eta|\varepsilon)=\int_{(\Omega\times\{\pm 1,\pm 2\})^{n}}d\xi_{1}\dots d\xi_{n}\,\eta(\xi_{1})\dots\eta(\xi_{n})\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n}|\varepsilon)

with

(4.0.10)

\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n}|\varepsilon)=\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\tau\in S_{n}}\Bigg[\sum_{j=0}^{\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor}\frac{n!}{j!(n-2j)!}(-1)^{n-j}2^{-j}\left\langle\prod_{l=2j+1}^{n}:e^{i\sqrt{\pi}\sigma_{\tau_{l}}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x_{\tau_{l}})}:\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}\\
\times\bigg(\prod_{l^{\prime}=1}^{j}A(\xi_{\tau_{2l^{\prime}-1}},\xi_{\tau_{2l^{\prime}}}|\varepsilon)\mathbf{1}_{\{\sigma_{\tau_{2l^{\prime}-1}}\sigma_{\tau_{2l^{\prime}}}=\pm 4\}}\bigg)\Bigg],

where
S_{n}
denotes the group of permutations of the set
[n]
.

Furthermore, for each
K\subset\Omega
compact,
M>0
and
R>0
there exists a constant
C_{K,M,R}
independent of
\varepsilon,\eta
and
n
such that

(4.0.11)

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\varepsilon\in(0,1)\\
\|\eta\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega\times\{\pm 1,\pm 2\})}\leq M\\
\mathrm{supp}(\eta(\cdot,\sigma))\subset K\end{subarray}}|\mathcal{M}_{n}(\eta|\varepsilon)|\leq C_{K,M,R}\frac{n!}{R^{n}}.

Proof.

Recall from (
4.0.3
) and (
3.0.2
) that the renormalized partition function is

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(\mu\eta|\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=\bigg\langle\exp\bigg\{-\mu\int_{\Omega\times\{\pm 1,\pm 2\}}d\xi\,\eta(\xi):e^{i\sqrt{\pi}\sigma\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x)}:\bigg\}\bigg\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}

\displaystyle\quad\times\exp\bigg\{-\frac{\mu^{2}}{2}\int_{(\Omega\times\{\pm 2\})^{2}}d\xi_{1}\,d\xi_{2}\,\eta(\xi_{1})\eta(\xi_{2})A(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}|\varepsilon)\bigg\},

since
\int_{\Omega\times\{\pm 1,\pm 2\}}d\xi\,\eta(\xi):e^{i\sqrt{\pi}\sigma\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x)}:
is a bounded random variable, the expectation factor above is an entire function of
\mu
. Furthermore, for any
\varepsilon>0

\bigg|\int_{(\Omega\times\{\pm 2\})^{2}}d\xi_{1}\,d\xi_{2}\,\eta(\xi_{1})\eta(\xi_{2})A(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}|\varepsilon)\bigg|<\infty,

hence the second factor in
\mathcal{Z}(\mu\eta|\varepsilon)
is an entire function of
\mu
and therefore
\mathcal{Z}(\mu\eta|\varepsilon)
is entire. We can then expand both factors and change the order of expectation and integration

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(\mu\eta|\varepsilon)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-\mu)^{k}}{k!}\int_{(\Omega\times\{\pm 1,\pm 2\})^{k}}d\xi_{1}\dots d\xi_{k}\,\eta(\xi_{1})\dots\eta(\xi_{k})\left\langle\prod_{l=1}^{k}:e^{i\sqrt{\pi}\sigma_{l}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x_{l})}:\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}
