\displaystyle\quad\times\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-\mu^{2})^{j}}{2^{j}j!}\int_{(\Omega\times\{\pm 1,\pm 2\})^{2j}}d\xi_{1}\dots d\xi_{2j}\,\eta(\xi_{1})\dots\eta(\xi_{2j})\prod_{l^{\prime}=1}^{j}A(\xi_{2l^{\prime}-1},\xi_{2l^{\prime}}|\varepsilon)\mathbf{1}_{\{\sigma_{2l^{\prime}-1}\sigma_{2l^{\prime}}=\pm 4\}}.

Finally, relabeling the integration variables and symmetrizing in the arguments gives us the desired representation.

Since
\mathcal{Z}
is entire and
\mathcal{M}_{n}(\eta|\varepsilon)=\partial_{\mu}^{n}|_{\mu=0}\mathcal{Z}(\mu\eta|\varepsilon)
then by Cauchy’s integral formula

\mathcal{M}_{n}(\eta|\varepsilon)=\frac{n!}{2\pi i}\oint_{|w|=R}dw\frac{\mathcal{Z}(w\eta|\varepsilon)}{w^{n+1}}.

From Proposition
4.2
we then get

\displaystyle\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\varepsilon\in(0,1),\eta\in C_{c}(\Omega\times\{\pm 1,\pm 2\})\\
\|\eta\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega\times\{\pm 1,\pm 2\})}\leq M\\
\mathrm{supp}(\eta(\cdot,\sigma))\subset K\end{subarray}}|\mathcal{M}_{n}(\eta|\varepsilon)|\leq\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\varepsilon\in(0,1),\eta\in C_{c}(\Omega\times\{\pm 1,\pm 2\})\\
\|\eta\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega\times\{\pm 1,\pm 2\})}\leq M\\
\mathrm{supp}(\eta(\cdot,\sigma))\subset K\\
|w|=R\end{subarray}}|\mathcal{Z}(w\eta|\varepsilon)|\frac{n!}{R^{n}}\leq C_{K,M,R}\frac{n!}{R^{n}}.

∎

Next, we will need a tool for showing that the kernel
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}
from (
4.0.10
) satisfies a certain uniqueness property.

Lemma 4.7
.

For
\varepsilon>0
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n}|\varepsilon)
is the unique continuous function of
\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n}
for which

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n!}\frac{\partial}{\partial\alpha_{1}}\dots\frac{\partial}{\partial\alpha_{n}}\mathcal{M}_{n}(\alpha_{1}f_{1}+\dots+\alpha_{n}f_{n}|\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{(\Omega\times\{\pm 1,\pm 2\})^{n}}d\xi_{1}\dots d\xi_{n}\,f_{1}(\xi_{1})\dots f_{n}(\xi_{n})\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n}|\varepsilon),

for all
f_{1},\dots,f_{n}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega\times\{\pm 1,\pm 2\})
,

Proof.

The fact that
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}
satisfies this condition can be proven exactly as in
[
8
, Lemma 5.6]
and we omit the details of this claim.

For uniqueness, let
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{1}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{2}
be two such kernels. Since both are integral kernels for the object in question, for any fixed
\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{n}\in\{\pm 1,\pm 2\}
and
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega^{n})

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega^{n}}\!d^{2n}x\,f(x_{1},...,x_{n})\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{1}((x_{1},\sigma_{1}),...,(x_{n},\sigma_{n}))=\int_{\Omega^{n}}\!d^{2n}x\,f(x_{1},...,x_{n})\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{2}((x_{1},\sigma_{1}),...,(x_{n},\sigma_{n})).

Thus
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{1}=\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{2}
, as elements of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega^{n})
and since we are dealing with continuous functions, this means that
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{1}=\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{2}
.
∎

As a final ingredient for proving convergence of the renormalized partition function, we will need an expression for
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}
in terms of the renormalized potential (for which we have good bounds).

Lemma 4.8
.

Let
\eta\in C_{c}(\Omega\times\{\pm 1,\pm 2\})
and choose
t
small enough so that we can use Corollary
3.2
. Then
\mathcal{M}_{n}(\eta|\varepsilon)
defined in Lemma
4.6
can be expressed as

\int_{(\Omega\times\{\pm 1,\pm 2\})^{n}}d\xi_{1}\dots d\xi_{n}\,\eta(\xi_{1})\dots\eta(\xi_{n})\\
\quad\times\sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor}\frac{n!}{k!}2^{-k}(-1)^{k}\prod_{j=1}^{k}\Big(A(\xi_{2j-1},\xi_{2j}|\varepsilon)+\tilde{v}_{t}^{2}(\xi_{2j-1},\xi_{2j}|\varepsilon)\Big)\mathbf{1}_{\{\sigma_{2j-1}\sigma_{2j}=\pm 4\}}\\
\times\sum_{l=1}^{n-2k}\frac{(-1)^{l}}{l!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq n_{1},\dots,n_{l}\leq n-2k:\\
n_{1}+\dots+n_{l}=n-2k\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{n_{1}!\dots n_{l}!}\prod_{j=1}^{l}\tilde{v}_{t}^{n_{j}}(\xi_{2k+1+n_{1}+\dots+n_{j-1}},\dots,\xi_{2k+n_{1}+\dots+n_{j}}|\varepsilon)\\
\times\bigg\langle\prod_{j=1}^{l}\left(e^{i\sqrt{\pi}\sum_{m=2k+1+n_{1}+\dots+n_{j-1}}^{2k+n_{1}+\dots+n_{j}}\sigma_{m}\varphi_{\sqrt{t}}(x_{m})}-\mathbf{1}_{\{n_{j}=2\}}\mathbf{1}_{\{\sigma_{m}\sigma_{m+1}=-4\}}\right)\bigg\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\sqrt{t})}.

Proof.

Noting from Lemma
4.6
that
\mathcal{M}_{n}(\eta|\varepsilon)=\partial_{\mu}^{n}|_{\mu=0}\mathcal{Z}(\mu\eta|\varepsilon)
and we can write
\mathcal{Z}(\mu\eta|\varepsilon)=F_{1}(\mu)F_{2}(\mu)
, where

F_{1}(\mu)=\bigg\langle\exp\bigg\{-\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\mu^{n}}{n!}\int_{(\Omega\times\{\pm 1,\pm 2\})^{n}}d\xi_{1}\dots d\xi_{n}\,\eta(\xi_{1})\dots\eta(\xi_{n})\tilde{v}_{t}^{n}(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n}|\varepsilon)\\
\times\left(e^{i\sqrt{\pi}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sigma_{j}\varphi_{\sqrt{t}}(x_{j})}-\mathbf{1}_{\{n=2\}}\mathbf{1}_{\{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}=\pm 4\}}\right)\bigg\}\bigg\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\sqrt{t})}

and

F_{2}(\mu)=\exp\{-\frac{\mu^{2}}{2}\int_{(\Omega\times\{\pm 1,\pm 2\})^{2}}d\xi_{1}\,d\xi_{2}\,\eta(\xi_{1})\eta(\xi_{2})\big(A(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}|\varepsilon)+\tilde{v}_{t}^{2}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}|\varepsilon)\big)\mathbf{1}_{\{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}=\pm 4\}}\}.

So, we need to calculate derivatives of
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
at
0
. Note that
F_{2}
is entire and non-zero, and
\mathcal{Z}
is entire by Lemma
4.6
, thus
F_{1}
is entire. By Cauchy’s integral formula, Fubini, etc. we can change the order of derivatives and expectation, so

\displaystyle F_{1}^{(k)}(0)

\displaystyle=\bigg\langle\frac{\partial^{k}}{\partial\mu^{k}}\bigg|_{\mu=0}\exp\bigg\{-\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\mu^{n}}{n!}\int_{(\Omega\times\{\pm 1,\pm 2\})^{n}}d\xi_{1}\dots d\xi_{n}\,\eta(\xi_{1})\dots\eta(\xi_{n})

\displaystyle\qquad\times\tilde{v}_{t}^{n}(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n}|\varepsilon)\left(e^{i\sqrt{\pi}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sigma_{j}\varphi_{\sqrt{t}}(x_{j})}-\mathbf{1}_{\{n=2\}}\mathbf{1}_{\{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}=\pm 4\}}\right)\bigg\}\bigg\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\sqrt{t})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=1}^{k}\frac{(-1)^{l}}{l!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq n_{1},\dots,n_{l}\leq k:\\
n_{1}+\dots+n_{l}=k\end{subarray}}\frac{k!}{n_{1}!\dots n_{l}!}\bigg\langle\prod_{j=1}^{l}\bigg(\int_{(\Omega\times\{\pm 1,\pm 2\})^{n_{j}}}d\xi_{1}\dots d\xi_{n_{j}}\,\eta(\xi_{1})\dots\eta(\xi_{n_{j}})

\displaystyle\qquad\times\tilde{v}_{t}^{n_{j}}(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n_{j}}|\varepsilon)\left(e^{i\sqrt{\pi}\sum_{m=1}^{n_{j}}\sigma_{m}\varphi_{\sqrt{t}}(x_{m})}-\mathbf{1}_{\{n_{j}=2\}}\mathbf{1}_{\{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}=\pm 4\}}\right)\bigg)\bigg\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\sqrt{t})}

\displaystyle=\int_{(\Omega\times\{\pm 1,\pm 2\})^{k}}d\xi_{1}\dots d\xi_{k}\,\eta(\xi_{1})\dots\eta(\xi_{k})\sum_{l=1}^{k}\frac{(-1)^{l}}{l!}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq n_{1},\dots,n_{l}\leq k:\\
n_{1}+\dots+n_{l}=k\end{subarray}}\frac{k!}{n_{1}!\dots n_{l}!}\prod_{j=1}^{l}\tilde{v}_{t}^{n_{j}}(\xi_{1+n_{1}+\dots+n_{j-1}},\dots,\xi_{n_{1}+\dots+n_{j}}|\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\bigg\langle\prod_{j=1}^{l}\left(e^{i\sqrt{\pi}\sum_{m=1+n_{1}+\dots+n_{j-1}}^{n_{1}+\dots+n_{j}}\sigma_{m}\varphi_{\sqrt{t}}(x_{m})}-\mathbf{1}_{\{n_{j}=2\}}\mathbf{1}_{\{\sigma_{m}\sigma_{m+1}=\pm 4\}}\right)\bigg\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\sqrt{t})}.

For
F_{2}
we have that
F_{2}(0)=1
. For odd
n
,
F_{2}^{(n)}(0)=0
and for even
n=2k
we get the formula

\displaystyle F_{2}^{(2k)}(0)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{k}\frac{(2k)!}{2^{k}k!}\left[\int_{(\Omega\times\{\pm 1,\pm 2\})^{2}}d\xi_{1}\,d\xi_{2}\,\eta(\xi_{1})\eta(\xi_{2})\Big(A(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}|\varepsilon)+\tilde{v}_{t}^{2}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}|\varepsilon)\Big)\mathbf{1}_{\{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}=\pm 4\}}\right]^{k}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{k}\frac{(2k)!}{2^{k}k!}\int_{(\Omega\times\{\pm 1,\pm 2\})^{2k}}d\xi_{1}\dots d\xi_{2k}\,\eta(\xi_{1})\dots\eta(\xi_{2k})

\displaystyle\quad\times\prod_{j=1}^{k}\Big(A(\xi_{2j-1},\xi_{2j}|\varepsilon)+\tilde{v}_{t}^{2}(\xi_{2j-1},\xi_{2j}|\varepsilon)\Big)\mathbf{1}_{\{\sigma_{2j-1}\sigma_{2j}=\pm 4\}}.

Now, since
F_{2}^{(2k+1)}(0)=0
, we can write

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{n}(\eta|\varepsilon)=\partial_{\mu}^{n}|_{\mu=0}\mathcal{Z}(\mu\eta|\varepsilon)=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}F_{1}^{(n-k)}(0)F_{2}^{(k)}(0)=\sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor}\binom{n}{2k}F_{1}^{(n-2k)}(0)F_{2}^{(2k)}(0),

so

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{n}(\eta|\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor}\frac{n!}{(2k)!(n-2k)!}(-1)^{k}\frac{(2k)!}{2^{k}k!}\int_{(\Omega\times\{\pm 1,\pm 2\})^{2k}}d\xi_{1}\dots d\xi_{2k}\,\eta(\xi_{1})\dots\eta(\xi_{2k})

\displaystyle\quad\times\prod_{j=1}^{k}\Big(A(\xi_{2j-1},\xi_{2j}|\varepsilon)+\tilde{v}_{t}^{2}(\xi_{2j-1},\xi_{2j}|\varepsilon)\Big)\mathbf{1}_{\{\sigma_{2j-1}\sigma_{2j}=\pm 4\}}

\displaystyle\quad\times\int_{(\Omega\times\{\pm 1,\pm 2\})^{n-2k}}d\xi_{1}\dots d\xi_{n-2k}\,\eta(\xi_{1})\dots\eta(\xi_{n-2k})\sum_{l=1}^{n-2k}\frac{(-1)^{l}}{l!}

\displaystyle\quad\times\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq n_{1},\dots,n_{l}\leq n-2k:\\
n_{1}+\dots+n_{l}=n-2k\end{subarray}}\frac{(n-2k)!}{n_{1}!\dots n_{l}!}\prod_{j=1}^{l}\tilde{v}_{t}^{n_{j}}(\xi_{1+n_{1}+\dots+n_{j-1}},\dots,\xi_{n_{1}+\dots+n_{j}}|\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\quad\times\bigg\langle\prod_{j=1}^{l}\left(e^{i\sqrt{\pi}\sum_{m=1+n_{1}+\dots+n_{j-1}}^{n_{1}+\dots+n_{j}}\sigma_{m}\varphi_{\sqrt{t}}(x_{m})}-\mathbf{1}_{\{n_{j}=2\}}\mathbf{1}_{\{\sigma_{m}\sigma_{m+1}=\pm 4\}}\right)\bigg\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\sqrt{t})}

\displaystyle=\int_{(\Omega\times\{\pm 1,\pm 2\})^{n}}d\xi_{1}\dots d\xi_{n}\,\eta(\xi_{1})\dots\eta(\xi_{n})

\displaystyle\quad\times\sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor}\frac{n!}{k!}2^{-k}(-1)^{k}\prod_{j=1}^{k}\Big(A(\xi_{2j-1},\xi_{2j}|\varepsilon)+\tilde{v}_{t}^{2}(\xi_{2j-1},\xi_{2j}|\varepsilon)\Big)\mathbf{1}_{\{\sigma_{2j-1}\sigma_{2j}=\pm 4\}}

\displaystyle\quad\times\sum_{l=1}^{n-2k}\frac{(-1)^{l}}{l!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq n_{1},\dots,n_{l}\leq n-2k:\\
n_{1}+\dots+n_{l}=n-2k\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{n_{1}!\dots n_{l}!}\prod_{j=1}^{l}\tilde{v}_{t}^{n_{j}}(\xi_{2k+1+n_{1}+\dots+n_{j-1}},\dots,\xi_{2k+n_{1}+\dots+n_{j}}|\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\quad\times\bigg\langle\prod_{j=1}^{l}\left(e^{i\sqrt{\pi}\sum_{m=2k+1+n_{1}+\dots+n_{j-1}}^{2k+n_{1}+\dots+n_{j}}\sigma_{m}\varphi_{\sqrt{t}}(x_{m})}-\mathbf{1}_{\{n_{j}=2\}}\mathbf{1}_{\{\sigma_{m}\sigma_{m+1}=\pm 4\}}\right)\bigg\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\sqrt{t})}.

This concludes the proof.
∎

To finally prove convergence of the renormalized partition function as well as Theorem
4.1
, we begin by providing a bound on the kernels
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}
.

Lemma 4.9
.

There exists a
t_{0}>0
such that for
n\geq 1
and
0<t<t_{0}
there exists a function
g_{t}\in L_{loc}^{1}((\Omega\times\{\pm 1,\pm 2\})^{n})
, independent of
\varepsilon
, such that for
\varepsilon^{2}<t

|\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n}|\varepsilon)|\leq g_{t}(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n}).

Proof.

The representation in Lemma
4.8
(combined with Lemma
4.7
) shows that
|\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}|
can be bounded by sums of products of factors of the form

G_{1}(\widehat{\xi}_{1},\widehat{\xi}_{2}|\varepsilon):=\big|A(\widehat{\xi}_{1},\widehat{\xi}_{2}|\varepsilon)+\tilde{v}_{t}^{2}(\widehat{\xi}_{1},\widehat{\xi}_{2}|\varepsilon)\big|\mathbf{1}_{\{\hat{\sigma}_{1}\hat{\sigma}_{2}=\pm 4\}},

G_{2}(\xi^{\prime}_{1},\dots,\xi^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}|\varepsilon):=\big|\tilde{v}_{t}^{n^{\prime}}(\xi^{\prime}_{1},\dots,\xi^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}|\varepsilon)\big|,\qquad\text{with $\sigma_{1}^{\prime}\sigma_{2}^{\prime}\neq\pm 4$ when $n^{\prime}=2$},

and

G_{3}(\widetilde{\xi}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{\xi}_{2k}|\varepsilon):=\bigg\langle\prod_{j=1}^{k}\left|\tilde{v}_{t}^{2}(\widetilde{\xi}_{2j-1},\widetilde{\xi}_{2j}|\varepsilon)\right|\Big|e^{i\sqrt{4\pi}\big(\widetilde{\sigma}_{2j-1}\varphi_{\sqrt{t}}(\widetilde{x}_{2j-1})+\widetilde{\sigma}_{2j}\varphi_{\sqrt{t}}(\widetilde{x}_{2j})\big)}-1\Big|\bigg\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\sqrt{t})}

where
\{\widehat{\xi}_{1},\widehat{\xi}_{2}\},\{\xi^{\prime}_{1},\dots,\xi^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}\}
and
\{\widetilde{\xi}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{\xi}_{2k}\}
are disjoint subsets of the integration variables
\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n}
of
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}
. Thus it is enough to prove local integrability for each function
G_{i}
separately.

For
G_{1}
, this follows from Lemma
4.4
, for
G_{2}
, this follows from Proposition
3.1
and for
G_{3}
, this follows from Lemma
4.5
and Lemma
4.3
.
∎

We now prove that the kernels
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}
converge as
\varepsilon\to 0
. Combining with previous results, this will allow proving convergence of the renormalized partition function.

Lemma 4.10
.

For
\xi_{i}\neq\xi_{j}
for
i\neq j
,

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n}|\varepsilon)=\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n}),

with

(4.0.12)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n})=\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\tau\in S_{n}}\Bigg[\sum_{j=0}^{\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor}\frac{n!}{j!(n-2j)!}(-1)^{n-j}2^{-j}\left\langle\prod_{l=2j+1}^{n}:e^{i\sqrt{\pi}\sigma_{\tau_{l}}\varphi(x_{\tau_{l}})}:\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}}

\displaystyle\times\bigg(\prod_{l^{\prime}=1}^{j}A(\xi_{\tau_{2l^{\prime}-1}},\xi_{\tau_{2l^{\prime}}})\mathbf{1}_{\{\sigma_{\tau_{2l^{\prime}-1}}\sigma_{\tau_{2l^{\prime}}}=\pm 4\}}\bigg)\Bigg],

where
A(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})=\left\langle\mathop{:}e^{i\sqrt{\pi}\sigma_{1}\varphi(x_{1})}\mathop{:}\mathop{:}e^{i\sqrt{\pi}\sigma_{2}\varphi(x_{2})}\mathop{:}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}}-\left\langle\mathop{:}e^{i\sqrt{\pi}\sigma_{1}\varphi(x_{1})}\mathop{:}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}}\left\langle\mathop{:}e^{i\sqrt{\pi}\sigma_{2}\varphi(x_{2})}\mathop{:}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}}=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}A(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}|\varepsilon)
, and the correlation functions are given by Lemma
2.6
.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.6

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\left\langle\prod_{l=2j+1}^{n}\mathop{:}e^{i\sqrt{\pi}\sigma_{\tau_{l}}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x_{\tau_{l}})}\mathop{:}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}=\left\langle\prod_{l=2j+1}^{n}\mathop{:}e^{i\sqrt{\pi}\sigma_{\tau_{l}}\varphi(x_{\tau_{l}})}\mathop{:}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}}.

A similar statement holds for the counter term

\displaystyle A(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}|\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=\left\langle\mathop{:}e^{i\sqrt{\pi}\sigma_{1}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x_{1})}\mathop{:}\mathop{:}e^{i\sqrt{\pi}\sigma_{2}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x_{2})}\mathop{:}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}

\displaystyle\qquad-\left\langle\mathop{:}e^{i\sqrt{\pi}\sigma_{1}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x_{1})}\mathop{:}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}\left\langle\mathop{:}e^{i\sqrt{\pi}\sigma_{2}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x_{2})}\mathop{:}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}.

So
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}(\cdot|\varepsilon)
given by (
4.0.10
) converges as
\varepsilon\to 0
to the function
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}(\cdot)
defined by (
4.0.12
).
∎

We now have all the ingredients needed for the proof.

Proof of Theorem
4.1
.

For
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}(\cdot)
given by (
4.0.12
) define

\mathcal{M}_{n}(\eta):=\int_{(\Omega\times\{\pm 1,\pm 2\})^{n}}d\xi_{1}\dots d\xi_{n}\,\eta(\xi_{1})\dots\eta(\xi_{n})\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n}).

It follows from Lemmas
4.9
–
4.10
that as
\varepsilon\to 0
,
\mathcal{M}_{n}(\eta|\varepsilon)
in Lemma
4.6
converges to
\mathcal{M}_{n}(\eta)
and is finite for all
\eta\in C_{c}(\Omega\times\{\pm 1,\pm 2\})
. Furthermore, if we write
K=\mathrm{supp}(\eta(\cdot,\sigma))
and
M=\|\eta\|_{\infty}
, (
4.0.11
) implies that for each
R>0
,
|\mathcal{M}_{n}(\eta|\varepsilon)|\leq C_{K,M,R}\frac{n!}{R^{n}}
then

\left|\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\mu^{n}}{n!}\mathcal{M}_{n}(\eta|\varepsilon)\right|\leq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{|\mu|^{n}}{n!}C_{K,M,R}\frac{n!}{R^{n}}\leq C_{K,M,R}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{|\mu|}{R}\right)^{n}

converges for
|\mu|<R
. Thus, by dominated convergence

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathcal{Z}(\mu\eta|\varepsilon)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\mu^{n}}{n!}\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathcal{M}_{n}(\eta|\varepsilon)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\mu^{n}}{n!}\mathcal{M}_{n}(\eta)=:\mathcal{Z}(\mu\eta),

which defines an entire function (since the radius of convergence is at least
R
for any
R>0
) and thus we have proved
(i)
and
(ii)
.

The proof of
(iii)
follows from Proposition
4.2

(iii)
, i.e. when
\eta(\cdot,\sigma)=\overline{\eta(\cdot,-\sigma)}
for
\sigma\in\{1,2\}
it holds that as
\varepsilon\to 0
,
\mathcal{Z}(\eta)>0
.

The proof of
(iv)
is also similar – see e.g.
[
8
, Proof of Theorem 5.1]
for details, though note that the assumption corresponding to (
4.0.4
) is missing in
[
8
]
. This is needed to control
[
8
, (5.69)]
. We thank E. Saksman and S. Vihko for bringing this to our attention.
∎

5.
The sine-Gordon Correlation Functions – Proof of Theorem
1.5

We are finally in a position to prove Theorem
1.5
. While the proof is a rather direct consequence of our control of the renormalized partition function and certain GFF correlation functions, it is still a bit lengthy, and in particular, notationally heavy. We thus split the proof into smaller steps.

Our first remark is that we can write the Wick ordered trigonometric functions as sums of Wick ordered exponentials. We also find it convenient to simplify notation involving the definition of
\mathop{\typecolon}\cdot\mathop{\typecolon}
. To do this, let us write for
f,\rho\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)

(5.0.1)

\displaystyle c_{\varepsilon}(f,\rho)=\frac{1}{4}\int_{(\Omega\times\{\pm 2\})^{2}}d\xi_{1}\,d\xi_{2}\,f(x_{1})\rho(x_{2})A(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}|\varepsilon),

where
A(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}|\varepsilon)
is as in (
4.0.2
). Note that since
f
and
\rho
do not depend on
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}
‚ we can actually write this as

(5.0.2)

c_{\varepsilon}(f,\rho)=\int_{\Omega^{2}}d^{2}x\,d^{2}y\,f(x)\rho(y)\mathcal{A}(x,y|\varepsilon),

where

(5.0.3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(x,y|\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=\left\langle\mathop{:}\cos(\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x))\mathop{:}\mathop{:}\cos(\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(y))\mathop{:}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}

\displaystyle\qquad-\left\langle\mathop{:}\cos(\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x))\mathop{:}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}\left\langle\mathop{:}\cos(\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(y))\mathop{:}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}

Note that by Lemma
2.6
,
\mathcal{A}(x,y)=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathcal{A}(x,y|\varepsilon)
also exists.

This allows writing

(5.0.4)

\mathop{\typecolon}\cos(\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon})\mathop{\typecolon}(h)=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\tau\in\{-2,2\}}\left(\mathop{:}e^{i\tau\sqrt{\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon}}\mathop{:}(h)-\mu c_{\varepsilon}(h,\rho)\right).

It is thus sufficient to study Wick ordered exponentials for the theorem. Our first step in doing this is to express the correlation functions of such Wick ordered exponentials for
\varepsilon>0
in terms of the renormalized partition function and GFF correlation functions. This is recorded in the following lemma.

Lemma 5.1
.

We have for
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
,
\rho\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
,
\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{n}\in\{-1,1\}
,
\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{n^{\prime}}\in\{-2,2\}
, and
f_{1},\dots,f_{n}
,
\widetilde{f}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{f}_{n^{\prime}}
,
g_{1},\dots,g_{m}
,
\widetilde{g}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{g}_{m^{\prime}}

\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
with disjoint supports:

\Bigg\langle\prod_{j=1}^{n}\mathop{:}e^{i\sigma_{j}\sqrt{\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon}}\mathop{:}(f_{j})\prod_{j^{\prime}=1}^{n^{\prime}}\left(\mathop{:}e^{i\tau_{j^{\prime}}\sqrt{\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon}}\mathop{:}(\widetilde{f}_{j^{\prime}})-\mu c_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{f}_{j^{\prime}},\rho)\right)\prod_{l=1}^{m}\partial\varphi_{\varepsilon}(g_{l})\prod_{l^{\prime}=1}^{m^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{g}_{l^{\prime}})\Bigg\rangle_{\mathrm{sG}(\mu\rho|\varepsilon,\Omega)}\\
\kern 5.0pt=\prod_{j=1}^{n}\partial_{s_{j}}|_{s_{j}=0}\prod_{j^{\prime}=1}^{n^{\prime}}\partial_{t_{j^{\prime}}}|_{t_{j^{\prime}}=0}\prod_{l=1}^{m}\partial_{u_{l}}|_{u_{l}=0}\prod_{l^{\prime}=1}^{m^{\prime}}\partial_{v_{l^{\prime}}}|_{v_{l^{\prime}}=0}\bigg[e^{\frac{1}{2}\sum_{l^{\prime},k^{\prime}=1}^{m^{\prime}}v_{l^{\prime}}v_{k^{\prime}}\left\langle\bar{\partial}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{g}_{l^{\prime}})\bar{\partial}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{g}_{k^{\prime}})\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}}\\
\quad\times e^{\sum_{l=1}^{m}\sum_{l^{\prime}=1}^{m^{\prime}}u_{l}v_{l^{\prime}}\left\langle\partial\varphi_{\varepsilon}(g_{l})\bar{\partial}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{g}_{l^{\prime}})\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}}e^{\frac{1}{2}\sum_{l,k=1}^{m}u_{l}u_{k}\left\langle\partial\varphi_{\varepsilon}(g_{l})\partial\varphi_{\varepsilon}(g_{k})\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}}e^{-\sum_{j^{\prime}=1}^{n^{\prime}}t_{j^{\prime}}\mu c_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{f}_{j^{\prime}},\rho)}\\
\times\frac{\mathcal{Z}(\eta_{\varepsilon}|\varepsilon)}{\mathcal{Z}(-\frac{\mu}{2}\rho|\varepsilon)}e^{\frac{1}{2}\int_{(\Omega\times\{\pm 2\})^{2}}d\xi_{1}d\xi_{2}\big(\eta_{\varepsilon}(\xi_{1})\eta_{\varepsilon}(\xi_{2})-\frac{\mu^{2}}{4}\rho(x_{1})\rho(x_{2})\big)A(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}|\varepsilon)}\bigg],

where
A(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}|\varepsilon)
is as in (
4.0.2
) and

(5.0.5)

\begin{split}\eta_{\varepsilon}(x,\sigma)=&-\sum_{j=1}^{n}s_{j}\delta_{\sigma,\sigma_{j}}e^{i\sigma_{j}\sqrt{\pi}\big(\sum_{l=1}^{m}u_{l}\left\langle\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x)\partial\varphi_{\varepsilon}(g_{l})\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}+\sum_{l^{\prime}=1}^{m^{\prime}}v_{l^{\prime}}\left\langle\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x)\bar{\partial}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{g}_{l^{\prime}})\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}\big)}f_{j}(x)\\
&-\sum_{j^{\prime}=1}^{n^{\prime}}t_{j^{\prime}}\delta_{\sigma,\tau_{j^{\prime}}}e^{i\tau_{j^{\prime}}\sqrt{\pi}\big(\sum_{l=1}^{m}u_{l}\left\langle\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x)\partial\varphi_{\varepsilon}(g_{l})\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}+\sum_{l^{\prime}=1}^{m^{\prime}}v_{l^{\prime}}\left\langle\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x)\bar{\partial}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{g}_{l^{\prime}})\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}\big)}\widetilde{f}_{j^{\prime}}(x)\\
&-\mathbf{1}_{\{\sigma\in\{-2,2\}\}}\frac{\mu}{2}e^{i\sigma\sqrt{\pi}\big(\sum_{l=1}^{m}u_{l}\left\langle\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x)\partial\varphi_{\varepsilon}(g_{l})\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}+\sum_{l^{\prime}=1}^{m^{\prime}}v_{l^{\prime}}\left\langle\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x)\bar{\partial}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{g}_{l^{\prime}})\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}\big)}\rho(x).\end{split}

While we do not emphasize it in our notation (to avoid horrendous looking formulas), note that
\eta_{\varepsilon}
depends on essentially all of our parameters in addition to
\varepsilon
– in particular, when carrying out the differentiations, one may need to differentiate
\eta_{\varepsilon}
as well. While notationally tedious, this representation has the benefit that we can use Theorem
4.1
to deduce convergence as
\varepsilon\to 0
. We turn to this after the proof of this lemma.

Proof.

Our starting point is to write (using the Leibniz integral rule)

\Bigg\langle\prod_{j=1}^{n}\mathop{:}e^{i\sigma_{j}\sqrt{\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon}}\mathop{:}(f_{j})\prod_{j^{\prime}=1}^{n^{\prime}}\left(\mathop{:}e^{i\tau_{j^{\prime}}\sqrt{\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon}}\mathop{:}(\widetilde{f}_{j^{\prime}})-\mu c_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{f}_{j^{\prime}},\rho)\right)\prod_{l=1}^{m}\partial\varphi_{\varepsilon}(g_{l})\prod_{l^{\prime}=1}^{m^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{g}_{l^{\prime}})\Bigg\rangle_{\mathrm{sG}(\mu\rho|\varepsilon,\Omega)}\\
=\frac{1}{Z_{\mathrm{sG}(\mu\rho|\varepsilon,\Omega)}}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\partial_{s_{j}}|_{s_{j}=0}\prod_{j^{\prime}=1}^{n^{\prime}}\partial_{t_{j^{\prime}}}|_{t_{j^{\prime}}=0}\prod_{l=1}^{m}\partial_{u_{l}}|_{u_{l}=0}\prod_{l^{\prime}=1}^{m^{\prime}}\partial_{v_{l^{\prime}}}|_{v_{l^{\prime}}=0}\Bigg[e^{-\sum_{j^{\prime}=1}^{n^{\prime}}t_{j^{\prime}}\mu c_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{f}_{j^{\prime}},\rho)}\\
\times\bigg\langle\exp\bigg\{\sum_{j=1}^{n}s_{j}\mathop{:}e^{i\sigma_{j}\sqrt{\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon}}\mathop{:}(f_{j})+\sum_{j^{\prime}=1}^{n^{\prime}}t_{j^{\prime}}\mathop{:}e^{i\tau_{j^{\prime}}\sqrt{\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon}}\mathop{:}(\widetilde{f}_{j^{\prime}})+\sum_{l=1}^{m}u_{l}\partial\varphi_{\varepsilon}(g_{l})+\sum_{l^{\prime}=1}^{m^{\prime}}v_{l^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{g}_{l^{\prime}})\\
+\mu\mathop{:}\cos(\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon})\mathop{:}(\rho)\bigg\}\bigg\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}\Bigg]

for
\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{n}\in\{-1,1\}
,
\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{n^{\prime}}\in\{-2,2\}
. This is justified since for
\varepsilon>0
, we are dealing either with bounded random variables, or exponentials of Gaussian random variables which are all integrable.

As one can guess from the statement of the lemma, the idea is to write this in terms of the renormalized partition function
\mathcal{Z}(\eta|\varepsilon)
for a suitable
\eta
depending on our parameters. Our first task is to get rid of the derivative terms in the exponential – without them, our GFF-expectation would already be of the form
Z(\eta|\varepsilon)
for a suitable
\eta
. This can be done with a routine Girsanov transform/completing the square. There is a minor technicality here, namely that
\partial\varphi_{\varepsilon}
and
\bar{\partial}\varphi_{\varepsilon}
are complex valued Gaussian fields, and the validity of Girsanov’s theorem is not immediate. However, there is a straightforward analyticity argument allowing the use of Girsanov here too – for details, see
[
8
, Proof of Lemma 2.6]
. This means that we can write

\displaystyle\bigg\langle\!e^{\sum_{j=1}^{n}s_{j}\mathop{:}e^{i\sigma_{j}\sqrt{\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon}}\!\mathop{:}(f_{j})+\sum_{j^{\prime}=1}^{n^{\prime}}t_{j^{\prime}}\mathop{:}e^{i\tau_{j^{\prime}}\sqrt{\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon}}\!\mathop{:}(\widetilde{f}_{j^{\prime}})+\sum_{l=1}^{m}u_{l}\partial\varphi_{\varepsilon}(g_{l})+\sum_{l^{\prime}=1}^{m^{\prime}}v_{l^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{g}_{l^{\prime}})+\mu\mathop{:}\cos(\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon})\mathop{:}(\rho)}\!\bigg\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}

\displaystyle\quad=Z(\eta_{\varepsilon}|\varepsilon)\,e^{\frac{1}{2}\sum_{l,k=1}^{m}u_{l}u_{k}\left\langle\partial\varphi_{\varepsilon}(g_{l})\partial\varphi_{\varepsilon}(g_{k})\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{l^{\prime},k^{\prime}=1}^{m^{\prime}}v_{l^{\prime}}v_{k^{\prime}}\left\langle\bar{\partial}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{g}_{l^{\prime}})\bar{\partial}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{g}_{k^{\prime}})\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times e^{\sum_{l=1}^{m}\sum_{l^{\prime}=1}^{m^{\prime}}u_{l}v_{l^{\prime}}\left\langle\partial\varphi_{\varepsilon}(g_{l})\bar{\partial}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{g}_{l^{\prime}})\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}},

where
\eta_{\varepsilon}(x,\sigma)
is as in the statement of the lemma.

Now it just remains to rewrite the ratio of partition functions in terms of a ratio of renormalized partition functions. This follows directly from the definition of the renormalized partition function (
4.0.3
):

\frac{Z(\eta_{\varepsilon}|\varepsilon)}{Z_{\mathrm{sG}(\mu\rho|\varepsilon,\Omega)}}=\frac{\mathcal{Z}(\eta_{\varepsilon}|\varepsilon)}{\mathcal{Z}(-\frac{\mu}{2}\rho|\varepsilon)}e^{\frac{1}{2}\int_{(\Omega\times\{\pm 2\})^{2}}d\xi_{1}\,d\xi_{2}\big(\eta_{\varepsilon}(\xi_{1})\eta_{\varepsilon}(\xi_{2})-\frac{\mu^{2}}{4}\rho(x_{1})\rho(x_{2})\big)A(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}|\varepsilon)}.

Putting everything together yields the claim.
∎

Thus to prove the first claim of Theorem
1.5
, it remains to prove that all relevant derivatives of: (i) the exponential of the quadratic term, (ii) the ratio of renormalized partition functions, and (iii) the factors involving the counter term converge as
\varepsilon\to 0
. We treat each of these cases separately.

Lemma 5.2
.

For any
S\subset\{1,\dots,m\}
and
S^{\prime}\subset\{1,\dots,m^{\prime}\}
, we have

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\prod_{l\in S}\!\partial_{u_{l}}|_{u_{l}=0}\prod_{l^{\prime}\in S^{\prime}}\!\partial_{v_{l^{\prime}}}|_{v_{l^{\prime}}=0}\bigg[e^{\frac{1}{2}\sum_{l,k=1}^{m}u_{l}u_{k}\left\langle\partial\varphi_{\varepsilon}(g_{l})\partial\varphi_{\varepsilon}(g_{k})\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}}\\
\times e^{\frac{1}{2}\sum_{l^{\prime},k^{\prime}=1}^{m^{\prime}}v_{l^{\prime}}v_{k^{\prime}}\left\langle\bar{\partial}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{g}_{l^{\prime}})\bar{\partial}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{g}_{k^{\prime}})\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}+\sum_{l=1}^{m}\sum_{l^{\prime}=1}^{m^{\prime}}u_{l}v_{l^{\prime}}\left\langle\partial\varphi_{\varepsilon}(g_{l})\bar{\partial}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{g}_{l^{\prime}})\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}}\bigg]\\
\quad=\prod_{l\in S}\partial_{u_{l}}|_{u_{l}=0}\prod_{l^{\prime}\in S^{\prime}}\partial_{v_{l^{\prime}}}|_{v_{l^{\prime}}=0}\bigg[e^{\frac{1}{2}\sum_{l,k=1}^{m}u_{l}u_{k}\left\langle\varphi(\partial g_{l})\varphi(\partial g_{k})\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}}}\\
\times e^{\frac{1}{2}\sum_{l^{\prime},k^{\prime}=1}^{m^{\prime}}v_{l^{\prime}}v_{k^{\prime}}\left\langle\varphi(\bar{\partial}\widetilde{g}_{l^{\prime}})\varphi(\bar{\partial}\widetilde{g}_{k^{\prime}})\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}}+\sum_{l=1}^{m}\sum_{l^{\prime}=1}^{m^{\prime}}u_{l}v_{l^{\prime}}\left\langle\varphi(\partial g_{l})\varphi(\bar{\partial}\widetilde{g}_{l^{\prime}})\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}}}\bigg].

In particular, the limit exists and is finite.

Proof.

Let us first note that the derivatives in question (when evaluated at zero) are polynomials in the relevant covariances. We also have (integrating by parts) e.g.

\left\langle\partial\varphi_{\varepsilon}(g_{l})\partial\varphi_{\varepsilon}(g_{k})\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}=\left\langle\varphi_{\varepsilon}(\partial g_{l})\varphi_{\varepsilon}(\partial g_{k})\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}.

Moreover, since we differentiate only once with respect to each parameter and then set the parameters to zero, we only ever encounter covariances of the form
\left\langle\varphi_{\varepsilon}(f)\varphi_{\varepsilon}(g)\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}
with
f
and
g
having disjoint support (since we are assuming in Theorem
1.5
that the test functions have disjoint support). Thus convergence of the individual covariances (and consequently of the relevant derivatives) follows from the uniform convergence provided by Lemma
2.5
. This concludes the proof.
∎

Let us then turn to the ratio of the renormalized partition functions.

Lemma 5.3
.

For
S_{1}\subset\{1,\dots,n\}
,
S_{2}\subset\{1,\dots,n^{\prime}\}
,
S_{3}\subset\{1,\dots,m\}
and
S_{4}\subset\{1,\dots,m^{\prime}\}
,

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\prod_{j\in S_{1}}\partial_{s_{j}}|_{s_{j}=0}\prod_{j^{\prime}\in S_{2}}\partial_{t_{j^{\prime}}}|_{t_{j^{\prime}}=0}\prod_{l\in S_{3}}\partial_{u_{l}}|_{u_{l}=0}\prod_{l^{\prime}\in S_{4}}\partial_{v_{l^{\prime}}}|_{v_{l^{\prime}}=0}\frac{\mathcal{Z}(\eta_{\varepsilon}|\varepsilon)}{\mathcal{Z}(-\frac{\mu}{2}\rho|\varepsilon)}

\displaystyle\quad=\prod_{j\in S_{1}}\partial_{s_{j}}|_{s_{j}=0}\prod_{j^{\prime}\in S_{2}}\partial_{t_{j^{\prime}}}|_{t_{j^{\prime}}=0}\prod_{l\in S_{3}}\partial_{u_{l}}|_{u_{l}=0}\prod_{l^{\prime}\in S_{4}}\partial_{v_{l^{\prime}}}|_{v_{l^{\prime}}=0}\frac{\mathcal{Z}(\eta)}{\mathcal{Z}(-\frac{\mu}{2}\rho)},

where

(5.0.6)

\displaystyle\eta(x,\sigma)=

\displaystyle-\sum_{j=1}^{n}s_{j}\delta_{\sigma,\sigma_{j}}e^{-i\sigma_{j}\sqrt{\pi}\big(\sum_{l=1}^{m}u_{l}\left\langle\varphi(x)\varphi(\partial g_{l})\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}}+\sum_{l^{\prime}=1}^{m^{\prime}}v_{l^{\prime}}\left\langle\varphi(x)\varphi(\bar{\partial}\widetilde{g}_{l^{\prime}})\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}}\big)}f_{j}(x)

\displaystyle-\sum_{j^{\prime}=1}^{n^{\prime}}t_{j^{\prime}}\delta_{\sigma,\tau_{j^{\prime}}}e^{-i\tau_{j^{\prime}}\sqrt{\pi}\big(\sum_{l=1}^{m}u_{l}\left\langle\varphi(x)\varphi(\partial g_{l})\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}}+\sum_{l^{\prime}=1}^{m^{\prime}}v_{l^{\prime}}\left\langle\varphi(x)\varphi(\bar{\partial}\widetilde{g}_{l^{\prime}})\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}}\big)}\widetilde{f}_{j^{\prime}}(x)

\displaystyle-\mathbf{1}_{\sigma\in\{-2,2\}}\frac{\mu}{2}e^{-i\sigma\sqrt{\pi}\big(\sum_{l=1}^{m}u_{l}\left\langle\varphi(x)\varphi(\partial g_{l})\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}}+\sum_{l^{\prime}=1}^{m^{\prime}}v_{l^{\prime}}\left\langle\varphi(x)\varphi(\bar{\partial}\widetilde{g}_{l^{\prime}})\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}}\big)}\rho(x).

In particular, the limit in question exists and is finite.

Proof.

First of all, we point out the denominator does not depend on the parameters we are differentiating with respect to and since
\mu
and
\rho
do not depend on
\varepsilon
, its convergence follows immediately from Theorem
4.1
part (i). Moreover, since
\rho
is real and independent of
\sigma
‚ part (iii) of the same theorem implies that the denominator is positive. Thus it is sufficient to focus entirely on the numerator.

Since Lemma
2.7
implies that
\eta_{\varepsilon}\to\eta
locally uniformly in everything, the (locally uniform) convergence of
\mathcal{Z}(\eta_{\varepsilon}|\varepsilon)
to
\mathcal{Z}(\eta)
follows from Theorem
4.1
part (iv). The fact that this extends to derivatives follows from the fact that
\mathcal{Z}(\eta_{\varepsilon}|\varepsilon)
is an entire function of all of the parameters (since we are dealing with either Gaussian or bounded random variables) so by locally uniform convergence,
\mathcal{Z}(\eta)
is entire and the derivatives converge.
∎

As a final ingredient for proving convergence, we show that the derivatives of the factors involving the counter term converge.

Lemma 5.4
.

For
S_{1}\subset\{1,\dots,n\}
,
S_{2}\subset\{1,\dots,n^{\prime}\}
,
S_{3}\subset\{1,\dots,m\}
and
S_{4}\subset\{1,\dots,m^{\prime}\}
,

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\prod_{j\in S_{1}}\partial_{s_{j}}|_{s_{j}=0}\prod_{j^{\prime}\in S_{2}}\partial_{t_{j^{\prime}}}|_{t_{j^{\prime}}=0}\prod_{l\in S_{3}}\partial_{u_{l}}|_{u_{l}=0}\prod_{l^{\prime}\in S_{4}}\partial_{v_{l^{\prime}}}|_{v_{l^{\prime}}=0}\bigg[e^{-\sum_{j^{\prime}=1}^{n^{\prime}}t_{j^{\prime}}\mu c_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{f}_{j^{\prime}},\rho)}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\times e^{\frac{1}{2}\int_{(\Omega\times\{\pm 2\})^{2}}d\xi_{1}\,d\xi_{2}\big(\eta_{\varepsilon}(\xi_{1})\eta_{\varepsilon}(\xi_{2})-\frac{\mu^{2}}{4}\rho(x_{1})\rho(x_{2})\big)A(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}|\varepsilon)}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\prod_{j\in S_{1}}\partial_{s_{j}}|_{s_{j}=0}\prod_{j^{\prime}\in S_{2}}\partial_{t_{j^{\prime}}}|_{t_{j^{\prime}}=0}\prod_{l\in S_{3}}\partial_{u_{l}}|_{u_{l}=0}\prod_{l^{\prime}\in S_{4}}\partial_{v_{l^{\prime}}}|_{v_{l^{\prime}}=0}\bigg[\exp\bigg\{\frac{\mu^{2}}{8}\int_{(\Omega\times\{\pm 2\})^{2}}d\xi_{1}\,d\xi_{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\Big(e^{i\nu_{1}\sqrt{\pi}\big(\sum_{l=1}^{m}u_{l}\left\langle\varphi(x_{1})\varphi(\partial g_{l})\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}}+\sum_{l^{\prime}=1}^{m^{\prime}}v_{l^{\prime}}\left\langle\varphi(x_{1})\varphi(\bar{\partial}\widetilde{g}_{l^{\prime}})\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}}\big)}

\displaystyle\quad\times e^{i\nu_{2}\sqrt{\pi}\big(\sum_{l=1}^{m}u_{l}\left\langle\varphi(x_{2})\varphi(\partial g_{l})\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}}+\sum_{l^{\prime}=1}^{m^{\prime}}v_{l^{\prime}}\left\langle\varphi(x_{2})\varphi(\bar{\partial}\widetilde{g}_{l^{\prime}})\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}}\big)}-1\Big)\rho(x_{1})\rho(x_{2})A(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{\mu}{2}\sum_{j^{\prime}=1}^{n^{\prime}}t_{j^{\prime}}\int_{(\Omega\times\{\pm 2\})^{2}}d\xi_{1}\,d\xi_{2}\,\delta_{\nu_{1},\tau_{j^{\prime}}}\Big(e^{i\tau_{j^{\prime}}\sqrt{\pi}\sum_{l=1}^{m}u_{l}\big(\left\langle\varphi(x_{1})\varphi(\partial g_{l})\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}}-\left\langle\varphi(x_{2})\varphi(\partial g_{l})\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}}\big)}

\displaystyle\quad\times e^{i\nu_{2}\sqrt{\pi}\sum_{l^{\prime}=1}^{m^{\prime}}v_{l^{\prime}}\big(\left\langle\varphi(x_{1})\varphi(\bar{\partial}\widetilde{g}_{l^{\prime}})\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}}-\left\langle\varphi(x_{2})\varphi(\bar{\partial}\widetilde{g}_{l^{\prime}})\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}}\big)}-1\Big)\widetilde{f}_{j^{\prime}}(x_{1})\rho(x_{2})A(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j^{\prime},k^{\prime}=1:\\
j^{\prime}\neq k^{\prime}\end{subarray}}^{n^{\prime}}t_{j^{\prime}}t_{k^{\prime}}\int_{(\Omega\times\{\pm 2\})^{2}}d\xi_{1}\,d\xi_{2}\,\delta_{\tau_{j^{\prime}},\nu_{1}}\delta_{\tau_{k^{\prime}},\nu_{2}}

\displaystyle\quad\qquad\times e^{i\tau_{j^{\prime}}\sqrt{\pi}\big(\sum_{l=1}^{m}u_{l}\left\langle\varphi(x_{1})\varphi(\partial g_{l})\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}}+\sum_{l^{\prime}=1}^{m^{\prime}}v_{l^{\prime}}\left\langle\varphi(x_{1})\varphi(\bar{\partial}\widetilde{g}_{l^{\prime}})\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}}\big)}

\displaystyle\quad\times e^{i\tau_{k^{\prime}}\sqrt{\pi}\big(\sum_{l=1}^{m}u_{l}\left\langle\varphi(x_{2})\varphi(\partial g_{l})\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}}+\sum_{l^{\prime}=1}^{m^{\prime}}v_{l^{\prime}}\left\langle\varphi(x_{2})\varphi(\bar{\partial}\widetilde{g}_{l^{\prime}})\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}}\big)}\widetilde{f}_{j^{\prime}}(x_{1})\widetilde{f}_{k^{\prime}}(x_{2})A(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\bigg\}\bigg],

where
A(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}|\varepsilon)
is as in (
4.0.2
). In particular, the limit exists and is finite. Above we wrote
\xi_{i}=(x_{i},\nu_{i})
with
x_{i}\in\Omega
and
\nu_{i}\in\{\pm 2\}
.

Proof.

Let us begin by writing out in detail the quantity in question using Lemma
5.1
. Since the symbols
\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{n}
are now reserved, let us call our integration variables
\xi_{i}=(x_{i},\nu_{i})
. Also, let us introduce some notation that will make the quantities slightly less horrendous looking. For this purpose, we write

F_{\varepsilon}(x)=\sum_{l=1}^{m}u_{l}\langle\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x)\partial\varphi_{\varepsilon}(g_{l})\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}+\sum_{l^{\prime}=1}^{m^{\prime}}v_{l^{\prime}}\langle\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x)\bar{\partial}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{g}_{l^{\prime}})\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}.

With this notation, we have

\displaystyle\eta_{\varepsilon}(x,\nu)\!=-\!\sum_{j=1}^{n}\!s_{j}\delta_{\nu,\sigma_{j}}e^{i\sigma_{j}\sqrt{\pi}F_{\varepsilon}(x)}f_{j}(x)-\!\sum_{j^{\prime}=1}^{n^{\prime}}\!t_{j^{\prime}}\delta_{\nu,\tau_{j^{\prime}}}e^{i\tau_{j^{\prime}}\sqrt{\pi}F_{\varepsilon}(x)}\widetilde{f}_{j^{\prime}}(x)-\frac{\mu}{2}\mathbf{1}_{\nu\in\{-2,2\}}e^{i\nu\sqrt{\pi}F_{\varepsilon}(x)}\!\rho(x).

Note that when integrating
\eta_{\varepsilon}(\xi_{1})\eta_{\varepsilon}(\xi_{2})
over
(\Omega\times\{\pm 2\})^{2}
, the
s_{j}
terms disappear from
\eta_{\varepsilon}
since we have
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\in\{\pm 2\}
but
\sigma_{j}\in\{\pm 1\}
so always
\delta_{\nu_{i},\sigma_{j}}=0
in this case.

Before looking at the quantity of interest, let us note that we can rewrite
c_{\varepsilon}(f,\rho)
in a slightly more convenient way. We claim that for
\tau\in\{\pm 2\}
, we can write

\displaystyle c_{\varepsilon}(f,\rho)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{(\Omega\times\{\pm 2\})^{2}}d\xi_{1}\,d\xi_{2}\,\delta_{\nu_{1},\tau}f(x_{1})\rho(x_{2})A(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}|\varepsilon).

To see why this is the case, note that

\frac{1}{2}\int_{(\Omega\times\{\pm 2\})^{2}}d\xi_{1}\,d\xi_{2}\,\delta_{\nu_{1},\tau}f(x_{1})\rho(x_{2})A(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}|\varepsilon)\\
\quad=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\nu_{2}\in\{\pm 2\}}\int_{\Omega^{2}}d^{2}x_{1}\,d^{2}x_{2}\,f(x_{1})\rho(x_{2})\Big(\big\langle\mathop{:}e^{i\tau\sqrt{\pi}\varphi(x_{1})}\mathop{:}\mathop{:}e^{i\nu_{2}\sqrt{\pi}\varphi(x_{2})}\mathop{:}\big\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}\\
-\big\langle\mathop{:}e^{i\tau\sqrt{\pi}\varphi(x_{1})}\mathop{:}\big\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}\big\langle\mathop{:}e^{i\nu_{2}\sqrt{\pi}\varphi(x_{2})}\mathop{:}\big\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}\Big)\\
\quad=\int_{\Omega^{2}}d^{2}x_{1}\,d^{2}x_{2}\,f(x_{1})\rho(x_{2})\Big(\big\langle\mathop{:}e^{i\tau\sqrt{\pi}\varphi(x_{1})}\mathop{:}\mathop{:}\cos(\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi(x_{2}))\mathop{:}\big\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}\\
-\big\langle\mathop{:}e^{i\tau\sqrt{\pi}\varphi(x_{1})}\mathop{:}\big\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}\big\langle\mathop{:}\cos(\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi(x_{2}))\mathop{:}\big\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}\Big).

Writing
\mathop{:}e^{i\tau\sqrt{\pi}\varphi(x_{1})}\mathop{:}=\mathop{:}\cos(\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi(x_{1}))\mathop{:}+i\tfrac{\tau}{2}\mathop{:}\sin(\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi(x_{1}))\mathop{:}
and noting that the sine-terms are odd under
\varphi\mapsto-\varphi
, while the cosine-terms are even and the law of the regularized GFF is centered, so the expectations involving the sine terms vanish and indeed we can write
c_{\varepsilon}(f,\rho)
this way.

After these preparatory remarks, we find

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{(\Omega\times\{\pm 2\})^{2}}d\xi_{1}\,d\xi_{2}\left(\eta_{\varepsilon}(\xi_{1})\eta_{\varepsilon}(\xi_{2})-\frac{\mu^{2}}{4}\rho(x_{1})\rho(x_{2})\right)A(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}|\varepsilon)-\sum_{j^{\prime}=1}^{n^{\prime}}t_{j^{\prime}}\mu c_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{f}_{j^{\prime}},\rho)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mu^{2}}{8}\int_{(\Omega\times\{\pm 2\})^{2}}d\xi_{1}\,d\xi_{2}\left(e^{i\nu_{1}\sqrt{\pi}F_{\varepsilon}(x_{1})+i\nu_{2}\sqrt{\pi}F_{\varepsilon}(x_{2})}-1\right)\rho(x_{1})\rho(x_{2})A(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}|\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\kern 5.0pt+\frac{1}{2}\!\sum_{j_{1}^{\prime},j_{2}^{\prime}=1}^{n^{\prime}}t_{j_{1}^{\prime}}t_{j_{2}^{\prime}}\int_{(\Omega\times\{\pm 2\})^{2}}\!d\xi_{1}\,d\xi_{2}\,\delta_{\nu_{1},\tau_{j_{1}^{\prime}}}\delta_{\nu_{2},\tau_{j_{2}^{\prime}}}e^{i\tau_{j_{1}^{\prime}}\sqrt{\pi}F_{\varepsilon}(x_{1})+i\tau_{j_{2}^{\prime}}\sqrt{\pi}F_{\varepsilon}(x_{2})}\widetilde{f}_{j_{1}^{\prime}}(x_{1})\widetilde{f}_{j_{2}^{\prime}}(x_{2})A(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}|\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\kern 5.0pt+\frac{\mu}{2}\sum_{j^{\prime}=1}^{n^{\prime}}t_{j^{\prime}}\int_{(\Omega\times\{\pm 2\})^{2}}d\xi_{1}\,d\xi_{2}\,\delta_{\nu_{1},\tau_{j^{\prime}}}\left(e^{i\tau_{j^{\prime}}\sqrt{\pi}F_{\varepsilon}(x_{1})+i\nu_{2}\sqrt{\pi}F_{\varepsilon}(x_{2})}-1\right)\widetilde{f}_{j^{\prime}}(x_{1})\rho(x_{2})A(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}|\varepsilon).

For
\varepsilon>0
, this is clearly an entire function of the parameters
s,t,u,v,\mu
.

Note that by Lemma
2.6
and Lemma
2.7
we see that
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}A(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}|\varepsilon)=A(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})
and
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\left\langle\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x)\varphi_{\varepsilon}(f)\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}=\left\langle\varphi(x)\varphi(f)\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}}
, so the integrand converges pointwise to the integrand in the statement of the lemma. To conclude, we need an estimate allowing the use of dominated convergence theorem. Let us start with an estimate for
A
. If
\nu_{1}=\nu_{2}
‚ then
A
is bounded, so we can focus on the case
\nu_{1}\neq\nu_{2}
. By (
4.0.2
), we can write

\displaystyle A((x_{1},\nu_{1}),(x_{2},-\nu_{1})|\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=c_{\sqrt{4\pi}}^{2}\varepsilon^{-2}e^{-2\pi\left\langle\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x_{1})^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}\!-2\pi\!\left\langle\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x_{2})^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}}\left(e^{4\pi\left\langle\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x_{1})\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x_{2})\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}}-1\right)\!.

Lemma
2.5
implies that for any compact
K\subset\Omega
, there exists a constant
C_{K}
such that for small enough
\varepsilon
(“small enough” depending only on
K
)

\varepsilon^{-2}e^{-2\pi\left\langle\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x_{1})^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}-2\pi\left\langle\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x_{2})^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}}\leq C_{K}

for
x_{1},x_{2}\in K
.

Moreover, since
\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}\geq 0
, we have again for any compact
K\subset\Omega
the existence of a constant
C_{K}
(possibly different from above)

0\leq e^{4\pi\left\langle\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x_{1})\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x_{2})\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}}-1\leq e^{4\pi\int_{0}^{\infty}ds\,\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x_{1},x_{2})}=e^{4\pi G_{\Omega}(x_{1},x_{2})}\leq C_{K}|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{-2}

for
x_{1},x_{2}\in K
. Thus for any compact
K\subset\Omega
, there exists a
C_{K}>0
such that for small enough
\varepsilon>0
and any
x_{1},x_{2}\in K

A((x_{1},\nu_{1}),(x_{2},-\nu_{1})|\varepsilon)\leq C_{K}|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{-2}.

Let us now turn to arguing that we can use dominated convergence. First of all, for the
t_{j_{1}^{\prime}}t_{j_{2}^{\prime}}
-terms, note that we never differentiate twice with respect to a
t
-parameter. Since
\widetilde{f}_{j_{1}^{\prime}}
and
\widetilde{f}_{j_{2}^{\prime}}
have disjoint supports for
j_{1}^{\prime}\neq j_{2}^{\prime}
, we have no issue with dominated convergence.

Let us then look at the
\mu^{2}
-term. Here
A(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}|\varepsilon)
is bounded if
\nu_{1}=\nu_{2}
‚ so we only need to worry about the
\nu_{1}=-\nu_{2}
term. For this, the uniform Lipschitz continuity and boundedness of
F_{\varepsilon}
(from Lemma
2.7
) implies that we have for some constant
C
(not depending on
\varepsilon
)

\left|e^{i\nu_{1}\sqrt{\pi}(F_{\varepsilon}(x_{1})-F_{\varepsilon}(x_{2}))}-1\right|\leq C|x_{1}-x_{2}|.

Thus using our bound on
A
, we can use dominated convergence. The reasoning for the
\mu t_{j^{\prime}}
terms is similar (if
\nu_{1}=\tau_{j^{\prime}}=\nu_{2}
, then
A
is bounded and we can use dominated convergence, while if
\nu_{1}=\tau_{j^{\prime}}=-\nu_{2}
, we use the same bound on
A
and the uniform boundedness and uniform Hölder continuity of
F_{\varepsilon}
).

Thus the uniform Lipschitz estimate of Lemma
2.7
implies that in the
\mu^{2}
-and
\mu t_{j^{\prime}}
-terms above, we can bound the integrand by a constant (depending on essentially all of our parameters apart from
\varepsilon
) times
|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{-1}\mathbf{1}_{K}
. This singularity is integrable in two dimensions so the use of dominated convergence is justified and we see that the
\mu^{2}
and
\mu t_{j^{\prime}}
-terms converge (locally uniformly in the parameters) as
\varepsilon\to 0
. For the
t_{j^{\prime}}t_{k^{\prime}}
-terms, we note that since we differentiate only once with respect to each parameter, we never encounter the diagonal terms
j^{\prime}=k^{\prime}
. Thus since
\widetilde{f}_{j^{\prime}}
and
\widetilde{f}_{k^{\prime}}
have disjoint supports, the integrand is uniformly bounded and again by the dominated convergence theorem, all of the relevant terms converge (locally uniformly).

Since we have locally uniform convergence of an entire function, we conclude that also its derivatives converge to the derivatives of the limit. This concludes the proof.
∎

Putting everything together takes care of our first task:

Proof of Theorem
1.5
, part (i).

The statement follows from decomposing Wick ordered trigonometric functions into Wick ordered exponentials, combining Lemmas
5.1
–
5.4
and using the product rule for differentiation.
∎

We now turn to the second statement of Theorem
1.5
, namely analyticity of the correlation functions as a function of
\mu
in a neighborhood of the real axis. Again, it is sufficient to focus on the Wick ordered exponentials instead of Wick ordered trigonometric functions. We have in fact already done all of the preliminary work and we can start the actual proof.

Proof of Theorem
1.5
, part (ii).

From Lemmas
5.1
–
5.4
, we see that it is enough to establish that the limiting objects appearing in Lemmas
5.2
–
5.4
are analytic in
\mu
in a neighborhood of the real axis. For Lemma
5.2
, there is no dependence on
\mu
. Also for Lemma
5.4
, we see that as a function of
\mu
, the limiting object is a polynomial times an exponential of a quadratic polynomial – thus an entire function. So we only need to focus on the (derivatives of the) ratio of the renormalized partition functions. For this, we note first that by Theorem
4.1
parts (ii) and (iii)

\mu\mapsto\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}(-\frac{\mu}{2}\rho)}

is analytic in a neighborhood of the real axis. On the other hand, one readily checks from Theorem
4.1
part (ii) that in the notation of Lemma
5.3
,

\prod_{j\in S_{1}}\partial_{s_{j}}|_{s_{j}=0}\prod_{j^{\prime}\in S_{2}}\partial_{t_{j^{\prime}}}|_{t_{j^{\prime}}=0}\prod_{l\in S_{3}}\partial_{u_{l}}|_{u_{l}=0}\prod_{l^{\prime}\in S_{4}}\partial_{v_{l^{\prime}}}|_{v_{l^{\prime}}=0}\,\mathcal{Z}(\eta)

(for
\eta
as in Lemma
5.3
) is an entire function of
\mu
. Putting everything together, we see that the limiting correlation functions are analytic in
\mu
in a neighborhood of the real axis – this concludes the proof.
∎

We now turn to the final statement in Theorem
1.5
. This is slightly involved as it requires some combinatorial arguments and analysis of GFF correlation functions from the renormalized potential.

Two preliminary remarks:

•

From our proof of item (i) of Theorem
1.5
, we see that the convergence is uniform in
\mu
in some neighborhood of the origin (this boils down to the uniform convergence of the renormalized partition function from Theorem
4.1
– the other terms being explicit). This implies in particular that the Taylor coefficients (when expanding at zero) of

\mu\mapsto\Bigg\langle\prod_{j=1}^{p}\partial\varphi_{\varepsilon}(f_{j})\prod_{j^{\prime}=1}^{p^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(f_{j^{\prime}}^{\prime})\prod_{k=1}^{q}\mathop{:}\cos(\sqrt{\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon})\mathop{:}(g_{k})\prod_{k^{\prime}=1}^{q^{\prime}}\mathop{:}\sin(\sqrt{\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon})\mathop{:}(g_{k^{\prime}}^{\prime})\\
\times\prod_{l=1}^{r}\mathop{\typecolon}\cos(\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon})\mathop{\typecolon}(h_{l})\Bigg\rangle_{\mathrm{sG}(\mu\rho|\varepsilon,\Omega)}

converge to those of

\mu\!\mapsto\!\Bigg\langle\prod_{j=1}^{p}\!\partial\varphi(f_{j})\!\prod_{j^{\prime}=1}^{p^{\prime}}\!\bar{\partial}\varphi(f_{j^{\prime}}^{\prime})\!\prod_{k=1}^{q}\!\mathop{:}\cos(\sqrt{\pi}\varphi)\mathop{:}(g_{k})\!\!\prod_{k^{\prime}=1}^{q^{\prime}}\!\!\mathop{:}\sin(\sqrt{\pi}\varphi)\mathop{:}(g_{k^{\prime}}^{\prime})\!\prod_{l=1}^{r}\!\mathop{\typecolon}\cos(\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi)\mathop{\typecolon}(h_{l})\!\Bigg\rangle_{\mathrm{sG}(\mu\rho|\Omega)}.

•

It is again enough to focus on Wick ordered exponentials instead of Wick ordered trigonometric functions.

The point of these remarks is that it is sufficient for us to compute the Taylor coefficients of

\Bigg\langle\prod_{j=1}^{n}\mathop{:}e^{i\sigma_{j}\sqrt{\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon}}\mathop{:}(f_{j})\prod_{j^{\prime}=1}^{n^{\prime}}\left(\mathop{:}e^{i\tau_{j^{\prime}}\sqrt{\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon}}\mathop{:}(\widetilde{f}_{j^{\prime}})-\mu c_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{f}_{j^{\prime}},\rho)\right)\prod_{l=1}^{m}\partial\varphi_{\varepsilon}(g_{l})\prod_{l^{\prime}=1}^{m^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{g}_{l^{\prime}})\Bigg\rangle_{\mathrm{sG}(\mu\rho|\varepsilon,\Omega)}

with
\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{n}\in\{-1,1\}
,
\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{n^{\prime}}\in\{-2,2\}
, and try to control their
\varepsilon\to 0
asymptotics. For computing the Taylor coefficients, we begin with the following simple but general fact.

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
X,Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n}
and
Z
be complex random variables for which
Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n},Z
are bounded and
X
satisfies
\mathbb{E}\big[e^{\alpha|X|}\big]<\infty
for all
\alpha>0
, also let
c_{1},\dots,c_{n}\in\mathbb{C}
be deterministic. There exists an open disk centered at the origin in which

\mu\mapsto\frac{\mathbb{E}\Big[X\prod_{j=1}^{n}(Y_{j}-\mu c_{j})e^{\mu Z}\Big]}{\mathbb{E}[e^{\mu Z}]}

is analytic and in this disk has the following series expansion

\displaystyle\sum_{p=0}^{\infty}\frac{\mu^{p}}{p!}\sum_{S\subset[n]}(-1)^{n-|S|}\binom{p}{n-|S|}(n-|S|)!\Bigg(\prod_{j\notin S}c_{j}\Bigg)\Bigg\langle X\prod_{k\in S}Y_{k};Z;\dots;Z\Bigg\rangle^{\mathsf{T}},

where the binomial coefficient is understood as zero if
p<n-|S|
, and

\displaystyle\Bigg\langle X\prod_{k\in S}Y_{k};Z;\dots;Z\Bigg\rangle^{\mathsf{T}}

is the joint cumulant of
X\prod_{k\in S}Y_{k}
and
p-(n-|S|)
instances of
Z
.

Proof.

We begin by simply expanding the product over
j
:

\frac{\mathbb{E}\Big[X\prod_{j=1}^{n}(Y_{j}-\mu c_{j})e^{\mu Z}\Big]}{\mathbb{E}[e^{\mu Z}]}=\sum_{S\subset[n]}(-1)^{n-|S|}\mu^{n-|S|}\Bigg(\prod_{j\notin S}c_{j}\Bigg)\frac{\mathbb{E}\Big[X\prod_{k\in S}Y_{k}e^{\mu Z}\Big]}{\mathbb{E}[e^{\mu Z}]}.

Since the quantities inside the expectations are integrable by our assumptions (for any
\mu\in\mathbb{C}
), one readily checks by Fubini, dominated convergence and Morera’s theorem that the expectations are entire functions. Since
\mathbb{E}[e^{\mu Z}]=1
for
\mu=0
, we conclude that there is a disk surrounding the origin in which
\mathbb{E}[e^{\mu Z}]\neq 0
. In this disk, the ratio of these two entire functions is analytic, so we conclude that the function in question is indeed analytic in some disk.

To compute the series expansion, we first note that

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{E}\Big[X\prod_{k\in S}Y_{k}e^{\mu Z}\Big]}{\mathbb{E}[e^{\mu Z}]}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\bigg|_{t=0}\log\mathbb{E}\Big[e^{tX\prod_{k\in S}Y_{k}+\mu Z}\Big].

We recognize this logarithm as the joint cumulant generating function of the random variables
X\prod_{k\in S}Y_{k}
and
Z
. With similar arguments as before, our assumptions ensure that this cumulant generating function is an analytic function of
t
and
\mu
in some neighborhood of the origin and in this neighborhood, we have

\displaystyle\log\mathbb{E}\Big[e^{tX\prod_{k\in S}Y_{k}+\mu Z}\Big]

\displaystyle=\sum_{p,q=0}^{\infty}\frac{t^{p}\mu^{q}}{p!q!}\Bigg\langle X\prod_{k\in S}Y_{k};\dots;X\prod_{k\in S}Y_{k};Z;\dots;Z\Bigg\rangle^{\mathsf{T}},

where there are
p
entries of
X\prod_{k\in S}Y_{k}
and
q
entries of
Z
.

Evaluating the
t
derivative at zero, we find that there exists some disk in which we have

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{E}\Big[X\prod_{j=1}^{n}(Y_{j}-\mu c_{j})e^{\mu Z}\Big]}{\mathbb{E}[e^{\mu Z}]}=\sum_{q=0}^{\infty}\sum_{S\subset[n]}(-1)^{n-|S|}\frac{\mu^{n-|S|+q}}{q!}\Bigg(\prod_{j\notin S}c_{j}\Bigg)\Bigg\langle X\prod_{k\in S}Y_{k};Z;\dots;Z\Bigg\rangle^{\mathsf{T}},

where there are
q
entries of
Z
in the cumulant.

Making a change of variables in the sum, where we make
n-|S|+q
our new summation variable
p
, we write this as

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{E}\Big[X\prod_{j=1}^{n}(Y_{j}-\mu c_{j})e^{\mu Z}\Big]}{\mathbb{E}[e^{\mu Z}]}

\displaystyle\qquad=\sum_{p=0}^{\infty}\frac{\mu^{p}}{p!}\sum_{S\subset[n]}(-1)^{n-|S|}\binom{p}{n-|S|}(n-|S|)!\Bigg(\prod_{j\notin S}c_{j}\Bigg)\Bigg\langle X\prod_{k\in S}Y_{k};Z;\dots;Z\Bigg\rangle^{\mathsf{T}},

where there are
p-(n-|S|)

Z
entries in the cumulant and we understand the binomial coefficient as zero if
p<n-|S|
. This concludes the proof.
∎

We now apply this to the setting relevant to us.

Lemma 5.6
.

For
\varepsilon>0
, we have
(\,
for
\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{n}\in\{-1,1\}
,
\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{n^{\prime}}\in\{-2,2\}\,)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{p}}{\partial\mu^{p}}\bigg|_{\mu=0}\!\!\Bigg\langle\prod_{j=1}^{n}\mathop{:}e^{i\sigma_{j}\sqrt{\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon}}\mathop{:}(f_{j})\!\prod_{j^{\prime}=1}^{n^{\prime}}\!\Big(\mathop{:}e^{i\tau_{j^{\prime}}\sqrt{\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon}}\mathop{:}(\widetilde{f}_{j^{\prime}})-\mu c_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{f}_{j^{\prime}},\rho)\Big)\!\prod_{l=1}^{m}\partial\varphi_{\varepsilon}(g_{l})\!\prod_{l^{\prime}=1}^{m^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{g}_{l^{\prime}})\Bigg\rangle_{\mathrm{sG}(\mu\rho|\varepsilon,\Omega)}

\displaystyle\kern 5.0pt=\int_{\Omega^{n+n^{\prime}+m+m^{\prime}+p}}d^{2n}x\,d^{2n^{\prime}}y\,d^{2m}z\,d^{2m^{\prime}}w\,d^{2p}u\prod_{j=1}^{n}f_{j}(x_{j})\!\prod_{j^{\prime}=1}^{n^{\prime}}\widetilde{f}_{j^{\prime}}(y_{j^{\prime}})\!\prod_{l=1}^{m}(-\partial g_{l}(z_{l}))\!\prod_{l^{\prime}=1}^{m^{\prime}}(-\bar{\partial}\widetilde{g}_{l^{\prime}}(w_{l^{\prime}}))

\displaystyle\quad\times\prod_{k=1}^{p}\rho(u_{k})\sum_{S\subset[n^{\prime}]}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S^{\prime}\subset[p]:\\
|S|=|S^{\prime}|\end{subarray}}\sum_{\pi:S\leftrightarrow S^{\prime}}\prod_{s\in S}(-\mathcal{A}(y_{s},u_{\pi(s)}|\varepsilon))\Bigg\langle\prod_{j=1}^{n}\mathop{:}e^{i\sigma_{j}\sqrt{\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x_{j})}\mathop{:}\prod_{l=1}^{m}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(z_{l})\prod_{l^{\prime}=1}^{m^{\prime}}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(w_{l^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\prod_{k^{\prime}\notin S}\big(\mathop{:}e^{i\tau_{k^{\prime}}\sqrt{\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(y_{k^{\prime}})}\mathop{:}\big);\big(\mathop{:}\cos(\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(u_{\alpha_{1}}))\mathop{:}\big);\dots;\big(\mathop{:}\cos(\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(u_{\alpha_{p-|S^{\prime}|}}))\mathop{:}\big)\bigg\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}^{\mathsf{T}},

where,
\{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{p-|S^{\prime}|}\}=[p]\setminus S^{\prime}
and the sum over
\pi
is a sum over bijections between
S
and
S^{\prime}
.

Proof.

We apply Lemma
5.5
with
X=\prod_{j=1}^{n}\mathop{:}e^{i\sigma_{j}\sqrt{\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon}}\mathop{:}(f_{j})\prod_{l=1}^{m}\partial\varphi_{\varepsilon}(g_{l})\prod_{l^{\prime}=1}^{m^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{g}_{l^{\prime}})
,
Y_{j}=\mathop{:}e^{i\tau_{j}\sqrt{\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon}}\mathop{:}(\widetilde{f}_{j})
,
c_{j}=c_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{f}_{j},\rho)
, and
Z=\mathop{:}\cos(\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon})\mathop{:}(\rho)
.

To be precise,
X
may not satisfy
\mathbb{E}(e^{\alpha|X|})<\infty
, but replacing
X
by
\widetilde{X}=X\mathbf{1}_{\{|X|\leq R\}}
, we can make use of this formula and then let
R\to\infty
. The validity of this argument is readily justified with the Cauchy integral formula, Fubini, and dominated convergence.

We thus find that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{p}}{\partial\mu^{p}}\bigg|_{\mu=0}\Bigg\langle\prod_{j=1}^{n}\mathop{:}e^{i\sigma_{j}\sqrt{\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon}}\mathop{:}(f_{j})\!\prod_{j^{\prime}=1}^{n^{\prime}}\!\!\Big(\mathop{:}e^{i\tau_{j^{\prime}}\sqrt{\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon}}\mathop{:}(\widetilde{f}_{j^{\prime}})-\mu c_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{f}_{j^{\prime}},\rho)\Big)\!\prod_{l=1}^{m}\partial\varphi_{\varepsilon}(g_{l})\!\prod_{l^{\prime}=1}^{m^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{g}_{l^{\prime}})\Bigg\rangle_{\mathrm{sG}(\mu\rho|\varepsilon,\Omega)}

\displaystyle\kern 5.0pt=\sum_{S\subset[n^{\prime}]}(-1)^{n^{\prime}-|S|}\binom{p}{n^{\prime}-|S|}(n^{\prime}-|S|)!\bigg(\prod_{j^{\prime}\notin S}c_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{f}_{j^{\prime}},\rho)\bigg)\Bigg\langle\prod_{j=1}^{n}\mathop{:}e^{i\sigma_{j}\sqrt{\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon}}\mathop{:}(f_{j})\prod_{l=1}^{m}\partial\varphi_{\varepsilon}(g_{l})

\displaystyle\qquad\prod_{l^{\prime}=1}^{m^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{g}_{l^{\prime}})\prod_{k^{\prime}\in S}\big(\mathop{:}e^{i\tau_{k^{\prime}}\sqrt{\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon}}\mathop{:}(\widetilde{f}_{k^{\prime}})\big);\big(\mathop{:}\cos(\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon})\mathop{:}(\rho)\big);\dots;\big(:\cos(\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon})\mathop{:}(\rho)\big)\Bigg\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}^{\mathsf{T}},

where we have
p-(n^{\prime}-|S|)
instances of
\mathop{:}\cos(\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon})\mathop{:}(\rho)
(and the interpretation that for
p<n^{\prime}-|S|
, the binomial coefficient is zero).

Next we use multilinearity of the cumulants and Fubini to write this as

\int_{\Omega^{n+n^{\prime}+m+m^{\prime}+p}}d^{2n}x\,d^{2n^{\prime}}y\,d^{2m}z\,d^{2m^{\prime}}w\,d^{2p}u\prod_{j=1}^{n}f_{j}(x_{j})\prod_{j^{\prime}=1}^{n^{\prime}}\widetilde{f}_{j^{\prime}}(y_{j^{\prime}})\prod_{l=1}^{m}(-\partial g_{l}(z_{l}))\prod_{l^{\prime}=1}^{m^{\prime}}(-\bar{\partial}\widetilde{g}_{l^{\prime}}(w_{l^{\prime}}))\\
\quad\times\prod_{k=1}^{p}\rho(u_{k})\sum_{S\subset[n^{\prime}]}(-1)^{n^{\prime}-|S|}\binom{p}{n^{\prime}-|S|}(n^{\prime}-|S|)!\mathcal{A}(y_{\alpha_{1}},u_{1}|\varepsilon)\dots\mathcal{A}(y_{\alpha_{n^{\prime}-|S|}},u_{n^{\prime}-|S|}|\varepsilon)\\
\qquad\times\Bigg\langle\prod_{j=1}^{n}\mathop{:}e^{i\sigma_{j}\sqrt{\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x_{j})}\mathop{:}\prod_{l=1}^{m}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(z_{l})\prod_{l^{\prime}=1}^{m^{\prime}}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(w_{l^{\prime}})\prod_{k^{\prime}\in S}\big(\mathop{:}e^{i\tau_{k^{\prime}}\sqrt{\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(y_{k^{\prime}})}\mathop{:}\big);\\
\big(\mathop{:}\cos(\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(u_{n^{\prime}-|S|+1}))\mathop{:}\big);\dots;\big(\mathop{:}\cos(\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(u_{p}))\mathop{:}\big)\Bigg\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}^{\mathsf{T}},

where we have written
[n^{\prime}]\setminus S=\{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n^{\prime}-|S|}\}
.

Next, we note that if we would replace
u_{1},\dots,u_{n^{\prime}-|S|}
in the
A
-terms by any subset of
\{u_{1},\dots,u_{p}\}
, this would not change the value of the integral. So if in the
S
sum we make
[n^{\prime}]\setminus S
the summation variable (and still call it
S
), we find that this integral can be written as

\int_{\Omega^{n+n^{\prime}+m+m^{\prime}+p}}d^{2n}x\,d^{2n^{\prime}}y\,d^{2m}z\,d^{2m^{\prime}}w\,d^{2p}u\prod_{j=1}^{n}f_{j}(x_{j})\prod_{j^{\prime}=1}^{n^{\prime}}\widetilde{f}_{j^{\prime}}(y_{j^{\prime}})\prod_{l=1}^{m}(-\partial g_{l}(z_{l}))\prod_{l^{\prime}=1}^{m^{\prime}}(-\bar{\partial}\widetilde{g}_{l^{\prime}}(w_{l^{\prime}}))\\
\kern 5.0pt\times\prod_{k=1}^{p}\rho(u_{k})\sum_{S\subset[n^{\prime}]}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S^{\prime}\subset[p]:\\
|S|=|S^{\prime}|\end{subarray}}\sum_{\pi:S\leftrightarrow S^{\prime}}\prod_{s\in S}(-\mathcal{A}(y_{s},u_{\pi(s)}|\varepsilon))\Bigg\langle\prod_{j=1}^{n}\mathop{:}e^{i\sigma_{j}\sqrt{\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x_{j})}\mathop{:}\prod_{l=1}^{m}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(z_{l})\prod_{l^{\prime}=1}^{m^{\prime}}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(w_{l^{\prime}})\\
\prod_{k^{\prime}\notin S}\big(\mathop{:}e^{i\tau_{k^{\prime}}\sqrt{\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(y_{k^{\prime}})}\mathop{:}\big);\big(\mathop{:}\cos(\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(u_{\alpha_{1}}))\mathop{:}\big);\dots;\big(\mathop{:}\cos(\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(u_{\alpha_{p-|S^{\prime}|}}))\mathop{:}\big)\Bigg\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}^{\mathsf{T}},

where, as in the statement of the lemma,
\{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{p-|S^{\prime}|}\}=[p]\setminus S^{\prime}
and the sum over
\pi
is a sum over bijections between
S
and
S^{\prime}
. This concludes the proof.
∎

This representation has two benefits. First of all, we see immediately from Lemma
2.6
that the integrand converges as
\varepsilon\to 0
. Secondly, as we argue now, the integrand is symmetric in the
u
variables. The precise statement is as follows.

Lemma 5.7
.

The function

(5.0.7)

C_{\varepsilon}(x,\sigma,y,\tau,z,w,u)\\
=\sum_{S\subset[n^{\prime}]}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S^{\prime}\subset[p]:\\
|S|=|S^{\prime}|\end{subarray}}\sum_{\pi:S\leftrightarrow S^{\prime}}\prod_{s\in S}(-\mathcal{A}(y_{s},u_{\pi(s)}|\varepsilon))\Bigg\langle\prod_{j=1}^{n}\mathop{:}e^{i\sigma_{j}\sqrt{\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x_{j})}\mathop{:}\prod_{l=1}^{m}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(z_{l})\prod_{l^{\prime}=1}^{m^{\prime}}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(w_{l^{\prime}})\\
\prod_{k^{\prime}\notin S}\big(\mathop{:}e^{i\tau_{k^{\prime}}\sqrt{\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(y_{k^{\prime}})}\mathop{:}\big);\big(\mathop{:}\cos(\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(u_{\alpha_{1}}))\mathop{:}\big);\dots;\big(\mathop{:}\cos(\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(u_{\alpha_{p-|S^{\prime}|}}))\mathop{:}\big)\Bigg\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}^{\mathsf{T}}

is the unique smooth function which satisfies:

•

C_{\varepsilon}(\cdot,\nu u)=C_{\varepsilon}(\cdot,u)
for each
\nu
which is a permutation of
[p]

(
here we write
\nu u=(u_{\nu(1)},\dots,u_{\nu(p)}))
.

•

C_{\varepsilon}(x,\sigma,y,\tau,z,w,u)\to 0
if one of the
z
or
w
variables tends to
\partial\Omega
.

•

For each
\rho\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
and
f_{1},\dots,f_{n}
,
\widetilde{f}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{f}_{n^{\prime}}
,
g_{1},\dots,g_{m}
,
\widetilde{g}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{g}_{m^{\prime}}

\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
with disjoint supports (not necessarily disjoint from the support of
\rho
), we have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{p}}{\partial\mu^{p}}\bigg|_{\mu=0}\Bigg\langle\prod_{j=1}^{n}\mathop{:}e^{i\sigma_{j}\sqrt{\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon}}\mathop{:}(f_{j})\!\prod_{j^{\prime}=1}^{n^{\prime}}\!\!\Big(\mathop{:}e^{i\tau_{j^{\prime}}\sqrt{\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon}}\mathop{:}(\widetilde{f}_{j^{\prime}})-\mu c_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{f}_{j^{\prime}},\rho)\Big)\!\prod_{l=1}^{m}\partial\varphi_{\varepsilon}(g_{l})\!\prod_{l^{\prime}=1}^{m^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{g}_{l^{\prime}})\Bigg\rangle_{\mathrm{sG}(\mu\rho|\varepsilon,\Omega)}

\displaystyle\kern 5.0pt=\int_{\Omega^{n+n^{\prime}+m+m^{\prime}+p}}d^{2n}x\,d^{2n^{\prime}}y\,d^{2m}z\,d^{2m^{\prime}}w\,d^{2p}u\prod_{j=1}^{n}f_{j}(x_{j})\!\prod_{j^{\prime}=1}^{n^{\prime}}\widetilde{f}_{j^{\prime}}(y_{j^{\prime}})\!\prod_{l=1}^{m}(-\partial g_{l}(z_{l}))\!\prod_{l^{\prime}=1}^{m^{\prime}}(-\bar{\partial}\widetilde{g}_{l^{\prime}}(w_{l^{\prime}}))

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\prod_{k=1}^{p}\rho(u_{k})\,C_{\varepsilon}(x,\sigma,y,\tau,z,w,u).

Proof.

Let us first prove uniqueness. Write
\Delta_{\varepsilon}
for the difference of two such kernels. Arguing exactly as in the proof of Lemma
4.7
(writing
\rho=\sum_{j=1}^{p}\alpha_{j}\rho_{j}
and differentiating once with respect to each
\alpha_{i}
, one finds that

\displaystyle\int\prod_{j=1}^{n}f_{j}(x_{j})\prod_{j^{\prime}=1}^{n^{\prime}}\widetilde{f}_{j^{\prime}}(y_{j^{\prime}})\prod_{l=1}^{m}(-\partial g_{l}(z_{l}))\prod_{l^{\prime}=1}^{m^{\prime}}(-\bar{\partial}\widetilde{g}_{l^{\prime}}(w_{l^{\prime}}))\prod_{k=1}^{p}\rho_{k}(u_{k})\Delta_{\varepsilon}(x,y,z,w,u)=0.

Integrating by parts in
z_{1}
and using smoothness, we see that

\displaystyle\partial_{z_{1}}\int\prod_{j=1}^{n}f_{j}(x_{j})\prod_{j^{\prime}=1}^{n^{\prime}}\widetilde{f}_{j^{\prime}}(y_{j^{\prime}})\prod_{l=2}^{m}(-\partial g_{l}(z_{l}))\prod_{l^{\prime}=1}^{m^{\prime}}(-\bar{\partial}\widetilde{g}_{l^{\prime}}(w_{l^{\prime}}))\prod_{k=1}^{p}\rho_{k}(u_{k})\Delta_{\varepsilon}(x,y,z,w,u)=0,

where we integrate over all other variables except
z_{1}
. Thus this integral is an antiholomorphic function that vanishes at the boundary and must vanish identically as a function of
z_{1}
. We can continue to argue like this to see that for every
z,w

\displaystyle\int\prod_{j=1}^{n}f_{j}(x_{j})\prod_{j^{\prime}=1}^{n^{\prime}}\widetilde{f}_{j^{\prime}}(y_{j^{\prime}})\prod_{k=1}^{p}\rho_{k}(u_{k})\Delta_{\varepsilon}(x,y,z,w,u)=0.

Thus for each fixed
z,w
,
\Delta_{\varepsilon}(x,y,z,w,u)=0
in the sense of distributions and as we are dealing with a continuous function, it must vanish identically.

It remains to check that
C_{\varepsilon}
satisfies the conditions of the lemma. The fact that
C_{\varepsilon}
vanishes if one of the
z
or
w
variables tends to the boundary follows from the basic fact that
\varphi_{\varepsilon}
vanishes almost surely on the boundary (since
\mathsf{p}_{\Omega}(s,x,x)
vanishes for
x\in\partial\Omega
).

Since we proved in Lemma
5.6
that
C_{\varepsilon}
is a kernel for the Taylor coefficient, it just remains to check that
C_{\varepsilon}
satisfies the symmetry condition. This follows readily from the fact that cumulants are permutation invariant and by making suitable changes of variables in the
S
and
\pi
sums in the definition of
C_{\varepsilon}
.
∎

The point of this uniqueness result is that to identify the
\varepsilon\to 0
limit of the Taylor coefficient as an integral with a GFF correlation function as its kernel, it is sufficient to find some integrability bounds for
C_{\varepsilon}
, and this we can do by representing and bounding the Taylor coefficient with the results of Lemmas
5.1
–
5.4
(as well as some associated bounds). This reasoning allows us to conclude the proof of Theorem
1.5
.

Proof of Theorem
1.5
, part (iii).

As discussed above Lemma
5.5
, we know that the Taylor coefficients of part (iii) of Theorem
1.5
are given as the
\varepsilon\to 0
limits of the corresponding regularized Taylor coefficients. Thus it remains to prove that

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{\partial^{p}}{\partial\mu^{p}}\bigg|_{\mu=0}\!\!\Bigg\langle\prod_{j=1}^{n}\!\mathop{:}e^{i\sigma_{j}\sqrt{\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon}}\mathop{:}(f_{j})\!\prod_{j^{\prime}=1}^{n^{\prime}}\!\!\Big(\!\mathop{:}e^{i\tau_{j^{\prime}}\sqrt{\pi}\varphi_{\varepsilon}}\mathop{:}(\widetilde{f}_{j^{\prime}})\!-\!\mu c_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{f}_{j^{\prime}},\rho)\!\Big)\!\prod_{l=1}^{m}\!\partial\varphi_{\varepsilon}(g_{l})\!\prod_{l^{\prime}=1}^{m^{\prime}}\!\bar{\partial}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{g}_{l^{\prime}})\!\!\Bigg\rangle_{\mathrm{sG}(\mu\rho|\varepsilon,\Omega)}

\displaystyle\kern 5.0pt=\int_{\Omega^{n+n^{\prime}+m+m^{\prime}+p}}d^{2n}x\,d^{2n^{\prime}}y\,d^{2m}z\,d^{2m^{\prime}}w\,d^{2p}u\prod_{j=1}^{n}f_{j}(x_{j})\!\prod_{j^{\prime}=1}^{n^{\prime}}\widetilde{f}_{j^{\prime}}(y_{j^{\prime}})\!\prod_{l=1}^{m}(-\partial g_{l}(z_{l}))\!\prod_{l^{\prime}=1}^{m^{\prime}}(-\bar{\partial}\widetilde{g}_{l^{\prime}}(w_{l^{\prime}}))

\displaystyle\qquad\times\prod_{k=1}^{p}\rho(u_{k})\sum_{S\subset[n^{\prime}]}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S^{\prime}\subset[p]:\\
|S|=|S^{\prime}|\end{subarray}}\sum_{\pi:S\leftrightarrow S^{\prime}}\prod_{s\in S}(-\mathcal{A}(y_{s},u_{\pi(s)}))\Bigg\langle\prod_{j=1}^{n}\mathop{:}e^{i\sigma_{j}\sqrt{\pi}\varphi(x_{j})}\mathop{:}\prod_{l=1}^{m}\varphi(z_{l})\prod_{l^{\prime}=1}^{m^{\prime}}\varphi(w_{l^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\prod_{k^{\prime}\notin S}\big(\mathop{:}e^{i\tau_{k^{\prime}}\sqrt{\pi}\varphi(y_{k^{\prime}})}\mathop{:}\big);\big(\mathop{:}\cos(\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi(u_{\alpha_{1}}))\mathop{:}\big);\dots;\big(\mathop{:}\cos(\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi(u_{\alpha_{p-|S^{\prime}|}}))\mathop{:}\big)\Bigg\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\mathsf{T}},

where
A(x,y)=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}A(x,y|\varepsilon)
and the limiting cumulants are defined from the limiting moments through the moments to cumulants map (and Lemma
2.6
). Part of the statement is that this is an integrable function.

To do this, we note that first of all, we know from Lemma
2.6
and Lemma
5.6
that our integrand converges pointwise almost everywhere to the limiting integrand. Thus our only task is to justify interchanging the order of the limit and the integral – by dominated convergence. To do this, we write the integrand in a different way.

By Lemma
5.1
, we can write our Taylor coefficient as

\frac{\partial^{p}}{\partial\mu^{p}}\bigg|_{\mu=0}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\partial_{s_{j}}|_{s_{j}=0}\prod_{j^{\prime}=1}^{n^{\prime}}\partial_{t_{j^{\prime}}}|_{t_{j^{\prime}}=0}\prod_{l=1}^{m}\partial_{u_{l}}|_{u_{l}=0}\prod_{l^{\prime}=1}^{m^{\prime}}\partial_{v_{l^{\prime}}}|_{v_{l^{\prime}}=0}\bigg[e^{\frac{1}{2}\sum_{l,k=1}^{m}u_{l}u_{k}\left\langle\partial\varphi_{\varepsilon}(g_{l})\partial\varphi_{\varepsilon}(g_{k})\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}}\\
\qquad\times e^{\frac{1}{2}\sum_{l^{\prime},k^{\prime}=1}^{m^{\prime}}v_{l^{\prime}}v_{k^{\prime}}\left\langle\bar{\partial}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{g}_{l^{\prime}})\bar{\partial}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{g}_{k^{\prime}})\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}+\sum_{l=1}^{m}\sum_{l^{\prime}=1}^{m^{\prime}}u_{l}v_{l^{\prime}}\left\langle\partial\varphi_{\varepsilon}(g_{l})\bar{\partial}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{g}_{l^{\prime}})\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}}\\
\times\frac{\mathcal{Z}(\eta_{\varepsilon}|\varepsilon)}{\mathcal{Z}(-\frac{\mu}{2}\rho|\varepsilon)}e^{\frac{1}{2}\int_{(\Omega\times\{\pm 2\})^{2}}d\xi_{1}\,d\xi_{2}\big(\eta_{\varepsilon}(\xi_{1})\eta_{\varepsilon}(\xi_{2})-\frac{\mu^{2}}{4}\rho(x_{1})\rho(x_{2})\big)A(x_{1},x_{2}|\varepsilon)}e^{-\sum_{j^{\prime}=1}^{n^{\prime}}t_{j^{\prime}}\mu c_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{f}_{j^{\prime}},\rho)}\bigg]

with the same choice of
\eta_{\varepsilon}
as in Lemma
5.1
.

By carrying out all of the derivatives, we find a representation for the Taylor coefficient as an integral

\int_{\Omega^{n+n^{\prime}+m+m^{\prime}+p}}d^{2n}x\,d^{2n^{\prime}}y\,d^{2m}z\,d^{2m^{\prime}}w\,d^{2p}u\prod_{j=1}^{n}f_{j}(x_{j})\prod_{j^{\prime}=1}^{n^{\prime}}\widetilde{f}_{j^{\prime}}(y_{j^{\prime}})\prod_{l=1}^{m}(-\partial g_{l}(z_{l}))\prod_{l^{\prime}=1}^{m^{\prime}}(-\bar{\partial}\widetilde{g}_{l^{\prime}}(w_{l^{\prime}}))\\
\times\prod_{k=1}^{p}\rho(u_{k})\,\widetilde{C}_{\varepsilon}(x,\sigma,y,\tau,z,w,u)

where
\widetilde{C}_{\varepsilon}
involves products of terms like
\langle\varphi_{\varepsilon}(z_{l})\varphi_{\varepsilon}(z_{k})\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}(\varepsilon)}
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{j}|\varepsilon)
from (
4.0.10
) (possibly multiplied by factors of continuous functions due to
\eta_{\varepsilon}
) and quantities such as
A(x,y|\varepsilon)\allowbreak\times(e^{i(H_{\varepsilon}(x)-H_{\varepsilon}}-1)
for some
H_{\varepsilon}
which is Lipschitz continuous uniformly in
\varepsilon
. All of these types of quantities are uniformly (in
\varepsilon
) integrable separately (with similar arguments as in the proofs of part (i) of Theorem
1.5
and the proof of Theorem
4.1
) and they do not share any of the integration variables. This means that we have some kernel for the Taylor coefficient that is uniformly integrable. We can then symmetrize this kernel in the
u
-variables (without affecting uniform integrability) and using Lemma
5.7
, we see that
C_{\varepsilon}
is also uniformly integrable. This justifies the use of the dominated convergence theorem and concludes the proof.
∎

6.
Basic Correlations of the Ising Model

Let
\alpha
be a real-valued function. Recall that we work with continuously differentiable complex-valued solutions
f
of the following
\mathbb{R}
-linear equation

(6.0.1)

\bar{\partial}_{z}f=-i\alpha\bar{f},

in the weak sense. We call a function
f
satisfying (
6.0.1
)
(
\alpha
)massive holomorphic
7
7

7

This is more restrictive than the traditional
generalized analytic
or
pseudo-holomorphic
terminology
[
15
,
62
]
, referring to general cases where
\alpha
is not restricted to be real, or has otherwise prescribed phases.
. We also consider
spinors
, i.e., functions with prescribed
-1
multiplicative monodromies around isolated points. Even so, the coefficient
\alpha
is always single-valued.

For notational simplicity, we denote any constant only depending on quantities
q_{1},q_{2},\ldots
by
C(q_{1},q_{2},\ldots)
, with the understanding that they are not all the same. We will continue to use the big-O notation for estimates for which no such uniformity is assumed.

We reserve analysis specific to correlations to Section
6.2
. For now, we recall how the regularity of
\partial_{\bar{z}}
data translates to that of the solution in the form of the following standard result:

Proposition 6.1
.

Let a function
g
be defined in
B_{r}=B_{r}(0)
such that
\left\|g\right\|_{L^{q}(B_{r})}<\infty
for some
q\geq 2
. Then its
Cauchy transform

(6.0.2)

[\mathcal{C}g](z):=[\mathcal{C}_{B_{r}}g](z)=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{B_{r}}\frac{g(w)}{z-w}d^{2}w

has the weak derivative
\partial_{\bar{z}}[\mathcal{C}g]=g
. Furthermore
\mathcal{C}g
and its weak
\partial_{z}
-derivative
\mathcal{S}g
(the
Beurling transform
of
g
) satisfy

(6.0.3)

\left\|\mathcal{C}g\right\|_{L^{q}(B_{r})}\leq 6r\left\|g\right\|_{L^{q}(B_{r})},\quad\text{and}\quad\left\|\mathcal{S}g\right\|_{L^{q}(B_{r})}\leq\mathbf{S}_{q}\left\|g\right\|_{L^{q}(B_{r})},

for some constant
\mathbf{S}_{q}
. If
q>2
, we have the Hölder seminorm bound

(6.0.4)

\displaystyle\left\|\mathcal{C}g\right\|_{C^{1-\frac{2}{q}}(B_{r})}:=\sup_{x,y\in B_{r},x\neq y}\frac{|\mathcal{C}g(x)-\mathcal{C}g(y)|}{|x-y|^{1-\frac{2}{q}}}\leq C(q)\left\|g\right\|_{L^{q}(B_{r})}.

If in addition
g
is locally
\beta
-Hölder continuous,
then
\mathcal{C}g
is continuously differentiable with derivatives bounded pointwise in the smaller disk
B_{r/4}
, with estimate

(6.0.5)

\left\|g\right\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{r/4})}+\left\|\mathcal{S}g\right\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{r/4})}\leq C({\beta})\left(\left\|g\right\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{r})}+r^{\beta}\left\|g\right\|_{C^{\beta}(B_{r})}\right).

Proof.

The
L^{q}
and
C^{\alpha}
-estimates are exactly
[
2
, (4.16), Theorems 4.3.12, 4.3.13, 4.5.3]
. Differentiability follows from
[
2
, Theorem 4.7.1]
.

For the pointwise bound, we need to be mindful of the discontinuity at the boundary. Fix a bump function
\rho
which is identically
1
in
B_{3r/4}
,
0
outside of
B_{r}
, and differentiable with
|\nabla\rho|\leq\frac{16}{r}
. Then,
[
2
, (4.123)]
yields

\max_{B_{r/4}}|\mathcal{S}g|-\min_{B_{r/4}}|\mathcal{S}g|\leq r^{\beta}\left\|\mathcal{S}[\rho g]\right\|_{C^{\beta}(B_{r})}\leq C({\beta})\left({r^{\beta}}\left\|g\right\|_{C^{\beta}(B_{r})}+{\left\|g\right\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{r})}}\right).

On the other hand, by (
6.0.3
),

\min_{B_{r/4}}\left|\mathcal{S}g\right|\leq Cr^{-1}\left\|\mathcal{S}g\right\|_{L^{2}(B_{r})}\leq Cr^{-1}\left\|g\right\|_{L^{2}(B_{r})}\leq C\left\|g\right\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{r})}

Then it is straightforward to derive (
6.0.5
).
∎

Corollary 6.2
.

Suppose
\partial_{\bar{z}}g_{1}=g_{2}
almost everywhere in
B_{r}
for (single-valued) functions
g_{1},g_{2}
such that
g_{1}\in L^{2}(B_{r})
and
g_{2}\in L^{q}(B_{r})
for some
q>2
. Then

\displaystyle\|g_{1}\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{r/2})}

\displaystyle\leq\Big(C(q)r^{1-\frac{2}{q}}\|g_{2}\|_{L^{q}(B_{r})}+C{\|g_{1}\|_{L^{\infty}(\partial B_{3r/4})}}\Big),\qquad\text{and}

\displaystyle\|g_{1}\|_{C^{1-\frac{2}{q}}(B_{r/2})}

\displaystyle\leq\Big(C(q)\|g_{2}\|_{L^{q}(B_{r})}+Cr^{-\left(1-\frac{2}{q}\right)}{\|g_{1}\|_{L^{\infty}(\partial B_{3r/4})}}\Big).

Proof.

Consider
\mathcal{C}g_{2}^{\dagger}:=\mathcal{C}g_{2}-\mathcal{C}g_{2}(0)
, which is Hölder continuous by (
6.0.4
) and in fact
|\mathcal{C}g_{2}^{\dagger}|\leq r^{1-\frac{2}{q}}\|\mathcal{C}g_{2}\|_{C^{1-\frac{2}{q}}(B_{r})}
in
B_{r}
. Then the holomorphic function
g_{1}-\mathcal{C}g_{2}^{\dagger}
may be estimated separately using Cauchy’s formula: showing the second bound without loss of generality,

\displaystyle g_{1}(z)-g_{1}(z^{\prime})=\mathcal{C}g_{2}^{\dagger}(z)-\mathcal{C}g_{2}^{\dagger}(z^{\prime})+\oint_{\partial B_{3r/4}}\frac{dw}{2\pi i}\frac{z-z^{\prime}}{(w-z)(w-z^{\prime})}(g_{1}(w)-\mathcal{C}g_{2}^{\dagger}(w)),

so we have for
z,z^{\prime}\in B_{r/2}

\frac{|g_{1}(z)-g_{1}(z^{\prime})|}{|z-z^{\prime}|^{1-\frac{2}{q}}}\leq\|\mathcal{C}g_{2}\|_{C^{1-\frac{2}{q}}(B_{r})}+\frac{16|z-z^{\prime}|^{\frac{2}{q}}}{r}\Big(\|g_{1}\|_{L^{\infty}(\partial B_{3r/4})}+r^{1-\frac{2}{q}}\|\mathcal{C}g_{2}\|_{C^{1-\frac{2}{q}}(B_{r})}\Big).

By Proposition
6.1
,

\|g_{1}\|_{C^{1-\frac{2}{q}}(B_{r/2})}\leq C(q)\|g_{2}\|_{L^{q}(B_{r})}+Cr^{-(1-\frac{2}{q})}\|g_{1}\|_{L^{\infty}(\partial B_{3r/4})}.

∎

Remark 6.3
.

We note two applications of the above theorems directly in the massive holomorphic setup:

•

A locally bounded function
f
satisfying (
6.0.1
) with locally smooth data
\alpha
is continuously differentiable: this follows from bootstrapping local boundedness to local Hölder continuity, then differentiability, as above. A simple corollary is that a locally bounded function
f
satisfying (
6.0.1
) classically in a domain except at some isolated points in fact satisfies it at those points as well.

•

Moreover, a family
\{f_{\iota}\}_{\iota\in I}
of such functions satisfying
|f_{\iota}|\leq C
in a domain
\Omega
is equicontinuous in any compact subset, and therefore by Arzelà-Ascoli has a subsequence converging uniformly on compact subsets (in particular, the limit is
\alpha
-massive holomorphic). We may diagonalize to get convergence in all of
\Omega
.

6.1.
Existence and Uniqueness

6.1.1.
Definitions of the correlations
