As mathematical objects, the Ising correlations to be constructed are functions of the position of each inserted field, which are putative limits of suitably renormalized correlations in the corresponding discrete massive model. The
-1
multiplicative monodromy of fermions around spins and disorders requires some care: we will first fix the positions of spins and disorder insertions
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}
in the original domain
\Omega
then consider the correlations as functions of the fermion insertion(s)
z_{j}
which is multiplied by
-1
whenever
z_{j}
moves around any
a_{j^{\prime}}
. In other words, they are single valued functions as long as
z_{j}
are taken on the
double cover
of
\Omega\setminus\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\}
.

In addition, the sign of the correlation is uniquely determined as soon as the branch of individual square roots
\sqrt{z-a_{j}}
are fixed near
a_{j}
(i.e.
\sqrt{z-a_{j}}
has been defined on the lift of a punctured neighborhood of
a_{j}
in
\Omega
to the double cover).

We first define a distinguished set of correlations called
basic correlations
.

Definition 6.4
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{C}
be a bounded simply connected domain and
\alpha
a real-valued function on
\Omega
(see Remark
6.6
for regularity assumptions). We define the
basic correlations of the
\alpha
-massive Ising model on
\Omega
with plus boundary condition
as follows.

For distinct points
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in\Omega
and
z,w\in\Omega\setminus\{a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{n}\}
, we consider correlations involving spins
\sigma
, disorder
\mu
, and fermion
\psi
, specifically any of the following combinations:

•

(disorder-fermion case) where
\hat{k}
signifies the absence of the
k
-th spin,

f(z):=\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hat{k}}}{{\cdots}}\sigma_{a_{n}}\mu_{a_{k}}\psi_{z}\rangle_{\alpha;\Omega}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{\alpha;\Omega}}=\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hat{k}}}{{\cdots}}\sigma_{a_{n}}\mu_{a_{k}}\psi_{z}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{\alpha}},

or

•

(fermion-fermion case) where
\eta
is any complex constant,

f(z):=\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}{\cdots}\sigma_{a_{n}}\psi_{z}\psi^{[\eta]}_{w}\rangle_{\alpha;\Omega}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{\alpha;\Omega}}=\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}{\cdots}\sigma_{a_{n}}\psi_{z}\psi^{[\eta]}_{w}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{\alpha}}.

Then the correlation
f(z)
, as a function of the fermion insertion point
z
, is defined as the unique spinor having
-1
multiplicative monodromy around each of
a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{n}
(i.e.
f
is a single-valued function on the double cover of
\Omega\setminus\{a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{n}\}
), massive holomorphic in
\Omega
away from other insertions, having the asymptotics:

(S)

near a spin insertion at
a\in\Omega
, it has a
spin-type asymptotic
, i.e., there is
b=b(a)=b(a;f)\in\mathbb{R}
such that
f(z)=be^{i\pi/4}(z-a)^{-1/2}+o(|z-a|^{-1/2})
;

(D)

near a disorder insertion at
a^{\prime}\in\Omega
, it has a
disorder-type asymptotic
, i.e.,
f(z)=-e^{-i\pi/4}(z-a^{\prime})^{-1/2}+o(|z-a^{\prime}|^{-1/2})
;

(F)

near a fermion insertion at
w\in\Omega
, it has a
fermion-type asymptotic
, i.e.,
f(z)=\bar{\eta}(z-w)^{-1}+o(|z-w|^{-1})
;

and extending continuously to each
\zeta\in\partial\Omega
where it satisfies the boundary condition

(RH)

\overline{f(\zeta)}=\tau_{\zeta}f(\zeta),\mbox{ or equivalently }f^{2}(\zeta)\in\tau_{\zeta}^{-1}\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0},

where
\tau_{\zeta}
is the unit complex number in the direction of the tangent at
\zeta
to
\partial\Omega
, oriented counterclockwise.

The asymptotics (D), (S), and (F) are the same as expected from the following operator product expansions (OPEs): as
z\to a,w
, we may replace within correlations

(6.1.1)

\sigma_{a}\psi_{z}\sim e^{i\pi/4}\mu_{a}(z-a)^{-1/2};\quad\mu_{a}\psi_{z}\sim-e^{-i\pi/4}\sigma_{a}(z-a)^{-1/2};\quad\psi_{z}\psi_{w}^{[\eta]}\sim\bar{\eta}(z-w)^{-1},

which is identical to the critical case
[
21
]
; the mass affects the correction terms, see
[
23
]
.

Remark 6.5
.

The defining properties of the basic correlations are compatible with conformal covariance, as physically expected. Since
\varphi^{\prime}(\zeta)\tau_{\zeta}=|\varphi^{\prime}(\zeta)|\tau_{\varphi(\zeta)}
, for any
f
satisfying (RH) on
\partial\Omega
and a conformal map
\varphi:\Omega^{\prime}\to\Omega
, the pullback
\varphi^{*}f:=(f\circ\varphi)\cdot(\varphi^{\prime})^{1/2}
also satisfies (RH) on
\partial\Omega^{\prime}
. Going further, it is straightforward to verify that the asymptotics (S), (D), and (F) show covariance which is consistent with the physical transformation rules:

\sigma_{\Omega^{\prime}}\mapsto(\sigma_{\Omega}\circ\varphi)\cdot|\varphi^{\prime}|^{1/8},\quad\mu_{\Omega^{\prime}}\mapsto(\mu_{\Omega}\circ\varphi)\cdot|\varphi^{\prime}|^{1/8},\quad\psi_{\Omega^{\prime}}\mapsto(\psi_{\Omega}\circ\varphi)\cdot(\varphi^{\prime})^{1/2},\quad\psi_{\Omega^{\prime}}^{[\eta]}\mapsto\psi_{\Omega}^{[\eta{(\varphi^{\prime})^{1/2}}]}\circ\varphi.

Under such transformations, the mass itself transforms covariantly:

\partial_{\bar{z}}\left[\varphi^{*}f\right]=\left((\partial_{\bar{z}}f)\circ\varphi\right)\cdot\overline{\varphi^{\prime}}\sqrt{\varphi^{\prime}}=\left[-i(\alpha\circ\varphi)|{\varphi^{\prime}}|\right]\overline{\varphi^{*}f}\text{, or }\alpha\mapsto(\alpha\circ\varphi)|\varphi^{\prime}|,

i.e.,
\varphi^{*}f
is
(\alpha\circ\varphi)|\varphi^{\prime}|
-massive holomorphic. That is, the basic correlations (and all correlations, as we highlight below) remain unchanged under conformal domain transformations if we substitute all terms within correlations according to the above rules.

Remark 6.6
.

For the full set of Ising correlations, we only need to consider
\alpha
which is smooth up to the boundary of a smooth domain
\Omega
.

Nonetheless, we may equivalently define the basic correlations in the case of less smooth
\Omega
using the pull-back on
\mathbb{D}
and the conformal covariance rules in Remark
6.5
; see also (
6.1.6
) and Remark
6.12
for more covariance statements. While less straightforward, the advantage of this approach is that we may define (RH) on cases where
\partial\Omega
does not admit tangents. This is helpful since the basic correlations also serve as concrete massive holomorphic functions or spinors which happen to be basic ingredients in the relevant analysis.

In fact, for basic correlations, we only assume that
\alpha
is locally smooth and continuous up to the boundary of a domain
\Omega
with piecewise smooth and Lipschitz boundary, stating any necessary additional regularity in the individual results (see the beginning of Section
6.2
).

In subsequent sections, we show existence and uniqueness of these basic correlations, as well as their properties and estimates. Specifically:

•

We show any correlation satisfying the properties outlined above exists and is unique in Section
6.1.3
.

•

We give the
L^{p}
-estimate, crucial in the series expansion process, in Corollary
6.25
.

•

We show the correlations are smooth with respect to local movements of the insertions in Proposition
6.30
.

The real fermions and complex fermions are related as follows: within correlations,

(6.1.2)

\psi^{[\eta]}=\frac{1}{2}(\bar{\eta}\psi+\eta\psi^{*})\text{ is real, equivalently }\psi=\bar{\eta}^{-1}\psi^{[\eta]}+i\bar{\eta}^{-1}\psi^{[i\eta]}\text{ and }\overline{\psi}=\psi^{*}.

That is, under complex conjugation of any correlation, complex fermions
\psi,\psi^{*}
turn into each other whereas other fields (spin, disorder, energy, and real fermion) stay the same. Any two insertions among fermions and/or disorders anticommute among themselves; otherwise the correlations do not depend on the order of the insertions.

To insert more fermions, we use a Pfaffian decomposition (
6.1.15
), which may be taken either as a definition or a consequence of requiring anticommutation of fermions and the OPE behavior (
6.1.1
) whenever two fermion insertions are merged: see Proposition
6.16
. To insert more disorders, we insert spins at those points, insert as many fermions, and take fermions to spins and use (
6.1.1
). See Proposition
6.15
for compatibility of such a procedure with the above definitions.

Then, the only remaining nontrivial construction at this stage, apart from energy which will be introduced in Section
7.3
, is that of pure spin correlations (for this, we do assume
\alpha
is smooth up to the boundary). We will show in Lemma
6.26
(the asymptotic for
f^{\dagger 1}
with
\nu=-1/2
,
o_{-1/2}=1
) that, in fact, the asymptotic (D) in Definition
6.4
may be expanded further:

(6.1.3)

\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hat{k}}}{{\cdots}}\sigma_{a_{n}}\mu_{a_{k}}\psi_{z}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{\alpha}}=-\frac{e^{-i\pi/4}e^{2\alpha(a_{k})|z-a_{k}|}}{\sqrt{z-a_{k}}}\left(1+2\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{(\alpha)}(a_{k})(z-a_{k})+o(|z-a_{k}|)\right),

which also defines the coefficient
\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{(\alpha)}(a_{k})
for the form
\mathrm{Re}\left[\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{(\alpha)}(a_{k})da_{k}\right]
, which is a closed and conformally covariant differential form by Corollary
6.33
. Then we recover the massive pure spin correlations by,

(6.1.4)

\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{0}}=\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{0}}\exp\bigg\{\mathrm{Re}\bigg[\int_{a_{b}\in\partial\Omega}^{a_{1}}\Big(\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{(\alpha)}(a_{1}^{\prime})-\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{(0)}(a_{1}^{\prime})\Big)da_{1}^{\prime}\bigg]\bigg\},

where
{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{0}}
is the scaling limit of the critical Ising spin correlations. We show in Section
6.3.3
that this ratio is well-defined, i.e. the result is independent of how we do the integration. We state well-definedness of the massive spin correlation
\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{\alpha}
in Proposition
6.44
.

6.1.2.
Analysis of massive holomorphic functions

A central fact about the local and global properties of massive holomorphic functions, going back to Bers and Vekua
[
15
,
62
]
, is the following
similarity principle
.

Theorem 6.7
.

Suppose
f
satisfies (
6.0.1
), possibly with isolated points of singularity or monodromy, in
\mathbb{D}
with
\alpha\in L^{q}(\mathbb{D})
for some
q\geq 2
. Then there exists a (single-valued) function
s
such that
\underline{f}:=e^{-s}f
is holomorphic at all points where
f
is regular. Conversely, given a holomorphic
\underline{f}
, possibly with isolated points of singularity or monodromy, there exists an
s
such that
f=e^{s}\underline{f}
satisfies (
6.0.1
) on all points where
f
is regular.

In both cases,
s
is
\gamma_{q}=\left(1-\frac{2}{q}\right)
-Hölder continuous on
\overline{\mathbb{D}}
if
q>2
, and
s
is unique, as long as we require
\mathrm{Im}s\equiv 0
on
\partial\mathbb{D}
and
\mathrm{Re}s(z_{0})=0
at some point
z_{0}\in\mathbb{D}
. We have the following quantitative bounds

(6.1.5)

\|s\|_{W^{1,q}(\mathbb{D})}\leq C\|\alpha\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{D})}\qquad\text{ and},\text{ if }q>2\text{, }\quad\|s\|_{C^{\gamma_{q}}(\mathbb{D})}\leq C\|\alpha\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{D})}.

Under this correspondence,
\underline{f}
is called the
holomorphic part
of
f
.

Proof.

See, e.g.,
[
4
, Lemma 3.1, Theorem 4.1]
. Note carefully that the proofs of these results only concern the ratios (in their notation)
\bar{w}/w,\bar{F}/F
, which are single-valued and of unit norm even if
w,F
are double-valued or have a singularity: these results apply to our case as well. The Hölder estimate comes directly from Morrey’s inequality.
∎

We define notions such as the order of zeros and poles and meromorphicity for
f
identical to those of
\underline{f}
. Since we may pull-back massive holomorphic functions on any simply connected domain to
\mathbb{D}
as in Remark
6.5
, we also define the notion of
holomorphic part
on general
\Omega
. It is natural to transform the holomorphic part covariantly, which means
s
is invariant:

(6.1.6)

s\mapsto s\circ\varphi;\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\underline{f}\mapsto(\underline{f}\circ\varphi)\cdot(\varphi^{\prime})^{1/2}.

Remark 6.8
.

We emphasize already that the holomorphic part coming from a massive fermion correlation
\left<\mathcal{O}\psi_{z}\right>_{\alpha}
, etc., is typically
not
the corresponding critical correlation
\left<\mathcal{O}\psi_{z}\right>_{0}
, etc., even up to a real scalar multiple; the reason is that the delicately prescribed phases in Definition
6.4
are disrupted by
e^{s}
. Nevertheless, see also Lemma
6.22
.

Remark 6.9
.

The Hölder estimate for the factor
e^{s}
implies that, around any pole or regular point
a\in\mathbb{C}
, there exists a unique
c\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
such that

(6.1.7)

f(z)=c(z-a)^{n}+O(|z-a|^{n+\varepsilon})\mbox{ as }z\to a,

for some
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
. Still, note that if
f_{1},f_{2}
are two solutions both satisfying (
6.1.7
) with common
c,n
, their difference is a solution which satisfies as
z\to a

(6.1.8)

f_{1}(z)-f_{2}(z)=O(|z-a|^{n+\varepsilon})\mbox{, and thus }f_{1}(z)-f_{2}(z)\sim c^{\prime}(z-a)^{n+1}\mbox{ for some }c^{\prime}\in\mathbb{C}.

This is the basis for the so-called
formal power series expansion
, cf.
[
15
,
62
]
.

Similarly, if
f
branches around
a
, so does
\underline{f}
and it may be expressed locally in terms of half-integer powers of
(z-a)
. If there are a finite number of negative powers (as there will be in this article),
f
satisfies above asymptotics with the integer
n
replaced by a half-integer
\nu
.

Using a version of Green’s theorem in complex variables (Green-Riemann’s theorem) on
f^{2}
, we see that, around any closed contour
\gamma
and its interior
D
where
f
satisfies (
6.0.1
),

(6.1.9)

\oint_{\gamma}f^{2}dz=2i\int_{D}\bar{\partial}_{z}f^{2}(z)d^{2}z=4\int_{D}\alpha(z)|f(z)|^{2}d^{2}z\in\mathbb{R}.

This suggests that locally we may define a function
h(z)=\mathrm{Im}[\int_{z_{0}}^{z}f^{2}dz]
henceforth called a
square integral
, where the line integral is done along any contour to
z
from an arbitrary fixed point
z_{0}
. The choice of
z_{0}
amounts to a choice of a global additive constant, and the resulting integral is invariant under deformations of the integration contour that do not cross a singularity.

Note also that (
6.1.9
) should always be applied on
planar
(i.e. not on double cover) subsets
D\subset\Omega
, since
f^{2}
is always single-valued. In other words, the definition
h(z)=\mathrm{Im}[\int_{z_{0}}^{z}f^{2}dz]
does not depend on the branch choice of
f
.

The following comparison principle for
h=\mathrm{Im}[\int f^{2}dz]
is standard for quasi-linear elliptic PDEs such as one obeyed by
h
(see Remark
6.12
). We give a ‘constructive’ proof which clearly shows that it is valid as long as the two functions have identical singularities.

Lemma 6.10
.

Suppose
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
satisfy (
6.0.1
) on a punctured neighborhood of
a
, possibly both branching at
a
but in any case such that
h_{1}=\mathrm{Im}[\int f_{1}^{2}dz]
and
h_{2}=\mathrm{Im}[\int f_{2}^{2}dz]
are single-valued in the punctured neighborhood. Assume in addition that
f_{1}^{2}-f_{2}^{2}
extends continuously to
a
.

Then
h_{1}-h_{2}
extends continuously to
a
. If
h_{1}-h_{2}
attains a local extremum at
a
, then
h_{1}-h_{2}
is a constant or, equivalently,
f_{1}^{2}\equiv f_{2}^{2}
.

Proof.

Assume
f_{1}-f_{2}
and
f_{1}+f_{2}
are not identically zero (if either is,
h_{1}-h_{2}
extends to
a
trivially). Applying (
6.1.7
), we may expand near
a
,

\displaystyle f_{1}(z)-f_{2}(z)

\displaystyle=c_{1}(z-a)^{r_{1}}+O(|z-a|^{r_{1}+\varepsilon})\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle f_{1}(z)+f_{2}(z)

\displaystyle=c_{2}(z-a)^{r_{2}}+O(|z-a|^{r_{2}+\varepsilon}),

where
c_{1},c_{2}\neq 0
and, say,
r_{1}\geq r_{2}
.
Since
(f_{1}^{2}-f_{2}^{2})(z)=c_{1}c_{2}(z-a)^{r_{1}+r_{2}}+O(|z-a|^{r_{1}+r_{2}+\varepsilon})
extends to
a
continuously, we must have
r_{1}+r_{2}\geq 0
.

Then it is clear that
h_{1}-h_{2}
extends continuously to
a
: integrate
f_{1}^{2}-f_{2}^{2}
along any line to get the value of
(h_{1}-h_{2})(a)
, which is well-defined since the oscillation of
h_{1}-h_{2}
on small circles around
a
must be small.

Moreover,
a
cannot be a local extremum. To see this, for simplicity assume
a=0
and
c_{1}c_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
(modifying
\theta_{0}
below if necessary), then it is easy to see that for small
t>0
, we have

(h_{1}-h_{2})(e^{i\theta_{0}}t)-(h_{1}-h_{2})(0)=\mathrm{Im}\bigg[\int_{0}^{t}e^{i(r_{1}+r_{2}+1)\theta_{0}}x^{r_{1}+r_{2}}\big(c_{1}c_{2}+O(t^{\varepsilon})\big)dx\bigg],

such that, for
t\ll(c_{1}c_{2})^{1/\varepsilon}
, the values of
h_{1}-h_{2}
at
z=e^{i\theta_{0}}t
are respectively strictly greater or smaller than its value at
z=0
for
\theta_{0}=\frac{\pi}{2(r_{1}+r_{2}+1)},-\frac{\pi}{2(r_{1}+r_{2}+1)}
.
∎

Lemma 6.11
.

Let
f
satisfy (
6.0.1
) on the double cover of
\Omega\setminus\{a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{n}\}
. Suppose the boundary condition (RH) holds and the asympotic of
f
is of the type (S) from Definition
6.4
near each
a_{j}
, if any. Then
f\equiv 0
.

Proof.

Let
b_{j}=b(a_{j})\in\mathbb{R}
. Consider
h=\mathrm{Im}[\int f^{2}dz]
. Since

f^{2}(z)=ib_{j}^{2}(z-a_{j})^{-1}+O(|z-a_{j}|^{-1+\varepsilon}),

we see that
h(z)=b_{j}^{2}\log|z-a_{j}|+O(1)
near
z=a_{j}
.

Integrating
f^{2}dz
counterclockwise along
\partial\Omega
(i.e. in the direction of
\tau_{z}
), the boundary condition (RH) implies that
h
is constant along
\partial\Omega
, so let us fix the additive constant such that
h\equiv 0
on
\partial\Omega
. In addition, if any
f(\zeta)\neq 0
on
\zeta\in\partial\Omega
, integrating inwards from a boundary point
\zeta\in\partial\Omega
perpendicular to
\partial\Omega
(i.e. in the direction of
i\tau_{\zeta}
), the same boundary condition (RH) would imply that
h>0
at points near
\zeta
.

However, by Lemma
6.10
,
h
can only have a local extremum at one of the
a_{j}
with
b_{j}\neq 0
, where
h\to-\infty
. Therefore,
\max_{\Omega\setminus\{a_{1}\ldots,a_{n}\}}h
has to be attained on
\partial\Omega
, where
h\equiv 0
. Therefore,
f\equiv 0
at all points on
\partial\Omega
. Then the holomorphic part
\underline{f}\equiv 0
on
\partial\Omega
as well. So, the meromorphic function
\underline{f}^{2}
has to be identically zero since it vanishes on a boundary segment of non-zero length, and therefore
f\equiv 0
on
\Omega
.
∎

Remark 6.12
.

We collect here some properties of the square integral
h=\mathrm{Im}[\int f^{2}dz]
, easily verifiable or used in the proof above.

•

Under the conformal covariance
f\mapsto(f\circ\varphi)\cdot(\varphi^{\prime})^{1/2}
, we have invariance
h\mapsto h\circ\varphi
. This is a direct consequence of the change of variables formula for line integrals.

•

h
satisfies the quasi-linear PDE

\Delta h=4\mathrm{Im}\bigg[\partial_{\bar{z}}\partial_{z}\int f^{2}dz\bigg]=-4\alpha|f|^{2}=-4\alpha|\nabla h|.

•

In view of Theorem
6.7
, the asymptotics (S) and (D) from Definition
6.4
are simply

(S)

h
is bounded above in a neighborhood of
a
(which forces it to satisfy an asymptotic of the form
h(z)=b^{2}\log|z-a|+O(1)
for some
b\in\mathbb{R}
);

(D)

h(z)=-\log|z-a|+O(1)
near
a
.

•

The boundary condition (RH) for
f
is equivalent to requiring that
h=\mathrm{Im}[\int f^{2}dz]
is constant along the boundary and has
non-positive outer normal derivative
.

6.1.3.
The space
V_{\Omega}^{\alpha}

Let distinct points
a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{n};w_{1},\ldots,w_{n^{\prime}}\in\Omega
be given for
n,n^{\prime}\geq 0
. Define the vector space
V_{\Omega}^{\alpha}=V_{\Omega}^{\alpha}(a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{n};w_{1},\ldots,w_{n^{\prime}})
as the real vector space of spinors
f
on
\Omega
whose monodromies are at
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}
, satisfy (
6.0.1
) at each point away from
a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{n},w_{1},\ldots,w_{n^{\prime}}
and (RH) on
\partial\Omega
, and have controlled rates of blow-up near those singularities:

(6.1.10)

\begin{split}&f(z)(z-a_{j})^{\frac{1}{2}}\text{ is bounded near each $a_{j}$, and }\\
&f(z)(z-w_{j^{\prime}})\,\text{ is bounded near $w_{j^{\prime}}$}.\end{split}

Then, thanks to Theorem
6.7
, we may define the following quantities for each element
f\in V_{\Omega}^{\alpha}
:

(6.1.11)

\beta({a_{j}})=\beta({a_{j}};f):=\lim_{z\to a_{j}}-e^{i\pi/4}\sqrt{z-a_{j}}f(z);

(6.1.12)

\gamma(w_{j^{\prime}})=\gamma({w_{j^{\prime}}};f):=\lim_{z\to w}(z-w_{j^{\prime}})f(z).

There exists a linear map
V_{\Omega}^{\alpha}\to\mathbb{R}^{n+2n^{\prime}}
given by

(6.1.13)

f\mapsto\big(\mathrm{Re}[\beta({a_{1}})],\ldots,\mathrm{Re}[\beta({a_{n}})];\,\mathrm{Re}[\gamma({w_{1}})],\mathrm{Im}[\gamma({w_{1}})],\ldots,\mathrm{Re}[\gamma({w_{n^{\prime}}})],\mathrm{Im}[\gamma({w_{n^{\prime}}})]\big).

This map is injective by Lemma
6.11
: any
f
in the kernel is bounded near
w_{1},\ldots,w_{n^{\prime}}
by Remark
6.9
, and therefore is massive holomorphic at those points by the first point of Remark
6.3
. So we have
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}V_{\Omega}^{\alpha}\leq n+2n^{\prime}
, and we will show that in fact the dimension is exactly
n+2n^{\prime}
in the following.

Proposition 6.13
.

The map (
6.1.13
) is surjective. In other words,
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}V_{\Omega}^{\alpha}=n+2n^{\prime}
.

Proof.

We already have existence in the critical case, i.e.
\alpha(z)\equiv 0
. This was proven in
[
21
, Section 7]
, but the actual construction will be useful for the massive case as well, so we now recall its idea.

Let us briefly consider the case of the upper half plane
\mathbb{H}
. Given
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n},w_{1},\ldots,w_{n^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{H}
, consider the real vector space
V_{\mathbb{H}}^{0}
whose elements are holomorphic spinors of the type

\underline{f}_{\mathbb{H}}(z)=\frac{r_{n+2n^{\prime}-1}z^{n+2n^{\prime}-1}+\cdots+r_{1}z+r_{0}}{(z-w_{1})(z-\overline{w_{1}})\cdots(z-w_{n^{\prime}})(z-\overline{w_{n^{\prime}}})\sqrt{(z-a_{1})(z-\overline{a_{1}})\cdots(z-a_{n})(z-\overline{a_{n}})}}

for real numbers
r_{0},\ldots,r_{n+2n^{\prime}-1}
. Note that, aside from the complication at infinity,
V_{\mathbb{H}}^{0}
is a fitting name: any
\underline{f}_{\mathbb{H}}\in V_{\mathbb{H}}^{0}
has the necessary bulk asymptotics and satisfies (RH) on
\partial\mathbb{H}=\mathbb{R}
, i.e.
\underline{f}_{\mathbb{H}}\in\mathbb{R}
there.

Now, suppose a bounded
\Omega
and
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n},w_{1},\ldots,w_{n^{\prime}}\in\Omega
are given. Then, for a fixed Riemann map
\varphi:\Omega\to\mathbb{H}
, we may consider spinors of the form
\varphi^{*}\underline{f}_{\mathbb{H}}=(\underline{f}_{\mathbb{H}}\circ\varphi)\cdot\sqrt{\varphi^{\prime}}
, where
\underline{f}_{\mathbb{H}}\in V_{\mathbb{H}}^{0}\left(\varphi(a_{1}),\ldots,\varphi(a_{n});\varphi(w_{1}),\ldots,\varphi(w_{n^{\prime}})\right)
. As in Remark
6.5
, it is simple to check that each
\varphi^{*}\underline{f}_{\mathbb{H}}
belongs to
V_{\Omega}^{0}
, except possibly for the validity of (RH) at
\varphi^{-1}(\infty)
.

The space
V_{\mathbb{H}}^{0}
is graded by the order of decay at infinity, i.e. each
\underline{f}_{\mathbb{H}}
must decay at one rate among
z^{-1},\ldots,z^{-n-2n^{\prime}}
. Since
\varphi
must have a order
1
boundary pole at
\varphi^{-1}(\infty)
(easily seen by an explicit Möbius map
\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{H}
or otherwise), the grades respectively correspond to
\varphi^{*}\underline{f}_{\mathbb{H}}
having a zero at
\varphi^{-1}(\infty)
of order
0,\ldots,n+2n^{\prime}-1
(in particular,
\varphi^{*}\underline{f}_{\mathbb{H}}
is continuous at
\varphi^{-1}(\infty)
, and thus satisfies (RH) there). Since each
\varphi^{*}\underline{f}_{\mathbb{H}}\in V_{\Omega}^{0}
, we have
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}V_{\Omega}^{0}=n+2n^{\prime}
, graded by the order of zero at
\varphi^{-1}(\infty)
.

Then, fix a basis
v_{1},\ldots,v_{n+2n^{\prime}}\in V_{\Omega}^{0}
respectively representing each grade, and use Theorem
6.7
to get massive spinors
v^{\prime}_{1}=e^{s_{1}}v_{1},\ldots,v^{\prime}_{n+2n^{\prime}}=e^{s_{n+2n^{\prime}}}v_{n+2n^{\prime}}\in V_{\Omega}^{\alpha}
. These
(n+2n^{\prime})
spinors are linearly independent and therefore span
V_{\Omega}^{\alpha}
, since nonvanishing factors
e^{s_{1}},\ldots,e^{s_{n+2n^{\prime}}}
cannot modify the order of zero at
\varphi^{-1}(\infty)
. So
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}V_{\Omega}^{\alpha}=n+2n^{\prime}
.
∎

Corollary 6.14
.

The correlations defined in Definition
6.4
uniquely exist.

Proof.

Uniqueness was discussed above. We already have the existence of the fermion-fermion correlations, simply by considering the preimage of vectors of the type
(0,\ldots,0;\mathrm{Re}[\eta],-\mathrm{Im}[\eta])
under the map (
6.1.13
). For the disorder-fermion correlations, without loss of generality consider the preimage
f
of the vector
(1,0,\ldots,0;)
with
n^{\prime}=0
. We claim
f
is precisely
\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{2}}{\cdots}\sigma_{a_{n}}\mu_{a_{1}}\psi_{z}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{\alpha}}
. We only need to check that in this case
\mathrm{Im}[\beta({a_{1}};f)]=0
, i.e.,
\beta({a_{1}};f)=1
.

Cutting out small circles
B_{r}(a_{1})\cup\cdots\cup B_{r}(a_{n})=:B
from
\Omega
and applying (
6.1.9
),

\oint_{\partial\Omega}f^{2}dz-\oint_{\partial B_{r}(a_{1})}f^{2}dz-\cdots-\oint_{\partial B_{r}(a_{n})}f^{2}dz=2i\int_{\Omega\setminus B}\partial_{\bar{z}}f^{2}d^{2}z=4\int_{\Omega\setminus B}\alpha|f|^{2}d^{2}z\in\mathbb{R}.

But the boundary condition (RH) also implies that
\oint_{\partial\Omega}f^{2}dz\in\mathbb{R}
. Taking imaginary parts of both sides, and noting that as
r\to 0
,
\oint_{\partial B_{r}(a_{j})}f^{2}dz\to 2\pi\beta({a_{j}})^{2}
, we finally have

2\mathrm{Im}[\beta({a_{1}})]=2\mathrm{Im}[\beta({a_{1}})]\cdot\mathrm{Re}[\beta({a_{1}})]=\mathrm{Im}\left[\beta({a_{1}})^{2}\right]=\mathrm{Im}[-\beta({a_{2}})^{2}-\cdots-\beta({a_{n}})^{2}]=0,

since
\beta({a_{2}}),\ldots,\beta({a_{n}})\in i\mathbb{R}
.
∎

We will make use of calculations of this type many more times, so it will be useful to have a notation for it, generally as a bilinear form. For spinors
f,g
satisfying (
6.0.1
) possibly with distinct masses
\alpha_{f},\alpha_{g}
on
\Omega\setminus\left(B_{\varepsilon}(a_{1})\cup\cdots\cup B_{\varepsilon}(a_{n})\cup B_{\varepsilon}(w_{1})\cup\cdots\cup B_{\varepsilon}(w_{n^{\prime}})\right)=:\Omega\setminus B_{\varepsilon}
and branching around
B_{\varepsilon}(a_{1}),\cdots,B_{\varepsilon}(a_{n})
, define
GR_{\varepsilon}(f,g)
as the left side of the equality

(6.1.14)

\oint_{\partial B=\partial B_{\varepsilon}(a_{1})\cup\cdots\cup\partial B_{\varepsilon}(w_{n^{\prime}})}fgdz=\oint_{\partial\Omega}fgdz+2i\int_{\Omega\setminus B_{\varepsilon}}i(\alpha_{f}\bar{f}g+\alpha_{g}f\bar{g})d^{2}z,

where the RHS comes from applying Green-Riemann’s theorem as above. Note that the first term on the RHS is purely real as long as
f,g
satisfy (RH), and the second term is also real if
\alpha_{f}=\alpha_{g}
. Let
GR_{0}(f,g):=\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}GR_{\varepsilon}(f,g)
. For example, in the absence of
w_{j}
’s (i.e.
n^{\prime}=0
),
GR_{0}(f,g)={2\pi}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\beta({a_{j}};f)\beta({a_{j}};g)
. We are already able to show the bulk of well-definedness statements.

Proposition 6.15
.

Let
f^{w^{\eta}}(z)=\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}{\cdots}\sigma_{a_{n}}\psi_{z}\psi_{w}^{[\eta]}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{\alpha}}
and
f_{k}(z)=\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hat{k}}}{{\cdots}}\sigma_{a_{n}}\mu_{a_{k}}\psi_{z}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{\alpha}}
for all
k=1,2,\ldots,n
.
Then,

•

b(a_{k};f^{w^{\eta}})=\mathrm{Re}[\bar{\eta}f_{k}(w)]
, or

e^{-\frac{i\pi}{4}}\sqrt{z-a_{k}}\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}{\cdots}\sigma_{a_{n}}\psi_{z}\psi_{w}^{[\eta]}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{\alpha}}\xrightarrow{z\to a_{k}}\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hat{k}}}{{\cdots}}\sigma_{a_{n}}\mu_{a_{k}}\psi_{w}^{[\eta]}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{\alpha}}.

•

\mathrm{Re}[\bar{\eta}^{\prime}f^{w^{\eta}}(w^{\prime})]=-\mathrm{Re}[\bar{\eta}f^{(w^{\prime})^{\eta^{\prime}}}(w)]
, or

\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}{\cdots}\sigma_{a_{n}}\psi_{w^{\prime}}^{[\eta^{\prime}]}\psi_{w}^{[\eta]}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{\alpha}}=-\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}{\cdots}\sigma_{a_{n}}\psi_{w}^{[\eta]}\psi_{w^{\prime}}^{[\eta^{\prime}]}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{\alpha}},

•

b(a_{j};f_{k})=-b(a_{k};f_{j})
for all
j\neq k
, or

\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hat{j,k}}}{{\cdots}}\sigma_{a_{n}}\mu_{a_{k}}\mu_{a_{j}}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{\alpha}}=-\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hat{j,k}}}{{\cdots}}\sigma_{a_{n}}\mu_{a_{j}}\mu_{a_{k}}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{\alpha}}.

Proof.

The first statement follows from setting
\mathrm{Im}\big[GR_{0}(f^{w^{\eta}},f_{k})\big]
to zero, making sure to cut out a disk around
w
whenever
f^{w^{\eta}}
is involved, etc. That is, writing
b_{j}^{w^{\eta}}:=b(a_{j};f^{w^{\eta}})
and
b_{j}^{k}=b(a_{j};f_{k})
,

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathrm{Im}\bigg[\sum_{j}\oint_{\partial B_{\varepsilon}(a_{j})}f^{w^{\eta}}(z)f_{k}(z)dz+\oint_{\partial B_{\varepsilon}(w)}f^{w^{\eta}}(z)f_{k}(z)dz\bigg]

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\neq k}\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathrm{Im}\bigg[\oint_{\partial B_{\varepsilon}(a_{j})}\big(b_{j}^{w^{\eta}}b^{k}_{j}e^{i\pi/2}(z-a_{j})^{-1}+o(|z-a_{j}|^{-1})\big)dz\bigg]

\displaystyle\quad+\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathrm{Im}\bigg[\oint_{\partial B_{\varepsilon}(a_{k})}\big(-b_{k}^{w^{\eta}}(z-a_{k})^{-1}+o(|z-a_{k}|^{-1})\big)dz\bigg]

\displaystyle\quad+\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathrm{Im}\bigg[\oint_{\partial B_{\varepsilon}(w)}\big(\bar{\eta}(z-w)^{-1}+o(|z-w|^{-1})\big)f_{k}(z)dz\bigg]

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\neq k}\mathrm{Im}\big[-2\pi b_{j}^{w^{\eta}}b_{j}^{k}\big]-\mathrm{Im}\big[2\pi ib_{k}^{w^{\eta}}\big]+\mathrm{Im}\big[\bar{\eta}2\pi if_{k}(w)\big]

\displaystyle=-2\pi b_{k}^{w^{\eta}}+2\pi\mathrm{Re}\big[\bar{\eta}f_{k}(w)\big].

The second and third statements are derived using similar arguments for
\mathrm{Im}\big[GR_{0}(f^{w^{\eta}},f^{(w^{\prime})^{\eta^{\prime}}})\big]
and
\mathrm{Im}\big[GR_{0}(f_{j},f_{k})\big]
, respectively.
∎

Now we are ready to consider multi-point fermion insertions in spin-weighted correlations. For this purpose, we define multi-point correlations by first considering correlations of the form

\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}{\cdots}\sigma_{a_{n}}\psi^{[\eta_{1}]}_{z_{1}}\cdots\psi^{[\eta_{2n^{\prime}-1}]}_{z_{2n^{\prime}-1}}\psi_{z}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{\alpha}}\in V_{\Omega}^{\alpha}(a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{n};z_{1},\ldots,z_{2n^{\prime}-1}),

where
\psi_{z}
has the behavior (
6.1.1
) whenever approaching other insertions. For example,

\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}{\cdots}\sigma_{a_{n}}\psi^{[\eta_{1}]}_{z_{1}}\cdots\psi^{[\eta_{2n^{\prime}-1}]}_{z_{2n^{\prime}-1}}\psi_{z}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{\alpha}}\sim-\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}{\cdots}\sigma_{a_{n}}\psi^{[\eta_{2}]}_{z_{2}}\cdots\psi^{[\eta_{2n^{\prime}-1}]}_{z_{2n^{\prime}-1}}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{\alpha}}\frac{\overline{\eta_{1}}}{z-z_{1}}\text{ as }z\to z_{1},

the negative sign coming from anticommuting
z
to the left
2n^{\prime}-1
times in order to use (
6.1.1
). Then we may extend to general fermions by (
6.1.2
).

In any case, as Proposition
6.16
below states, such a definition is equivalent to defining multi-point correlations using a Pfaffian decomposition, as expected physically. We will write
\Psi
for either of
\psi,\psi^{*}
, or equivalently
\psi^{[\eta]}
with
|\eta|=1
.

Proposition 6.16
.

Any two fermion insertions among
\psi^{[\eta]},\psi,\psi^{*}
anticommute. We may decompose

(6.1.15)

\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}{\cdots}\sigma_{a_{n}}\Psi_{z_{1}}\cdots\Psi_{z_{2n^{\prime}}}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{\alpha}}=\operatorname{Pf}\left(\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}{\cdots}\sigma_{a_{n}}\Psi_{z_{j}}\Psi_{z_{k}}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{\alpha}}\right)_{j,k=1}^{2n^{\prime}}.

Proof.

Antisymmetry in two-point correlations is immediate from Proposition
6.15
and linearity in the definition of the complex fermions.

For (
6.1.15
) we begin by assuming the first
2n^{\prime}-1

\Psi
’s are of the form
\psi^{[\eta]}
and the last
\psi
as above. In this case, the equivalence to the Pfaffian decomposition definition goes through the exact same argument as in the critical case (see e.g.
[
37
, Section 6.6]
or
[
21
, Proposition 2.24]
): in short, one shows the Pfaffian recursive relation

\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}{\cdots}\sigma_{a_{n}}\psi^{[\eta_{1}]}_{z_{1}}\cdots\psi^{[\eta_{2n^{\prime}-1}]}_{z_{2n^{\prime}-1}}\psi_{z}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{\alpha}}=\sum_{j=1}^{2n^{\prime}-1}(-1)^{j}\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}{\cdots}\sigma_{a_{n}}\psi^{[\eta_{1}]}_{z_{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hat{j}}}{{\cdots}}\psi^{[\eta_{2n^{\prime}-1}]}_{z_{2n^{\prime}-1}}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{\alpha}}\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}{\cdots}\sigma_{a_{n}}\psi^{[\eta_{j}]}_{z_{j}}\psi_{z}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{\alpha}}.

by showing that both sides have the same poles, (S) asymptotic at
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}
, and the (RH) boundary condition. Note that antisymmetry of any two fermions within the
2n^{\prime}
-point correlation is immediate from the
2
-point correlation case and the right-hand side of (
6.1.15
).

Then the case of general
\Psi
follows by linearity.
∎

6.2.
Estimates of the Basic Correlations

In order to simplify notation, define

(6.2.1)

P_{a}(z):=\min\left({\frac{1}{\sqrt{|z-a|}}},{\frac{\sqrt{\operatorname{dist}(a,\partial\Omega)}}{{|z-a|}}}\right).

Additionally, in order to give uniform bounds which are valid under small or favorable perturbations, we use the following notation for constants bounding various relevant quantities:

•

0<\delta_{1,k}\leq\frac{1}{16}|a_{1}-a_{k}|
;

•

0<\delta_{1}\leq\frac{1}{16}|a_{1}-a_{k}|
for all
k=2,\ldots,n
;

•

0<\delta_{0}\leq\frac{1}{16}|a_{j}-a_{k}|
for all
j<k
;

•

0<\delta_{w}\leq\min_{1\leq j\leq n}\min(\frac{1}{16}|a_{j}-w|,\operatorname{dist}(w,\partial\Omega))
;

•

\|\alpha\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq\kappa
;

•

\|\alpha\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\leq\kappa_{\infty}
;

•

\|\nabla\alpha\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\leq\kappa_{\infty}^{\prime}
;

•

\operatorname{dist}(z,\partial\Omega)^{2}|\nabla\alpha(z)|\leq\kappa_{b}
;

•

|m_{0}|\leq M
,

along with the assumptions needed to make such constants finite if they are involved in a bound (e.g. if a bound involves
\kappa_{\infty}
, we assume
\alpha\in L^{\infty}(\Omega)
). In Section
6.3
,
m_{0}
will stand for the point around which power series in
m
are expanded, after replacing
\alpha
by
m\alpha
.

6.2.1.
Critical (massless) correlations

In this section, we give estimates of the
critical
correlations, which are (massless) holomorphic and admit usual complex analytic techniques and conformal invariance. First, we estimate the spinor in the simplest cases:

Lemma 6.17
.

Let
\Omega=\mathbb{D}
. Then

(6.2.2)

f(z):=\frac{\langle\mu_{a}\psi_{z}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{a}\rangle_{0}}=\frac{e^{-i\pi/4}}{\sqrt{\frac{z-a}{1-\bar{a}z}}}\sqrt{\frac{1-|a|^{2}}{(1-\bar{a}z)^{2}}}=\frac{e^{-i\pi/4}}{\sqrt{{z-a}}}\sqrt{\frac{1-|a|^{2}}{1-\bar{a}z}},

and we have
|f(z)|\leq C\cdot P_{a}(z)
in all of
\mathbb{D}
.

Proof.

Note that we have an explicit formula in the case of
a=0\in\mathbb{D}
: the expression
{e^{-i\pi/4}}/{\sqrt{z}}
satisfies (D) and (RH). For general
a\in\mathbb{D}
, seeing that the conformal map
z\mapsto(z-a)/(1-\bar{a}z)
fixes
\mathbb{D}
and sends
a
to
0
, we get the explicit formula using Remark
6.5
. The desired estimate follows from

\left|\frac{1-|a|^{2}}{(z-a)(1-\bar{a}z)}\right|\leq 2\min\left({\frac{1}{{|z-a|}}},\frac{{\operatorname{dist}(a,\partial\Omega)}}{|z-a|^{2}}\right),

using
1-|a|\leq|1-\bar{a}z|\leq 2
and
|z-a|\leq|1-\bar{a}z|
(e.g. consider that the conformal map above maps to the interior of
\mathbb{D}
).
∎

Lemma 6.18
.

Let
\Omega
be smooth and write
f(z)=\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\mu_{a_{1}}\psi_{z}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{0}}
.
Then we have

\left|f(z)\right|\leq C(n,\Omega)\bigg[P_{a_{1}}(z)+\sum_{k=2}^{n}|b_{k}|P_{a_{k}}(z)\bigg],

for constants
b_{k}:=b(a_{k};f)=i\beta(a_{k};f)
, which satisfy

|b_{k}|=\bigg|\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hat{1,k}}}{{\cdots}}\sigma_{a_{n}}\mu_{a_{1}}\mu_{a_{k}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{0}}\bigg|\leq C(\Omega,\delta_{1,k})\sqrt{\operatorname{dist}(a_{1},\partial\Omega)\operatorname{dist}(a_{k},\partial\Omega)}.

Proof.

First, suppose
\Omega=\mathbb{D}
.
Consider
h=\mathrm{Im}[\int f^{2}dz]
, which is harmonic away from the insertions
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}
. In addition, examining the asymptotics and boundary conditions from Remark
6.12
, we may explicitly verify the decomposition

h=G_{a_{1}}-b_{2}^{2}G_{a_{2}}-\cdots-b_{n}^{2}G_{a_{n}},\qquad\text{or}\qquad f^{2}/2=\partial_{z}G_{a_{1}}-b_{2}^{2}\partial_{z}G_{a_{2}}-\cdots-b_{n}^{2}\partial_{z}G_{a_{n}},

where
-G_{a}
is the Dirichlet Laplace Green’s function on
\mathbb{D}
, i.e.,
G_{a}=G_{0}\circ\varphi_{a}
and
G_{0}=-\log|z|
,
\varphi_{a}(z)=\frac{z-a}{1-\bar{a}z}
, as in the proof of Lemma
6.17
. Since a single
G_{a}
happens to be exactly the square integral for
f_{a}(z):={\langle\mu_{a}\psi_{z}\rangle_{0}}/{\langle\sigma_{a}\rangle_{0}}
, we may use Lemma
6.17
to estimate each derivative on the right; it remains to show
b_{k}^{2}\leq C(\delta_{1,k})\operatorname{dist}(a_{1},\partial\mathbb{D})\operatorname{dist}(a_{k},\partial\mathbb{D})
for
k\geq 2
, since identification as a disorder correlation will follow from Proposition
6.15
. First, since
\partial_{n}h=-|f|^{2}\leq 0
by (RH) on the boundary and each
f_{a}^{2}
has a single pole
\frac{-i}{z-a}
in the bulk,

\displaystyle 0\geq\int_{\partial\mathbb{D}}\partial_{n}h|dz|

\displaystyle=-\int_{\partial\mathbb{D}}|f_{a_{1}}|^{2}|dz|+b_{2}^{2}\int_{\partial\mathbb{D}}|f_{a_{2}}|^{2}|dz|+\cdots+b_{n}^{2}\int_{\partial\mathbb{D}}|f_{a_{n}}|^{2}|dz|

\displaystyle=-\oint_{\partial\mathbb{D}}f_{a_{1}}^{2}dz+b_{2}^{2}\oint_{\partial\mathbb{D}}f_{a_{2}}^{2}dz+\cdots+b_{n}^{2}\oint_{\partial\mathbb{D}}f_{a_{n}}^{2}dz

\displaystyle=-2\pi i\left[-i+b_{2}^{2}i+\cdots+b_{n}^{2}i\right]=-2\pi(1-b_{2}^{2}-\cdots-b_{n}^{2}),

where we used (RH) multiple times. So all
b_{k}
’s are bounded by
1
, and we may assume henceforth
0<8\operatorname{dist}(a_{1},\partial\mathbb{D})\leq\delta_{1,k}<1
, since otherwise we may choose large enough
C(\delta_{1,k})
to ensure
C(\delta_{1,k})\operatorname{dist}(a_{1},\partial\mathbb{D})\operatorname{dist}(a_{k},\partial\mathbb{D})\geq 1
.

In particular, the
(\delta_{1,k}/2)
-ball around
a_{1}
intersects with
\partial\mathbb{D}
. We claim that the point
z_{0}\in\partial\mathbb{D}
closest to
a_{k}
in
\partial\mathbb{D}\setminus B_{\delta_{1,k}/2}(a_{1})
satisfies
|z_{0}-a_{k}|\leq 2\operatorname{dist}(a_{k},\partial\mathbb{D})
. Indeed, if
z_{0}
is the minimizer on all of
\partial\mathbb{D}
,
|z_{0}-a_{k}|
is simply
\operatorname{dist}(a_{k},\partial\mathbb{D})
; otherwise, let
z_{1}
be the true minimizer, attained in
\partial\mathbb{D}\cap B_{\delta_{1,k}/2}(a_{1})
. Considering the triangle
\triangle a_{k}a_{1}z_{1}
, we know that
|a_{1}-a_{k}|\geq\delta_{1,k}
,
|a_{k}-z_{1}|=\operatorname{dist}(a_{k},\partial\mathbb{D})
, and
2\pi/3\leq\angle a_{k}a_{1}z_{1}\leq\pi
. Then, using the second law of cosines, it is easy to see that
\operatorname{dist}(a_{k},\partial\mathbb{D})\geq\delta_{1,k}
, and we have
|z_{0}-a_{k}|\leq|z_{0}-a_{1}|+|a_{1}-a_{k}|\leq 2\delta_{1,k}\leq 2\operatorname{dist}(a_{k},\partial\mathbb{D})
.

Then, this bound, along with Lemma
6.17
, yields

\displaystyle|f_{a_{k}}(z_{0})|^{2}=\frac{1-|a_{k}|^{2}}{|z_{0}-a_{k}||1-|a_{k}|^{2}+\overline{a_{k}}(a_{k}-z_{0})|}\geq\frac{1}{8\operatorname{dist}(a_{k},\partial\mathbb{D})},

and,
|a_{1}-z_{0}|\geq\delta_{1,k}/2
, and the assumptions
|a_{1}|\geq 7/8
and
\operatorname{dist}(a_{1},\partial\mathbb{D})\leq\delta_{1,k}/8
yield

\displaystyle|f_{a_{1}}(z_{0})|^{2}\!=\!\frac{1-|a_{1}|^{2}}{|z_{0}-a_{1}||1-|a_{1}|^{2}+\overline{a_{1}}(a_{1}-z_{0})|}\!\leq\!\frac{2\operatorname{dist}(a_{1},\partial\mathbb{D})}{\frac{1}{2}\delta_{1,k}(\frac{7}{16}\delta_{1,k}-2\operatorname{dist}(a_{1},\partial\mathbb{D}))}\!\leq\!\frac{64\operatorname{dist}(a_{1},\partial\mathbb{D})}{3\delta_{1,k}^{2}}.

Since
h,G_{a_{1}},\ldots,G_{a_{n}}
all have non-positive outer normal derivatives, we have

0\geq\partial_{n}h(z_{0})\geq\partial_{n}G_{a_{1}}(z_{0})-b_{k}^{2}\partial_{n}G_{a_{k}}(z_{0})=b_{k}^{2}|f_{a_{k}}(z_{0})|^{2}-|f_{a_{1}}(z_{0})|^{2},

which yields the desired bound.

For general
\Omega
, we may fix a Riemann map (whose derivative and its reciprocal being bounded above by
C(\Omega)
) and pull-back to
\mathbb{D}
as usual, which gives the
\Omega
dependence in the final statement.
∎

Lemma 6.19
.

Let
\Omega=\mathbb{D}
and
f(z)={\langle\psi_{z}\psi_{w}^{[\eta]}\rangle_{0}}
. Then we have

|f(z)|\leq\frac{2|\eta|}{|z-w|}.

Additionally, in
B_{d/8}(w)
, where
d=\operatorname{dist}(w,\partial\mathbb{D})=1-|w|
, we have

|f(z)|\geq\frac{|\eta|}{2|z-w|}.

Proof.

By dividing by a real scalar if necessary, we may assume
|\eta|=1
. For
w=0
and
\eta=e^{i\pi/4}
, we may verify by hand (cf.
[
36
, Section 1.6]
: the
\frac{1}{z-w}
in the reference becomes
\frac{e^{-\frac{i\pi}{4}}}{z-w}
due to a constant difference in the definition of (RH)) that

{\left\langle\psi_{z}\psi_{0}^{\left[\exp{i\frac{\pi}{4}}\right]}\right\rangle_{0}}=e^{-\frac{i\pi}{4}}\frac{z+1}{z}.

Then it is straightforward to modify
\eta
using the covariance rule of
\psi
:

{\bigg\langle\psi_{z}\psi_{0}^{\left[\exp{i\left(\frac{\theta_{0}}{2}+\frac{\pi}{4}\right)}\right]}\bigg\rangle_{0}}=e^{-\frac{i\pi}{4}}\frac{e^{i\theta_{0}}z+1}{e^{\frac{i\theta_{0}}{2}}z}.

Finally, using
\frac{z-w}{1-\bar{w}z}
as the involution of
\mathbb{D}
sending
w
to
0
with positive derivative at
w
,

\displaystyle\Bigg|{\bigg\langle\psi_{z}\psi_{w}^{\left[\exp{i\left(\frac{\theta_{0}}{2}+\frac{\pi}{4}\right)}\right]}\bigg\rangle_{0}}\Bigg|=\Bigg|e^{-\frac{i\pi}{4}}\sqrt{\frac{1-|w|^{2}}{(1-\bar{w}z)^{2}}}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{1-|w|^{2}}{(1-|w|^{2})^{2}}}\cdot\frac{e^{i\theta_{0}}\left(\frac{z-w}{1-\bar{w}z}\right)+1}{e^{\frac{i\theta_{0}}{2}}\left(\frac{z-w}{1-\bar{w}z}\right)}\Bigg|

\displaystyle=\Bigg|\sqrt{\frac{1}{(1-\bar{w}z)^{2}}}\cdot\frac{e^{i\theta_{0}}\left(\frac{z-w}{1-\bar{w}z}\right)+1}{\left(\frac{z-w}{1-\bar{w}z}\right)}\Bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{|z-w|}.

Finally, the ball
B_{d/8}(w)
maps into
B_{1/2}(0)
under the Möbius transformation
\frac{z-w}{1-\bar{w}z}
, as seen from direct computation or Koebe. Then the desired lower bound follows.
∎

Proposition 6.20
.

Let
\Omega
be smooth and write
f(z)=\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\sigma_{a_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\psi_{z}\psi_{w}^{[\eta]}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{0}}
.
Then we have

(6.2.3)

\displaystyle|f(z)|\!\leq\!C(n,\Omega)\Bigg[\sum_{j=1}^{n}|b_{j}|P_{a_{j}}(z)\!+\!\Bigg[\frac{|\eta|}{\sqrt{\operatorname{dist}(w,\partial\Omega))}}+\!\sum_{k=1}^{n}\frac{|\eta|}{\sqrt{|w-a_{k}|}}\Bigg]\!P_{w}(z)\!+\frac{|\eta|}{|z-w|}\Bigg],

for constants
b_{j}:=b(a_{j};f)=i\beta(a_{j};f)
, which satisfy

|b_{j}|=\bigg|\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hat{j}}}{{\cdots}}\sigma_{a_{n}}\mu_{a_{j}}\psi_{w}^{[\eta]}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{0}}\bigg|.

Proof.

As above, we only show it on
\mathbb{D}
. Write
f_{\operatorname{flat}}
for the correlation from Lemma
6.19
taken on the same domain and with the same
w
. Define
e(w)
as the real constant in the expansion

\displaystyle f(z)=\frac{\bar{\eta}}{z-w}+i\eta e(w)+O(|z-w|)\text{ as }z\to w,

and similarly for
f_{\operatorname{flat}}
and
e_{\operatorname{flat}}(w)
. The phase of the constant term is thus fixed, since otherwise we would have nonzero additive monodromy around the logarithmic singularity of
h=\mathrm{Im}[\int f^{2}dz]
at
w
: since (RH) forces
h
to be constant on
\partial\Omega
by Remark
6.12
, this cannot happen.

Again comparing the asymptotics (D), (S), and (F) for
h
and
h_{\operatorname{flat}}=\mathrm{Im}[\int f_{\operatorname{flat}}^{2}dz]
as in the proof of Lemma
6.18
(and using the same notation for the Green’s functions
G_{a}=\mathrm{Im}[\int f_{a}^{2}dz]
and
b_{j}=b(a_{j})
), we have

h=-b_{1}^{2}G_{a_{1}}-\dots-b_{n}^{2}G_{a_{n}}+h_{\operatorname{flat}}+(e(w)-e_{\operatorname{flat}}(w))G_{w}.

Note that we get two functions corresponding to the singularity at
w
:
h_{\operatorname{flat}}
which provides the degree 1 singularity, and
G_{w}
term calibrates it at logarithmic order. The coefficients
b_{j}
are identified by Proposition
6.15
.

Then Lemmas
6.17
,
6.18
,
6.19
would imply the desired results as in the proof of Lemma
6.18
, given the following bound (still assuming
\Omega=\mathbb{D}
):

Lemma 6.21
.

Write
\delta_{d}:=\min(|w-a_{1}|,\ldots,|w-a_{n}|,\operatorname{dist}(w,\partial\Omega))
. Then we have

|e(w)-e_{\operatorname{flat}}(w)|\leq\frac{C(n)|\eta|^{2}}{\delta_{d}}.

Proof of Lemma
6.21
.

Consider the two meromorphic functions
f_{r}:=-b_{1}^{2}f_{a_{1}}^{2}-\cdots-b_{n}^{2}f_{a_{n}}^{2}+f_{\operatorname{flat}}^{2}
and
f_{w}^{2}
. We know that
f^{2}
, which is a linear combination of the two functions, is a meromorphic function which is a square of another meromorphic function, single-valued in a
\delta_{d}
-neighborhood of
w
. Thanks to Lemma
6.19
which bounds
|f_{\operatorname{flat}}^{2}(z)|\geq\frac{|\eta|^{2}}{2|z-w|^{2}}
(for
|z-w|<\frac{\delta_{d}}{8}
) and Lemma
6.17
which bounds each
|f_{a_{k}}^{2}(z)|\leq\frac{C}{|z-a_{k}|}
, along with
|b_{k}|\leq C(n)\max_{1\leq j\leq n}{\frac{|\eta|}{\sqrt{|w-a_{j}|}}}
, there exists some radius
\delta_{d}/C(n)
such that there is no zero of
f_{r}
in
B_{\delta_{d}/C(n)}(w)
, only a double pole at
w
. Then the same Lemmas in turn give the bound
|f_{r}|\leq\frac{C(n)|\eta|^{2}}{\delta_{d}^{2}}
on
\partial B_{\delta_{d}/C(n)}(w)
.

However,
|f_{w}^{2}|\geq C(n)/\delta_{d}
on
\partial B_{\delta_{d}/C(n)}(w)
by (
6.2.2
). So, by Rouché’s theorem, there exists a constant
C(n)
such that if
|e(w)-e_{\operatorname{flat}}(w)|\geq C(n)|\eta|^{2}/\delta_{d}
then the number of zeros minus the number of poles of
f^{2}=f_{r}+(e(w)-e_{\operatorname{flat}}(w))f_{w}^{2}
in
B_{\delta_{d}/C(n)}(w)
is
-1
, i.e. identical to that of
f_{w}^{2}
. Since there is already a unique double pole at
w
,
f^{2}
must also have a simple zero, contradicting the assumption that
f
is single valued near
w
.
∎

This concludes the proof of Proposition
6.20
.
∎

6.2.2.
Massive correlations

We start our quantitative estimates by noting the following useful exception to Remark
6.8
.

Lemma 6.22
.

Let
f(z)=\frac{\langle\mu_{a}\psi_{z}\rangle_{\alpha;\Omega}}{\langle\sigma_{a}\rangle_{\alpha;\Omega}}
. Then its holomorphic part
\underline{f}
, with
s
normalized so that
\mathrm{Re}[s(a)]=0
, is precisely the corresponding critical spinor
\underline{f}(z)=\frac{\langle\mu_{a}\psi_{z}\rangle_{0;\Omega}}{\langle\sigma_{a}\rangle_{0;\Omega}}
. In addition, we have

(6.2.4)

0<c(\kappa)\leq\oint_{\partial\Omega}f^{2}dz=\int_{\partial\Omega}|f|^{2}|dz|=-\int_{\partial\Omega}\partial_{n}h|dz|\leq C(\kappa),

where

\partial_{n}
refers to the outer normal derivative.

If
\Omega
is smooth, for any
\nu\in(0,1)
and
z\in\Omega
, we have

|h(z)|=h(z)\leq{C(\kappa,\Omega,\nu)}\left[\left(\frac{\operatorname{dist}(a,\partial\Omega)}{|z-a|}\right)^{\nu}+1\right].

Proof.

For the first part, note that
\underline{f}
is an element of
V^{0}_{\Omega}(a)
. That also means that under the linear map (
6.1.13
)
\underline{f}
would be taken to
\mathrm{Re}[e^{-s(a)}]
. We have already shown in the proof of Corollary
6.14
that such vectors in fact satisfy
\mathrm{Im}[\beta_{a}(\underline{f})]=\mathrm{Im}[e^{-s(a)}]=0
, i.e.,
\mathrm{Re}[e^{-s(a)}]=\exp\{-\mathrm{Re}[s(a)]\}=1
, i.e.,
\underline{f}
is the desired critical correlation.

The equivalence of the three line integrals in (
6.2.4
) is a matter of simple calculation from the boundary condition (RH). In addition, they are easily seen to be invariant under conformal pull-back, so we may fix
\varphi:\mathbb{D}\to\Omega
such that
\varphi(0)=a
and compute instead
\int_{\partial\mathbb{D}}|\varphi^{*}f|^{2}|dz|
. By the above discussion, the holomorphic part is simply the critical correlation, for which we have an explicit formula:

\varphi^{*}f(z)=\frac{\langle\mu_{0}\psi_{z}\rangle_{(\alpha\circ\varphi)|\varphi^{\prime}|;\mathbb{D}}}{\langle\sigma_{0}\rangle_{(\alpha\circ\varphi)|\varphi^{\prime}|;\mathbb{D}}}=e^{s(z)}\frac{\langle\mu_{0}\psi_{z}\rangle_{0;\mathbb{D}}}{\langle\sigma_{0}\rangle_{0;\mathbb{D}}}=e^{s(z)}\frac{e^{-i\pi/4}}{\sqrt{z}}.

Then the trace, Sobolev, and Cauchy-Schwarz inequalities give (see also
[
4
, Proposition 8.4]
, which translates a
W^{1,2}
for
2s
to a
W^{1,q}
bound for
e^{2s}
for
q<2
)

\frac{C}{\|s\|_{W^{1,2}(\mathbb{D})}}\leq\frac{\left(\int_{\partial\mathbb{D}}|dz|\right)^{2}}{\int_{\partial\mathbb{D}}|e^{-2s}||dz|}\leq\int_{\partial\mathbb{D}}|f_{\mathbb{D}}|^{2}|dz|=\int_{\partial\mathbb{D}}|e^{2s}||dz|\leq C\|s\|_{W^{1,2}(\mathbb{D})},

so we have (
6.2.4
), since from (
6.1.5
) and by the change of variables formula we have
\|s\|_{W^{1,2}(\mathbb{D})}\leq C\|(\alpha\circ\varphi)|\varphi^{\prime}|\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{D})}=C\|\alpha\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}
.

Similarly, integrating on the straight ray
[z/|z|,z]
from
z/|z|\in\partial\mathbb{D}
to
z
, we have

\displaystyle|h\circ\varphi(z)|=\Bigg|\int_{z/|z|}^{z}\bigg(e^{s(z^{\prime})}\frac{e^{-i\pi/4}}{\sqrt{z^{\prime}}}\bigg)^{2}dz^{\prime}\Bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\|e^{2s}\|_{L^{1/\nu}([z/|z|,z])}\bigg(\int_{{[z/|z|,z]}}\frac{1}{|z^{\prime}|^{\frac{1}{1-\nu}}}|dz^{\prime}|\bigg)^{{1-\nu}}

\displaystyle\leq C(\nu)\|{s}\|_{W^{1,\frac{2}{1+\nu}}(\mathbb{D})}\cdot C|z|^{-\nu}\leq C(\kappa,\nu)|z|^{-\nu},

applying
[
4
, Proposition 8.4]
and the trace inequality again, on, say, the half of
\mathbb{D}
cut by the ray
[-z/|z|,z/|z|]
. Then it remains to note that for
z\in\Omega
, such that
|z-a|\leq\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{dist}(a,\partial\Omega)
, we have

\left|\varphi^{-1}(z)\right|\geq\frac{|z-a|}{16\operatorname{dist}(a,\partial\Omega)},

by, e.g., Koebe. If
|z-a|>\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{dist}(a,\partial\Omega)
we may simply appeal to the bound for the case
|z-a|=\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{dist}(a,\partial\Omega)
, thanks to the maximum principle, which gives rise to the constant term.
∎

We now improve the existence statement of Corollary
6.14
to a quantitative bound.

Proposition 6.23
.

Let
\Omega
be smooth and
f(z)=\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\mu_{a_{1}}\psi_{z}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{\alpha}}
. Then, for all
2\leq j\leq n
and
\nu\in(0,1)
, we have

|\beta({a_{j}};f)|=|b(a_{j})|\leq C(\kappa,\Omega,\nu)\left[\left(\frac{\operatorname{dist}(a_{1},\partial\Omega)}{\delta_{1}}\right)^{\nu}+1\right].

Proof.

Consider
f_{1}(z)=\frac{\langle\mu_{a_{1}}\psi_{z}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\rangle_{\alpha}}
,
h=\mathrm{Im}[\int f^{2}dz]
, and
h_{1}=\mathrm{Im}[\int f_{1}^{2}dz]
, normalized to be zero on
\partial\Omega
, as usual. We claim that
h\leq h_{1}
: it suffices to show that
f^{2}-f_{1}^{2}
extends continuously to
a_{1}
and therefore
h-h_{1}
, which can only blow-up to
-\infty
at
a_{2},\ldots,a_{n}
, cannot have a bulk maximum, by Lemma
6.10
. This holds since

(6.2.5)

\displaystyle f+f_{1}=-2e^{-i\pi/4}(z-a_{1})^{-1/2}+o(|z-a_{1}|^{-1/2})\quad\text{and}\quad f-f_{1}=O(|z-a_{1}|^{1/2}),

from (
6.1.7
) and (
6.1.8
).

Thanks to the continuity of
h
away from
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}
and the maximum principle, we may infer the following topological facts about the subdomains of
\Omega
:

•

Any sets of type
\{h<r\},\{h>r\}
for
r\in\mathbb{R}
must contain a singularity where
h
tends to
-\infty,+\infty
, respectively.

•

\{h>0\}
is a connected set: any connected component must include
a_{1}
. Its boundary includes
\partial\Omega
since, if there exists a boundary point near which
h\leq 0
, we would have
f\equiv 0
(see Remark
6.12
and the proof of Lemma
6.11
).

•

Therefore, the connected components of
\{h<0\}
are all simply connected, each containing one or more of
a_{2},\ldots,a_{n}
, since any hole would be a component of
\{h>0\}
.

•

There is
K=C(\kappa,\Omega,\nu)\left[\left(\frac{\operatorname{dist}(a,\partial\Omega)}{\delta_{1}}\right)^{\nu}+1\right]>0
, such that
\{h>K\}
is a simply connected set. To see this, consider a smooth crosscut
\omega\subset\Omega
dividing
\Omega
into one component
\Omega_{1}
containing
a_{1}
and the other
\Omega_{2}
containing all others. We may take
\omega
so that its minimum distance to
a_{1}
is at least
\delta_{1}

. Let
K=\max_{\omega}h_{1}
, which satisfies the bound of Lemma
6.22
, then
\{h_{1}>K\}
is contained within
\Omega_{1}
, since
h_{1}
must be less than
K
in
\Omega_{2}
. Then, because
h\leq h_{1}
, we know that
\{h>K\}\subset\{h_{1}>K\}\subset\Omega_{1}
. Since any hole surrounded by
\{h>K\}
has to contain some other
a_{j}
,
\{h>K\}
cannot have any hole.

•

The boundaries of these simply connected sets, i.e., level sets of the form
\{h=r\}
, are all Lipschitz and piecewise differentiable, since its tangent direction is explicitly given by the direction of
\overline{f^{2}}
. Zeroes of
f
are isolated and the level lines bifurcate at angles greater than zero, since
f
vanishes like a polynomial to the highest order, thanks to Theorem
6.7
.

Given this, define
O_{j}\subset\Omega
as the connected component of
\{h<0\}
containing
a_{j}
for
j\geq 2
. If
b(a_{j})=0
there is nothing to estimate, so assume
b_{j}:=b(a_{j})\neq 0
.

Define
f_{j}(z):=\frac{\langle\mu_{a_{1}}\psi_{z}\rangle_{-\alpha;O_{j}}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\rangle_{-\alpha;O_{j}}}
, i.e., the correlation of the
(-\alpha)
-massive model on
O_{j}
. It is easy to verify that
-if/b_{j}
is
(-\alpha)
-massive as well, with
\mathrm{Im}[\int(-if/b_{j})^{2}dz]=-h/(b_{j})^{2}
, so we may compare
-h/(b_{j})^{2}
with
h_{j}:=\mathrm{Im}[\int f_{j}^{2}dz]
within
O_{j}
. We claim
-h/(b_{j})^{2}\leq h_{j}
: both are
0
on
\partial O_{j}
and
-h/(b_{j})^{2}-h_{j}
cannot have a bulk minimum, since the singularities of
-if/b_{j},f_{j}
at
a_{j}
cancel out as in (
6.2.5
) and the difference could only blow-up to
+\infty
at some other
a_{j^{\prime}}\in O_{j}
, if any. By Lemma
6.22
, this implies that (note
\|\alpha\|_{L^{2}(O_{j})}\leq\|\alpha\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq\kappa
):

c(\kappa)\leq\int_{\partial O_{j}}-\partial_{n}h_{j}(z)|dz|\leq\int_{\partial O_{j}}\partial_{n}h/(b_{j})^{2}|dz|.

Finally, the divergence theorem on the function
(h+1)^{2}
and the domain
{\{0<h<K\}}
gives (note minus signs in front of inner boundary from
\partial_{n}
):

\displaystyle\int_{\{0<h<K\}}\Delta(h+1)^{2}(z)d^{2}z

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{\partial\Omega}\partial_{n}(h+1)^{2}(z)|dz|-\int_{\partial\{h>K\}}\partial_{n}(h+1)^{2}(z)|dz|-\int_{\partial\{h<0\}}\partial_{n}(h+1)^{2}(z)|dz|.

Let us estimate each term separately.

Since
\Delta(h+1)^{2}=2(h+1)\Delta h+2|\nabla h|^{2}=-8(h+1)\alpha|\nabla h|+2|\nabla h|^{2}\geq-32(h+1)^{2}\alpha^{2}
, we have

\int_{\{0<h<K\}}\Delta(h+1)^{2}(z)d^{2}z\geq-32(K+1)^{2}\kappa^{2}.

By the boundary condition

\int_{\partial\Omega}\partial_{n}(h+1)^{2}(z)|dz|=\int_{\partial\Omega}2\partial_{n}h(z)|dz|\leq 0.

Note that
f(z)=\frac{\langle\mu_{a_{1}}\psi_{z}\rangle_{\alpha;\{h>K\}}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\rangle_{\alpha;\{h>K\}}}
on
\{h>K\}
(
h|_{\{h>K\}}
satisfies (D) and (RH), cf. Remark
6.12
). Therefore, by Lemma
6.22

-\int_{\partial\{h>K\}}\partial_{n}(h+1)^{2}(z)|dz|=-\int_{\partial\{h>K\}}2\partial_{n}h(z)|dz|\leq C(\kappa).

Since
\partial_{n}h\geq 0
on
\partial\{h<0\}
,

\int_{\partial\{h<0\}}\partial_{n}(h+1)^{2}(z)|dz|=\int_{\partial\{h<0\}}2\partial_{n}h(z)|dz|\geq\int_{\partial O_{j}}2\partial_{n}h(z)|dz|\geq 2b_{j}^{2}c(\kappa,|\Omega|).

Combining these with the divergence theorem above, we get the desired estimate.
∎

Corollary 6.24
.

In the same setting as Proposition
6.23
, we have

|f(z)|\leq C(\kappa_{\infty},n,\Omega)\bigg(P_{a_{1}}(z)+\sum_{k=2}^{n}|b_{k}|P_{a_{k}}(z)\bigg),

for constants
b_{k}:=b(a_{k};f)=i\beta(a_{k};f)
which satisfy

|b_{k}|=\bigg|\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hat{1,k}}}{{\cdots}}\sigma_{a_{n}}\mu_{a_{1}}\mu_{a_{k}}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{\alpha}}\bigg|\leq C(\kappa_{\infty},n,\Omega,{\delta_{0}})\min\left(\sqrt{\operatorname{dist}(a_{1},\partial\Omega)},\sqrt{\operatorname{dist}(a_{k},\partial\Omega)}\right).

Proof.

Consider the decomposition
f=e^{s}\underline{f}
from Theorem
6.7
. The holomorphic part
\underline{f}
is in the space
V_{\Omega}^{0}(a_{1},...,a_{n})
, mapped to the vector
(\mathrm{Re}[e^{-s(a_{1})}],\mathrm{Re}[ib(a_{2})e^{-s(a_{2})}],...,\mathrm{Re}[ib(a_{n})e^{-s(a_{n})}])
under the map (
6.1.13
). Note that
e^{-s}
is globally bounded by
C(\kappa_{q},q,\Omega)
by (
6.1.5
), (
6.1.6
).

In other words, we have

(6.2.6)

\underline{f}(z)=\mathrm{Re}[e^{-s(a_{1})}]\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\mu_{a_{1}}\psi_{z}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{0}}+\mathrm{Re}[ib(a_{2})e^{-s(a_{2})}]\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\sigma_{a_{3}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\mu_{a_{2}}\psi_{z}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{0}}\\
+\dots+\mathrm{Re}[ib(a_{n})e^{-s(a_{n})}]\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n-1}}\mu_{a_{n}}\psi_{z}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{0}},

where the disorder-fermion correlations satisfy the bound coming from Lemma
6.18
and (
6.1.5
) finishes the proof of the first estimate. In particular, along with Proposition
6.23
, we know that
\|f\|_{L^{3/2}(\Omega)}\leq C(\kappa_{\infty},n,\Omega,{\delta_{1}})
.

Consider the
critical
correlation

f_{k;0}(z):=\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hat{k}}}{{\cdots}}\sigma_{a_{n}}\mu_{a_{k}}\psi_{z}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{0}},

and compute the imaginary part of the bilinear form
GR_{0}(f,f_{k;0})
(i.e. LHS of (
6.1.14
)):

\displaystyle 2\pi(b(a_{k};f)+b(a_{1};f_{k;0}))=-2\mathrm{Im}\bigg[\int_{\Omega}\alpha\bar{f}f_{k;0}d^{2}z\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq 2\kappa_{\infty}\|f\|_{L^{3/2}(\Omega)}\|f_{k;0}\|_{L^{3}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq C(\kappa_{\infty},n,\Omega,{\delta_{1}})\sqrt{\operatorname{dist}(a_{k},\partial\Omega)},

where we used Lemma
6.18
to bound
f_{k;0}
. The critical coefficient
b(a_{1};f_{k;0})
is bounded by
C(\Omega,{\delta_{1}})\sqrt{\operatorname{dist}(a_{k},\partial\Omega)}
also by Lemma
6.18
. We may replace
a_{k}
by
a_{1}
using the (anti)symmetry in
b_{k}
(by Proposition
6.15
), at the cost of replacing
\delta_{1}
by
\delta_{0}
.
∎

Using the same decomposition argument, we get the following bound.

Corollary 6.25
.

Let
\Omega
be smooth and
f(z)=\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\sigma_{a_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\psi_{z}\psi_{w}^{[\eta]}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{\alpha}}
. Then we have

(6.2.7)

|f(z)|\leq C(\kappa_{\infty},n,\Omega,\delta_{0})|\eta|\Bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left[\sum_{k=1}^{n}\min\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{|w-a_{k}|}},\frac{\sqrt{\operatorname{dist}(a_{j},\partial\Omega)}}{|w-a_{k}|}\right)\right]P_{a_{j}}(z)\\
+\Bigg[\frac{1}{\sqrt{\operatorname{dist}(w,\partial\Omega)}}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{1}{\sqrt{|w-a_{j}|}}\Bigg]P_{w}(z)+\frac{1}{|z-w|}\Bigg).

Proof.

In this case, we have

b(a_{j};f)=\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hat{j}}}{{\cdots}}\sigma_{a_{n}}\mu_{a_{j}}\psi_{w}^{[\eta]}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{\alpha}}.

Let us consider the analog of (
6.2.6
) in this case. In addition to terms appearing in (
6.2.6
), we have an additional term in the decomposition

\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\sigma_{a_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\psi_{z}\psi_{w}^{[e^{\overline{s(w)}}\eta]}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{0}},

which is the only term which gives rise to the two latter terms centered at
w
in (
6.2.7
), by Proposition
6.20
. Let us carefully collect the contributions to the
P_{a_{j}}(z)
term: we have contributions from

•

\mathrm{Re}[ib(a_{j})e^{-s(a_{j})}]\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hat{j}}}{{\cdots}}\sigma_{a_{n}}\mu_{a_{j}}\psi_{z}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{0}}
, which by Theorem
6.7
and Lemma
6.18
contributes

C(\kappa_{\infty},n,\Omega)\Bigg|\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hat{j}}}{{\cdots}}\sigma_{a_{n}}\mu_{a_{j}}\psi_{w}^{[\eta]}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{0}}\Bigg|P_{a_{j}}(z),

which is bounded by the first term in (
6.2.7
) by Lemma
6.18
;

•

\mathrm{Re}[ib(a_{k})e^{-s(a_{k})}]\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hat{k}}}{{\cdots}}\sigma_{a_{n}}\mu_{a_{k}}\psi_{z}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{0}}
for
j\neq k
, which by Lemma
6.18
contributes

C(\kappa_{\infty},n,\Omega)\Bigg|\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hat{k}}}{{\cdots}}\sigma_{a_{n}}\mu_{a_{k}}\psi_{w}^{[\eta]}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{0}}\Bigg|P_{a_{j}}(z),

which is bounded by the first term in (
6.2.7
) by Lemma
6.18
;

•

\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\sigma_{a_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\psi_{z}\psi_{w}^{[e^{\overline{s(w)}}\eta]}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{0}}
, which by Proposition
6.20
contributes

C(n,\Omega)\Bigg|\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hat{j}}}{{\cdots}}\sigma_{a_{n}}\mu_{a_{j}}\psi_{w}^{[e^{\overline{s(w)}}\eta]}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{0}}\Bigg|P_{a_{j}}(z),

which is bounded by the first term in (
6.2.7
) by Theorem
6.7
and Lemma
6.18
.

∎

6.3.
Logarithmic derivatives and series expansions

6.3.1.
Second order estimates

Extracting the logarithmic derivative takes more care. To discuss higher order singularities, we define the bigger space
V_{\Omega;\nu}^{\alpha}\supset V_{\Omega;-\frac{1}{2}}^{\alpha}=V_{\Omega}^{\alpha}
by replacing the order
1/2
in (
6.1.10
) by a half-integer
-\nu\geq 1/2
. We have the following explicit decomposition of spinors in this space:

Lemma 6.26
.

Let
\Omega
be smooth. Suppose
f\in V_{\Omega;\nu}^{\alpha}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})
. Then, as
z\to a_{j}
, for some complex numbers
o_{\nu+l}=o_{\nu+l}(a_{j})=o_{\nu+l}(a_{j};f)
,
l=0,1,2
, we have the asymptotic expansions: defining

\displaystyle f^{\dagger 0}(z):=

\displaystyle\,f(z),

\displaystyle f^{\dagger 1}(z):=

\displaystyle\,f(z)+e^{-\frac{i\pi}{4}}\frac{\alpha(a_{j})\overline{o_{\nu}}}{\nu+1}(\overline{z-a_{j}})^{\nu+1}+e^{-\frac{i\pi}{4}}o_{\nu}\cdot(z-a_{j})^{\nu},

\displaystyle f^{\dagger 2}(z):=

\displaystyle f^{\dagger 1}(z)+e^{-\frac{i\pi}{4}}\overline{o_{\nu}}\cdot\left[\frac{\partial_{z}\alpha(a_{j})}{\nu+1}\cdot(z-a_{j})\overline{({z-a_{j}})^{\nu+1}}+\frac{\partial_{\bar{z}}\alpha(a_{j})}{\nu+2}\cdot\overline{({z-a_{j}})^{\nu+2}}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+\,e^{-\frac{i\pi}{4}}\alpha(a_{j})\cdot{\left(\frac{\alpha(a_{j}){o_{\nu}}}{\nu+1}({z-a_{j}})^{\nu+1}\overline{(z-a_{j})}+\frac{{\overline{o_{\nu+1}}}}{\nu+2}{(\overline{z-a_{j}})}^{\nu+2}\right)}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+e^{-\frac{i\pi}{4}}o_{\nu+1}\cdot(z-a_{j})^{\nu+1},

we have

\displaystyle f^{\dagger 0}(z)=

\displaystyle-e^{-\frac{i\pi}{4}}o_{\nu}\cdot(z-a_{j})^{\nu}+O(|z-a_{1}|^{\nu+\frac{3}{4}}),

\displaystyle f^{\dagger 1}(z)=

\displaystyle\,-e^{-\frac{i\pi}{4}}o_{\nu+1}\cdot(z-a_{j})^{\nu+1}+O(|z-a_{j}|^{\nu+\frac{13}{8}}),

\displaystyle f^{\dagger 2}(z)=

\displaystyle\,-e^{-\frac{i\pi}{4}}o_{\nu+2}\cdot(z-a_{j})^{\nu+2}+o(|z-a_{j}|^{\nu+2}).

Note the exponents in the two big
O
terms may be improved to the respective next smallest integers
a posteriori
.

We also use the following notation for the special cases

o_{-\frac{1}{2}}(a_{j};f)=\beta(a_{j};f)=\beta(z=a_{j};f(z))\quad\text{ and }\quad o_{\frac{1}{2}}(a_{j};f)=\lambda(a_{j};f)=\lambda(z=a_{j};f(z)).

In addition, the collection of
o_{\nu+l}(a_{j};f)
for
\nu+l<-1/2
and
\mathrm{Re}[\beta(a_{j};f)]
for all
a_{j}
together uniquely determine
f
.

Proof.

From Theorem
6.7
, we already know that
f(z)=-e^{-\frac{i\pi}{4}}o_{\nu}\cdot(z-a_{j})^{\nu}+O(|z-a_{j}|^{\nu+\frac{3}{4}})
for some
o_{\nu}
.

Then
f^{\dagger 1}(z)
is a spinor which is itself
O(|z-a_{j}|^{\nu+\frac{3}{4}})
and
\partial_{\bar{z}}f^{\dagger 1}(z)=-i\alpha(z)\overline{f(z)}+e^{-\frac{i\pi}{4}}\overline{o_{\nu}}\cdot\alpha(a_{j})\cdot(\overline{z-a_{j}})^{\nu}=O(|z-a_{j}|^{\nu+\frac{3}{4}})
for
z\neq a_{j}
. Then we see that
\partial_{\bar{z}}\left[\frac{f^{\dagger 1}(z)}{(z-a_{j})^{\nu+1}}\right]=\frac{\partial_{\bar{z}}\left[f^{\dagger 1}(z)\right]}{{(z-a_{j})^{\nu+1}}}=O(|z-a_{j}|^{-1/4})
and the
L^{2}
function
\frac{f^{\dagger 1}(z)}{(z-a_{j})^{\nu+1}}
is in fact
\frac{5}{8}
-Hölder continuous in a neighborhood of
a_{j}
by Corollary
6.2
.

Then, knowing
f^{\dagger 1}(z)=-e^{-\frac{i\pi}{4}}o_{\nu+1}\cdot(z-a_{j})^{\nu+1}+O(|z-a_{j}|^{\nu+\frac{13}{8}})
for some
o_{\nu+1}
, we may play the same game with the spinor
f^{\dagger 2}(z)
and the function
\frac{f^{\dagger 2}(z)}{(z-a_{j})^{\nu+2}}=O(|z-a_{j}|^{-\frac{3}{8}})
(i.e. a priori in
L^{2}
). For this purpose, we have the sufficient bound

\displaystyle{\partial_{\bar{z}}\left[f^{\dagger 2}(z)\right]}=

\displaystyle\,{-i\alpha(z)\overline{f(z)}+e^{-\frac{i\pi}{4}}\left[\partial_{z}\alpha(a_{j})\cdot(z-a_{j})+\partial_{\bar{z}}\alpha(a_{j})\cdot\overline{(z-a_{j})}\right]\cdot\overline{{(o_{\nu}}\cdot({z-a_{j}})^{\nu})}}

\displaystyle+{e^{-\frac{i\pi}{4}}\alpha(a_{j})\cdot\overline{\left({o_{\nu}}\cdot({z-a_{j}})^{\nu}+\frac{\alpha(a_{j})\overline{o_{\nu}}}{\nu+1}(\overline{z-a_{j}})^{\nu+1}+o_{\nu+1}\cdot(z-a_{j})^{\nu+1}\right)}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,O(|z-a_{j}|^{\nu+\frac{13}{8}}).

If
f_{1},f_{2}
have the same collection of
o_{\nu+l}(a_{j};f)
for
\nu+l<-1/2
and
\mathrm{Re}[\beta(a_{j};f)]
, simply note that
f_{1}-f_{2}
is an element of
V_{\Omega}^{\alpha}
which is taken to zero under the map (
6.1.13
) and is thus zero.
∎

Remark 6.27
.

The coefficients
o_{\nu},o_{\nu+1},o_{\nu+2}
from Lemma
6.26
can be extracted using a Cauchy-type formula against holomorphic spinors
z^{-\nu-1},z^{-\nu-2},z^{-\nu-3}
, respectively: for example, from applying a Green-Riemann’s formula in a small annulus
B_{r}(a_{j})\setminus B_{t}(a_{j})
then sending
t\downarrow 0
, we have for
l=0,1,2

(6.3.1)

\displaystyle o_{\nu+l}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{\partial B_{r}(a_{j})}f^{\dagger l}(z)(z-a_{j})^{-\nu-l-1}dz-2\int_{B_{r}(a_{j})}\alpha(z)\overline{f^{\dagger l}(z)}(z-a_{j})^{-\nu-l-1}d^{2}z.

In fact, similar ideas allow us to give a uniform bound:

Lemma 6.28
.

Suppose
\Omega
is smooth, and let
f(z)
be either
\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\mu_{a_{1}}\psi_{z}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{\alpha}}
or
\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\psi_{z}\psi_{w}^{[\eta]}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{\alpha}}
. If
0<2d\leq\min(\operatorname{dist}(a_{j},\partial\Omega),\delta_{0})
and
d_{w}=\min_{k=1,2,\ldots,n}|w-a_{k}|
, there is a constant
C=C(\Omega,\kappa_{\infty},n,\delta_{0},\kappa_{b})
such that for
z\in B_{d/4}(a_{j})

(6.3.2)

\displaystyle\left|f(z)+e^{-i\pi/4}\beta(a_{j})\cdot(z-a_{j})^{-1/2}+2e^{-i\pi/4}\alpha(a_{j})\overline{\beta(a_{j})}\cdot(\overline{z-a_{j}})^{1/2}\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\cdot\big(d^{-1}|\beta(a_{j})|+|\eta|d^{-1/2}d_{w}^{-1/2}\big){|z-a_{j}|^{1/2}}{|z-w|^{-1}},

where we omit the
\eta
term and
|z-w|^{-1}
factor in the case of the disorder-fermion correlation.

See also Corollaries
6.24
and
6.25
for estimates of
\beta(a_{j})
.

Proof.

Let us handle the case of
f(z)=\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\psi_{z}\psi_{w}^{[\eta]}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{\alpha}}
; the other case is simpler.

Write
f^{w}(z):=(z-w)f(z)
and note that in
B_{d}(a_{j})
, by Corollary
6.25
,

(6.3.3)

\displaystyle\left|\sqrt{z-a_{j}}\cdot f^{w}(z)\right|

\displaystyle\leq C(\Omega,\kappa_{\infty},n,\delta_{0})\big(|\beta(a_{j})||w-z|+|\eta|d^{1/2}d_{w}^{-1/2}\big),

so

\left|\partial_{\bar{z}}(\sqrt{z-a_{j}}\cdot f^{w}(z))\right|=\left|\sqrt{z-a_{j}}\cdot\partial_{\bar{z}}f^{w}(z)\right|\leq C(\Omega,\kappa_{\infty},n,\delta_{0})\big(|\beta(a_{j})||w-z|+|\eta|d^{1/2}d_{w}^{-1/2}\big),

which yields, with Corollary
6.2
,

(6.3.4)

\|\sqrt{z-a_{j}}\cdot f^{w}(z)\|_{C^{3/4}(B_{d/2}(a_{j}))}\leq C(\Omega,\kappa_{\infty},n,\delta_{0})\big(|\beta(a_{j})|+|\eta|d^{1/2}d_{w}^{-1/2}\big)d^{-3/4}.

Write
f_{b}(z)=-e^{-\frac{i\pi}{4}}\beta(a_{j})\cdot(z-a_{j})^{-\frac{1}{2}}-2e^{-\frac{i\pi}{4}}\alpha(a_{j})\overline{\beta(a_{j})}\cdot(\overline{z-a_{j}})^{\frac{1}{2}}
and
f_{b}^{w}(z):=(z-w)f_{b}(z)
. In fact,
\frac{f^{w}(z)-f^{w}_{b}(z)}{\sqrt{z-a_{j}}}
is single-valued and is in
L^{2}(B_{d/2}(a_{j}))
: compute

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{\bar{z}}\frac{f^{w}(z)-f^{w}_{b}(z)}{\sqrt{z-a_{j}}}\right|

\displaystyle=\Bigg|\frac{-i\alpha(z)\overline{f(z)}+e^{-\frac{i\pi}{4}}\alpha(a_{j})\overline{\beta(a_{j})}\cdot(\overline{z-a_{j}})^{-\frac{1}{2}}}{(z-w)^{-1}\sqrt{z-a_{j}}}\Bigg|

\displaystyle=\Bigg|\frac{\alpha(z)(\overline{f(z)+e^{-\frac{i\pi}{4}}\beta(a_{j})\cdot({z-a_{j}})^{-\frac{1}{2}})}+(\alpha(z)-\alpha(a_{j}))\overline{e^{-\frac{i\pi}{4}}\beta(a_{j})\cdot({z-a_{j}})^{-\frac{1}{2}})}}{(z-w)^{-1}\sqrt{z-a_{j}}}\Bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\Bigg|\frac{\alpha(z)(\overline{({z-a_{j}})^{\frac{1}{2}}f^{w}(z)+e^{-\frac{i\pi}{4}}\beta(a_{j})\cdot(z-w))}}{|{z-a_{j}}|}\cdot\frac{z-w}{\overline{z-w}}\Bigg|+\left|\frac{(\alpha(z)-\alpha(a_{j}))\overline{\beta(a_{j}))}}{(z-w)^{-1}(z-a_{j})}\right|

\displaystyle\leq C(\Omega,\kappa_{\infty},n,\delta_{0})\big(|\beta(a_{j})|+|\eta|d^{1/2}d_{w}^{-1/2}\big)\left(d^{-3/4}|z-a_{j}|^{-1/4}+\|\nabla\alpha\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{d/2}(a_{j}))}\right),

where we used (
6.3.4
) on the first term (note that
({z-a_{j}})^{\frac{1}{2}}f^{w}(z)+e^{-\frac{i\pi}{4}}\beta(a_{j})(z-w)=0
at
z=a_{j}
and the latter term also trivially satisfies a Hölder bound of the form (
6.3.4
)).

Then we may use Corollary
6.2
with
q=3
and (
6.3.3
) to say that, in
B_{d/2}(a_{j})
, the modulus of
\frac{f^{w}(z)-f^{w}_{b}(z)}{\sqrt{z-a_{j}}}
in
B_{d/4}(a_{j})
is at most

\displaystyle C(\Omega,\kappa_{\infty},n,\delta_{0})\big(|\beta(a_{j})|+|\eta|d^{1/2}d_{w}^{-1/2}\big)\left(d^{-1}+d^{1/3}\big(d^{-3/4+5/12}+d^{2/3}\|\nabla\alpha\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{d/2}(a_{j}))}\big)\right),

which fits into the desired form of estimate.
∎

Remark 6.29
.

A much simplified version of the argument as in the proof of Lemma
6.28
easily yields, for
z\in B_{\delta_{w}/2}(w)
,

(6.3.5)

\displaystyle\Bigg|\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\psi_{z}\psi_{w}^{[\eta]}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{\alpha}}-\frac{\bar{\eta}}{z-w}+2i\eta\cdot\alpha(w)\cdot\log|z-w|\Bigg|\leq C(\Omega,\kappa_{\infty},n,\delta_{0},\kappa_{b},\delta_{w}).

That is, for
C=C(\Omega,\kappa_{\infty},n,\delta_{0},\kappa_{b},\delta_{w})
, we have that

\displaystyle f^{\dagger}(z)=\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\psi_{z}\psi_{w}^{[\eta]}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{\alpha}}-\frac{\bar{\eta}}{z-w}+2i\eta\cdot\alpha(w)\cdot\log|z-w|

satisfies

\displaystyle\big|\partial_{\bar{z}}f^{\dagger}(z)\big|=\Bigg|-i{\alpha(z)\overline{\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\psi_{z}\psi_{w}^{[\eta]}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{\alpha}}}}+i\alpha(w)\overline{\frac{\bar{\eta}}{{z-w}}}\Bigg|\leq C\cdot|z-w|^{-1/4},

with identical bound on
f^{\dagger}(z)
itself, implies that
f^{\dagger}
is continuous and satisfies the above bound by Corollary
6.2
.

Proposition 6.30
.

Suppose
\Omega
is smooth. Then the correlations defined in Definition
6.4
are twice differentiable under movements of spin and disorder insertions. The asymptotic expansions justified in Lemma
6.26
(e.g. (
6.1.3
)) may be differentiated term-by-term to uniquely identify the derivative.

Proof.

First, take the case of
f(a_{1};z):=\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{2}}{\cdots}\sigma_{a_{n}}\mu_{a_{1}}\psi_{z}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{\alpha}}
. For small
t>0
, consider without loss of generality (see points (1) and (2) below for general settings)
d_{t}(z):=f(a_{1}+t;z)-f(a_{1};z)
, which is a massive holomorphic spinor defined on a common double cover of
\Omega\setminus\left(\{a_{2},\ldots,a_{n}\}\cup B_{2t}(a_{1})\right)
.

Write
f^{[w^{\eta}]}(z):=\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}{\cdots}\sigma_{a_{n}}\psi_{z}\psi^{[{\eta}]}_{w}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{\alpha}}
and consider the following variant of
\mathrm{Im}[GR_{2t}(d_{t},f^{[w^{\eta}]})]
(note from (
6.1.14
) that the other contributions are zero) for
0<4t<t_{1}\ll 1
and
0<t_{2}
:

(6.3.6)

\mathrm{Im}\!\left[\oint_{\partial B_{t_{1}}(a_{1})}\!d_{t}\cdot f^{[w^{\eta}]}dz+\oint_{\partial B_{t_{2}}(a_{2})\cup\cdots\cup\partial B_{t_{2}}(a_{n})}\!d_{t}\cdot f^{[w^{\eta}]}dz+\oint_{\partial B_{t_{2}}(w)}\!d_{t}\cdot f^{[w^{\eta}]}dz\right]\!=0,

where, letting
t_{2}\downarrow 0
, the second term becomes purely real by (D) and the third term tends to
2\pi i\bar{\eta}d_{t}(w)
. Therefore, taking
\eta=1,i
, we can bound the imaginary and real parts of
d_{t}(w)
, respectively, using
\mathrm{Im}[\oint_{B_{t_{1}}(a_{1})}d_{t}\cdot f^{[w^{\eta}]}dz]
.

In particular, if we divide
d_{t}
by a renormalization factor
M_{t}>0
such that
\int_{\partial B_{t_{1}}(a_{1})}\frac{|d_{t}|}{M_{t}}|dz|\equiv 1
, we get a subsequential
\alpha
-massive limit
g
to which
d_{t}/M_{t}
converges uniformly on compact subsets of
\Omega\setminus(B_{t_{1}}(a_{1})\cup\{a_{2},\ldots,a_{n}\})
as
t\downarrow 0
(see Remark
6.3
) with
\int_{\partial B_{t_{1}}(a_{1})}|g||dz|=1
. In addition, since
d_{t}/M_{t}
has the (S) asymptotic at every
a_{2},\ldots,a_{n}
and the (RH) boundary condition,
g
has (S) asymptotic at those points also (e.g. use dominated convergence on (
6.3.1
)) and the (RH) boundary condition. Also note that the convergence
f(a_{1}+t;\cdot)\to f(a_{1};\cdot)
as
t\downarrow 0
, uniformly on compact subsets of
\Omega\setminus\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\}
, may be done using similar precompactness arguments (i.e. use the uniform-on-compacts bound from Corollary
6.24
to exhibit a subsequential limit; by the same dominated convergence argument, the limit must have a (D) asymptotic at
a_{1}
, (S) at
a_{2},\ldots,a_{n}
, and the (RH) boundary condition).

We first show that
M_{t}=O(t)
. For contradiction, assume
M_{t}/t\to\infty
, possibly along a subsequence. For small enough
t_{1}
, we consider instead

d_{t}^{\dagger}=\left(f(a_{1}+t;z)+e^{-\frac{i\pi}{4}}(z-a_{1}-t)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\right)-\left(f(a_{1};z)+e^{-\frac{i\pi}{4}}(z-a_{1})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\right)=:d_{t}-D_{t},

which satisfies
M_{t}^{-1}d_{t}^{\dagger}\to g
on
\Omega\setminus B_{t_{1}}(a_{1})
, since
M_{t}^{-1}D_{t}\to 0
uniformly on compact subsets under the assumption. Instead of
f^{[w^{\eta}]}(z)=\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}{\cdots}\sigma_{a_{n}}\psi_{z}\psi^{[{\eta}]}_{w}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{\alpha}}
, take
a spinor
g^{[w^{\eta}]}
which is
\alpha
-massive in
B_{2t_{1}}(a_{1})\setminus\{w\}
and satisfies

g^{[w^{\eta}]}(z)\sim\frac{\bar{\eta}}{z-w}\quad\text{ and }\quad c(t_{1},\kappa_{\infty})\leq\frac{g^{[w^{\eta}]}(z)}{|\eta||z-a_{1}|^{1/2}|w-a_{1}|^{-1/2}|z-w|^{-1}}\leq C(t_{1},\kappa_{\infty}).

We will show the existence of such a spinor in Lemma
6.31
. Write
B_{t}:=B_{2t_{1}}(a_{1})\setminus B_{4t}(a_{1})
, then we may verify the two-sided identity

\displaystyle\mathrm{Im}\bigg[\oint_{\partial B_{2t_{1}}(a_{1})}d_{t}^{\dagger}\cdot g^{[w^{\eta}]}dz-2\pi i\bar{\eta}d_{t}^{\dagger}(w)\bigg]

\displaystyle=\lim_{t_{2}\downarrow 0}\,\mathrm{Im}\bigg[\oint_{\partial B_{2t_{1}}(a_{1})}d_{t}^{\dagger}\cdot g^{[w^{\eta}]}dz-\oint_{\partial B_{4t}(a_{1})}d_{t}^{\dagger}\cdot g^{[w^{\eta}]}dz-\oint_{\partial B_{t_{2}}(w)}d_{t}^{\dagger}\cdot g^{[w^{\eta}]}dz\bigg]

\displaystyle=\,\mathrm{Im}\left[2i\int_{B_{t}}\partial_{\bar{z}}\left(d_{t}^{\dagger}\cdot g^{[w^{\eta}]}\right)d^{2}z\right]

\displaystyle=\,\mathrm{Im}\left[2i\int_{B_{t}}\partial_{\bar{z}}\left((-D_{t})\cdot g^{[w^{\eta}]}\right)d^{2}z\right]

\displaystyle=\,\mathrm{Im}\left[2\int_{B_{t}}\alpha\cdot{D_{t}}\cdot\overline{g^{[w^{\eta}]}}d^{2}z\right],

where
0<4t<|w-a_{1}|<2t_{1}
and
d^{\dagger}_{t}(z)=O(|z-a_{1}|^{1/2})
by Lemma
6.28
. Since we also have
D_{t}=O(t|z-a_{1}|^{-3/2})
in
B_{t}
, dividing by
M_{t}
and applying (versions of) the dominated convergence theorem (as
t\downarrow 0
), we see in fact by the definition of
g(w)
that the convergence
M_{t}^{-1}d_{t}(w),M_{t}^{-1}d_{t}^{\dagger}(w)\xrightarrow{t\downarrow 0}g(w)
may be extended to any fixed
w\in B_{2t_{1}}(a_{1})\setminus\{a_{1}\}
, where we have

(6.3.7)

\mathrm{Im}\left[2\pi i\bar{\eta}g(w)\right]=\mathrm{Im}\bigg[\oint_{\partial B_{2t_{1}}(a_{1})}g\cdot g^{[w^{\eta}]}dz\bigg]=O(|w-a_{1}|^{-1/2}).

Therefore,
g\in V_{\alpha;-\frac{1}{2}}^{\Omega}
. Write
o(t):=o_{-\frac{3}{2}}(a_{1}+t;f(a_{1}+t;z))\equiv 0
as integrals using (
6.3.1
), then note that

(6.3.8)

0\equiv M_{t}^{-1}\left(o(t)-o(0)\right)\xrightarrow{t\downarrow 0}o_{-\frac{3}{2}}(a_{1};g)-\tfrac{1}{2}o_{-\frac{1}{2}}(a_{1};f(a_{1};\cdot)).

Since
o_{-\frac{3}{2}}(a_{1};g)=0
and
o_{-\frac{1}{2}}(a_{1};f(a_{1};\cdot))=1
, we have a contradiction, hence
M_{t}=O(t)
.

Since there is still a subsequential
\alpha
-massive limit
g
as
t\downarrow 0
outside of
B_{t_{1}}(a_{1})
, it would remain to show that we can still extend
g
everywhere and uniquely characterize it by the term-by-term differentiation coming from (
6.1.3
). Inspecting the previous argument from (
6.3.6
), we cannot disregard
t^{-1}D_{t}
anymore:
t^{-1}d^{\dagger}_{t}(z)
converges to
g^{\dagger}(z):=g(z)+\frac{e^{\frac{i\pi}{4}}}{2(z-a)^{3/2}}
, in particular
g
may be extended to
B_{2t_{1}}(a_{1})\setminus\{a_{1}\}
. So, instead of (
6.3.7
), we have

\mathrm{Im}\left[2\pi i\bar{\eta}g^{\dagger}(w)\right]=\mathrm{Im}\Bigg[\oint_{\partial B_{2t_{1}}(a_{1})}g^{\dagger}(z)\cdot g^{[w^{\eta}]}(z)dz+2\int_{B_{2t_{1}}(a_{1})}\alpha(z)\cdot\frac{e^{\frac{i\pi}{4}}\overline{g^{[w^{\eta}]}(z)}}{2(z-a)^{3/2}}d^{2}z\Bigg],

which is still
O(|w-a_{1}|^{-1})
. In particular,
g^{\dagger}
is strictly smaller than the explicit singularity
\frac{e^{\frac{i\pi}{4}}}{2(w-a)^{3/2}}
, and
g\in V_{\alpha;-\frac{3}{2}}^{\Omega}
(and,
g
is not identically zero). The unique identification of the factors
o_{-\frac{3}{2}}(a_{1};g)
and
o_{-\frac{1}{2}}(a_{1};g)
as those coming from term-by-term differentiation can then be performed as in (
6.3.8
).

Now, we carefully track the ingredients in the previous argument to claim that one may carry out similar proofs on other derivatives. We had:

(1)

Explicit identification of the singular part
-e^{-\frac{i\pi}{4}}(z-a_{1})^{-\frac{1}{2}}
, for which differentiability can be shown independently;

(2)

The kernels
f^{[w^{\eta}]},g^{[w^{\eta}]}
, the latter having a
\frac{1}{2}
-order zero at
a_{1}
