, sufficient for dominated convergence against the
\frac{3}{2}
-order pole coming from differentiation of the singular part.

To extend the point (1) for other cases, we note:

•

The singular part of
f(a_{1};z)
at
a_{j}
,
j>1
is
b(a_{j};f(a_{1};\cdot))\cdot e^{-\frac{i\pi}{4}}(z-a_{1})^{-\frac{1}{2}}
, so we need to show that
b(a_{j};f(a_{1};\cdot))
is differentiable as a function of
a_{j}
. By differentiating (
6.3.1
) under the integral sign, we see that it is at least differentiable as a function of
a_{1}
. However, since

b(a_{j};f(a_{1};\cdot))=\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hat{1,j}}}{{\cdots}}\sigma_{a_{n}}\mu_{a_{1}}\mu_{a_{j}}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{\alpha}}

by Proposition
6.15
, we see that it must be differentiable as a function of
a_{j}
by symmetry.

•

For the case
f(z)=\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}{\cdots}\sigma_{a_{n}}\psi_{z}\psi_{w}^{[\eta]}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{\alpha}}
, note that
d_{t}
in this case does not have a pole at
w
and we may use the same argument, given that the
b
-coefficients are given by

b(a_{k};f)=\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hat{k}}}{{\cdots}}\sigma_{a_{n}}\mu_{a_{k}}\psi_{w}^{[\eta]}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{\alpha}},

again by Proposition
6.15
. This is differentiable as a function of
a_{k}
by the previous proof.

•

For the second derivatives, we use the first three terms from Lemma
6.26
as the explicit singular part. Here, we again start from differentiability under movements of disorder insertions, then establish differentiability of relevant singular coefficients, then carry out the same proof.

To address the point (2) for second derivatives, we need a counterpart of
g^{[w^{\eta}]}
with
\frac{3}{2}
-order zero at a given
a
. Therefore, we state and prove the following lemma for both cases.

Lemma 6.31
.

Let
l=\frac{1}{2},\frac{3}{2}
. Then for any
\eta\in\mathbb{C}
and
w\in B_{2t_{1}}(a)
, there exists some
g^{[w^{\eta}]}
in
B_{2t_{1}}(a)
such that

g^{[w^{\eta}]}(z)\sim\frac{\bar{\eta}}{z-w}\quad\text{ and }\quad c(t_{1},\kappa_{\infty})\leq\frac{g^{[w^{\eta}]}(z)}{\left|\frac{(z-a)^{l}\bar{\eta}}{(w-a)^{l}(z-w)}\right|}\leq C(t_{1},\kappa_{\infty}).

Proof of Lemma
6.31
.

Consider the holomorphic function
F(z):=\frac{(z-a)^{l}\bar{\eta}}{(w-a)^{l}(z-w)}
and note that we need a version of Theorem
6.7
where the boundary condition for
s
is replaced by the condition
s(w)=0
: such a result is indeed known, see
[
62
, Theorem 3.13]
or
[
14
, Theorem 3]
.
∎

This concludes the proof of Proposition
6.30
.
∎

A similar but simpler precompactness argument yields the following, noticing that the limit preserves the defining conditions (S), (D), (F), (RH) away from
a_{n}
. The convergence of the
\beta
,
\lambda
-coefficients is again clear from (
6.3.1
). We omit further details.

Proposition 6.32
.

Suppose
a_{n}
is sent to some
a_{b}\in\partial\Omega
. Then

\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\psi_{z}\psi_{w}^{[\eta]}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{\alpha}}\hskip-1.0pt\to\hskip-1.0pt\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n-1}}\psi_{z}\psi_{w}^{[\eta]}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n-1}}\rangle_{\alpha}}\text{ and }\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\mu_{a_{1}}\psi_{z}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{\alpha}}\hskip-1.0pt\to\hskip-1.0pt\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n-1}}\mu_{a_{1}}\psi_{z}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n-1}}\rangle_{\alpha}},

uniformly on compact subsets away from
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n-1}
. In addition, any quantity obtained as
\beta
or
\lambda
of the above correlations (in the sense of Lemma
6.26
) converges to the corresponding quantity of the limit as well.

Corollary 6.33
.

Let
\Omega
be smooth. Define
\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{(\alpha)}(a_{1})
by

(6.3.9)

2\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{(\alpha)}(a_{1})=\lambda\left(z=a_{1};\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\mu_{a_{1}}\psi_{z}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{\alpha}}\right),

in the sense of Lemma
6.26
.
Then
\mathrm{Re}\left[\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{(\alpha)}(a_{1})da_{1}\right]=\mathrm{Re}\left[\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{(\alpha)}(a_{1})\right]dx_{1}-\mathrm{Im}\left[\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{(\alpha)}(a_{1})\right]dy_{1}
is a closed form, where
a_{1}=x_{1}+iy_{1}
.

Under a conformal map
\varphi
defined in
\Omega
, it satisfies the covariance rule

(6.3.10)

\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{(\alpha)}(a_{1})=\mathcal{A}_{\varphi(\Omega)}^{(|(\varphi^{-1})^{\prime}|\cdot(\alpha\circ\varphi^{-1}))}(\varphi(a_{1}))\cdot\varphi^{\prime}(a_{1})+\frac{\varphi^{\prime\prime}(a_{1})}{8\varphi^{\prime}(a_{1})}.

Proof.

Let
f(z)=\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\mu_{a_{1}}\psi_{z}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{\alpha}}
. Then the asymptotics for the derivatives with respect to
a_{1}
, by Proposition
6.30
and only showing the holomorphic terms from Lemma
6.26
and
o_{\frac{3}{2}}=o_{\frac{3}{2}}(a_{1};f)
, are given by

\displaystyle\partial_{x_{1}}f(z)=-e^{-i\pi/4}\bigg(\frac{1}{2(z-a_{1})^{3/2}}-\frac{\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{(\alpha)}(a_{1})}{(z-a_{1})^{1/2}}+\bigg[2\partial_{x_{1}}\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{(\alpha)}(a_{1})+\frac{3}{2}o_{\frac{3}{2}}\bigg](z-a_{1})^{1/2}+\cdots\bigg)

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{1}}f(z)=-e^{-i\pi/4}\bigg(\frac{i}{2(z-a_{1})^{3/2}}-\frac{\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{(\alpha)}(a_{1})i}{(z-a_{1})^{1/2}}+\bigg[2\partial_{y_{1}}\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{(\alpha)}(a_{1})+\frac{3i}{2}o_{\frac{3}{2}}\bigg](z-a_{1})^{1/2}+\cdots\bigg).

Then, as in the proof of Proposition
6.15
,
\mathrm{Im}[GR_{0}(\partial_{x_{a_{1}}}f,\partial_{x_{a_{1}}}f)]=0
and
\mathrm{Im}[GR_{0}(\partial_{y_{a_{1}}}f,\partial_{y_{a_{1}}}f)]\allowbreak=0
yield (the anti-holomorphic terms not contributing a nonzero term)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Im}\!\left[2\partial_{x_{a_{1}}}\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{(\alpha)}(a_{1})+\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{(\alpha)}(a_{1})^{2}+\frac{3}{2}o_{\frac{3}{2}}\right]=0\quad\text{and}\quad\mathrm{Im}\!\left[2i\partial_{y_{a_{1}}}\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{(\alpha)}(a_{1})-\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{(\alpha)}(a_{1})^{2}-\frac{3}{2}o_{\frac{3}{2}}\right]=0,

respectively. Since
\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{(\alpha)}
is twice differentiable, combining the two identities yields the desired closedness. The calculation for the covariance rule is identical to the critical case, given Lemma
6.26
: see, e.g.,
[
20
, (1.10)]
.
∎

6.3.2.
Series expansion and continuity under mass perturbation

We now move to a major analytical goal of this section, which is to show that the Ising correlations with mass
\alpha
scaled by a constant
m\in\mathbb{R}
may be expanded as a power series in
m
around any fixed
m_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
. Such expansion also allows us to study how correlations behave as we perturb the mass function
\alpha
.

For fixed
m_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
, notice that the correlations
f^{[w^{\eta}]}(z):=\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\psi_{z}\psi^{[\eta]}_{w}\rangle_{m_{0}\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{m_{0}\alpha}}
serve as the Green’s functions solving the
\mathbb{R}
-linear equation
\partial_{\bar{z}}f^{[w^{\eta}]}+im_{0}\alpha\overline{f^{[w^{\eta}]}}=\pi\bar{\eta}\delta_{w}
on the double cover of
\Omega\setminus\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\}
with boundary condition (S) and (RH): if
\phi\in L^{q}(B^{\prime})
(
q>1
) is a real function defined in a ball
B^{\prime}
in
\Omega
away from
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}
(for example, a branch of some data
\phi
globally defined on the double cover), for any ball
B
whose closure is contained in
B^{\prime}
we have

\displaystyle\oint_{\partial{B}}\int_{B^{\prime}}\phi(w)\left[f^{[w^{\eta}]}(z)-\frac{\bar{\eta}}{z-w}\right]d^{2}wdz=

\displaystyle\int_{B^{\prime}}\phi(w)\oint_{\partial{B}}\left[f^{[w^{\eta}]}(z)-\frac{\bar{\eta}}{z-w}\right]dzd^{2}w

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{B^{\prime}}\frac{\phi(w)}{2i}\int_{B}\left[-im_{0}\alpha(z)\overline{f^{[w^{\eta}]}(z)}\right]d^{2}zd^{2}w

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{i}{2}\int_{B}im_{0}\alpha(z)\Bigg[\overline{\int_{B^{\prime}}\phi(w)f^{[w^{\eta}]}(z)d^{2}w}\Bigg]d^{2}z,

where we have used Green-Riemann’s theorem on the function
f^{[w^{\eta}]}(z)-\frac{\bar{\eta}}{z-w}\in L^{2}(B^{\prime})
(see Corollary
6.25
, and (
6.1.7
) for the fact that the next singularity must be of strictly smaller order). We may apply the formula since this function is in e.g.
W^{1,\frac{3}{2}}(B)
(where Green-Riemann’s theorem still holds from elementary density arguments and the trace inequality) with weak
\partial_{\bar{z}}
-derivative given by
-im_{0}\alpha\overline{f^{[w^{\eta}]}}
: consider
\mathcal{C}_{B^{\prime}}[-im_{0}\alpha\overline{f^{[w^{\eta}]}}]\in W^{1,\frac{3}{2}}(B^{\prime})
(recall the notation
\mathcal{C}_{B}
from (
6.0.2
)), then
f^{[w^{\eta}]}(z)-\frac{\bar{\eta}}{z-w}-\mathcal{C}_{B^{\prime}}[-im_{0}\alpha\overline{f^{[w^{\eta}]}}](z)\in L^{2}(B^{\prime})
has zero
\partial_{\bar{z}}
-derivative almost everywhere (i.e. holomorphic) in
B^{\prime}
, and thus in
W^{1,\frac{3}{2}}(B)
.

Since
B
was arbitrary, we must have, almost everywhere,

\displaystyle\partial_{\bar{z}}\left[\int_{B^{\prime}}\phi(w)f^{[w^{\eta}]}(z)d^{2}w\right]=

\displaystyle\,\partial_{\bar{z}}\left[\int_{B^{\prime}}\frac{\bar{\eta}\phi(w)}{z-w}d^{2}w\right]-im_{0}\alpha(z)\overline{\int_{B^{\prime}}\phi(w)f^{[w^{\eta}]}(z)d^{2}w}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\pi\bar{\eta}\phi(z)-im_{0}\alpha(z)\overline{\int_{B^{\prime}}\phi(w)f^{[w^{\eta}]}(z)d^{2}w},

as desired.

Accordingly, within correlations, for complex numbers
c=a+bi
define the linear combination (and often write
\psi^{[\star]}
for
\psi^{[1]}
or
\psi^{[i]}
)

(6.3.11)

c\star\psi^{[\star]}:=a\psi^{[\star=1]}-b\psi^{[\star=i]}=\frac{c\psi+\bar{c}\psi^{*}}{2}.

Then we may attempt to solve the equations

\partial_{\bar{z}}A_{p+1}+im_{0}\alpha\overline{A_{p+1}}=-i\alpha\overline{A_{p}}\quad\text{and}\quad\partial_{\bar{z}}B_{p+1}+im_{0}\alpha\overline{B_{p+1}}=-i\alpha\overline{B_{p}},

for
A_{p}(z)=A_{p}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n};z)
, etc., with the initial condition

(6.3.12)

A_{0}(z)=\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\mu_{a_{1}}\psi_{z}\rangle_{m_{0}\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{m_{0}\alpha}}\text{ \quad and \quad}B_{0}(z)=\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\psi_{z}\psi^{[\eta]}_{w}\rangle_{m_{0}\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{m_{0}\alpha}},

by

(6.3.13)

\begin{split}&A_{p+1}(z)=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\Omega}\left[i\alpha(x)\overline{A_{p}(x)}\right]\star\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\psi_{z}\psi^{[\star]}_{x}\rangle_{m_{0}\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{m_{0}\alpha}}d^{2}x,\quad\text{and}\\
&B_{p+1}(z)=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\Omega}\left[i\alpha(x)\overline{B_{p}(x)}\right]\star\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\psi_{z}\psi^{[\star]}_{x}\rangle_{m_{0}\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{m_{0}\alpha}}d^{2}x.\end{split}

We may expect, at least formally, that:

(6.3.14)

\begin{split}\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\mu_{a_{1}}\psi_{z}\rangle_{m\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{m\alpha}}&=\sum_{p=0}^{\infty}A_{p}(z){(-1)^{p}(m-m_{0})^{p}},\quad\text{and}\\
\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\psi_{z}\psi^{[\eta]}_{w}\rangle_{m\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{m\alpha}}&=\sum_{p=0}^{\infty}B_{p}(z){(-1)^{p}(m-m_{0})^{p}},\end{split}

since the right hand sides may be formally differentiated and shown to satisfy the equation
\partial_{\bar{z}}f+im\alpha\bar{f}=0
away from
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n},w
: e.g.

\displaystyle\partial_{\bar{z}}\sum_{p=0}^{\infty}A_{p}\cdot{(-1)^{p}(m-m_{0})^{p}}

\displaystyle=-im_{0}\alpha\sum_{p=0}^{\infty}\overline{A_{p}}\cdot{(-1)^{p}(m-m_{0})^{p}}-i\alpha\sum_{p=1}^{\infty}\overline{A_{p-1}}\cdot{(-1)^{p}(m-m_{0})^{p}}

\displaystyle=-im\alpha\sum_{p=0}^{\infty}\overline{A_{p}}\cdot{(-1)^{p}(m-m_{0})^{p}}.

We show that this is indeed the case. First note the bounds we already have for the integrands.

Remark 6.34
.

Note that the definition (
6.3.13
) implies that (with
\mathfrak{A}_{0}(z):=A_{0}(z)
) there is some function
\mathfrak{A}_{p}(u,z)=\mathfrak{A}_{p}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n};u,z)
such that the following equality is true at the level of integrands (note
\mathfrak{A}_{p-1}(u)=\mathfrak{A}_{p-1}(u_{1},\ldots,u_{p-1},u_{p})
with
u_{p}
taking the place of
z
) for
p\geq 1
:

(6.3.15)

A_{p}(z)=\int_{\Omega^{p}}\mathfrak{A}_{p}(u,z)d^{2p}u=\int_{\Omega^{p}}\frac{1}{\pi}\left[i\alpha(u_{p})\overline{\mathfrak{A}_{p-1}(u)}\right]\star\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\psi_{z}\psi^{[\star]}_{u_{p}}\rangle_{m_{0}\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{m_{0}\alpha}}d^{2p}u,

for
u=(u_{1},u_{2},\ldots,u_{p})\in\Omega^{p}
. Then we have for all
p\geq 0
(we set
z=u_{p+1}
)

(6.3.16)

\displaystyle\left|\mathfrak{A}_{p}(u,u_{p+1})\right|

\displaystyle\leq C^{p}\left|\frac{\langle\mu_{a_{1}}\sigma_{a_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\psi_{u_{1}}\rangle_{m_{0}\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{m_{0}\alpha}}\right|\prod_{k=1}^{p}\bigg|\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\psi_{u_{k+1}}\psi^{[\star]}_{u_{k}}\rangle_{m_{0}\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{m_{0}\alpha}}\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{C^{p}}{\sqrt{|u_{1}-a_{j}|}}\prod_{k=1}^{p}\Bigg(\sum_{j,j^{\prime}=1}^{n}\frac{1}{|u_{k}-a_{j}|}\Bigg(\frac{\sqrt{\operatorname{dist}(a_{j^{\prime}},\partial\Omega)}}{\sqrt{|u_{k+1}-a_{j^{\prime}}|}}+1\Bigg)+\frac{1}{|u_{k}-u_{k+1}|}\Bigg),

for
C=C(\kappa_{\infty},n,M,\Omega,\delta_{0})
, since by Corollaries
6.24
and
6.25
we have

\left|\frac{\langle\mu_{a_{1}}\sigma_{a_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\psi_{u_{1}}\rangle_{m_{0}\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{m_{0}\alpha}}\right|\leq C\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{1}{\sqrt{|u_{1}-a_{j}|}},

and

(6.3.17)

\bigg|\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\psi_{u_{k+1}}\psi^{[\star]}_{u_{k}}\rangle_{m_{0}\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{m_{0}\alpha}}\bigg|\leq C\sum_{j,j^{\prime}=1}^{n}\frac{1}{|u_{k}-a_{j}|}\frac{\sqrt{\operatorname{dist}(a_{j^{\prime}},\partial\Omega)}}{\sqrt{|u_{k+1}-a_{j^{\prime}}|}}\\
+C\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{1}{\sqrt{|u_{k}-a_{j}|}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{|u_{k}-u_{k+1}|}}+\frac{C}{|u_{k}-u_{k+1}|},

and we use the AM-GM inequality
\frac{2}{\sqrt{|u_{k}-a_{j}|}\sqrt{|u_{k}-u_{k+1}|}}\leq\frac{1}{{|u_{k}-a_{j}|}}+\frac{1}{{|u_{k}-u_{k+1}|}}
.

Similarly, we have

(6.3.18)

B_{p}(z)\!=\!\int_{\Omega^{p}}\!\mathfrak{B}_{p}(w;u,z)d^{2p}u=\!\int_{\Omega^{p}}\!\frac{1}{\pi}\left[i\alpha(u_{p})\overline{\mathfrak{B}_{p-1}(w;u)}\right]\star\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\psi_{z}\psi^{[\star]}_{u_{p}}\rangle_{m_{0}\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{m_{0}\alpha}}d^{2p}u,

with (we set
u_{0}=w
and again use Corollary
6.25
, but in a slightly different form from (
6.3.17
))

(6.3.19)

\left|\mathfrak{B}_{p}(u_{0};u,u_{p+1})\right|\leq{C^{p}|\eta|}\cdot\prod_{k=0}^{p}\Bigg(\sum_{j,j^{\prime}=1}^{n}\frac{1}{\sqrt{|u_{k}-a_{j}|}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{|u_{k+1}-a_{j^{\prime}}|}}\\
+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{1}{\sqrt{|u_{k}-a_{j}|}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{|u_{k}-u_{k+1}|}}+\frac{1}{|u_{k}-u_{k+1}|}\Bigg).

Proposition 6.35
.

Let
\Omega
be smooth
(let
\delta_{w}=1
in case of
A_{p}
). There is a constant
c=c(M,\Omega,\kappa_{\infty},n,\delta_{0},\delta_{w})>0
such that the two series (
6.3.14
) converge absolutely, uniformly on compact subsets of
\Omega\cup\partial\Omega
away from
a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{n},w
, for
m\in(m_{0}-c,m_{0}+c)
.

Proof.

We first show that the series converges. The following lemma gives a useful uniform bound.

Lemma 6.36
.

For
p\geq 1
and for
C=C(M,\Omega,\kappa_{\infty},n,\delta_{0})
, we have

(6.3.20)

\displaystyle|A_{p}(z)|\leq\int_{\Omega^{p}}\left|\mathfrak{A}_{p}(u,z)\right|d^{2p}u

\displaystyle\leq C^{p}\Bigg[1+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{\sqrt{\operatorname{dist}(a_{j},\partial\Omega)}}{\sqrt{|z-a_{j}|}}\Bigg],

\displaystyle|B_{1}(z)|\leq\int_{\Omega}|\mathfrak{B}_{1}(w;u,z)|d^{2}u

\displaystyle\leq C|\eta|\Bigg[\sum_{j,j^{\prime}=1}^{n}\frac{1}{\sqrt{|z-a_{j}||w-a_{j^{\prime}}|}}+\left|\log|z-w|\right|\Bigg],

\displaystyle|B_{p}(z)|\leq\int_{\Omega^{p}}|\mathfrak{B}_{p}(w;u,z)|d^{2p}u

\displaystyle\leq C^{p}|\eta|\Bigg[\sum_{j,j^{\prime}=1}^{n}\frac{1}{\sqrt{|z-a_{j}||w-a_{j^{\prime}}|}}\Bigg]\text{ for }p\geq 2.

Proof.

These bounds directly follow by integrating (
6.3.16
) and (
6.3.19
) sequentially from
u_{1}
to
u_{p}
(e.g. inductively showing that they integrate to (
6.3.20
)), using the uniform integral bound

\int_{\Omega}\frac{1}{|u-a|^{\rho}|u-a^{\prime}|^{\rho^{\prime}}}d^{2}u\leq C(\rho,\rho^{\prime},\Omega),

for
\rho+\rho^{\prime}<2
. The only time
\rho+\rho^{\prime}=2
happens is for
B_{1}
, where we have the contribution

\int_{\Omega}\frac{1}{|u_{0}-u_{1}||u_{1}-u_{2}|}d^{2}u_{1}\leq C(\Omega)\left(1+|\log|u_{0}-u_{2}||\right),

giving rise to the logarithmic term.
∎

Let us keep presenting the
f(z)=\sum_{p=0}^{\infty}B_{p}(z){(-1)^{p}(m-m_{0})^{p}}
case without loss of generality. Then, from (
6.3.20
) note that, on compact subsets
z\in\Omega^{\prime}\Subset(\Omega\cup\partial\Omega)\setminus\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{n},w\}
, we have for
p\geq 1

(6.3.21)

|B_{p}(z)|\leq C(\operatorname{dist}(\Omega^{\prime},\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{n},w\}))\cdot C(M,\Omega,\kappa_{\infty},n,\delta_{0},\delta_{w})^{p}|\eta|.

So there is a radius
c=c(M,\Omega,\kappa_{\infty},n,\delta_{0},\delta_{w})>0
where both series converge absolutely, uniformly on compacts.

Now, given any smooth function
\phi
with compact support away from
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n},w
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}f\phi_{\bar{z}}d^{2}z

\displaystyle=\sum_{p=0}^{\infty}\left[\int_{\Omega}B_{p}\phi_{\bar{z}}d^{2}z\right]{(-1)^{p}(m-m_{0})^{p}}=\sum_{p=0}^{\infty}\left[\int_{\Omega}-\phi\cdot\partial_{\bar{z}}B_{p}d^{2}z\right]{(-1)^{p}(m-m_{0})^{p}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\phi\Bigg[im\alpha\overline{\sum_{p=0}^{\infty}B_{p}\cdot{(-1)^{p}(m-m_{0})^{p}}}\Bigg]d^{2}z=\int_{\Omega}\phi\left[im\alpha\overline{f}\right]d^{2}z,

where we used Fubini and integrated by parts using (
6.1.9
), leveraging the formal calculation. So
f
solves
\partial_{\bar{z}}f=-im\alpha\bar{f}
away from
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n},w
.

Given the bound (
6.3.20
), we see that
f\in V^{m\alpha}_{\Omega}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n};w)
. We know that the singularity at
w
is not perturbed in the highest order. In addition, by definition, we have

(6.3.22)

-e^{i\pi/4}\lim_{z\to a_{j}}\mathfrak{B}_{p}(w;u,z)\cdot(z-a_{j})^{1/2}\\
=\frac{1}{\pi}\left[i\alpha(u_{p})\overline{\mathfrak{B}_{p-1}(w;u)}\right]\star\beta\bigg(z=a_{j};\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\psi_{z}\psi^{[\star]}_{u_{p}}\rangle_{m_{0}\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{m_{0}\alpha}}\bigg)\in i\mathbb{R}.

We may exchange the limit
-e^{i\pi/4}\lim_{z\to a_{j}}f(z)\cdot(z-a_{j})^{1/2}
with the sum and the integral using dominated convergence; so we have
\beta(a_{j};f)\in i\mathbb{R}
. This uniquely identifies the limit (Corollary
6.14
), therefore (
6.3.14
) holds.
∎

Remark 6.37
.

In fact, tracking the integration of bounds (
6.3.16
) and (
6.3.19
) from the proof of Lemma
6.36
more carefully, we may easily note that
A_{p},B_{p}
are continuous functions of the insertion points
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}
and (for the
B_{p}
case)
w
, away from one another and the boundary.

For example, if the only insertion point was
a_{1}
, we may do a change of variable
u_{k}-a_{1}=:u_{k}^{\prime}
and offload the dependence on
a_{1}
onto the integration domain; then it is easy to see from dominated convergence (and the fact that the integrands themselves are locally continuous functions of the insertion points, see Proposition
6.30
) that the value of the integral depends continuously on
a_{1}
, away from the boundary. Given more insertion points far apart from one another and the boundary, we may partition
\Omega
into subdomains in each of which there is one insertion point only and apply the dominated convergence argument.

Now, we specialize to the case of the series expansion for the logarithmic derivatives.

Corollary 6.38
.

Consider the functions
A_{p}
in (
6.3.13
) for
p\geq 1
. Then there are some complex numbers
\beta_{(p)}:=\beta(a_{1};A_{p})\in i\mathbb{R},\,\beta^{\prime}_{(p)}:=\beta^{\prime}(a_{1};A_{p})\in i\mathbb{R}\,,\lambda_{(p)}=\lambda(a_{1};A_{p})
such that as
z\to a_{1}

(6.3.23)

A_{p}(z)=-e^{-\frac{i\pi}{4}}\left(\beta_{(p)}\cdot(z-a_{1})^{-\frac{1}{2}}+\lambda_{(p)}\cdot({z-a_{1}})^{\frac{1}{2}}+\beta^{\prime}_{(p)}\cdot(\overline{z-a_{1}})^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)+o(|z-a_{1}|^{\frac{1}{2}}).

In addition, if
0<2d\leq\min\big(\operatorname{dist}(a_{1},\partial\Omega),\delta_{0}\big)
, there is a constant
C=C(M,\Omega,\kappa_{\infty},n,\delta_{0},\kappa_{b})
such that in
B_{d/16}(a_{1})

(6.3.24)

\displaystyle\left|A_{p}(z)+e^{-\frac{i\pi}{4}}\beta_{(p)}\cdot(z-a_{1})^{-\frac{1}{2}}+e^{-\frac{i\pi}{4}}\beta^{\prime}_{(p)}\cdot(\overline{z-a_{1}})^{\frac{1}{2}}\right|\leq C^{p}d^{-\frac{1}{2}}|z-a_{1}|^{\frac{1}{2}},

and in fact

(6.3.25)

|\beta_{(p)}|\leq C^{p}d^{\frac{1}{2}},\qquad|\beta_{(p)}^{\prime}|\leq C^{p}d^{\frac{1}{2}},\qquad\text{and}\qquad|\lambda_{(p)}|\leq C^{p}d^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

Proof.

Note that
A_{0}
also satisfies (
6.3.23
) and (
6.3.24
) by Lemmas
6.26
and
6.28
(the phase condition on
\beta_{(p)}
and (
6.3.25
) do not hold for
A_{0}
).

Then, we can use an argument similar to the proof of Lemma
6.28
, showing these bounds inductively. Let
p\geq 1
. By (
6.3.20
) and the induction hypothesis (or the equivalent bounds for
A_{0}
), we know that
(z-a_{1})^{1/2}A_{p}(z)
satisfies, in
B_{d}(a_{1})
,
|(z-a_{1})^{1/2}A_{p}(z)|\leq C^{p}d^{\frac{1}{2}}
and

|\partial_{\bar{z}}[(z-a_{1})^{1/2}A_{p}(z)]|=|\alpha(z)\cdot(z-a_{1})^{1/2}A_{p-1}(z)|\leq C^{p}.

Thus we have by Corollary
6.2
, with
q=8
,
\|\sqrt{z-a_{1}}\cdot A_{p}(z)\|_{C^{3/4}(B_{d/2}(a_{j}))}\leq C^{p}d^{-\frac{1}{4}}
, so

\big|\sqrt{z-a_{1}}\cdot A_{p}(z)+e^{-i\pi/4}\beta_{(p)}\big|\leq C^{p}d^{-\frac{1}{4}}|z-a_{1}|^{\frac{3}{4}},

for some
\beta_{(p)}
, as desired. Then (
6.3.20
) shows the corresponding bound in (
6.3.25
) and a calculation akin to (
6.3.22
) shows that
\beta_{(p)}\in i\mathbb{R}
.

For the remaining part (i.e. the existence of
\lambda_{(p)}
and
\beta^{\prime}_{(p)}
in (
6.3.23
) and the corresponding estimates), write, with some
\beta^{\prime}_{(p)}
to be specified later,

A_{p}^{\dagger}(z):=A_{p}(z)+e^{-i\pi/4}\beta_{(p)}\cdot(z-a_{1})^{-1/2}+e^{-i\pi/4}\beta^{\prime}_{(p)}\cdot(\overline{z-a_{1}})^{1/2}

and compute in
B_{d/2}(a_{1})

\displaystyle\bigg|\partial_{\bar{z}}\frac{A_{p}^{\dagger}(z)}{\sqrt{z-a_{1}}}\bigg|=\bigg|\frac{-i\alpha(z)(\overline{m_{0}A_{p}(z)+A_{p-1}(z)})+\frac{e^{-i\pi/4}}{2}\beta^{\prime}_{(p)}\cdot(\overline{z-a_{1}})^{-1/2}}{\sqrt{z-a_{1}}}\bigg|.

Since
\left|A_{p}(z)+e^{-i\pi/4}\beta_{(p)}\cdot(z-a_{1})^{-1/2}\right|\leq C^{p}d^{-\frac{1}{4}}|z-a_{1}|^{\frac{1}{4}}
and better bounds hold for
A_{p-1}
by the induction hypothesis, we may set
\beta^{\prime}_{(p)}=-2i\alpha(a_{1})(m_{0}\overline{\beta_{(p)}}+\overline{\beta_{(p-1)}})\in i\mathbb{R}
(note that this choice implies the bound for
\beta^{\prime}_{(p)}
in (
6.3.25
)) and carry out the argument as in the proof of Lemma
6.28
, i.e., it is already easy to see that the expansion (
6.3.23
) holds with
some

\lambda_{(p)}
.

To give a concrete bound, note that we have

\bigg|\frac{A_{p}^{\dagger}(z)}{\sqrt{z-a_{1}}}\bigg|\leq C^{p}d^{-\frac{1}{4}}|z-a_{1}|^{-\frac{1}{4}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\bigg|\partial_{\bar{z}}\frac{A_{p}^{\dagger}(z)}{\sqrt{z-a_{1}}}\bigg|\leq C^{p}d^{-\frac{1}{4}}|z-a_{1}|^{-\frac{1}{4}}.

Then Corollary
6.2
(with
q=3
) gives, say, in
B_{d/4}(a_{j})
,

\bigg|\frac{A_{p}^{\dagger}(z)}{\sqrt{z-a_{1}}}\bigg|\leq C^{p}(d^{\frac{1}{3}+\frac{1}{3}\cdot\frac{3}{2}}+d^{-\frac{1}{2}})

as desired.
∎

Corollary 6.39
.

Let
\lambda(a_{j};f)
be defined as in Lemma
6.26
. Recall the definition

2\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{(m\alpha)}(a_{1})=\lambda\left(z=a_{1};\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\mu_{a_{1}}\psi_{z}\rangle_{m\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{m\alpha}}\right),

then, there is
c=c(M,\Omega,\kappa_{\infty},n,\delta_{0},\kappa_{b})
such that for
m\in(m_{0}-c,m_{0}+c)
, we have

(6.3.26)

2\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{(m\alpha)}(a_{1})=\sum_{p=0}^{\infty}\lambda\left(a_{1};A_{p}\right){(-1)^{p}(m-m_{0})^{p}}.

In particular, we have

(6.3.27)

\left|\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{(m\alpha)}(a_{1})-\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{(m_{0}\alpha)}(a_{1})\right|\leq\frac{C(M,\Omega,\kappa_{\infty},n,\delta_{0},\kappa_{b})}{d^{\frac{1}{2}}}.

If
a_{b}\in\partial\Omega
,

(6.3.28)

\int_{a_{b}}^{a_{1}}\Big(\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{(m\alpha)}(a_{1}^{\prime})-\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{(m_{0}\alpha)}(a_{1}^{\prime})\Big)da_{1}^{\prime}=\sum_{p=1}^{\infty}{(-1)^{p}(m-m_{0})^{p}}\int_{a_{b}}^{a_{1}}\frac{1}{2}\lambda\left(a_{1}^{\prime};A_{p}\right)da_{1}^{\prime}.

In fact, we have

(6.3.29)

\left|\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{(\alpha)}(a_{1})-\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{(0)}(a_{1})\right|\leq\frac{C(\Omega,\kappa_{\infty},n,\delta_{0},\kappa_{b})}{d^{\frac{1}{2}}}.

Proof.

Using Cauchy’s formula (
6.3.1
) and the bounds (
6.3.24
) and (
6.3.25
), the first two statements follow from Fubini and dominated convergence.

The third statement follows since each coefficient with
p\geq 1
in the series expansion for
\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{(m\alpha)}(a_{1})
obeys the
d^{-\frac{1}{2}}
bound of the desired form by (
6.3.25
), and the fourth follows by another application of Fubini.

The fifth statement (
6.3.29
) follows by setting
M=2
and adding, say, at most
2/c
copies of the third estimate from
0
to
m_{0}=1
, since the radius of convergence around any point in
[0,2)
is bounded below by
c
.
∎

Lemma 6.40
.

Suppose
\alpha_{N}
and
\alpha
all satisfy the following bounds for a fixed
\kappa_{b}
:

\operatorname{dist}(z,\partial\Omega)^{2}|\nabla\alpha_{N}(z)|\leq\kappa_{b}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\operatorname{dist}(z,\partial\Omega)^{2}|\nabla\alpha(z)|\leq\kappa_{b}.

Let
\alpha_{N}\to\alpha
pointwise. Then the basic correlations converge: for fixed
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n},w,z,m,\eta
,

\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\psi_{z}\psi^{[\eta]}_{w}\rangle_{\alpha_{N}}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{\alpha_{N}}}\to\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\psi_{z}\psi^{[\eta]}_{w}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{\alpha}}\text{;}\qquad\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\mu_{a_{1}}\psi_{z}\rangle_{\alpha_{N}}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{\alpha_{N}}}\to\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\mu_{a_{1}}\psi_{z}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{\alpha}}.

As a result, all correlations obtained as coefficients
\beta,\lambda
of the above correlations (in the sense of Lemma
6.26
) also converge.

As a consequence, we have

\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{\alpha_{N}}\to\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{\alpha},

where this quantity is calculated recursively from (
6.1.4
) with fixed choices and ordering of paths
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n}
, connecting some boundary points
a_{b1},\dots,a_{bn}
to
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}
, respectively.

Proof.

Replace
\alpha
by
m\alpha
. The pointwise convergence of basic correlations under
\alpha_{N}\to\alpha
within any single radius of convergence of the series expansion provided by Proposition
6.35
follows from dominated convergence. Then, as in the proof of Corollary
6.39
, we may find a finite sequence of masses
0=m_{0}<m_{1}<\cdots<m_{K}=1
such that each
m_{i+1}
is in the radius of convergence around
m_{i}
, to inductively extend the convergence to
m=1
.

The convergence of functions in the bulk implies the coefficient convergence, using dominated convergence on (
6.3.1
) as usual. Then, we may use the bound in Corollary
6.39
to again use dominated convergence as
\alpha_{N}\to\alpha
and obtain pure spin correlation convergence.
∎

6.3.3.
Well-Definedness of Pure Spin Correlations

We finish this section by removing the implicit dependence on the ordering and paths used for the pure spin correlation in Lemma
6.40
, i.e., showing that the closed forms
\mathrm{Re}[\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{(\alpha)}(a_{1})da_{1}]
(see Corollary
6.33
) and the recursive definition (
6.1.4
) indeed give rise to a well-defined spin correlation. Concretely, we need to check that the correlations do not have a monodromy around any other spin
a_{2},\ldots,a_{n}
, and do not depend on the boundary point chosen as the starting point of the line integral or the order in which we add the spins.

Let us first point out the general strategy for extracting (differences of) forms
\mathrm{Re}[\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{(\alpha)}(a_{1})]da_{1}
. Suppose we write,
f_{n}(z)=\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\mu_{a_{1}}\psi_{z}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{\alpha}}
,
h_{n}=\mathrm{Im}[\int f^{2}_{n}dz]
,
\mathcal{A}_{n}(a_{1}):=\frac{1}{2}\lambda(a_{1};f_{n})
, etc., for fixed distinct points
a_{2},a_{3},\ldots
in a smooth domain
\Omega
. Then it is straightforward to check that

(6.3.30)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{n+1}(a_{1})-\mathcal{A}_{n}(a_{1})=\frac{i}{4}\lim_{z\to a_{1}}\left[f_{n+1}^{2}(z)-f_{n}^{2}(z)\right],

thanks to Lemma
6.26
(specifically, the asymptotic for
f^{\dagger 1}
with
\nu=-1/2
and
o_{\nu}=1
).

Lemma 6.41
.

In the above setting, we have
\mathrm{Re}\left[\int_{\partial B_{r}(a_{2})}\mathcal{A}_{n}(a_{1}^{\prime})da_{1}^{\prime}\right]\xrightarrow{r\downarrow 0}0
for each
n
.

Proof.

Note that for
n=1
we do not have an insertion at
a_{2}
, so the integral is identically zero by Corollary
6.33
. Then, it suffices to show that for each
n
we have

\mathrm{Re}\bigg[\int_{\partial B_{r}(a_{2})}\big(\mathcal{A}_{n+1}(a_{1})-\mathcal{A}_{n}(a_{1})\big)da_{1}\bigg]\xrightarrow{r\downarrow 0}0.

Therefore, let us study the function
(f_{n+1}^{2}-f_{n}^{2})(z)
for
a_{1}\in B_{r_{0}}(a_{2})
for small enough
r_{0}
, so that we may take
\delta_{1}=\frac{1}{16}|a_{1}-a_{2}|
and
a_{3},\ldots,a_{n}\notin B_{4r_{0}}(a_{2})\subset\Omega
. We have an a priori bound for these functions for
z\in B_{2r_{0}}(a_{2})\cup\partial B_{2r_{0}}(a_{2})
thanks to Corollary
6.24
and Proposition
6.23
(take
\nu=1/4
in the latter):

(6.3.31)

\left|f_{n}(z)\right|\leq C(\kappa_{\infty},n,\Omega,r_{0})\cdot\bigg({\frac{|a_{1}-a_{2}|^{-1/4}}{|z-a_{2}|^{1/2}}+\frac{1}{|z-a_{1}|^{1/2}}}\bigg).

In addition, since
f_{n+1}-f_{n}
is an
\alpha
-holomorphic spinor whose blow-up at
a_{1}
is canceled, we may use Theorem
6.7
to write

f_{n+1}(z)-f_{n}(z)=e^{s(z)}\underline{f}(z),

for a holomorphic spinor
\underline{f}
and some
s
satisfying
|s|\leq C(\kappa_{\infty},r_{0})
. We know
\underline{f}^{2}
is a meromorphic function which has at most a simple pole at
a_{2}
, zero at
a_{1}
, and still satisfies a bound coming from (
6.3.31
) on, say,
\partial B_{2r_{0}}(a_{2})
. The maximum principle on
\frac{z-a_{2}}{z-a_{1}}\cdot\underline{f}^{2}(z)
easily gives, for
z\in B_{2r_{0}}(a_{2})\cup\partial B_{2r_{0}}(a_{2})
,

|f_{n+1}(z)-f_{n}(z)|^{2}=|e^{s(z)}|^{2}|\underline{f}(z)|^{2}\leq C(\kappa_{\infty},n,\Omega,r_{0})\cdot|a_{1}-a_{2}|^{-1/2}\cdot\frac{|z-a_{1}|}{|z-a_{2}|}.

Combining this bound with (
6.3.31
), we have

|f_{n+1}^{2}(z)-f_{n}^{2}(z)|\leq C(\kappa_{\infty},n,\Omega,r_{0})\cdot\frac{|a_{1}-a_{2}|^{-1/2}}{|z-a_{2}|}.

Then the function
(z-a_{1})(f_{n+1}^{2}-f_{n}^{2})(z)
satisfies

\left|(z-a_{2})(f_{n+1}^{2}-f_{n}^{2})(z)\right|\leq C(\kappa_{\infty},n,\Omega,r_{0})\cdot|a_{1}-a_{2}|^{-1/2},

and

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{\bar{z}}\left[(z-a_{2})(f_{n+1}^{2}-f_{n}^{2})(z)\right]\right|

\displaystyle=\left|2i\alpha(z)\cdot(z-a_{1})\cdot(|f_{n}(z)|^{2}-|f_{n+1}(z)|^{2})\right|

\displaystyle\leq C(\kappa_{\infty},n,\Omega,r_{0})\cdot|a_{1}-a_{2}|^{-1/2}.

By Corollary
6.2
(with
q=4
), we have for
z\in B_{r_{0}}(a_{2})

\left|(z-a_{2})(f_{n+1}^{2}-f_{n}^{2})(z)-ib(a_{1})^{2}\right|\leq C(\kappa_{\infty},n,\Omega,r_{0})\cdot|a_{1}-a_{2}|^{-1/2}\cdot|z-a_{2}|^{1/2},

where
ib(a_{1})^{2}:=\lim_{z\to a_{2}}(z-a_{2})(f_{n+1}^{2}-f_{n}^{2})(z)
for
b(a_{1})\in\mathbb{R}
by (S) asymptotic at
a_{2}
for
f_{n},f_{n+1}
.

Finally, using (
6.3.30
), we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Re}\bigg[\int_{\partial B_{r}(a_{2})}\big(\mathcal{A}_{n+1}(a_{1})-\mathcal{A}_{n}(a_{1})\big)da_{1}\bigg]=

\displaystyle\,\mathrm{Re}\bigg[\int_{\partial B_{r}(a_{2})}-\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{b(a_{1})^{2}}{2(a_{2}-a_{1})}+O(1)\right)\,da_{1}\bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\mathrm{Re}\bigg[\int_{0}^{2\pi}-\frac{b(a_{2}+re^{i\theta})^{2}}{4re^{i\theta}}ire^{i\theta}d\theta\bigg]+O(r)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,O(r),

as desired.
∎

Lemma 6.42
.

Suppose
\Omega
is smooth and
\alpha
is smooth up to the boundary. Then

\mathrm{Re}\bigg[\int_{a_{b}}^{a_{1}}\Big(\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{(\alpha)}(a_{1}^{\prime})-\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{(0)}(a_{1}^{\prime})\Big)da_{1}^{\prime}\bigg]

does not depend on the choice of
a_{b}\in\partial\Omega
.

Proof.

It suffices to prove that

\mathrm{Re}\bigg[\int_{a_{b}^{1}}^{a_{b}^{2}}\Big(\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{(\alpha)}(a_{1}^{\prime})-\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{(0)}(a_{1}^{\prime})\Big)da_{1}^{\prime}\bigg]=0

for any
a_{b}^{1},a_{b}^{2}\in\partial\Omega
thanks to closedness.

Let us first assume
\alpha\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
. Thanks to conformal covariance (again from Corollary
6.33
, and note
da_{1}
transforms conformally as well), we may assume that
a_{1}\in B_{R}\cap\mathbb{H}\subset B_{3R}\cap\mathbb{H}\subset\Omega\subset\mathbb{H}
and
a_{2},\ldots,a_{n}\notin B_{3R}
, with segment
[-3R,3R]\subset\partial\Omega\cap\mathbb{R}
. By shrinking
R
if necessary, we may assume
\alpha\equiv 0
in
B_{2R}\cap\mathbb{H}
since
\alpha
has compact support.

Write
f(z)=\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\mu_{a_{1}}\psi_{z}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{\alpha}}
,
f_{(0)}(z)=\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\mu_{a_{1}}\psi_{z}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{0}}
and set
h=\mathrm{Im}[\int f^{2}dz]
,
h_{(0)}=\mathrm{Im}[\int f^{2}_{(0)}dz]
as usual. Then it suffices to show that
\mathrm{Re}\left[\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{(\alpha)}(a_{1})-\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{(0)}(a_{1})\right]
uniformly extends to zero as
a_{1}
approaches
[-R,R]
. As in (
6.3.30
), extract (where
z=x+iy
)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Re}\left[\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{(\alpha)}(a_{1})-\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{(0)}(a_{1})\right]

\displaystyle=\lim_{z\to a_{1}}\frac{1}{4}\mathrm{Im}\left[f^{2}-f_{(0)}^{2}\right](z)=\lim_{z\to a_{1}}\frac{1}{4}\partial_{x}(h(z)-h_{(0)}(z)).

Now, since
\Delta(h-h_{(0)})=0
and
h-h_{(0)}
is continuously differentiable (since the only possible discontinuity of its derivative
f^{2}-f_{(0)}^{2}
at
a_{1}
has been handled),
\partial_{x}(h-h_{(0)})
is also harmonic in
B_{2R}\cap\mathbb{H}
. By Corollary
6.24
, the size of
f^{2}-f_{(0)}^{2}
on
\partial B_{2R}\cap\mathbb{H}
is bounded above, uniformly in
a_{1}\in B_{R}\cap\mathbb{H}
. Due to harmonicity, we have
\partial_{x}(h(z)-h_{(0)}(z))|_{z=a_{1}}=O(\mathrm{Im}[a_{1}])
as
a_{1}
approaches
[-R,R]
, as desired.

Then, one obtains the general statement from Lemma
6.40
, by taking
\alpha_{N}
to be the product of
\alpha
with a suitable sequence of bump functions such that
\alpha_{N}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
and
\alpha_{N}\to\alpha
pointwise.
∎

Thanks to the previous two lemmas, we are able to freely deform the integration contours. It remains to show the following.

Lemma 6.43
.

The correlation
\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{\alpha}
, as defined by (
6.1.4
), does not depend on the order in which
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}
are introduced.

Proof.

Taking the
0
-spin and
1
-spin correlations as the trivial induction base cases, it suffices to check that any ratio

\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\sigma_{a}\sigma_{a^{\dagger}}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{\alpha}}

does not depend on whether
a
or
a^{\dagger}
is introduced first. Examining (
6.1.4
) and the well-definedness of this ratio for
\alpha=0
, we need to show that

\mathrm{Re}\bigg[\int_{a_{b}}^{a}\mathcal{A}^{(\alpha;0)}_{a^{\prime}}da^{\prime}\bigg]+\mathrm{Re}\bigg[\int_{a_{b}^{\dagger}}^{a^{\dagger}}\mathcal{A}^{(\alpha;0)}_{a^{\prime\dagger};a}da^{\prime\dagger}\bigg]=\mathrm{Re}\bigg[\int_{a_{b}^{\dagger}}^{a^{\dagger}}\mathcal{A}^{(\alpha;0)}_{a^{\prime\dagger}}da^{\prime\dagger}\bigg]+\mathrm{Re}\bigg[\int_{a_{b}}^{a}\mathcal{A}^{(\alpha;0)}_{a^{\prime};a^{\dagger}}da^{\prime}\bigg],

where
\mathcal{A}^{(\alpha;0)}_{a^{\prime\dagger};a}:=\mathcal{A}^{(\alpha)}_{a^{\prime\dagger};a}-\mathcal{A}^{(0)}_{a^{\prime\dagger};a}
,

\mathcal{A}^{(\alpha)}_{a^{\prime}}:=\frac{1}{2}\lambda\left(z=a;\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\mu_{a}\psi_{z}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\sigma_{a}\rangle_{\alpha}}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{A}^{(\alpha)}_{a^{\prime};a}:=\frac{1}{2}\lambda\left(z=a^{\prime};\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\sigma_{a}\mu_{a^{\prime}}\psi_{z}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\sigma_{a}\sigma_{a^{\prime}}\rangle_{\alpha}}\right),

etc., and we integrate along some
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\dagger}
connecting
a_{b}
to
a
and
a_{b}^{\dagger}
to
a^{\dagger}
, respectively. We know from Corollary
6.33
and Lemma
6.41
that the integrals do not depend on the deformations of
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\dagger}
that fix the endpoints, so let us fix two concrete paths
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\dagger}
henceforth. In fact, we may write
g_{1}(a,a^{\dagger})
and
g_{2}(a,a^{\dagger})
for the left and right hand sides, respectively, and extend them to functions
g_{1}(a^{\prime},a^{\prime\dagger})
and
g_{2}(a^{\prime},a^{\prime\dagger})
for
a^{\prime}
and
a^{\prime\dagger}
in small neighborhoods of
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\dagger}
, respectively.

Let us show
g_{1}\equiv g_{2}
in that neighborhood. Write
a^{\prime}=x^{\prime}+iy^{\prime}
and
a^{\prime\dagger}=x^{\prime\dagger}+iy^{\prime\dagger}
. It suffices to show that each mixed second derivative coincides, i.e.,

(6.3.32)

\begin{split}&\partial_{x^{\prime}x^{\prime\dagger}}g_{1}(a^{\prime},a^{\prime\dagger})=\partial_{x^{\prime}x^{\prime\dagger}}g_{2}(a^{\prime},a^{\prime\dagger}),\quad\partial_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime\dagger}}g_{1}(a^{\prime},a^{\prime\dagger})=\partial_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime\dagger}}g_{2}(a^{\prime},a^{\prime\dagger}),\\
&\partial_{y^{\prime}x^{\prime\dagger}}g_{1}(a^{\prime},a^{\prime\dagger})=\partial_{y^{\prime}x^{\prime\dagger}}g_{2}(a^{\prime},a^{\prime\dagger}),\quad\partial_{y^{\prime}y^{\prime\dagger}}g_{1}(a^{\prime},a^{\prime\dagger})=\partial_{y^{\prime}y^{\prime\dagger}}g_{2}(a^{\prime},a^{\prime\dagger});\end{split}

or

\displaystyle\partial_{x^{\prime}}\mathrm{Re}[\mathcal{A}^{(\alpha;0)}_{a^{\prime\dagger};a^{\prime}}]=\partial_{x^{\prime\dagger}}\mathrm{Re}[\mathcal{A}^{(\alpha;0)}_{a^{\prime};a^{\prime\dagger}}],\quad-\partial_{x^{\prime}}\mathrm{Im}[\mathcal{A}^{(\alpha;0)}_{a^{\prime\dagger};a^{\prime}}]=\partial_{y^{\prime\dagger}}\mathrm{Re}[\mathcal{A}^{(\alpha;0)}_{a^{\prime};a^{\prime\dagger}}],

\displaystyle\partial_{y^{\prime}}\mathrm{Re}[\mathcal{A}^{(\alpha;0)}_{a^{\prime\dagger};a^{\prime}}]=-\partial_{x^{\prime\dagger}}\mathrm{Im}[\mathcal{A}^{(\alpha;0)}_{a^{\prime};a^{\prime\dagger}}],\quad\partial_{y^{\prime}}\mathrm{Im}[\mathcal{A}^{(\alpha;0)}_{a^{\prime\dagger};a^{\prime}}]=\partial_{y^{\prime\dagger}}\mathrm{Im}[\mathcal{A}^{(\alpha;0)}_{a^{\prime};a^{\prime\dagger}}],

since we may integrate them from the boundary. To elaborate, (
6.3.32
) would imply that the gradients
\nabla_{a^{\prime}}
of
\partial_{x^{\prime\dagger}}g_{1}(a^{\prime},a^{\prime\dagger})
(resp.
\partial_{y^{\prime\dagger}}g_{1}(a^{\prime},a^{\prime\dagger})
) and
\partial_{x^{\prime\dagger}}g_{2}(a^{\prime},a^{\prime\dagger})
(resp.
\partial_{y^{\prime\dagger}}g_{2}(a^{\prime},a^{\prime\dagger})
) are the same, and as
a^{\prime}\to a_{b}
, by Proposition
6.32
, we have the same boundary values

\partial_{x^{\prime\dagger}}g_{1}(a^{\prime},a^{\prime\dagger}),\,\partial_{x^{\prime\dagger}}g_{2}(a^{\prime},a^{\prime\dagger})\to\mathrm{Re}[\mathcal{A}^{(\alpha;0)}_{a^{\prime\dagger}}]\quad\text{and}\quad\partial_{y^{\prime\dagger}}g_{1}(a^{\prime},a^{\prime\dagger}),\,\partial_{y^{\prime\dagger}}g_{2}(a^{\prime},a^{\prime\dagger})\to-\mathrm{Im}[\mathcal{A}^{(\alpha;0)}_{a^{\prime\dagger}}].

By Proposition
6.30
, we may extract

\partial_{x^{\prime}}\mathcal{A}^{(\alpha)}_{a^{\prime\dagger};a^{\prime}}:=\frac{1}{2}\lambda\left(z=a^{\prime\dagger};\partial_{x^{\prime}}\left(\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\sigma_{a^{\prime}}\mu_{a^{\prime\dagger}}\psi_{z}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\sigma_{a^{\prime}}\sigma_{a^{\prime\dagger}}\rangle_{\alpha}}\right)\right),\text{ etc.}.

However, under the same differentiation with respect to
a^{\prime}=x^{\prime}+iy^{\prime}
, note that the fixed singularity at
a^{\prime\dagger}
vanishes: we have, e.g.,

(6.3.33)

\begin{split}\partial_{x^{\prime}}\left(\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\sigma_{a^{\prime}}\mu_{a^{\prime\dagger}}\psi_{z}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\sigma_{a^{\prime}}\sigma_{a^{\prime\dagger}}\rangle_{\alpha}}\right)&=-2\cdot e^{-\frac{i\pi}{4}}\cdot\partial_{x^{\prime}}\mathcal{A}^{(\alpha)}_{a^{\prime\dagger};a^{\prime}}\cdot\sqrt{z-a^{\prime\dagger}}+o(|z-a^{\prime\dagger}|^{1/2}),\\
\partial_{y^{\prime}}\left(\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\sigma_{a^{\prime}}\mu_{a^{\prime\dagger}}\psi_{z}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\sigma_{a^{\prime}}\sigma_{a^{\prime\dagger}}\rangle_{\alpha}}\right)&=-2\cdot e^{-\frac{i\pi}{4}}\cdot\partial_{y^{\prime}}\mathcal{A}^{(\alpha)}_{a^{\prime\dagger};a^{\prime}}\cdot\sqrt{z-a^{\prime\dagger}}+o(|z-a^{\prime\dagger}|^{1/2}),\end{split}

whereas

(6.3.34)

\begin{split}\partial_{x^{\prime}}\left(\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\sigma_{a^{\prime}}\mu_{a^{\prime\dagger}}\psi_{z}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\sigma_{a^{\prime}}\sigma_{a^{\prime\dagger}}\rangle_{\alpha}}\right)&=-\frac{e^{-\frac{i\pi}{4}}}{2}\cdot\beta^{(\alpha)}(a^{\prime};a^{\prime\dagger})\cdot({z-a^{\prime}})^{-3/2}+o(|z-a^{\prime}|^{-3/2}),\\
\partial_{y^{\prime}}\left(\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\sigma_{a^{\prime}}\mu_{a^{\prime\dagger}}\psi_{z}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\sigma_{a^{\prime}}\sigma_{a^{\prime\dagger}}\rangle_{\alpha}}\right)&=-\frac{ie^{-\frac{i\pi}{4}}}{2}\cdot\beta^{(\alpha)}(a^{\prime};a^{\prime\dagger})\cdot({z-a^{\prime}})^{-3/2}+o(|z-a^{\prime}|^{-3/2}),\end{split}

where
\beta^{(\alpha)}(a^{\prime};a^{\prime\dagger})=\beta\left(z=a^{\prime};\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\sigma_{a^{\prime}}\mu_{a^{\prime\dagger}}\psi_{z}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\sigma_{a^{\prime}}\sigma_{a^{\prime\dagger}}\rangle_{\alpha}}\right)=-i\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\mu_{a^{\prime}}\mu_{a^{\prime\dagger}}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\sigma_{a^{\prime}}\sigma_{a^{\prime\dagger}}\rangle_{\alpha}}
by Proposition
6.15
. Analogous formulas hold for the
x^{\prime\dagger},y^{\prime\dagger}
derivatives of
\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\sigma_{a^{\prime\dagger}}\mu_{a^{\prime}}\psi_{z}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\sigma_{a^{\prime}}\sigma_{a^{\prime\dagger}}\rangle_{\alpha}}
, and studying
\mathrm{Im}[GR_{0}]
between the four combinations between the derivatives of either function (see (
6.1.14
) and the proof of Proposition
6.15
) yields

\displaystyle\mathrm{Im}\left[\partial_{x^{\prime}}\mathcal{A}^{(\alpha)}_{a^{\prime\dagger};a^{\prime}}\cdot\beta^{(\alpha)}(a^{\prime\dagger};a^{\prime})+\partial_{x^{\prime\dagger}}\mathcal{A}^{(\alpha)}_{a^{\prime};a^{\prime\dagger}}\cdot\beta^{(\alpha)}(a^{\prime};a^{\prime\dagger})\right]=0;

\displaystyle\mathrm{Im}\left[\partial_{x^{\prime}}\mathcal{A}^{(\alpha)}_{a^{\prime\dagger};a^{\prime}}\cdot i\beta^{(\alpha)}(a^{\prime\dagger};a^{\prime})+\partial_{y^{\prime\dagger}}\mathcal{A}^{(\alpha)}_{a^{\prime};a^{\prime\dagger}}\cdot\beta^{(\alpha)}(a^{\prime};a^{\prime\dagger})\right]=0;

\displaystyle\mathrm{Im}\left[\partial_{y^{\prime}}\mathcal{A}^{(\alpha)}_{a^{\prime\dagger};a^{\prime}}\cdot i\beta^{(\alpha)}(a^{\prime\dagger};a^{\prime})+\partial_{y^{\prime\dagger}}\mathcal{A}^{(\alpha)}_{a^{\prime};a^{\prime\dagger}}\cdot i\beta^{(\alpha)}(a^{\prime};a^{\prime\dagger})\right]=0;

\displaystyle\mathrm{Im}\left[\partial_{y^{\prime}}\mathcal{A}^{(\alpha)}_{a^{\prime\dagger};a^{\prime}}\cdot\beta^{(\alpha)}(a^{\prime\dagger};a^{\prime})+\partial_{x^{\prime\dagger}}\mathcal{A}^{(\alpha)}_{a^{\prime};a^{\prime\dagger}}\cdot i\beta^{(\alpha)}(a^{\prime};a^{\prime\dagger})\right]=0.

Since
\beta^{(\alpha)}(a^{\prime};a^{\prime\dagger})=-\beta^{(\alpha)}(a^{\prime\dagger};a^{\prime})\in i\mathbb{R}
, it is easy to verify (
6.3.32
) if
\beta^{(\alpha)}(a;a^{\dagger})\neq 0
. In fact, thanks to the (second) differentiability of both
\mathcal{A}_{a^{\prime};a^{\prime\dagger}}^{(\alpha)}
and
\beta^{(\alpha)}(a^{\prime};a^{\prime\dagger})
with respect to
a^{\prime},a^{\prime\dagger}
, it suffices that
\beta^{(\alpha)}(a^{\prime};a^{\prime\dagger})
is nonzero for
(a^{\prime},a^{\prime\dagger})
in, e.g., a dense subset of
\Omega^{2}
.

For this genericity, suppose that there is some
a^{\prime\dagger}
and an open neighborhood
D\subset\Omega
such that
\beta^{(\alpha)}(a^{\prime};a^{\prime\dagger})=0
for all
a^{\prime}\in D
. Then, instead of (
6.3.34
), we have the behavior, where
\lambda=\lambda\left(z=a^{\prime};\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\sigma_{a^{\prime}}\mu_{a^{\prime\dagger}}\psi_{z}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\sigma_{a^{\prime}}\sigma_{a^{\prime\dagger}}\rangle_{\alpha}}\right)
,

\displaystyle\partial_{x^{\prime}}\left(\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\sigma_{a^{\prime}}\mu_{a^{\prime\dagger}}\psi_{z}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\sigma_{a^{\prime}}\sigma_{a^{\prime\dagger}}\rangle_{\alpha}}\right)=\frac{e^{-\frac{i\pi}{4}}}{2}\cdot\lambda\cdot({z-a^{\prime\dagger}})^{-1/2}+o(|z-a^{\prime\dagger}|^{-1/2}),

\displaystyle\partial_{y^{\prime}}\left(\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\sigma_{a^{\prime}}\mu_{a^{\prime\dagger}}\psi_{z}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\sigma_{a^{\prime}}\sigma_{a^{\prime\dagger}}\rangle_{\alpha}}\right)=\frac{ie^{-\frac{i\pi}{4}}}{2}\cdot\lambda\cdot({z-a^{\prime\dagger}})^{-1/2}+o(|z-a^{\prime\dagger}|^{-1/2}),

with both derivatives having the asymptotic (S) at
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}
and also
a^{\prime\dagger}
(since (
6.3.33
) is trivially (S)), as well as the boundary condition (RH). However, this means that both derivatives should have a real multiple of the (D) asymptotic at
a^{\prime}
by Corollary
6.14
(see also its proof), which forces
\lambda=0
, setting both derivatives to be identically zero as functions of
z
.

To see why this is a contradiction, choose some
a_{0}\in D
and
e_{0}>0
such that
L:=\{a_{0}+x:x\in(-e_{0},e_{0})\}\subset D
. We know that for all
a^{\prime}\in L
, the spinor
z\mapsto\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\sigma_{a^{\prime}}\mu_{a^{\prime\dagger}}\psi_{z}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\sigma_{a^{\prime}}\sigma_{a^{\prime\dagger}}\rangle_{\alpha}}
may be defined on a fixed double cover of
\Omega\setminus(\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{n},a^{\prime\dagger}\}\cup L)
. Then the derivative condition

\partial_{x^{\prime}}\left(\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\sigma_{a^{\prime}}\mu_{a^{\prime\dagger}}\psi_{z}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\sigma_{a^{\prime}}\sigma_{a^{\prime\dagger}}\rangle_{\alpha}}\right)\equiv 0

implies that there is a fixed spinor
f
on the double cover of
\Omega\setminus(\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{n},a^{\prime\dagger}\}\cup L)
such that
f(z)=\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\sigma_{a^{\prime}}\mu_{a^{\prime\dagger}}\psi_{z}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\sigma_{a^{\prime}}\sigma_{a^{\prime\dagger}}\rangle_{\alpha}}
for all
a^{\prime}\in L
. However, since
\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\sigma_{a^{\prime}}\mu_{a^{\prime\dagger}}\psi_{z}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\sigma_{a^{\prime}}\sigma_{a^{\prime\dagger}}\rangle_{\alpha}}
has a (square-root) zero at
z=a^{\prime}
by the assumption that
\beta^{(\alpha)}(a^{\prime};a^{\prime\dagger})=0
, we have
f(z)\to 0
as
z\to L
. This sets
f(z)\equiv 0
, as seen by, e.g. using Theorem
6.7
to write
f=e^{s}\underline{f}
and considering the holomorphic function
\underline{f}^{2}
which must vanish on
L
. This contradicts the fact that
f
must have a (D) asymptotic at
z=a^{\prime\dagger}
.

Therefore,
\beta^{(\alpha)}(\cdot;\cdot)
is nonzero at least on a dense subset of
\Omega^{2}
, and the proof is complete.
∎

Proposition 6.44
.

We have

(6.3.35)

\displaystyle 0<\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{\alpha}<C(\Omega,\kappa_{b},\kappa_{\infty},n,\delta_{all}),

where
\delta_{all}>0
is smaller than
\frac{1}{16}|a_{j}-a_{k}|
for any
j\neq k
and
\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{dist}(a_{j},\partial\Omega)
for any
j=1,\ldots,n
.

In addition, if
\alpha\equiv m<0
,
\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{m}
is exactly the continuous correlation obtained as a scaling limit in
[
54
,
22
]
.

Proof.

The critical correlation
\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{0}
satisfies (
6.3.35
) by
[
21
, Theorem 7.1]
(
q=0
case). Then the line integral in (
6.1.4
) is bounded by explicitly integrating (
6.3.29
), and it is easy to see that we may choose the integration contour for
a_{1}^{\prime}
uniformly away from other points and obtain (
6.3.35
) for the massive correlation.

For the constant mass case, while (
6.1.4
) expresses natural decorrelation near the boundary given by RSW-type estimates
[
29
,
55
]
, we note a purely continuous argument. To show that our
n
-spin correlation coincides with the correlation identified in the discussion in
[
54
, Section 1.3]
, it suffices to show that

\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\rangle_{0}\cdots\langle\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{0}}\cdot\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{0}}\cdot\exp\left\{\mathrm{Re}\bigg[\int_{a_{b}}^{a_{1}}\Big(\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{(\alpha)}(a_{1}^{\prime})-\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{(0)}(a_{1}^{\prime})\Big)da_{1}^{\prime}\bigg]\right\}

may be taken as closely to
1
as desired, by taking
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}
closely to
\partial\Omega
. Inductively, we may assume, for the
n-1
point case, that

\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{0}}=\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{2}}\rangle_{0}\cdots\langle\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{0}}\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{2}}\rangle_{0}\cdots\langle\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{0}}\to 1

under the same limit. The critical ratio
\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}}\rangle_{0}\cdots\langle\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{0}}\to 1
under the same limit due to
[
54
, (1.3)]
, so the discussion below holds for
\beta=\beta_{c}
. It remains to note that the exponential factor is also taken to
1
as
a_{1}\to a_{b}
again by (
6.3.29
).
∎

7.
Analyticity of Ising Correlations

In this section, we show that pure spin and spin-weighted Ising primary field correlations are analytic in the mass
m\in\mathbb{R}
and that the power series coefficients around
m_{0}=0
happen to be exactly as expected from formally differentiating the following discrete correspondence (at criticality, the
energy
is simply defined
\varepsilon_{w}=\frac{i}{2}\psi_{w}\psi_{w}^{*}
)

\langle\mathcal{O}^{\delta}\rangle_{m\alpha^{\delta}}=\frac{\langle\mathcal{O}^{\delta}\exp\{-{m}\int_{\Omega}\frac{d^{2}u}{\pi}\alpha^{\delta}(u)\varepsilon^{\delta}_{u}\}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\exp\{-{m}\int_{\Omega}\frac{d^{2}u}{\pi}\alpha^{\delta}(u)\varepsilon^{\delta}_{u}\}\rangle_{0}},

which we will not attempt to make rigorous in the continuum theory but use as a helpful heuristic. Specifically, we would expect that the expansion of a massive correlation around
m=0
takes the form

\langle\mathcal{O}\rangle_{m\alpha}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{\partial_{m}^{k}\langle\mathcal{O}\rangle_{0}}{k!}m^{k},

with, e.g.,

\partial_{m}\langle\mathcal{O}\rangle_{0}=\left\langle\mathcal{O}\right\rangle_{0}\left\langle\int_{\Omega}\frac{d^{2}u}{\pi}\alpha(u)\varepsilon_{u}\right\rangle_{0}-\left\langle{\mathcal{O}}\int_{\Omega}\frac{d^{2}u}{\pi}\alpha(u)\varepsilon_{u}\right\rangle_{0},

and so on.

Remark 7.1
.

Recall that a power series is absolutely convergent within its radius of convergence and its composition with an analytic function is still analytic, which allow for rearrangement, expansion, and re-grouping of terms in a product or composition of multiple series. Nonetheless, it will be useful to have a notion of uniformity across field insertion points staying uniformly away from each other. Given a power series

\sum_{p=0}^{\infty}{(m-m_{0})^{p}}E_{p,X},

for a function depending on a finite set of insertion points in
\Omega
indexed by
X
, we say the coefficients satisfy a
locally exponential bound
if

(7.0.1)

|E_{p,X}|\leq C(\Omega,M,\kappa_{\infty},\kappa_{b},\delta_{all},|X|)^{p},

where
\delta_{all}>0
is smaller than
\frac{1}{16}|x_{1}-x_{2}|
and
\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{dist}(x_{1},\partial\Omega)
for any
x_{1},x_{2}
indexed by
X
. Note that a uniform nonzero radius of convergence is provided by estimates of type (
7.0.1
), depending on only the six parameters therein.

In fact, the coefficients
E_{p,X}
will frequently be given by an integral of some function
\mathfrak{E}_{p,X}(\cdot)
over
\Omega^{p}
, as in Remark
6.34
, and we will have a bound of type (
7.0.1
) for the integral of
|\mathfrak{E}_{p,X}(\cdot)|
(i.e. integral of the absolute value of the integrand). This implies we are free to use Fubini to merge products of integrals over (powers of)
\Omega
into a single integral and, e.g., symmetrize over the
p
components.

In the following, any series expansion will be said to satisfy the
usual conditions
if they have such locally exponentially bounded coefficients (given as explicit bounded integrals involving
\alpha
) and depend continuously on the points indexed by
X
, away from one another and the boundary. Note that usual conditions are preserved under compositions of such series with other analytic functions.

7.1.
Pure spin correlations

Our goal is to prove the following. Recall the notation
\sigma_{A}=\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}
, etc. We will also write
\sigma_{A^{\dagger}}=\sigma_{a_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}
.

Proposition 7.2
.

For distinct points
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}
, the correlation
\log{\langle\sigma_{A}\rangle_{m\alpha}}
may be expanded around any
m=m_{0}
with the usual conditions. Near
m_{0}=0
, we have

(7.1.1)

\log\frac{\langle\sigma_{A}\rangle_{m\alpha}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{0}}=\sum_{p=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-m)^{p}}{p!}\int_{\Omega^{p}}\frac{d^{2p}u}{\pi^{p}}\,\prod_{j=1}^{p}\alpha(u_{j})\sum_{\Lambda\in\Pi_{p}}(-1)^{|\Lambda|-1}(|\Lambda|-1)!\\
\times\Bigg(\prod_{B\in\Lambda}\frac{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\prod_{k\in B}\varepsilon_{u_{k}}\right\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{0}}-\prod_{B\in\Lambda}\left\langle\prod_{k\in B}\varepsilon_{u_{k}}\right\rangle_{0}\Bigg),

where
\Pi_{p}
denotes the set of partitions of
\{1,\dots,p\}
.

Note that series expansion with locally exponentially bounded coefficients follows from Corollary
6.39
, using the fact that we may integrate the bound (
6.3.25
) avoiding other spins by closedness and Lemma
6.42
. Then, since
\lambda(a_{1}^{\prime};A_{p})
depends locally continuously on
a_{1}^{\prime},a_{2},\ldots,a_{n}
by Proposition
6.30
(and implicitly dominated convergence on (
6.3.1
)), another application of dominated convergence implies that
\int_{a_{b}}^{a_{1}}\frac{1}{2}\lambda(a_{1}^{\prime};A_{p})da_{1}^{\prime}
also depends locally continuously on
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}
, as desired. That is, the expansion is under usual conditions.

It remains to identify the expansion around
m_{0}=0
. To this end, we need the following. Recall the functions
A_{p}(z)
from (
6.3.13
), at
m_{0}=0
.

Proposition 7.3
.

For
p\geq 1
, we have

(7.1.2)

\displaystyle A_{p}(z)=\int_{\Omega^{p}}\mathfrak{A}^{\text{sym}}_{p}(u,z)d^{2p}u=\int_{\Omega^{p}}\frac{d^{2p}u}{\pi^{p}}\,\prod_{j=1}^{p}\alpha(u_{j})\frac{1}{p!}\sum_{\Lambda\in\Pi_{p}}(|\Lambda|-1)!(-1)^{|\Lambda|-1}

\displaystyle\times\sum_{S\in\Lambda}

\displaystyle\Bigg[\frac{\big\langle\sigma_{A^{\dagger}}\mu_{a_{1}}\psi_{z}\prod_{j\in S}\varepsilon_{u_{j}}\big\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{0}}-\frac{\left\langle\sigma_{A^{\dagger}}\mu_{a_{1}}\psi_{z}\right\rangle_{0}\big\langle\sigma_{A}\prod_{j\in S}\varepsilon_{u_{j}}\big\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{0}^{2}}\Bigg]\prod_{B\in\Lambda\setminus\{S\}}\frac{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\prod_{j\in B}\varepsilon_{u_{j}}\big\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{0}},

where
\mathfrak{A}^{\text{sym}}_{p}
is defined as the integrand on the right (see Remark
7.4
for its integrability).

Remark 7.4
.

The proof of Proposition
7.3
will only use equalities valid at the level of the integrand and permutations of components of
u
; therefore, the integrand in (
7.1.2
), which is evidently invariant under the swap between any
u_{j}
and
u_{j^{\prime}}
, is equal to

\mathfrak{A}^{\text{sym}}_{p}(u,z)=\frac{1}{p!}\sum_{s\in S_{p}}\mathfrak{A}_{p}(u_{s_{1}},u_{s_{2}},\ldots,u_{s_{p}},z),

where the function
\mathfrak{A}_{p}
is defined in Remark
6.34
. Then the estimates (
6.3.16
) and (
6.3.20
), also suitably symmetrized, hold for
|\mathfrak{A}^{\text{sym}}_{p}|
. In particular, the integral in (
7.1.2
) is well-defined.

Proof of Proposition
7.3
.

Since
\varepsilon=\frac{i}{2}\psi\psi^{*}
at criticality, the RHS for the
p=1
case resolves to

\displaystyle A_{1}(z)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\Omega}\alpha(u_{1})\Bigg[\frac{\left\langle\sigma_{A^{\dagger}}\mu_{a_{1}}\psi_{z}\varepsilon_{u_{1}}\right\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{0}}-\frac{\left\langle\sigma_{A^{\dagger}}\mu_{a_{1}}\psi_{z}\right\rangle_{0}\left\langle\sigma_{A}\varepsilon_{u_{1}}\right\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{0}^{2}}\Bigg]d^{2}u_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\Omega}\alpha(u_{1})\frac{i}{2}\Bigg[\frac{\left\langle\sigma_{A^{\dagger}}\mu_{a_{1}}\psi_{z}\psi_{u_{1}}\psi_{u_{1}}^{*}\right\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{0}}-\frac{\left\langle\sigma_{A^{\dagger}}\mu_{a_{1}}\psi_{z}\right\rangle_{0}\left\langle\sigma_{A}\psi_{u_{1}}\psi_{u_{1}}^{*}\right\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{0}^{2}}\Bigg]d^{2}u_{1}.

This is the induction base case, which we will now show.

Let us simplify the correlations in the integrand, carefully dealing with
\mu_{a_{1}}=\lim_{a^{\prime}_{1}\to a_{1}}e^{-i\frac{\pi}{4}}\allowbreak\times(a_{1}^{\prime}-a_{1})^{\frac{1}{2}}\sigma_{a_{1}}\psi_{a_{1}^{\prime}}
(within correlations, see (
6.1.1
)). We have

\displaystyle\frac{\left\langle\sigma_{A^{\dagger}}\mu_{a_{1}}\psi_{z}\psi_{u_{1}}\psi_{u_{1}}^{*}\right\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{0}}=\lim_{a^{\prime}_{1}\to a_{1}}e^{-i\pi/4}(a_{1}^{\prime}-a_{1})^{1/2}\frac{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\psi_{a_{1}^{\prime}}\psi_{z}\psi_{u_{1}}\psi_{u_{1}}^{*}\big\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{0}}=\lim_{a^{\prime}_{1}\to a_{1}}e^{-i\pi/4}(a_{1}^{\prime}-a_{1})^{1/2}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\Bigg[\frac{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\psi_{a^{\prime}_{1}}\psi_{z}\big\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{0}}\frac{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\psi_{u_{1}}\psi_{u_{1}}^{*}\right\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{0}}-\frac{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\psi_{a_{1}^{\prime}}\psi_{u_{1}}\big\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{0}}\frac{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\psi_{z}\psi_{u_{1}}^{*}\right\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{0}}+\frac{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\psi_{z}\psi_{u_{1}}\right\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{0}}\frac{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\psi_{a_{1}^{\prime}}\psi_{u_{1}}^{*}\big\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{0}}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{\left\langle\sigma_{A^{\dagger}}\mu_{a_{1}}\psi_{z}\right\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{0}}\frac{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\psi_{u_{1}}\psi_{u_{1}}^{*}\right\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{0}}-\frac{\left\langle\sigma_{A^{\dagger}}\mu_{a_{1}}\psi_{u_{1}}\right\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{0}}\frac{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\psi_{z}\psi_{u_{1}}^{*}\right\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{0}}+\frac{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\psi_{z}\psi_{u_{1}}\right\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{0}}\frac{\left\langle\sigma_{A^{\dagger}}\mu_{a_{1}}\psi_{u_{1}}^{*}\right\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{0}}.

Now we may simplify the integral above (see also (
6.1.2
) and the remark below for how correlations behave under conjugation):

\displaystyle A_{1}(z)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\Omega}\alpha(u_{1})\frac{i}{2}\Bigg[-\frac{\left\langle\sigma_{A^{\dagger}}\mu_{a_{1}}\psi_{u_{1}}\right\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{0}}\frac{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\psi_{z}\psi_{u_{1}}^{*}\right\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{0}}+\frac{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\psi_{z}\psi_{u_{1}}\right\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{0}}\frac{\left\langle\sigma_{A^{\dagger}}\mu_{a_{1}}\psi_{u_{1}}^{*}\right\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{0}}\Bigg]d^{2}u_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\Omega}\Bigg[i\alpha(u_{1})\frac{\left\langle\sigma_{A^{\dagger}}\mu_{a_{1}}\psi_{u_{1}}^{*}\right\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{0}}\Bigg]\star\frac{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\psi_{z}\psi_{u_{1}}^{[\star]}\big\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{0}}d^{2}u_{1},

thanks to (
6.3.11
), as desired. We see that a form of Pfaffian expansion is still valid for disorder insertions; for notational simplicity, we will henceforth write
\mu_{a}
as
\sigma_{a}\psi_{a}
to do such expansions, carefully converting the combination back to
\mu_{a}
at the end (respectively marked by
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sigma\psi}}{{=}}
and
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu}}{{=}}
).

Assuming (
7.1.2
) as the inductive hypothesis for
p
, we have (again using (
6.3.11
))

\displaystyle A_{p+1}(z)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\Omega}\alpha(u_{p+1})\frac{i}{2}\Bigg[\overline{A_{p}(u_{p+1})}\frac{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\psi_{z}\psi_{u_{p+1}}\right\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{0}}-A_{p}(u_{p+1})\frac{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\psi_{z}\psi_{u_{p+1}}^{*}\big\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{0}}\Bigg]d^{2}u_{p+1}.

If we plug in the inductive hypothesis for
p
, we will be left with the integral over
\Omega^{p+1}
(with measure
\frac{d^{2(p+1)}u}{\pi^{p+1}}\prod_{j=1}^{p+1}\alpha(u_{j})
as desired) of a linear combination of terms which are all products of
\sigma_{A}
-weighted Ising correlations. We check that they contribute all types of terms present in (
7.1.2
) for
p+1
with correct constants in front.

Fix
\Lambda\in\Pi_{p}
and
S\in\Lambda
, which fixes all the pre-factors in front of the correlations and the post-factor
\prod_{B\in\Lambda\setminus\{S\}}\frac{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\prod_{j\in B}\varepsilon_{u_{j}}\right\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{0}}
. Consider the first term in the summand in the second line of (
7.1.2
). We have the product

\displaystyle\frac{i}{2}\frac{\big\langle\sigma_{A^{\dagger}}\mu_{a_{1}}\psi_{u_{p+1}}^{*}\prod_{j\in S}\varepsilon_{u_{j}}\big\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{0}}

\displaystyle\frac{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\psi_{z}\psi_{u_{p+1}}\right\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{0}}-\frac{i}{2}\frac{\big\langle\sigma_{A^{\dagger}}\mu_{a_{1}}\psi_{u_{p+1}}\prod_{j\in S}\varepsilon_{u_{j}}\big\rangle_{0}}{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\big\rangle_{0}}\frac{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\psi_{z}\psi_{u_{p+1}}^{*}\big\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{0}}

(7.1.3)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sigma\psi}}{{=}}\left(\frac{i}{2}\right)^{|S|+1}

\displaystyle\frac{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\psi_{a_{1}}\psi_{u_{p+1}}^{*}\prod_{j\in S}\psi_{u_{j}}\psi_{u_{j}}^{*}\big\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{0}}\frac{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\psi_{u_{p+1}}\psi_{z}\right\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{0}}

\displaystyle-\left(\frac{i}{2}\right)^{|S|+1}\frac{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\psi_{a_{1}}\psi_{u_{p+1}}\prod_{j\in S}\psi_{u_{j}}\psi_{u_{j}}^{*}\big\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{0}}\frac{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\psi_{u_{p+1}}^{*}\psi_{z}\big\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{0}}.

Comparing with the recursive expansion for Pfaffians (e.g. see the proof of Proposition
6.16
), we see that the above two terms come from the expansion of the
2|S|+4
point correlation
\frac{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\psi_{a_{1}}\psi_{z}\prod_{j\in S\cup\{p+1\}}\psi_{u_{j}}\psi_{u_{j}}^{*}\right\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{0}}
that keeps
\psi_{z}
in the
2
-point correlation. In fact, thanks to the fact that
A_{p+1}
is an integral on all of
\Omega^{p+1}
, we may in total obtain
2|S|+2
out of the
2|S|+3
terms in the expansion by adding the terms where the places of each
u_{j}
and
u_{p+1}
are swapped; once integrated, this process would add
|S|
copies of the same integral, so we would need to divide by
|S|+1
to keep the same normalization for
A_{p+1}
. Marking this symmetrization for the integrand with
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle sym}}{{=}}
, we have, where
S^{\prime}=S\cup\{p+1\}
,

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle sym}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|S|+1}\left(\frac{i}{2}\right)^{|S|+1}\sum_{j^{\prime}\in S^{\prime}}\Bigg[\frac{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\psi_{u_{j^{\prime}}}^{*}\psi_{a_{1}}\prod_{j\in S^{\prime}\setminus\{j^{\prime}\}}\psi_{u_{j}}\psi_{u_{j}}^{*}\big\rangle_{0}}{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\big\rangle_{0}}\frac{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\psi_{u_{j^{\prime}}}\psi_{z}\big\rangle_{0}}{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\big\rangle_{0}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-\frac{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\psi_{u_{j^{\prime}}}\psi_{a_{1}}\prod_{j\in S^{\prime}\setminus\{j^{\prime}\}}\psi_{u_{j}}\psi_{u_{j}}^{*}\big\rangle_{0}}{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\big\rangle_{0}}\frac{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\psi_{u_{j^{\prime}}}^{*}\psi_{z}\big\rangle_{0}}{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\big\rangle_{0}}\Bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|S^{\prime}|}\left(\frac{i}{2}\right)^{|S|+1}\Bigg[\frac{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\psi_{a_{1}}\psi_{z}\prod_{j\in S^{\prime}}\psi_{u_{j}}\psi_{u_{j}}^{*}\big\rangle_{0}}{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\big\rangle_{0}}-\frac{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\prod_{j\in S^{\prime}}\psi_{u_{j}}\psi_{u_{j}}^{*}\big\rangle_{0}}{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\big\rangle_{0}}\frac{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\psi_{a_{1}}\psi_{z}\big\rangle_{0}}{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\big\rangle_{0}}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|S^{\prime}|}\Bigg[\frac{\big\langle\sigma_{A^{\dagger}}\mu_{a_{1}}\psi_{z}\prod_{j\in S^{\prime}}\varepsilon_{u_{j}}\big\rangle_{0}}{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\big\rangle_{0}}-\frac{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\prod_{j\in S^{\prime}}\varepsilon_{u_{j}}\big\rangle_{0}}{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\big\rangle_{0}}\frac{\big\langle\sigma_{A^{\dagger}}\mu_{a_{1}}\psi_{z}\big\rangle_{0}}{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\big\rangle_{0}}\Bigg].

This is exactly what the induction hypothesis seems to state for
p+1
, apart from the pre-factor
1/|S^{\prime}|
. This apparent mismatch comes from the fact that
S^{\prime}
is fixed to be the element of the partition
\Lambda^{\prime}:=\Lambda\cup\{S^{\prime}\}\setminus\{S\}\in\Pi_{p+1}
that contains
p+1
. This suggests another symmetrization, now between
u_{p+1}
and all other
u_{j}
, requiring another pre-factor
\frac{1}{p+1}
, correctly transforming
\frac{1}{p!}
to
\frac{1}{(p+1)!}
. Then any pair
(\Lambda^{\prime},S^{\prime})
where
\Lambda^{\prime}\in\Pi_{p+1}
and
|S^{\prime}|>1
appears exactly
|S^{\prime}|
times, precisely canceling out the mismatching pre-factor.

Now let us look at the second term in the second line of (
7.1.2
). In much a similar way as in the first term, we have
-\frac{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\prod_{j\in S}\varepsilon_{u_{j}}\right\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{0}}
times

\displaystyle\,\frac{i}{2}\frac{\big\langle\sigma_{A^{\dagger}}\mu_{a_{1}}\psi_{u_{p+1}}^{*}\big\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{0}}\frac{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\psi_{z}\psi_{u_{p+1}}\right\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{0}}-\frac{i}{2}\frac{\left\langle\sigma_{A^{\dagger}}\mu_{a_{1}}\psi_{u_{p+1}}\right\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{0}}\frac{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\psi_{z}\psi_{u_{p+1}}^{*}\big\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{0}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sigma\psi}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\,\frac{i}{2}\frac{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\psi_{a_{1}}\psi_{u_{p+1}}^{*}\big\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{0}}\frac{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\psi_{z}\psi_{u_{p+1}}\right\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{0}}-\frac{i}{2}\frac{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\psi_{a_{1}}\psi_{u_{p+1}}\right\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{0}}\frac{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\psi_{z}\psi_{u_{p+1}}^{*}\big\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{0}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\frac{i}{2}\frac{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\psi_{a_{1}}\psi_{u_{p+1}}^{*}\psi_{z}\psi_{u_{p+1}}\big\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{0}}+\frac{i}{2}\frac{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\psi_{a_{1}}\psi_{z}\right\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{0}}\frac{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\psi_{u_{p+1}}^{*}\psi_{u_{p+1}}\big\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{0}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\,\frac{\left\langle\sigma_{A^{\dagger}}\mu_{a_{1}}\psi_{z}\varepsilon_{u_{p+1}}\right\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{0}}-\frac{\left\langle\sigma_{A^{\dagger}}\mu_{a_{1}}\psi_{z}\right\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{0}}\frac{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\varepsilon_{u_{p+1}}\right\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{0}}.

This corresponds to
\Lambda^{\prime}=(\Lambda\cup\{p+1\})\in\Pi_{p+1}
, so we still have a mismatch by the factor
\frac{|\Lambda|}{p+1}
. The fact that
(-1)^{|\Lambda^{\prime}|}=-(-1)^{|\Lambda|}
is addressed by the shared factor
-\frac{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\prod_{j\in S}\varepsilon_{u_{j}}\right\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{0}}
. Note that each
S\in\Lambda
gives rise to the same term, i.e., if
\Lambda^{\prime}\in\Pi_{p+1}
with
\{p+1\}\in\Lambda^{\prime}
, the combination
(\Lambda^{\prime},\{p+1\})
would appear
|\Lambda^{\prime}|-1
times, so we can multiply by
|\Lambda^{\prime}|-1
instead (which exactly transforms
(|\Lambda|-1)!=(|\Lambda^{\prime}|-2)!
to
(|\Lambda^{\prime}|-1)!
as desired) and have
(\Lambda^{\prime},\{p+1\})
appear exactly once. Then, after the same symmetrization from above, we get that any
(\Lambda^{\prime},S^{\prime})
, with
|S^{\prime}|=1
, appears once, as desired, while the normalization
\frac{1}{p+1}
precisely supplies the remaining factor.

Therefore the inductive hypothesis is true for
p+1
and we have shown (
7.1.2
).
∎

Proof of Proposition
7.2
.

By Corollary
6.39
and (
6.1.4
), we have

\log\frac{\langle\sigma_{A^{\prime}}\rangle_{m\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{A^{\prime}}\rangle_{0}}\bigg|_{a_{1}^{\prime}=a_{b}}^{a_{1}^{\prime}=a_{1}}=\mathrm{Re}\bigg[\int_{a_{b}}^{a_{1}}\left(\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{(m\alpha)}-\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{(0)}\right)(a_{1}^{\prime})da_{1}^{\prime}\bigg]=\sum_{p=1}^{\infty}(-m)^{p}\cdot\mathrm{Re}\bigg[\int_{a_{b}}^{a_{1}}\frac{1}{2}\lambda\left(a_{1}^{\prime};A_{p}\right)da_{1}^{\prime}\bigg],

where
\sigma_{A^{\prime}}:=\sigma_{a_{1}^{\prime}}\sigma_{a_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}},A_{p}=A_{p}(a_{1}^{\prime},a_{2},\ldots,a_{n};\cdot)
.

We claim that we can extract
\lambda
from
A_{p}
by using the critical OPE
[
21
, (6.6)]
which we reproduce below:

\displaystyle\mu_{a}\psi_{z}

\displaystyle=-\frac{e^{-\frac{i\pi}{4}}}{\sqrt{z-a}}\left(\sigma_{a}+4\partial_{a}\sigma_{a}\cdot(z-a)+O(|z-a|^{2})\right),

(7.1.4)

\displaystyle\sigma_{a}\psi_{z}

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{\frac{i\pi}{4}}}{\sqrt{z-a}}\left(\mu_{a}+4\partial_{a}\mu_{a}\cdot(z-a)+O(|z-a|^{2})\right),

directly on the integrand
\mathfrak{A}^{\text{sym}}_{p}=\mathfrak{A}^{\text{sym}}_{p}(a_{1}^{\prime},a_{2},\ldots,a_{n};\cdot)
; in other words, we have

(7.1.5)

\displaystyle\lambda\left(a_{1}^{\prime};A_{p}\right)

\displaystyle=4\int_{\Omega^{p}}\frac{d^{2p}u}{\pi^{p}}\,\prod_{j=1}^{p}\alpha(u_{j})\frac{1}{p!}\sum_{\Lambda\in\Pi_{p}}(|\Lambda|-1)!(-1)^{|\Lambda|-1}

\displaystyle\hskip-25.0pt\times\sum_{C\in\Lambda}\Bigg[\frac{\big\langle\sigma_{A^{\dagger}}\partial_{a_{1}^{\prime}}\sigma_{a_{1}^{\prime}}\prod_{j\in C}\varepsilon_{u_{j}}\big\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A^{\prime}}\right\rangle_{0}}-\frac{\big\langle\sigma_{A^{\dagger}}\partial_{a_{1}^{\prime}}\sigma_{a_{1}^{\prime}}\big\rangle_{0}\big\langle\sigma_{A^{\prime}}\prod_{j\in C}\varepsilon_{u_{j}}\big\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A^{\prime}}\right\rangle_{0}^{2}}\Bigg]\prod_{B\in\Lambda\setminus\{C\}}\frac{\big\langle\sigma_{A^{\prime}}\prod_{j\in B}\varepsilon_{u_{j}}\big\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A^{\prime}}\right\rangle_{0}}

\displaystyle=4\int_{\Omega^{p}}\frac{d^{2p}u}{\pi^{p}}\,\prod_{j=1}^{p}\alpha(u_{j})\frac{1}{p!}\sum_{\Lambda\in\Pi_{p}}(|\Lambda|-1)!(-1)^{|\Lambda|-1}\cdot\partial_{a_{1}^{\prime}}\Bigg[\prod_{B\in\Lambda}\frac{\big\langle\sigma_{A^{\prime}}\prod_{j\in B}\varepsilon_{u_{j}}\big\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A^{\prime}}\right\rangle_{0}}\Bigg].

We also claim that we may integrate with respect to
a_{1}^{\prime}
from the boundary to
a_{1}
under the
\int_{\Omega^{p}}
sign, so we would have

\log\frac{\langle\sigma_{A}\rangle_{m\alpha}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{0}}-\log\frac{\langle\sigma_{A^{\dagger}}\rangle_{m\alpha}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A^{\dagger}}\right\rangle_{0}}=\sum_{p=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-m)^{p}}{p!}\int_{\Omega^{p}}\frac{d^{2p}u}{\pi^{p}}\,\prod_{j=1}^{p}\alpha(u_{j})\sum_{\Lambda\in\Pi_{p}}(-1)^{|\Lambda|-1}(|\Lambda|-1)!\\
\times\left(\prod_{B\in\Lambda}\frac{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\prod_{k\in B}\varepsilon_{u_{k}}\right\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{0}}-\prod_{B\in\Lambda}\frac{\left\langle\sigma_{A^{\dagger}}\prod_{k\in B}\varepsilon_{u_{k}}\right\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A^{\dagger}}\right\rangle_{0}}\right),

which yields (
7.1.1
) by inducting on
n=|A|
. Note that we also used that

\frac{\big\langle\sigma_{A^{\prime}}\prod_{j\in B}\varepsilon_{u_{j}}\big\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A^{\prime}}\right\rangle_{0}}\to\frac{\big\langle\sigma_{A^{\dagger}}\prod_{j\in B}\varepsilon_{u_{j}}\big\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A^{\dagger}}\right\rangle_{0}}\text{ as }\sigma_{a_{1}^{\prime}}\to\partial\Omega,

by Propositions
6.32
and
6.16
.

We show these two claims at one go, i.e.,

(7.1.6)

\mathrm{Re}\bigg[\int_{a_{b}}^{a_{1}}\lambda(a_{1}^{\prime};A_{p})da_{1}^{\prime}\bigg]=\int_{\Omega^{p}}\mathrm{Re}\left[\int_{a_{b}}^{a_{1}}\lambda(a_{1}^{\prime};\mathfrak{A}_{p}^{\text{sym}})da_{1}^{\prime}\right]d^{2p}u,

where
\lambda(a_{1}^{\prime};\mathfrak{A}_{p}^{\text{sym}})
is the symmetrization of

\displaystyle\lambda(a_{1}^{\prime};\mathfrak{A}_{p})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi}\left[i\alpha(u_{p})\overline{\mathfrak{A}_{p-1}(u)}\right]\star\lambda\Bigg(z=a_{1}^{\prime},\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}^{\prime}}\sigma_{a_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\psi_{z}\psi^{[\star]}_{u_{p}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}^{\prime}}\sigma_{a_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{0}}\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{\pi i}\left[i\alpha(u_{p})\overline{\mathfrak{A}_{p-1}(u)}\right]\star\frac{\langle\partial_{a_{1}^{\prime}}\mu_{a_{1}^{\prime}}\sigma_{a_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\psi^{[\star]}_{u_{p}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}^{\prime}}\sigma_{a_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{0}}.

Let
d=\frac{1}{4}\min(\operatorname{dist}(a_{1}^{\prime},\partial\Omega),\delta_{0})
. We extract

\int_{a_{b}}^{a_{1}}\lambda(a_{1}^{\prime};A_{p})da_{1}^{\prime}=-e^{i\pi/4}\int_{a_{b}}^{a_{1}}\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\left[\oint_{\partial B_{\varepsilon d}(a_{1}^{\prime})}\int_{\Omega^{p}}\frac{\mathfrak{A}_{p}(u,z)}{(z-a_{1}^{\prime})^{\frac{3}{2}}}d^{2p}u\,\frac{dz}{2\pi i}\right]da_{1}^{\prime}.

By (
6.3.24
) and dominated convergence, we may pull out the limit outside of the
da_{1}^{\prime}
integral. So, let us study the triple integral, which is equal to (fix a straight path
\gamma
from some
a_{b}
to
a_{1}
so that we may fix a uniform
\delta_{0}
, which is valid for
a_{1}^{\prime}
along
\gamma
, then write
\gamma_{\varepsilon}:=\bigcup_{a_{1}^{\prime}\in\gamma}B_{\varepsilon d}(a_{1}^{\prime})
and
\Omega_{\varepsilon}:=\Omega\setminus\gamma_{\varepsilon}
)

\displaystyle\int_{a_{b}}^{a_{1}}\oint_{\partial B_{\varepsilon d}(a_{1}^{\prime})}\int_{\Omega^{p}}\frac{\mathfrak{A}_{p}(u,z)\mathbf{1}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(u_{p})}{(z-a_{1}^{\prime})^{\frac{3}{2}}}d^{2p}u\,\frac{dz}{2\pi i}\,da_{1}^{\prime}+\int_{a_{b}}^{a_{1}}\oint_{\partial B_{\varepsilon d}(a_{1}^{\prime})}\int_{\Omega^{p}}\frac{\mathfrak{A}_{p}(u,z)\mathbf{1}_{\gamma_{\varepsilon}}(u_{p})}{(z-a_{1}^{\prime})^{\frac{3}{2}}}d^{2p}u\,\frac{dz}{2\pi i}\,da_{1}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\int_{a_{b}}^{a_{1}}\int_{\Omega^{p}}\frac{\lambda(a_{1}^{\prime};\mathfrak{A}_{p})\mathbf{1}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(u_{p})}{-e^{i\pi/4}}{d^{2p}u\,da_{1}^{\prime}}+\int_{a_{b}}^{a_{1}}\oint_{\partial B_{\varepsilon d}(a_{1}^{\prime})}\int_{\Omega^{p}}\frac{\mathfrak{A}_{p}(u,z)\mathbf{1}_{\gamma_{\varepsilon}}(u_{p})}{(z-a_{1}^{\prime})^{\frac{3}{2}}}d^{2p}u\,\frac{dz}{2\pi i}\,da_{1}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega^{p}}\int_{a_{b}}^{a_{1}}\frac{\lambda(a_{1}^{\prime};\mathfrak{A}_{p})\mathbf{1}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(u_{p})}{-e^{i\pi/4}}{da_{1}^{\prime}\,d^{2p}u}+\int_{a_{b}}^{a_{1}}\oint_{\partial B_{\varepsilon d}(a_{1}^{\prime})}\int_{\Omega^{p}}\frac{\mathfrak{A}_{p}(u,z)\mathbf{1}_{\gamma_{\varepsilon}}(u_{p})}{(z-a_{1}^{\prime})^{\frac{3}{2}}}d^{2p}u\,\frac{dz}{2\pi i}\,da_{1}^{\prime},

Note that
\mathfrak{A}_{p}
is meromorphic (on the double cover) unlike
A_{p}
, so we do not need to take the limit in order to use the residue formula, and Remark
6.34
justifies the multiple instances of Fubini for the first term.

For the remainder term (second term), note that
