\int_{\Omega^{p-1}}{\mathfrak{A}_{p}(u,z)}d^{2}u_{1}\cdots d^{2}u_{p-1}=\frac{1}{\pi}\left[i\alpha(u_{p})\overline{A_{p-1}(u_{p})}\right]\star\frac{\big\langle\sigma_{a_{1}^{\prime}}\sigma_{a_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\psi_{z}\psi^{[\star]}_{u_{p}}\big\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}^{\prime}}\sigma_{a_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{0}}\\
=\frac{i\alpha(u_{p})}{2\pi}\left[\overline{A_{p-1}(u_{p})}\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}^{\prime}}\sigma_{a_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\psi_{z}\psi_{u_{p}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}^{\prime}}\sigma_{a_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{0}}-{A_{p-1}(u_{p})}\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}^{\prime}}\sigma_{a_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\psi_{z}\psi^{*}_{u_{p}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}^{\prime}}\sigma_{a_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{0}}\right].

From (
6.3.24
) and using the same notation for
C
, we have for
u_{p}\in B_{d/16}(a_{1}^{\prime})

\left|A_{p-1}^{\dagger\dagger}(u_{p})\right|\leq{C^{p-1}d^{-\frac{1}{2}}}{{|u_{p}-a_{1}^{\prime}|}^{\frac{1}{2}}}\text{ for }\beta_{(p-1)}\in i\mathbb{R},\text{ where }A_{p-1}^{\dagger\dagger}(u_{p}):=A_{p-1}(u_{p})+\frac{e^{-\frac{i\pi}{4}}\beta_{(p-1)}}{\sqrt{u_{p}-a_{1}^{\prime}}}.

In addition, thanks to (anti)meromorphicity, the critical OPE (
7.1.4
), and the bounds (
6.2.3
), we have for
u_{p}\in B_{d/16}(a_{1}^{\prime}),z\in\partial B_{\varepsilon d}(a_{1}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}^{\prime}}\sigma_{a_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\psi_{z}\psi_{u_{p}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}^{\prime}}\sigma_{a_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{0}}+\frac{e^{\frac{i\pi}{4}}}{\sqrt{u_{p}-a_{1}^{\prime}}}\frac{\langle\mu_{a_{1}^{\prime}}\sigma_{a_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\psi_{z}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}^{\prime}}\sigma_{a_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{0}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C|u_{p}-a_{1}^{\prime}|^{\frac{1}{2}}}{d^{\frac{1}{2}}|z-u_{p}|},

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}^{\prime}}\sigma_{a_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\psi_{z}\psi^{*}_{u_{p}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}^{\prime}}\sigma_{a_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{0}}+\frac{e^{-\frac{i\pi}{4}}}{\overline{\sqrt{u_{p}-a_{1}^{\prime}}}}\frac{\langle\mu_{a_{1}^{\prime}}\sigma_{a_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\psi_{z}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}^{\prime}}\sigma_{a_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{0}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C|u_{p}-a_{1}^{\prime}|^{\frac{1}{2}}}{d^{\frac{1}{2}}|z-u_{p}|}.

Then it is straightforward to check that (the
1/|u_{p}-a_{1}^{\prime}|
term being in fact canceled)

\Bigg|\int_{\Omega^{p-1}}{\mathfrak{A}_{p}(u,z)}d^{2}u_{1}\cdots d^{2}u_{p-1}-\frac{\alpha(u_{p})}{\pi}\mathrm{Im}\Bigg[\frac{e^{\frac{i\pi}{4}}\overline{A_{p-1}^{\dagger\dagger}(u_{p})}}{{\sqrt{u_{p}-a_{1}^{\prime}}}}\Bigg]\frac{\langle\mu_{a_{1}^{\prime}}\sigma_{a_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\psi_{z}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}^{\prime}}\sigma_{a_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{0}}\Bigg|\leq C^{p}\frac{1}{|z-u_{p}|}.

We have, now for
C=C(\Omega,\kappa_{\infty},n,\delta_{0},\kappa_{b},\gamma)
,

\displaystyle\Bigg|\oint_{\partial B_{\varepsilon d}(a_{1}^{\prime})}\int_{\Omega}\int_{\Omega^{p-1}}{\mathfrak{A}_{p}(u,z)}d^{2}u_{1}\cdots d^{2}u_{p-1}\frac{\mathbf{1}_{\{\gamma_{\varepsilon}\cap B_{d/16}(a_{1}^{\prime})\}}(u_{p})}{(z-a_{1}^{\prime})^{\frac{3}{2}}}d^{2}u_{p}\,\frac{dz}{2\pi i}\Bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\!\Bigg|\int_{\Omega}\!\frac{\alpha(u_{p})\mathbf{1}_{\{\gamma_{\varepsilon}\cap B_{d/16}(a_{1}^{\prime})\}}(u_{p})}{\pi}\mathrm{Im}\!\Bigg[\frac{e^{\frac{i\pi}{4}}\overline{A_{p-1}^{\dagger\dagger}(u_{p})}}{{\sqrt{u_{p}-a_{1}^{\prime}}}}\Bigg]\!d^{2}u_{p}\!\cdot\!\oint_{\partial B_{\varepsilon d}(a_{1}^{\prime})}\!\!\frac{\langle\mu_{a_{1}^{\prime}}\sigma_{a_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\psi_{z}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}^{\prime}}\sigma_{a_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{0}}\frac{dz}{2\pi i(z-a_{1}^{\prime})^{\frac{3}{2}}}\Bigg|

\displaystyle\quad+C^{p}\int_{\partial B_{\varepsilon d}(a_{1}^{\prime})}\int_{\Omega}\frac{\mathbf{1}_{\{\gamma_{\varepsilon}\cap B_{d/16}(a_{1}^{\prime})\}}(u_{p})}{{|z-u_{p}|}|z-a_{1}^{\prime}|^{\frac{3}{2}}}d^{2}u_{p}\,{|dz|}\leq C^{p}\Bigg(\frac{\varepsilon d}{d^{\frac{1}{2}+1}}+\frac{\varepsilon d|\log(\frac{1}{\varepsilon})|}{(\varepsilon d)^{\frac{1}{2}}}\Bigg)\leq C^{p}\frac{\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}|\log\varepsilon|}{d^{\frac{1}{2}}},

using the above bound for
A_{p-1}^{\dagger\dagger}
and since

\int_{\{\gamma_{\varepsilon}\cap B_{d/16}(a_{1}^{\prime})\}}\!\frac{d^{2}u_{p}}{{|z-u_{p}|}}\leq C\varepsilon d\left|\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right|\kern 5.0pt\text{and}\kern 5.0pt\left|\oint_{\partial B_{\varepsilon d}(a_{1}^{\prime})}\!\frac{\langle\mu_{a_{1}^{\prime}}\sigma_{a_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\psi_{z}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}^{\prime}}\sigma_{a_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{0}}\frac{dz}{2\pi i(z-a_{1}^{\prime})^{\frac{3}{2}}}\right|\leq Cd^{-1},

by, e.g., a scaling argument and (
6.3.2
) respectively.

Similarly,

\displaystyle\Bigg|\oint_{\partial B_{\varepsilon d}(a_{1}^{\prime})}\int_{\Omega}\int_{\Omega^{p-1}}{\mathfrak{A}_{p}(u,z)}d^{2}u_{1}\cdots d^{2}u_{p-1}\frac{\mathbf{1}_{\{\gamma_{\varepsilon}\setminus B_{d/16}(a_{1}^{\prime})\}}(u_{p})}{(z-a_{1}^{\prime})^{\frac{3}{2}}}d^{2}u_{p}\,\frac{dz}{2\pi i}\Bigg|

\displaystyle=\Bigg|\int_{\gamma_{\varepsilon}\setminus B_{d/16}(a_{1}^{\prime})}\frac{4e^{\frac{i\pi}{4}}}{\pi}\left[i\alpha(u_{p})\overline{A_{p-1}(u_{p})}\right]\star\frac{\langle\partial_{a_{1}^{\prime}}\mu_{a_{1}^{\prime}}\sigma_{a_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\psi^{[\star]}_{u_{p}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}^{\prime}}\sigma_{a_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{0}}d^{2}u_{p}\Bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\,C^{p}\Bigg|\int_{\gamma_{\varepsilon}\setminus B_{d/16}(a_{1}^{\prime})}\Bigg(\frac{d^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\sqrt{|u_{p}-a_{1}^{\prime}|}}+1\Bigg)\cdot\frac{d^{-1/2}}{|u_{p}-a_{1}^{\prime}|^{2}}d^{2}u_{p}\Bigg|,

where in the last line we used (
6.3.20
) and Lemma
6.28
respectively on the two factors, since
\frac{\langle\partial_{a_{1}^{\prime}}\mu_{a_{1}^{\prime}}\sigma_{a_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\psi^{[\star]}_{u_{p}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}^{\prime}}\sigma_{a_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{0}}
may be recovered from the coefficient of
(z-a_{1}^{\prime})^{1/2}
of
\frac{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}^{\prime}}\sigma_{a_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\psi_{z}\psi^{[\star]}_{u_{p}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{a_{1}^{\prime}}\sigma_{a_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}\rangle_{0}}
by the critical OPE (
7.1.4
). Continuing on,

\displaystyle\leq C^{p}\left(\int_{\gamma_{\varepsilon}\setminus B_{d/16}(a_{1}^{\prime})}\frac{1}{|u_{p}-a_{1}^{\prime}|^{\frac{5}{2}}}d^{2}u_{p}+d^{-\frac{1}{2}}\int_{\gamma_{\varepsilon}\setminus B_{d/16}(a_{1}^{\prime})}\frac{1}{|u_{p}-a_{1}^{\prime}|^{2}}d^{2}u_{p}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C^{p}\left(\varepsilon\cdot d^{-\frac{1}{2}}+d^{-\frac{1}{2}}\cdot\varepsilon\cdot(1+\log|d|)\right)

\displaystyle\leq C^{p}\varepsilon d^{-\frac{1}{2}}(1+\log|d|),

using the fact that, e.g.,
\gamma_{\varepsilon}\setminus B_{d/16}(a_{1}^{\prime})
is contained in two wedges cornered at
a_{1}^{\prime}
whose angles are bounded by
C\cdot\varepsilon
.

Line integrating (in
a_{1}^{\prime}
) the above two estimates, we have

\left|\int_{a_{b}}^{a_{1}}\lambda(a_{1}^{\prime};A_{p})da_{1}^{\prime}-\int_{\Omega^{p}}\int_{a_{b}}^{a_{1}}{\lambda(a_{1}^{\prime};\mathfrak{A}_{p})\mathbf{1}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(u_{p})}da_{1}^{\prime}\,d^{2p}u\right|\leq C^{p}\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}|\log\varepsilon|,

where now
C=C(\Omega,\kappa_{\infty},n,\delta_{0},\gamma)
.

In addition, note that (
6.3.2
) (with preceding bounds, Lemma
6.18
and (
6.2.3
)) implies that

(7.1.7)

\displaystyle|\lambda(a_{1}^{\prime};\mathfrak{A}_{p})|\leq|{\mathfrak{A}_{p-1}(u)}|\cdot\left(\frac{d^{-1}}{|u_{p}-a_{1}^{\prime}||u_{p}-a_{2}|\cdots|u_{p}-a_{n}|}\right)\cdot P_{a_{1}^{\prime}}(u_{p}).

Since

\left|\mathbf{1}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(u_{p})-\mathbf{1}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{p}}(u)\right|\leq\sum_{k=1}^{p-1}\mathbf{1}_{\gamma_{\varepsilon}}(u_{k})\mathbf{1}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(u_{p}),

and by (
6.3.20
), for any
k<p
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega^{p-1}}

\displaystyle\left|\lambda(a_{1}^{\prime};\mathfrak{A}_{p})\mathbf{1}_{\gamma_{\varepsilon}}(u_{k})\mathbf{1}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(u_{p})\right|d^{2}u_{1}\cdots d^{2}u_{p-1}\leq\frac{C^{p}\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}d^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathbf{1}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(u_{p})}{|u_{p}-a_{1}^{\prime}|^{2}|u_{p}-a_{2}|\cdots|u_{p}-a_{n}|}.

Then we do the remaining integrals, first with respect to
u_{p}
:

\int_{\Omega}\frac{C^{p}\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}d^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathbf{1}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(u_{p})}{|u_{p}-a_{1}^{\prime}|^{2}|u_{p}-a_{2}|\cdots|u_{p}-a_{n}|}d^{2}u_{p}\leq C^{p}\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}d^{-\frac{1}{2}}|\log(\varepsilon d)|,

then doing the line integral finally gives

\left|\int_{a_{b}}^{a_{1}}\lambda(a_{1}^{\prime};A_{p})da_{1}^{\prime}-\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{p}}\int_{a_{b}}^{a_{1}}{\lambda(a_{1}^{\prime};\mathfrak{A}_{p})da_{1}^{\prime}\,d^{2p}u}\right|\leq{pC^{p}\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}}|\log\varepsilon|.

The identical bound holds when we put real parts in front of the line integrals. By domain symmetry, it is easy to see that the identical bound holds for
{\lambda(a_{1}^{\prime};\mathfrak{A}_{p}^{\text{sym}})}
.

Then, note that, if
\mathrm{Re}\big[\int_{a_{b}}^{a_{1}}{\lambda(a_{1}^{\prime};\mathfrak{A}_{p}^{\text{sym}})}{da_{1}^{\prime}}\big]
is (absolutely) integrable in
\Omega^{p}
, dominated convergence yields (
7.1.6
) as desired. We show this in the following lemma.

Lemma 7.5
.

\mathrm{Re}\big[\int_{a_{b}}^{a_{1}}{\lambda(a_{1}^{\prime};\mathfrak{A}_{p}^{\text{sym}})}{da_{1}^{\prime}}\big]
is (absolutely) integrable.

Proof of Lemma
7.5
.

Note that, since
\lambda(a_{1}^{\prime};\mathfrak{A}_{p}^{\text{sym}})
is a
\partial_{a^{\prime}}
-derivative of a real quantity (i.e. the integrand on the RHS of (
7.1.5
), see also the note below about boundary limits), we may choose
a_{b}
and the integration contour conveniently. For fixed
a_{1}
, we choose the contour
\gamma
of length
l\leq C(\Omega,n,\delta_{0},p)
and its unit-speed parametrization
\gamma:[-l,0]\to\Omega
satisfies, for each
j=2,\ldots,n
and
k=1,\ldots,p
, we have, with
\gamma(t)=a_{1}^{\prime}
,

|\gamma(t)-a_{j}|\geq C(\Omega,n,\delta_{0},p)\quad\text{and}\quad|\gamma(t)-u_{k}|\geq C(\Omega,n,\delta_{0},p)(|a_{1}-u_{k}|+|t|).

This can be done by identifying a wedge cornered at
a_{1}
, not containing any other
a_{2},\ldots,a_{n}
and
u_{1},\ldots,u_{p}
and having an angle bounded below by
C(\Omega,n,\delta_{0},p)
.

Having fixed
\gamma
, we first bound the non-symmetric
|\lambda(\gamma(t);\mathfrak{A}_{p})|
, since symmetrization will preserve this bound.
Let us carefully study the (
C(n,p)
many) terms of the expansion in the bound (
7.1.7
), where we substitute in (
6.3.16
) for
p-1
and
a_{1}=\gamma(t)
. Each term is a product of (
C(n,p)
many) factors of type (at the highest order)
|u_{k}-u_{k+1}|^{-1}
for
k<n
,
|a_{j}-u_{k}|^{-3/2}
for
j>1
,
|\gamma(t)-u_{k}|^{-3/2}
for
k<p
, and
|\gamma(t)-u_{p}|^{-2}
. We need to show that after the line integral we get small but strict improvements in the exponents. However, that is immediate from the following general computation: for
r_{1},\ldots,r_{N}\in(1,2)
and
e_{1},\ldots,e_{N}>0
, we have by Hölder (set
R:=r_{1}+\cdots+r_{N}
)

\int_{-l}^{0}\prod_{q=1}^{N}\frac{1}{|e_{q}+|t||^{r_{q}}}dt\leq\prod_{q=1}^{N}\left(\int_{-l}^{0}\frac{1}{|e_{q}+|t||^{R}}dt\right)^{r_{q}/R}\leq C(N,l)\prod_{q=1}^{N}{e_{q}}^{-r_{q}+\frac{r_{q}}{R}}.

∎

This concludes the proof of Proposition
7.2
.
∎

7.2.
Fermion and disorder correlations

In this section, we study the series expansion of spin-weighted correlations of fermions and disorders; since disorders are defined as the renormalized limit of the merger of spins and fermions, we study spin-weighted fermion correlations in significant detail first. Recall that we write
\Psi
for either of
\psi,\psi^{*}
, or equivalently a real fermion
\psi^{[\eta]}
with
|\eta|=1
.

7.2.1.
Fermion-fermion correlations

We will first treat the two-point correlation of fermions weighted by spin, in order to use it to expand general
2k
-point correlations.

Proposition 7.6
.

For distinct points
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}
,
z_{1},z_{2}
, the correlation
\frac{\langle\sigma_{A}\Psi_{z_{1}}\Psi_{z_{2}}\rangle_{m\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{A}\rangle_{m\alpha}}
has a series expansion around any
m=m_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
with the usual conditions. In particular, around
m_{0}=0
, we have

(7.2.1)

\displaystyle\frac{\langle\sigma_{A}\Psi_{z_{1}}\Psi_{z_{2}}\rangle_{m\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{A}\rangle_{m\alpha}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega^{p}}\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{p}(z_{2};u,z_{1})d^{2p}u

\displaystyle=\sum_{p=0}^{\infty}\left(-\frac{m}{\pi}\right)^{p}\frac{1}{p!}\int_{\Omega^{p}}d^{2p}u\prod_{k=1}^{p}\alpha(u_{k})\sum_{S\subset\{1,\ldots,p\}}\frac{\langle\sigma_{A}\Psi_{z_{1}}\Psi_{z_{2}}\prod_{j\in S}\varepsilon_{u_{j}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{A}\rangle_{0}}

\displaystyle\quad\times\sum_{\Lambda\in\Pi_{\{1,\ldots,p\}\setminus S}}(-1)^{|\Lambda|}|\Lambda|!\prod_{B\in\Lambda}\frac{\langle\sigma_{A}\prod_{j\in B}\varepsilon_{u_{j}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{A}\rangle_{0}},

where we again used the notation
\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}=\sigma_{A}
and the set
\Pi_{U}
of partitions of
U
, with the convention that
\Pi_{\emptyset}=\{\emptyset\}
.

Proof.

Due to real (bi)linearity, it suffices to show this result for
\Psi_{z_{1}}=\psi_{z_{1}}
and
\Psi_{z_{2}}=\psi_{z_{2}}^{[\eta]}
(see also (
6.1.2
)), when the coefficients are given by
B_{p}
in (
6.3.14
). Then we have series expansion under usual conditions by Remark
6.37
, (
6.2.7
) (for
p=0
), and (
6.3.20
) (for
p\geq 1
).

Around
m_{0}=0
, as in Proposition
7.3
, we will only use integrand-level identities and symmetrization on components of
u
. Therefore, we do automatically have a local exponential integral bound for
|\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{p}(z_{2};\cdot,z_{1})|
, obtained by symmetrization of the bounds (
6.3.20
) as in Remark
7.4
(note again that the integrand in (
7.2.1
) is invariant under any transposition).

The inductive strategy as in the proof of Proposition
7.3
applies here almost identically, so we leave out many details elaborated there. Noting that the
p=0
case holds trivially, let us assume (
7.2.1
) for
p
. Then, fixing
S
and
\Lambda
then omitting common parts as in (
7.1.3
), we see that the
S
-specific part for the
p+1
case would be written (again writing
S^{\prime}=S\cup\{p+1\}
and symmetrizing
p+1
with
j\in S
, i.e. over
S^{\prime}
)

\displaystyle\frac{i}{2}

\displaystyle\frac{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\psi^{*}_{u_{p+1}}\psi_{z_{2}}^{[\eta]}\prod_{j\in S}\varepsilon_{u_{j}}\big\rangle_{0}}{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\big\rangle_{0}}\frac{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\psi_{z_{1}}\psi_{u_{p+1}}\big\rangle_{0}}{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\big\rangle_{0}}-\frac{i}{2}\frac{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\psi_{u_{p+1}}\psi_{z_{2}}^{[\eta]}\prod_{j\in S}\varepsilon_{u_{j}}\big\rangle_{0}}{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\big\rangle_{0}}\frac{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\psi_{z_{1}}\psi_{u_{p+1}}^{*}\big\rangle_{0}}{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\big\rangle_{0}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle sym}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|S|+1}\left(\frac{i}{2}\right)^{|S|+1}\sum_{j^{\prime}\in S^{\prime}}\Bigg[\frac{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\psi_{z_{2}}^{[\eta]}\psi^{*}_{u_{j^{\prime}}}\prod_{j\in S^{\prime}\setminus\{j^{\prime}\}}\psi_{u_{j}}\psi_{u_{j}}^{*}\big\rangle_{0}}{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\big\rangle_{0}}\frac{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\psi_{u_{j^{\prime}}}\psi_{z_{1}}\big\rangle_{0}}{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\big\rangle_{0}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-\frac{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\psi_{z_{2}}^{[\eta]}\psi_{u_{j^{\prime}}}\prod_{j\in S^{\prime}\setminus\{j^{\prime}\}}\psi_{u_{j}}\psi_{u_{j}}^{*}\big\rangle_{0}}{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\big\rangle_{0}}\frac{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\psi^{*}_{u_{j^{\prime}}}\psi_{z_{1}}\big\rangle_{0}}{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\big\rangle_{0}}\Bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|S^{\prime}|}\Bigg[\frac{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\psi_{z_{1}}\psi_{z_{2}}^{[\eta]}\prod_{j\in S^{\prime}}\varepsilon_{u_{j}}\big\rangle_{0}}{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\big\rangle_{0}}-\frac{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\prod_{j\in S^{\prime}}\varepsilon_{u_{j}}\big\rangle_{0}}{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\big\rangle_{0}}\frac{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\psi_{z_{1}}\psi_{z_{2}}^{[\eta]}\big\rangle_{0}}{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\big\rangle_{0}}\Bigg].

Similarly to the proof of Proposition
7.3
, the first term gives rise to accurate
(\Lambda,S^{\prime})
terms in the
p+1
case where
|S^{\prime}|\geq 1
, after one more symmetrization of
p+1
and
1,2,\ldots,p
. The second term, on the other hand, corresponds to the
(\Lambda^{\prime},\emptyset)
terms, where
\Lambda^{\prime}=\Lambda\cup\{S^{\prime}\}
. The minus sign corrects
(-1)^{|\Lambda|}
pre-factor to
(-1)^{|\Lambda^{\prime}|}
, and after symmetrization the above term will appear
|S^{\prime}|
times. Since there are
|\Lambda^{\prime}|
choices of
S^{\prime}\in\Lambda^{\prime}
, we get the necessary pre-factor of
|\Lambda^{\prime}|
as well.
∎

Then, we state the following generalization for the
2k
-point correlations.

Lemma 7.7
.

For distinct points
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}
,
z_{1},\dots,z_{2k}
, the correlation
\frac{\langle\sigma_{A}\Psi_{z_{1}}\cdots\Psi_{z_{2k}}\rangle_{m\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{A}\rangle_{m\alpha}}
admits a series expansion around any
m=m_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
with the usual conditions. Around
m_{0}=0
, we have

(7.2.2)

\frac{\langle\sigma_{A}\Psi_{z_{1}}\cdots\Psi_{z_{2k}}\rangle_{m\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{A}\rangle_{m\alpha}}=\sum_{p=0}^{\infty}\left(-\frac{m}{\pi}\right)^{p}\frac{1}{p!}\int_{\Omega^{p}}d^{2}w_{1}\cdots d^{2}w_{p}\alpha(w_{1})\cdots\alpha(w_{p})\\
\times\sum_{S\subset\{1,\ldots,p\}}\frac{\langle\sigma_{A}\Psi_{z_{1}}\cdots\Psi_{z_{2k}}\prod_{j\in S}\varepsilon_{w_{j}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{A}\rangle_{0}}\sum_{\Lambda\in\Pi_{\{1,\ldots,p\}\setminus S}}(-1)^{|\Lambda|}|\Lambda|!\prod_{B\in\Lambda}\frac{\langle\sigma_{A}\prod_{j\in B}\varepsilon_{w_{j}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{A}\rangle_{0}}.

Our proof of Lemma
7.7
(specifically (
7.2.2
)), to be presented after introducing some tools, goes through an identity for critical spin-fermion-energy correlation functions, and this we find most convenient to prove through tools coming from Grassmann integrals – though it is likely that there are more combinatorial proofs as well.

While analyticity and the usual conditions are simple consequences of the corresponding properties for the two-point correlations and analyticity of the Pfaffian (see Remark
7.1
), we find it instructive to do the following manual counting.

Remark 7.8
.

Note the Pfaffian expansion (
6.1.15
) may be written as a sum of
\frac{(2k)!}{2^{k}k!}
terms, each having
k
factors in the form of
2
-point correlations. Counting non-negative integer partitions of
p
into
k
terms, we see that the bound (
7.0.1
) for the
2
-point correlation coefficients (which came from (
6.3.21
) and linearity) translate to the following local exponential bound for the coefficients of
(m-m_{0})^{p}
for the
2k
-point correlation:

(7.2.3)

\frac{(2k)!}{p!\cdot 2^{k}k!}\binom{p+k-1}{k-1}\cdot C(M,\Omega,\kappa_{\infty},n,\delta_{0},d_{z})^{p}\leq C(k,M,\Omega,\kappa_{\infty},n,\delta_{0},d_{z})^{p},

where
0<d_{z}\leq\min(\delta_{z_{1}},\ldots,\delta_{z_{2k}},\min_{j<j^{\prime}}|z_{j}-z_{j^{\prime}}|)
. That is, in the following where we merge fermion insertions with other fields, in order to claim local exponential bounded coefficients (and thus usual conditions, since local continuity can then be shown using arguments akin to Remark
6.37
), we need only check that the bound (
6.3.21
) is kept.

7.2.2.
Pfaffian expansion and Grassmann integrals

Our proof of Lemma
7.7
will be by induction, and it is convenient to have a recursive definition of the Pfaffian of a skew symmetric matrix. Recall that for a
2k\times 2k
skew symmetric matrix
M
, we have

(7.2.4)

\mathrm{Pf}(M)=\sum_{l=2}^{2k}(-1)^{l}M_{1,l}\mathrm{Pf}(M_{i,j})_{i,j\neq 1,l}.

Another basic fact about Pfaffians we will use is that
(\mathrm{Pf}(M))^{2}=\det(M)
(for a skew symmetric matrix
M
). In particular, if
\mathrm{Pf}(M)\neq 0
, then
M
is invertible.

We now review the connection between Pfaffians and Grassmann integrals. For a more detailed treatment, see
[
32
, Section I]
.

Our starting point is that we consider an exterior algebra with an even number of generators
b_{1},\dots,b_{2q}
. Then given any Grassmann monomial, namely an element of the exterior algebra of the form
b_{i_{1}}\cdots b_{i_{j}}
with
i_{1}<i_{2}<\dots<i_{j}
, we define the Grassmann integral

(7.2.5)

\int Db\,b_{i_{1}}\cdots b_{i_{j}}=\begin{cases}1,&j=2q\text{ (and }i_{k}=k\text{ for }k=1,\dots,2q)\\
0,&\text{else}\end{cases}.

Since Grassmann monomials form a (vector space) basis of the exterior algebra, we can extend the integral
\int Db
into a linear functional on the exterior algebra.

Since the square of a generator of the exterior algebra vanishes,
b_{i}^{2}=0
(by antisymmetry), an analytic function of the generators (defined by series expansion around the origin) produces an element of the exterior algebra and we can define its integral. In particular, for any skew symmetric
2q\times 2q
complex valued matrix
M
, we have by (
7.2.5
)

(7.2.6)

\int Db\,e^{\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{2q}b_{i}M_{ij}b_{j}}=\frac{1}{2^{q}q!}\int Db\Bigg(\sum_{i,j=1}^{2q}b_{i}M_{i,j}b_{j}\Bigg)^{q}

(since all other terms in the expansion of the exponential either vanish identically or integrate to zero due to their degree). Expanding the power, we see that the only terms that survive the
2q
-form sum (
7.2.6
) are of the form

\displaystyle\int Db\,e^{\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{2q}b_{i}M_{ij}b_{j}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{q}q!}\sum_{\sigma\in S_{2q}}\prod_{i=1}^{q}M_{\sigma(2i-1),\sigma(2i)}\int Db\,b_{\sigma(1)}\cdots b_{\sigma(2q)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{q}q!}\sum_{\sigma\in S_{2q}}\mathrm{sgn}(\sigma)\prod_{i=1}^{q}M_{\sigma(2i-1),\sigma(2i)}=\mathrm{Pf}(M).

For our purposes, we will actually find it more convenient to write suitable ratios of Grassmann integrals in terms of Pfaffians. More precisely, if
S
is any
2q\times 2q
skew symmetric complex matrix that is invertible, and
1\leq i_{1}<\dots<i_{j}\leq 2q
, then it is proven in
[
32
, Section I]
that

(7.2.7)

\displaystyle\frac{\int Db\,b_{i_{1}}\cdots b_{i_{j}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{2q}b_{i}(S^{-1})_{i,j}b_{j}}}{\int Db\,e^{-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{2q}b_{i}(S^{-1})_{i,j}b_{j}}}=\mathrm{Pf}(S_{i_{r},i_{s}})_{r,s=1}^{j}.

We now turn to expressing critical Ising correlation functions in terms of Grassmann integrals. We choose the matrix
S
in (
7.2.7
) suitably so that we recover spin-fermion Ising correlation functions. We will also show that by allowing
S
to depend on suitable parameters, we can obtain also energy correlation functions via differentiation with respect to these parameters.

We are interested in a correlation function of the form

\displaystyle\frac{\langle\sigma_{A}\Psi_{z_{1}}\cdots\Psi_{z_{2k}}\prod_{j\in C}\varepsilon_{w_{j}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{A}\rangle_{0}}.

Let us enumerate the points of
C
:
C=\{c_{1},\dots,c_{l}\}
. Since
\varepsilon=\frac{i}{2}\psi\psi^{*}
at criticality, it is enough for us to understand correlation functions of the form

(7.2.8)

\displaystyle\frac{\langle\sigma_{A}\Psi_{z_{1}}\cdots\Psi_{z_{2k}}\psi_{u_{1}}\psi_{w_{c_{1}}}^{*}\cdots\psi_{u_{l}}\psi_{w_{c_{l}}}^{*}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{A}\rangle_{0}}

and then take
u_{i}\to w_{c_{i}}
(and multiply by a suitable constant). Note that this is either analytic or anti-analytic (with branching) in the fermionic variables and does not vanish identically (as one can check from the OPEs). Thus the correlation function is non-zero at generic points.

To connect to Grassmann integrals, we introduce the
2(k+l)\times 2(k+l)
skew symmetric complex matrix for which we have

(7.2.9)

S_{ij}=\begin{cases}\frac{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\Psi_{z_{i}}\Psi_{z_{j}}\big\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{A}\rangle_{0}},&i,j\leq 2k\\
\frac{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\Psi_{z_{i}}\psi_{u_{j-2k}}\big\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{A}\rangle_{0}},&i\leq 2k<j\leq 2k+l\\
\frac{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\Psi_{z_{i}}\psi_{w_{c_{j-2k-l}}}^{*}\big\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{A}\rangle_{0}},&i\leq 2k,j>2k+l\\
\frac{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\psi_{u_{i-2k}}\psi_{u_{j-2k}}\big\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{A}\rangle_{0}},&2k<i,j\leq 2k+l\\
\frac{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\psi_{u_{i-2k}}\psi_{w_{c_{j-2k-l}}}^{*}\big\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{A}\rangle_{0}},&2k<i\leq 2k+l<j\\
\frac{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\psi_{w_{c_{i-2k-l}}}^{*}\psi_{w_{c_{j-2k-l}}}^{*}\big\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{A}\rangle_{0}},&2k+l<i,j\end{cases}

As the Pfaffian of this matrix is the correlation function (
7.2.8
) that we are considering, by (
6.1.15
), and the correlation function is non-vanishing generically, we see that
S
is invertible generically. Let us go on to define for some small enough parameters
t_{1},\dots,t_{l}
a matrix

(7.2.10)

(S(t)^{-1})_{i,j}=(S^{-1})_{i,j}-\sum_{q=1}^{l}t_{q}(\delta_{2k+q,i}\delta_{2k+l+q,j}-\delta_{2k+q,j}\delta_{2k+l+q,i}).

For small enough
t
, this matrix exists (generically).

Our point of introducing this matrix is the following fact.

Lemma 7.9
.

We have

(7.2.11)

\displaystyle\frac{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\Psi_{z_{1}}\cdots\Psi_{z_{2k}}\psi_{u_{1}}\psi_{w_{c_{1}}}^{*}\cdots\psi_{u_{l}}\psi_{w_{c_{l}}}^{*}\big\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{A}\rangle_{0}}

\displaystyle\qquad=\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial t_{1}}\cdots\frac{\partial}{\partial t_{l}}\right|_{t=0}\frac{\int Db\,b_{1}\cdots b_{2k}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{2(k+l)}b_{i}(S(t)^{-1})_{i,j}b_{j}}}{\int Db\,e^{-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{2(k+l)}b_{i}(S^{-1})_{i,j}b_{j}}}.

Proof.

Since the generators anticommute and even elements of the exterior algebra (namely linear combinations of products of an even number of generators) commute, we can write

\displaystyle e^{-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{2(k+l)}b_{i}(S(t)^{-1})_{i,j}b_{j}}

\displaystyle=e^{-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{2(k+l)}b_{i}(S^{-1})_{i,j}b_{j}+\sum_{q=1}^{l}t_{q}b_{2k+q}b_{2k+l+q}}

\displaystyle=e^{-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{2(k+l)}b_{i}(S^{-1})_{i,j}b_{j}}\prod_{q=1}^{l}(1+t_{q}b_{2k+q}b_{2k+l+q}).

We thus see that differentiation and setting
t=0
picks out the term

\displaystyle\frac{\int Db\,b_{1}\cdots b_{2k}b_{2k+1}b_{2k+l+1}\cdots b_{2k+l}b_{2k+2l}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{2(k+l)}b_{i}(S^{-1})_{i,j}b_{j}}}{\int Db\,e^{-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{2(k+l)}b_{i}(S^{-1})_{i,j}b_{j}}}.

By the definition of
S
and (
7.2.7
), we see that we can express this as the same Pfaffian as the correlation function we are after, so we have proven our claim.
∎

The point of this lemma is that we can express the right hand side of (
7.2.11
) as a ratio of Pfaffians and use the recursive property of Pfaffians to get a useful recursion for spin-fermion-energy correlation functions. The precise claim is as follows.

Lemma 7.10
.

We have the identity

(7.2.12)

\displaystyle\frac{\langle\sigma_{A}\Psi_{z_{1}}\cdots\Psi_{z_{2k}}\prod_{i\in C}\varepsilon_{w_{i}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{A}\rangle_{0}}

\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{l=2}^{2k}(-1)^{l}\sum_{U\subset C}\sum_{U_{1}\sqcup U_{2}=U}\frac{\langle\sigma_{A}\Psi_{z_{1}}\Psi_{z_{l}}\prod_{j_{1}\in U_{1}}\varepsilon_{w_{j_{1}}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{A}\rangle_{0}}\frac{\langle\sigma_{A}\Psi_{z_{2}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hat{l}}}{{\cdots}}\Psi_{z_{2k}}\prod_{j_{2}\in U_{2}}\varepsilon_{w_{j_{2}}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{A}\rangle_{0}}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{C\setminus U}}(-1)^{|\pi|}|\pi|!\prod_{B\in\pi}\frac{\langle\sigma_{A}\prod_{j\in B}\varepsilon_{w_{j}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{A}\rangle_{0}},

where if
U=C
, we interpret the sum over
\pi
as
1
.

Proof.

As mentioned before, we can replace energies by pairs of fermions. Also we can assume that we are first dealing with generic points where
S
is invertible, then extend to all points by taking limits.

We begin by using Lemma
7.9
to write

\displaystyle\frac{\langle\sigma_{A}\Psi_{z_{1}}\cdots\Psi_{z_{2k}}\psi_{u_{1}}\psi_{w_{c_{1}}}^{*}\cdots\psi_{u_{q}}\psi_{w_{c_{q}}}^{*}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{A}\rangle_{0}}

\displaystyle=\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial t_{1}}\cdots\frac{\partial}{\partial t_{q}}\right|_{t=0}\frac{\int Db\,b_{1}\cdots b_{2k}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{2(k+q)}b_{i}(S(t)^{-1})_{i,j}b_{j}}}{\int Db\,e^{-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{2(k+q)}b_{i}(S^{-1})_{i,j}b_{j}}}

\displaystyle=\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial t_{1}}\cdots\frac{\partial}{\partial t_{q}}\right|_{t=0}\frac{\mathrm{Pf}(-S(t)^{-1})}{\mathrm{Pf}(-S^{-1})}\mathrm{Pf}(S(t))_{i,j=1}^{2k}.

Next we use (
7.2.4
) for the last Pfaffian and find

\displaystyle\frac{\langle\sigma_{A}\Psi_{z_{1}}\cdots\Psi_{z_{2k}}\psi_{u_{1}}\psi_{w_{c_{1}}}^{*}\cdots\psi_{u_{q}}\psi_{w_{c_{q}}}^{*}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{A}\rangle_{0}}

\displaystyle\quad=\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial t_{1}}\cdots\frac{\partial}{\partial t_{q}}\right|_{t=0}\frac{\mathrm{Pf}(-S(t)^{-1})}{\mathrm{Pf}(-S^{-1})}\sum_{l=2}^{2k}(-1)^{l}S_{1,l}(t)\mathrm{Pf}(S_{i,j}(t))_{i,j\neq 1,l}

\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{l=2}^{2k}(-1)^{l}\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial t_{1}}\cdots\frac{\partial}{\partial t_{q}}\right|_{t=0}\frac{\mathrm{Pf}(-S^{-1})}{\mathrm{Pf}(-S(t)^{-1})}\frac{\int Db\,b_{1}b_{l}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{2(k+q)}b_{i}(S(t)^{-1})_{i,j}b_{j}}}{\int Db\,e^{-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{2(k+q)}b_{i}(S^{-1})_{i,j}b_{j}}}

\displaystyle\quad\qquad\times\frac{\int Db\,b_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hat{l}}}{{\cdots}}b_{2k}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{2(k+q)}b_{i}(S(t)^{-1})_{i,j}b_{j}}}{\int Db\,e^{-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{2(k+q)}b_{i}(S^{-1})_{i,j}b_{j}}}.

We now split this into a sum over all the possible ways the derivatives can act on the different factors:

\displaystyle\frac{\langle\sigma_{A}\Psi_{z_{1}}\cdots\Psi_{z_{2k}}\psi_{u_{1}}\psi_{w_{c_{1}}}^{*}\cdots\psi_{u_{q}}\psi_{w_{c_{q}}}^{*}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{A}\rangle_{0}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=2}^{2k}(-1)^{l}\sum_{U_{1}\sqcup U_{2}\sqcup U_{3}=C}\prod_{r_{1}\in U_{1}}\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial t_{r_{1}}}\right|_{t_{r_{1}}=0}\frac{\mathrm{Pf}(-S^{-1})}{\mathrm{Pf}(-S(t)^{-1})}

\displaystyle\quad\times\prod_{r_{2}\in U_{2}}\frac{\partial}{\partial t_{r_{2}}}\bigg|_{t_{r_{2}}=0}\frac{\int Db\,b_{1}b_{l}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{2(k+q)}b_{i}(S(t)^{-1})_{i,j}b_{j}}}{\int Db\,e^{-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{2(k+q)}b_{i}(S^{-1})_{i,j}b_{j}}}

\displaystyle\quad\times\prod_{r_{3}\in U_{3}}\frac{\partial}{\partial t_{r_{3}}}\bigg|_{t_{r_{3}}=0}\!\frac{\int Db\,b_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hat{l}}}{{\cdots}}b_{2k}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{2(k+q)}b_{i}(S(t)^{-1})_{i,j}b_{j}}}{\int Db\,e^{-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{2(k+q)}b_{i}(S^{-1})_{i,j}b_{j}}}.

The last two rows are readily expressed as correlation functions using Lemma
7.9
. For the remaining one, we will make use of the fact that if
f
is sufficiently smooth in a neighborhood of the origin and
f(0)=1
, we can then use Faà di Bruno’s formula to write

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial t_{1}}\cdots\frac{\partial}{\partial t_{m}}\right|_{t=0}\frac{1}{f(t)}=\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{m}}(-1)^{|\pi|}|\pi|!\prod_{B\in\pi}\Bigg(\prod_{j\in B}\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial t_{j}}\right|_{t_{j}=0}f(t)\Bigg).

Note that for our
f
, the derivatives generate spin-energy correlation functions. We thus find after using our genericity argument, merging
u_{j}
and
w_{c_{j}}
and multiplying by a suitable constant,

\displaystyle\frac{\langle\sigma_{A}\Psi_{z_{1}}\cdots\Psi_{z_{2k}}\prod_{j\in C}\varepsilon_{w_{j}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{A}\rangle_{0}}

\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{l=2}^{2k}(-1)^{l}\sum_{U_{1}\sqcup U_{2}\sqcup U_{3}=C}\frac{\langle\sigma_{A}\Psi_{z_{1}}\Psi_{z_{l}}\prod_{j_{1}\in U_{1}}\varepsilon_{w_{j_{1}}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{A}\rangle_{0}}\frac{\langle\sigma_{A}\Psi_{z_{2}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hat{l}}}{{\cdots}}\Psi_{z_{2k}}\prod_{j_{2}\in U_{2}}\varepsilon_{w_{j_{2}}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{A}\rangle_{0}}

\displaystyle\quad\qquad\times\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{U_{3}}}(-1)^{|\pi|}|\pi|!\prod_{B\in\pi}\frac{\langle\sigma_{A}\prod_{j_{3}\in B}\varepsilon_{w_{j_{3}}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{A}\rangle_{0}}.

Slightly reorganizing the sum, we recover our claim.
∎

We are now in a position to prove Lemma
7.7
.

Proof of Lemma
7.7
.

As already pointed out, analyticity and existence of the series expansion (with usual conditions) follows from the case of two fermion insertions (Proposition
7.6
) and expressing Proposition
6.16
. Thus it is sufficient to check that the expansion coefficients in the two series in the statement of the lemma match. We will do this by induction (the
p=0
case being obvious).

Let us start with the right hand side of the claim in Lemma
7.7
and plug Lemma
7.10
into it. We then have

\displaystyle\sum_{C\subset\{1,\ldots,p\}}\frac{\langle\sigma_{A}\Psi_{z_{1}}\cdots\Psi_{z_{2k}}\prod_{j\in C}\varepsilon_{w_{j}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{A}\rangle_{0}}\sum_{\Lambda\in\Pi_{\{1,\ldots,p\}\setminus C}}(-1)^{|\Lambda|}|\Lambda|!\prod_{B\in\Lambda}\frac{\langle\sigma_{A}\prod_{j\in B}\varepsilon_{w_{j}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{A}\rangle_{0}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=2}^{2k}(-1)^{l}\!\sum_{C\subset\{1,\dots,p\}}\sum_{U\subset C}\sum_{U_{1}\sqcup U_{2}=U}\sum_{\Lambda\in\Pi_{\{1,\dots,p\}\setminus C}}\!(-1)^{|\Lambda|}|\Lambda|!\sum_{\nu\in\Pi_{C\setminus U}}\!(-1)^{|\nu|}|\nu|!\prod_{B^{\prime}\in\nu}\frac{\langle\sigma_{A}\prod_{j^{\prime}\in B^{\prime}}\varepsilon_{w_{j^{\prime}}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{A}\rangle_{0}}

\displaystyle\quad\times\frac{\langle\sigma_{A}\Psi_{z_{1}}\Psi_{z_{l}}\prod_{j_{1}\in U_{1}}\varepsilon_{w_{j_{1}}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{A}\rangle_{0}}\frac{\langle\sigma_{A}\Psi_{z_{2}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hat{l}}}{{\cdots}}\Psi_{z_{2k}}\prod_{j_{2}\in U_{2}}\varepsilon_{w_{j_{2}}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{A}\rangle_{0}}\prod_{B\in\Lambda}\frac{\langle\sigma_{A}\prod_{j\in B}\varepsilon_{w_{j}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{A}\rangle_{0}}.

Next we change the order of the
U
and
C
sums, and note that summing over
C
is equivalent to summing over subsets of
\{1,\dots,p\}\setminus U
. This becomes

\displaystyle\sum_{l=2}^{2k}(-1)^{l}\sum_{U\subset\{1,\dots,p\}}\sum_{U_{1}\sqcup U_{2}=U}\frac{\langle\sigma_{A}\Psi_{z_{1}}\Psi_{z_{l}}\prod_{j_{1}\in U_{1}}\varepsilon_{w_{j_{1}}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{A}\rangle_{0}}\frac{\langle\sigma_{A}\Psi_{z_{2}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hat{l}}}{{\cdots}}\Psi_{z_{2k}}\prod_{j_{2}\in U_{2}}\varepsilon_{w_{j_{2}}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{A}\rangle_{0}}

\displaystyle\times\hskip-2.0pt\sum_{D\subset\{1,\dots,p\}\setminus U}\sum_{\Lambda\in\Pi_{\{1,\dots,p\}\setminus(U\cup D)}}\sum_{\nu\in\Pi_{D}}(-1)^{|\Lambda|+|\nu|}|\Lambda|!|\nu|!\prod_{B\in\Lambda}\frac{\langle\sigma_{A}\prod_{j\in B}\varepsilon_{w_{j}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{A}\rangle_{0}}\prod_{B^{\prime}\in\nu}\frac{\langle\sigma_{A}\prod_{j^{\prime}\in B^{\prime}}\varepsilon_{w_{j^{\prime}}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{A}\rangle_{0}}.

We now turn to the left hand side. By (
6.1.15
), (
7.2.4
), (
7.2.1
) and our induction assumption (
7.2.2
), we can write

\displaystyle\frac{\langle\sigma_{A}\Psi_{z_{1}}\cdots\Psi_{z_{2k}}\rangle_{m\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{A}\rangle_{m\alpha}}=\sum_{l=2}^{2k}(-1)^{l}\frac{\langle\sigma_{A}\Psi_{z_{1}}\Psi_{z_{l}}\rangle_{m\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{A}\rangle_{m\alpha}}\frac{\langle\sigma_{A}\Psi_{z_{2}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hat{l}}}{{\cdots}}\Psi_{z_{2k}}\rangle_{m\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{A}\rangle_{m\alpha}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=2}^{2k}(-1)^{l}\sum_{p,p^{\prime}=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-m)^{p+p^{\prime}}}{\pi^{p+p^{\prime}}\cdot p!(p^{\prime})!}\int_{\Omega^{p+p^{\prime}}}d^{2}w_{1}\cdots d^{2}w_{p}d^{2}u_{1}\cdots d^{2}u_{p^{\prime}}\alpha(w_{1})\cdots\alpha(w_{p})\alpha(u_{1})\cdots\alpha(u_{p^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\quad\times\sum_{C\subset\{1,\ldots,p\},C^{\prime}\subset\{1,\ldots,p^{\prime}\}}\frac{\langle\sigma_{A}\Psi_{z_{1}}\Psi_{z_{l}}\prod_{i\in C}\varepsilon_{w_{i}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{A}\rangle_{0}}\frac{\langle\sigma_{A}\Psi_{z_{2}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hat{l}}}{{\cdots}}\Psi_{z_{2k}}\prod_{i^{\prime}\in C^{\prime}}\varepsilon_{u_{i^{\prime}}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{A}\rangle_{0}}

\displaystyle\quad\times\sum_{\Lambda\in\Pi_{\{1,\ldots,p\}\setminus C},\Lambda^{\prime}\in\Pi_{\{1,\ldots,p^{\prime}\}\setminus C^{\prime}}}(-1)^{|\Lambda|+|\Lambda^{\prime}|}|\Lambda|!|\Lambda^{\prime}|!\prod_{B\in\Lambda}\frac{\langle\sigma_{A}\prod_{j\in B}\varepsilon_{w_{j}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{A}\rangle_{0}}\prod_{B^{\prime}\in\Lambda^{\prime}}\frac{\langle\sigma_{A}\prod_{j^{\prime}\in B^{\prime}}\varepsilon_{u_{j^{\prime}}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{A}\rangle_{0}}.

Using the symmetry in the
w,u
-variables, we can combine the
p,p^{\prime}
sums by noting that the combinatorial factor
\frac{(p+p^{\prime})!}{p!(p^{\prime})!}
amounts to summing over subsets
D
of
\{1,\dots,p+p^{\prime}\}
of size
p
. We will also form a partition of
\{1,\dots,p\}\setminus(C\cup C^{\prime})
from
\Lambda
and
\Lambda^{\prime}
. More precisely, we write (
p+p^{\prime}
having now been relabeled as
p
)

\displaystyle\frac{\langle\sigma_{A}\Psi_{z_{1}}\cdots\Psi_{z_{2k}}\rangle_{m\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{A}\rangle_{m\alpha}}=\sum_{l=2}^{2k}(-1)^{l}\sum_{p=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-m)^{p}}{\pi^{p}\cdot p!}\int_{\Omega^{p}}d^{2}w_{1}\cdots d^{2}w_{p}\,\alpha(w_{1})\cdots\alpha(w_{p})

\displaystyle\times\!\sum_{D\subset\{1,\dots,p\}}\sum_{C\sqcup C^{\prime}=D}\frac{\langle\sigma_{A}\Psi_{z_{1}}\Psi_{z_{l}}\prod_{i\in C}\varepsilon_{w_{i}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{A}\rangle_{0}}\frac{\langle\sigma_{A}\Psi_{z_{2}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hat{l}}}{{\cdots}}\Psi_{z_{2k}}\prod_{i^{\prime}\in C^{\prime}}\varepsilon_{w_{i^{\prime}}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{A}\rangle_{0}}

\displaystyle\times\sum_{I\subset\{1,\dots,p\}\setminus D}\sum_{\Lambda\in\Pi_{I},\Lambda^{\prime}\in\Pi_{(\{1,\dots,p\}\setminus D)\setminus I}}\!(-1)^{|\Lambda|+|\Lambda^{\prime}|}|\Lambda|!|\Lambda^{\prime}|!\prod_{B\in\Lambda}\!\frac{\langle\sigma_{A}\prod_{j\in B}\varepsilon_{w_{j}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{A}\rangle_{0}}\!\!\prod_{B^{\prime}\in\Lambda^{\prime}}\!\!\frac{\langle\sigma_{A}\prod_{j^{\prime}\in B^{\prime}}\varepsilon_{w_{j^{\prime}}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{A}\rangle_{0}}.

Apart from relabeling the summation indices, this is exactly what we found for the right hand side as well, so we are done.
∎

7.2.3.
Disorder insertions

For full spin-weighted fermion-disorder correlation functions, we recap the definition of such correlation functions using the OPE at criticality: for
k+l
even,

(7.2.13)

\displaystyle\langle\sigma_{V}\mu_{U}\Psi_{Z}\rangle_{m\alpha}

\displaystyle=\!e^{-il\frac{\pi}{4}}\!\lim_{u^{\prime}_{1}\to u_{1}}(u^{\prime}_{1}-u_{1})^{\frac{1}{2}}\cdots\!\lim_{u^{\prime}_{l}\to u_{l}}(u^{\prime}_{l}-u_{l})^{\frac{1}{2}}\langle\sigma_{V}\psi_{u^{\prime}_{1}}\sigma_{u_{1}}\!\cdots\psi_{u^{\prime}_{l}}\sigma_{u_{l}}\Psi_{Z}\rangle_{m\alpha}

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle=e^{-il\frac{\pi}{4}}\lim_{u^{\prime}_{1}\to u_{1}}(u^{\prime}_{1}-u_{1})^{\frac{1}{2}}\cdots\lim_{u^{\prime}_{l}\to u_{l}}(u^{\prime}_{l}-u_{l})^{\frac{1}{2}}\langle\sigma_{V}\sigma_{U}\psi_{U^{\prime}}\Psi_{Z}\rangle_{m\alpha}.

We note the following analog of Lemma
7.7
.

Lemma 7.11
.

For distinct points
v_{1},\dots,v_{n},u_{1},\dots,u_{l},z_{1},\dots,z_{k}
,
l+k
even, the correlation
\frac{\langle\sigma_{V}\mu_{U}\Psi_{Z}\rangle_{m\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{V}\sigma_{U}\rangle_{m\alpha}}
admits a series expansion with the usual conditions around any
m=m_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
. Around
m_{0}=0
, we have

(7.2.14)

\frac{\langle\sigma_{V}\mu_{U}\Psi_{Z}\rangle_{m\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{V}\sigma_{U}\rangle_{m\alpha}}=\sum_{p=0}^{\infty}\left(-\frac{m}{\pi}\right)^{p}\frac{1}{p!}\int_{\Omega^{p}}d^{2p}w\prod_{k=1}^{p}\alpha(w_{k})\sum_{S\subset\{1,\ldots,p\}}\frac{\langle\sigma_{V}\mu_{U}\Psi_{Z}\prod_{i\in S}\varepsilon_{w_{i}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{V}\sigma_{U}\rangle_{0}}\\
\times\sum_{\Lambda\in\Pi_{\{1,\ldots,p\}\setminus S}}(-1)^{|\Lambda|}|\Lambda|!\prod_{B\in\Lambda}\frac{\langle\sigma_{V}\sigma_{U}\prod_{j\in B}\varepsilon_{w_{j}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{V}\sigma_{U}\rangle_{0}}.

Proof.

Note that due to the OPE (
7.2.13
), this is the same claim as saying that the order of the limits
u^{\prime}_{i}\to u_{i}
can be interchanged with the sum/integral in a spin-fermion correlation, i.e., in the expansion of Lemma
7.7
, while keeping the usual conditions.

Given the decomposition of a general spin-fermion correlation using Pfaffian and the argument of Remark
7.8
, it suffices to note that the coefficients remain locally exponentially bounded under a spin-fermion merger.

Given any
\mathfrak{B}_{p}(x_{0};\cdot,x_{p+1})
or
\mathfrak{B}_{p}^{sym}(x_{0};\cdot,x_{p+1})
coming from the expansion of any
2
-point correlation
\frac{\langle\sigma_{A}\psi_{x_{p+1}}\psi_{x_{0}}^{[\eta]}\rangle_{m\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{A}\rangle_{m\alpha}}
, we have from (
6.3.20
), for
x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{p})
and
C=C(\kappa_{\infty},n,\Omega,\delta_{0})
,

\int_{\Omega^{p}}\left|(x_{p+1}-a_{k^{\prime}})^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathfrak{B}_{p}(x_{0};x,x_{p+1})\right|d^{2p}x\leq C^{p}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{1}{\sqrt{|x_{0}-a_{j}|}},

a bound that is clearly shared by
(x_{p+1}-a_{k^{\prime}})^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathfrak{B}^{sym}_{p}(x_{0};x,x_{p+1})
(recall we only symmetrize
x_{1}
to
x_{p}
), valid under
x_{p+1}\to a_{k^{\prime}}
. For fermion-fermion and disorder-disorder correlations, we have analogous bounds valid under
x_{p+1}\to a_{k^{\prime}}
and
x_{0}\to a_{k^{\prime\prime}}

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega^{p}}\left|(x_{p+1}-a_{k^{\prime}})^{\frac{1}{2}}(x_{0}-a_{k^{\prime\prime}})^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathfrak{B}_{p}(x_{0};x,x_{p+1})\right|d^{2p}x\leq C^{p}.

Given these bounds and (
7.2.3
), it is straightforward to check that, for fixed
V,U,Z
, there is a small radius for
m
in which we may exchange the limits with the sum and the integral in the expansion (
7.2.2
), all using dominated convergence.
∎

7.3.
Energy insertions

We now finally introduce the primary field
energy
, or
energy density
in massive settings, defined by the limit (within correlations with mass
m\alpha
)

(7.3.1)

\varepsilon_{w}=\frac{i}{2}\lim_{z\to w}\left[\psi_{z}\psi_{w}^{*}+\frac{m}{\pi}c(z,w)\right],

where we write

(7.3.2)

c(z,w)=\int_{\Omega}d^{2}y\alpha(y)\big(\langle\psi_{z}\psi^{*}_{w}\varepsilon_{y}\rangle_{0}-\langle\psi_{z}\psi^{*}_{w}\rangle_{0}\langle\varepsilon_{y}\rangle_{0}\big)=:\int_{\Omega}d^{2}y\alpha(y)\big\langle(\psi_{z}\psi_{w}^{*});\varepsilon_{y}\big\rangle_{0}^{\mathsf{T}},

and

(7.3.3)

\langle\mathcal{O}_{1};\mathcal{O}_{2}\rangle_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}=\langle\mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{2}\rangle_{0}-\langle\mathcal{O}_{1}\rangle_{0}\langle\mathcal{O}_{2}\rangle_{0}.

We need to show that the limit (
7.3.1
) exists. From
\psi=\psi^{[1]}+i\psi^{[i]}
,
\psi^{*}=\psi^{[1]}-i\psi^{[i]}
and (
6.3.5
) (and implicitly (
6.1.15
) in case there are more fermions), we know that

(7.3.4)

\begin{split}\psi_{z}\psi_{w}&=\frac{2}{z-w}+(\text{continuous function}),\quad\text{and}\\
\psi_{z}\psi_{w}^{*}&=-4mi\cdot\alpha(w)\log|z-w|+(\text{continuous function}),\end{split}

where the continuous remainders satisfy a bound that conforms to local uniform boundedness (where we do not require
z
and
w
to be uniformly separated).

For the second term, from (
6.1.15
), we have

(7.3.5)

\langle\psi_{z}\psi_{w}^{*}\psi_{y}\psi_{y}^{*}\rangle_{0}=\langle\psi_{z}\psi_{w}^{*}\rangle_{0}\langle\psi_{y}\psi_{y}^{*}\rangle_{0}+\langle\psi_{z}\psi_{y}^{*}\rangle_{0}\langle\psi_{w}^{*}\psi_{y}\rangle_{0}-\langle\psi_{z}\psi_{y}\rangle_{0}\langle\psi_{w}^{*}\psi_{y}^{*}\rangle_{0}.

When we express the energy insertions in the integrand in (
7.3.1
) using the critical relation
\varepsilon_{w}=\frac{i}{2}\psi_{w}\psi_{w}^{*}
then use (
7.3.5
) to simplify the 4-point correlation of fermions, the first term gets canceled out. The second term is
O(1)
when
y
tends towards
z,w
. For the third term, from (
7.3.4
), we have

\langle\psi_{z}\psi_{y}\rangle_{0}=\frac{2}{z-y}+O(1)\quad\text{and}\quad\langle\psi_{w}^{*}\psi_{y}^{*}\rangle_{0}=\frac{2}{\overline{w-y}}+O(1).

In addition, by Lemma
6.19
and (anti)holomorphicity, these
O(1)
terms are uniform only depending on the domain and the distance of
z,w
to the boundary. Then, since the integral of the third term becomes essentially a Cauchy transform, using arguments similar to Remark
6.29
, it is straightforward to show that

(7.3.6)

c(z,w)=4\pi i\cdot\alpha(w)\log|z-w|+(\text{continuous function}),

where again the continuous remainder is bounded by a uniform quantity only depending on the domain, the derivative of
\alpha
and the distance of
z,w
to the boundary.
Comparing with (
7.3.4
), we see that the singularity is precisely canceled and the limit (
7.3.1
) exists.

Therefore, we study the following limit

\displaystyle\left\langle\sigma_{V}\mu_{U}\Psi_{Z}\varepsilon_{w_{1}}\cdots\varepsilon_{w_{r}}\right\rangle_{m\alpha}=\left(\frac{i}{2}\right)^{r}\lim_{w^{\prime}_{1}\to w_{1}}\cdots\lim_{w^{\prime}_{r}\to w_{r}}\left\langle\sigma_{V}\mu_{U}\Psi_{Z}\prod_{j=1}^{r}\left(\psi_{w^{\prime}_{j}}\psi^{*}_{w_{j}}+\frac{m}{\pi}c(w^{\prime}_{j},w_{j})\right)\right\rangle_{m\alpha}.

From our work so far, using Lemma
7.11
, expanding the product and using (
7.3.2
), we can write

\displaystyle\frac{\langle\sigma_{V}\mu_{U}\Psi_{Z}\prod_{j=1}^{r}(\psi_{w_{j}^{\prime}}\psi_{w_{j}}^{*}+\frac{m}{\pi}c(w_{j}^{\prime},w_{j}))\rangle_{m\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{V}\sigma_{U}\rangle_{m\alpha}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{p=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-m)^{p}}{p!\cdot\pi^{p}}\int_{\Omega^{p}}d^{2p}x\alpha(x_{1})\cdots\alpha(x_{p})\sum_{C\subset\{1,\ldots,p\}}\sum_{\Lambda\in\Pi_{\{1,\ldots,p\}\setminus C}}(-1)^{|\Lambda|}|\Lambda|!

\displaystyle\quad\times\frac{\langle\sigma_{V}\mu_{U}\Psi_{Z}\prod_{j=1}^{r}(\psi_{w_{j}^{\prime}}\psi_{w_{j}}^{*}+\frac{m}{\pi}c(w_{j}^{\prime},w_{j}))\prod_{k\in C}\varepsilon_{x_{k}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{V}\sigma_{U}\rangle_{0}}\prod_{B\in\Lambda}\frac{\langle\sigma_{U}\sigma_{V}\prod_{l\in B}\varepsilon_{x_{l}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{V}\sigma_{U}\rangle_{0}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{I\subset\{1,\dots,r\}}\sum_{p=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-m)^{p}}{p!\cdot\pi^{p+|I|}}\int_{\Omega^{p+|I|}}d^{2p}x\prod_{i\in I}dy_{i}\alpha(x_{1})\cdots\alpha(x_{p})\prod_{i\in I}\alpha(y_{i})\sum_{C\subset\{1,\ldots,p\}}\sum_{\Lambda\in\Pi_{\{1,\ldots,p\}\setminus C}}

\displaystyle\quad\times(-1)^{|\Lambda|}|\Lambda|!\prod_{i\in I}\langle\psi_{w_{i}^{\prime}}\psi_{w_{i}}^{*};\varepsilon_{y_{i}}\rangle_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}\frac{\langle\sigma_{V}\mu_{U}\Psi_{Z}\prod_{j\notin I}\psi_{w_{j}^{\prime}}\psi_{w_{j}}^{*}\prod_{k\in C}\varepsilon_{x_{k}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{V}\sigma_{U}\rangle_{0}}\prod_{B\in\Lambda}\frac{\langle\sigma_{V}\sigma_{U}\prod_{l\in B}\varepsilon_{x_{l}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{V}\sigma_{U}\rangle_{0}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{q=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-m)^{q}}{q!\cdot\pi^{q}}\sum_{I\subset\{1,\dots,r\}}\sum_{p=0}^{q}\frac{q!}{p!}\delta_{q,p+|I|}\int_{\Omega^{q}}d^{2p}x\prod_{i\in I}dy_{i}\alpha(x_{1})\cdots\alpha(x_{p})\prod_{i\in I}\alpha(y_{i})\!\sum_{C\subset\{1,\ldots,p\}}\sum_{\Lambda\in\Pi_{\{1,\ldots,p\}\setminus C}}

\displaystyle\kern 5.0pt\times(-1)^{|\Lambda|}|\Lambda|!\prod_{i\in I}\left(-\langle\psi_{w^{\prime}_{i}}\psi_{w_{i}}^{*};\varepsilon_{y_{i}}\rangle_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)\frac{\langle\sigma_{V}\mu_{U}\Psi_{Z}\prod_{j\notin I}\psi_{w_{j}^{\prime}}\psi_{w_{j}}^{*}\prod_{k\in C}\varepsilon_{x_{k}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{V}\sigma_{U}\rangle_{0}}\!\prod_{B\in\Lambda}\!\frac{\langle\sigma_{V}\sigma_{U}\prod_{l\in B}\varepsilon_{x_{l}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{V}\sigma_{U}\rangle_{0}}.

Next we note that due to symmetry, we can write

\displaystyle\sum_{I\subset\{1,\dots,r\}}\sum_{p=0}^{q}\frac{q!}{p!}\delta_{q,p+|I|}\int_{\Omega^{q}}d^{2p}x\prod_{i\in I}dy_{i}\alpha(x_{1})\cdots\alpha(x_{p})\prod_{i\in I}\alpha(y_{i})\sum_{C\subset\{1,\ldots,p\}}\sum_{\Lambda\in\Pi_{\{1,\ldots,p\}\setminus C}}

\displaystyle\kern 5.0pt\times(-1)^{|\Lambda|}|\Lambda|!\prod_{i\in I}\left(-\langle\psi_{w^{\prime}_{i}}\psi_{w_{i}}^{*};\varepsilon_{y_{i}}\rangle_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)\frac{\langle\sigma_{V}\mu_{U}\Psi_{Z}\prod_{j\notin I}\psi_{w_{j}^{\prime}}\psi_{w_{j}}^{*}\prod_{k\in C}\varepsilon_{x_{k}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{V}\sigma_{U}\rangle_{0}}\prod_{B\in\Lambda}\frac{\langle\sigma_{V}\sigma_{U}\prod_{l\in B}\varepsilon_{x_{l}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{V}\sigma_{U}\rangle_{0}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{I\subset\{1,\dots,r\}}\sum_{\nu\in S_{I,[q]}}\int_{\Omega^{q}}d^{2q}x\alpha(x_{1})\cdots\alpha(x_{q})\sum_{C\subset\{1,\dots,q\}\setminus\nu(I)}\sum_{\Lambda\in\Pi_{\{1,\dots,q\}\setminus(\nu(I)\cup C)}}(-1)^{|\Lambda|}|\Lambda|!

\displaystyle\kern 5.0pt\times\prod_{i\in I}\left(-\langle\psi_{w^{\prime}_{i}}\psi_{w_{i}}^{*};\varepsilon_{y_{i}}\rangle_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)\frac{\langle\sigma_{V}\mu_{U}\Psi_{Z}\prod_{j\notin I}\psi_{w_{j}^{\prime}}\psi_{w_{j}}^{*}\prod_{k\in C}\varepsilon_{x_{k}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{V}\sigma_{U}\rangle_{0}}\prod_{B\in\Lambda}\frac{\langle\sigma_{V}\sigma_{U}\prod_{l\in B}\varepsilon_{x_{l}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{V}\sigma_{U}\rangle_{0}},

where
S_{I,[q]}
denotes the set of injective mappings from
I
into
\{1,\dots,q\}
(the number of which is
\frac{q!}{(q-|I|)!}
– explaining where the factor
\frac{q!}{p!}
goes). Note that moving the sums under the integral, the integrand has a limit as
w^{\prime}\to w
(i.e.
w_{1}^{\prime}\to w_{1},\ldots,w_{r}^{\prime}\to w_{r}
), so we claim that:

Lemma 7.12
.

For distinct points
v_{1},\dots,v_{n},u_{1},\dots,u_{l},z_{1},\dots,z_{k},w_{1},\ldots,w_{r}
,
l+k
even, the correlation
\frac{\langle\sigma_{V}\mu_{U}\Psi_{Z}\varepsilon_{W}\rangle_{m\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{V}\sigma_{U}\rangle_{m\alpha}}
admits a series expansion around any
m=m_{0}
with the usual conditions. For
m_{0}=0
, we have

(7.3.7)

\displaystyle\frac{\langle\sigma_{V}\mu_{U}\Psi_{Z}\varepsilon_{W}\rangle_{m\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{V}\sigma_{U}\rangle_{m\alpha}}

\displaystyle=\!\sum_{p=0}^{\infty}\!\left(-\frac{m}{\pi}\right)^{p}\!\frac{1}{p!}\!\int_{\Omega^{p}}\!d^{2p}x\alpha(x_{1})\cdots\alpha(x_{p})\!\sum_{I\subset\{1,\dots,r\}}\sum_{\nu\in S_{I,[p]}}\sum_{C\subset\{1,\dots,p\}\setminus\nu(I)}

\displaystyle\quad\times\sum_{\Lambda\in\Pi_{\{1,\dots,p\}\setminus(\nu(I)\cup C)}}(-1)^{|\Lambda|}|\Lambda|!\prod_{i\in I}\left(-\langle\varepsilon_{w_{i}};\varepsilon_{x_{\nu(i)}}\rangle_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\times\frac{\langle\sigma_{V}\mu_{U}\Psi_{Z}\prod_{j\notin I}\varepsilon_{w_{j}}\prod_{k\in C}\varepsilon_{x_{k}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{V}\sigma_{U}\rangle_{0}}\prod_{B\in\Lambda}\frac{\langle\sigma_{V}\sigma_{U}\prod_{l\in B}\varepsilon_{x_{l}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{V}\sigma_{U}\rangle_{0}}.

Proof.

As in the proof of Lemma
7.11
and Remark
7.8
, it suffices to note that as
w_{s}^{\prime}\to w_{s}
, the local exponential bound is preserved. Note that in addition to
w^{\prime}\to w
, we would be merging
u^{\prime}\to u
in the spin-fermion correlation as in (
7.2.13
):

(7.3.8)

\frac{\langle\sigma_{V}\sigma_{U}\psi_{U^{\prime}}\Psi_{Z}\prod_{j=1}^{r}(\psi_{w_{j}^{\prime}}\psi_{w_{j}}^{*}+\frac{m}{\pi}c(w_{j}^{\prime},w_{j}))\rangle_{m\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{V}\sigma_{U}\rangle_{m\alpha}},

which admits a Pfaffian decomposition in terms of the
2
-point correlations (after expanding
\prod_{j=1}^{r}
) and series expansion as in Remarks
7.1
.

Let
S,S^{\prime}
be disjoint subsets of
\{1,2,\ldots,r\}
. Then, let us collect together all terms in the expansion of (
7.3.8
) which share singular factors as all
w^{\prime}\to w
:

(7.3.9)

(\text{regular as $w^{\prime}\to w$})\cdot\prod_{s\in S}\frac{\langle\sigma_{V}\sigma_{U}\psi_{w_{s}^{\prime}}\psi_{w_{s}}^{*}\rangle_{m\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{V}\sigma_{U}\rangle_{m\alpha}}\cdot\prod_{s^{\prime}\in S^{\prime}}\left(\frac{m}{\pi}c(w_{s^{\prime}}^{\prime},w_{s^{\prime}})\right).

The first factor collects together factors coming from partitioning the following subset of the fermions in (
7.3.8
)

(7.3.10)

\psi_{U^{\prime}}\Psi_{Z}\prod_{j\in[r]\setminus(S\cup S^{\prime})}(\psi_{w_{j}^{\prime}}\psi_{w_{j}}^{*}+\frac{m}{\pi}c(w_{j}^{\prime},w_{j}))

into
2
-point correlations, but only those without any factor of the form
\frac{\langle\sigma_{V}\sigma_{U}\psi_{w_{j}^{\prime}}\psi_{w_{j}}^{*}\rangle_{m\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{V}\sigma_{U}\rangle_{m\alpha}}
. Note that, for this reason, in any two terms of type (
7.3.9
) which have the same
\mathfrak{S}=S\cup S^{\prime}
, the first factor is exactly the same: it is clear that they sum over the same set of pairwise partitions of (
7.3.10
), and the sign of the term in the decomposition is the same whether the nearest-neighbors
\psi_{w_{j}^{\prime}}\psi_{w_{j}}^{*}
were present in the original multi-point correlation and was paired off (i.e.
j\in S
), or the term
\frac{m}{\pi}c(w_{j}^{\prime},w_{j})
was chosen (
j\in S^{\prime}
), as seen directly from (
6.1.15
) or the well-known correspondence to the parity of the number of crossings in the chord diagram. Let us write
R(S\cup S^{\prime})=R(\mathfrak{S})
for the first factor.

Then we argue that, for any fixed
\mathfrak{S}
, the sum

\displaystyle\sum_{S\subset\mathfrak{S}}R(\mathfrak{S})\cdot\prod_{s\in S}\frac{\langle\sigma_{V}\sigma_{U}\psi_{w_{s}^{\prime}}\psi_{w_{s}}^{*}\rangle_{m\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{V}\sigma_{U}\rangle_{m\alpha}}\cdot\prod_{s^{\prime}\in S^{\prime}}\left(\frac{m}{\pi}c(w_{s^{\prime}}^{\prime},w_{s^{\prime}})\right)

\displaystyle\quad=R(\mathfrak{S})\cdot\prod_{s\in\mathfrak{S}}\left(\frac{\langle\sigma_{V}\sigma_{U}\psi_{w_{s}^{\prime}}\psi_{w_{s}}^{*}\rangle_{m\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{V}\sigma_{U}\rangle_{m\alpha}}+\frac{m}{\pi}c(w_{s}^{\prime},w_{s})\right),

has a series expansion with locally exponentially bounded coefficients that remain so under the spin-fermion merger
u^{\prime}\to u
and limit
w^{\prime}\to w
. Indeed, the former only affects
R(\mathfrak{S})
, and we may estimate each factor in
R(\mathfrak{S})
as in the proof of Lemma
7.11
. For the latter limit, we know that each
\frac{\langle\sigma_{V}\sigma_{U}\psi_{w_{s}^{\prime}}\psi_{w_{s}}^{*}\rangle_{m\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{V}\sigma_{U}\rangle_{m\alpha}}+\frac{m}{\pi}c(w_{s}^{\prime},w_{s})
has a series expansion in
m-m_{0}
with locally (we let
w_{s}^{\prime}
approach
w_{s}
, but away from other points and the boundary) exponentially bounded coefficients for
(m-m_{0})^{p}
for
p\geq 2
thanks to (
6.3.20
). Since
\frac{\langle\sigma_{V}\sigma_{U}\psi_{w_{s}^{\prime}}\psi_{w_{s}}^{*}\rangle_{m\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{V}\sigma_{U}\rangle_{m\alpha}}+\frac{m}{\pi}c(w_{s}^{\prime},w_{s})
itself is locally uniformly bounded by (
7.3.4
) and (
7.3.6
), we see that the coefficients of
(m-m_{0})^{0}
and
(m-m_{0})^{1}
must be locally bounded as well (again, letting
w_{s}^{\prime}\to w_{s}
away from other points and the boundary). The desired summability, integrability, and local continuity then follow from arguments similar to Remark
7.8
and the proof of Lemma
7.11
.
∎

8.
Coefficients of Doubled Ising Correlations

In this section, we identify series expansions of doubled Ising correlations around criticality, i.e.
m_{0}=0
. Recall that we denote the fields in the second model by
\tilde{\sigma},\tilde{\mu}
, etc., independent from
\sigma,\mu
, etc.

Our main goal is the proof of Theorem
1.4
, done in Section
8.3
, and in particular the doubled massive Ising primary field correlation near
m=0
expressed as a series expansion in terms of critical correlations. Recall from Remark
7.1
that the radius of convergence and other properties of the series expansions of single correlations are preserved in this case; we will therefore omit analytic statements, since results of this section are primarily about the identification of coefficients for the
m_{0}=0
case.

8.1.
Pure spin correlations

Recall the notation
\sigma_{A}=\sigma_{a_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{a_{n}}
within correlation functions.

Lemma 8.1
.

Around
m_{0}=0
, we have

(8.1.1)

\displaystyle\log\frac{\langle\sigma_{A}\rangle_{m\alpha}^{2}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{0}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{p=0}^{\infty}\left(-\frac{m}{\pi}\right)^{p}\frac{1}{p!}\int_{\Omega^{p}}d^{2p}u\,\prod_{j=1}^{p}\alpha(u_{j})\sum_{\Lambda\in\Pi_{p}}(-1)^{|\Lambda|-1}(|\Lambda|-1)!

\displaystyle\quad\times\Bigg(\prod_{B\in\Lambda}\frac{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\widetilde{\sigma}_{A}\prod_{k\in B}(\varepsilon_{u_{k}}+\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{u_{k}})\right\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\widetilde{\sigma}_{A}\right\rangle_{0}}-\prod_{B\in\Lambda}\left\langle\prod_{k\in B}(\varepsilon_{u_{k}}+\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{u_{k}})\right\rangle_{0}\Bigg).

Proof.

By Proposition
7.2
, it is sufficient for us to prove that

\displaystyle\sum_{\Lambda\in\Pi_{p}}(-1)^{|\Lambda|-1}(|\Lambda|-1)!\prod_{B\in\Lambda}\frac{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\widetilde{\sigma}_{A}\prod_{k\in B}(\varepsilon_{u_{k}}+\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{u_{k}})\right\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\widetilde{\sigma}_{A}\right\rangle_{0}}

\displaystyle\quad=2\sum_{\Lambda\in\Pi_{p}}(-1)^{|\Lambda|-1}(|\Lambda|-1)!\prod_{B\in\Lambda}\frac{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\prod_{k\in B}\varepsilon_{u_{k}}\right\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{0}}

and

\displaystyle\sum_{\Lambda\in\Pi_{p}}(-1)^{|\Lambda|-1}(|\Lambda|-1)!\prod_{B\in\Lambda}\left\langle\prod_{k\in B}(\varepsilon_{u_{k}}+\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{u_{k}})\right\rangle_{0}=2\sum_{\Lambda\in\Pi_{p}}(-1)^{|\Lambda|-1}(|\Lambda|-1)!\prod_{B\in\Lambda}\left\langle\prod_{k\in B}\varepsilon_{u_{k}}\right\rangle_{0}.

We will prove the first claim. The second one is identical up to simplifying notation.

First of all, we note that for any set
B\subset\{1,\dots,p\}
we have

(8.1.2)

\displaystyle\frac{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\widetilde{\sigma}_{A}\prod_{k\in B}(\varepsilon_{u_{k}}+\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{u_{k}})\right\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\widetilde{\sigma}_{A}\right\rangle_{0}}=\sum_{U\subset B}\frac{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\prod_{k\in U}\varepsilon_{u_{k}}\right\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{0}}\frac{\big\langle\widetilde{\sigma}_{A}\prod_{l\in B\setminus U}\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{u_{l}}\big\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\widetilde{\sigma}_{A}\right\rangle_{0}}.

Note that in the
U
sum,
U
and
U^{\mathsf{c}}
produce identical terms. Thus if we expand the product over
B
in the
\Lambda
sum, we see that we get
2^{|\Lambda|}
times a sum over all possible refinements
\nu
of
\Lambda
that are obtained by either splitting any given block in
\Lambda
into two parts or keeping the block as is, since each factor originates, with or without tildes, from some term on the RHS of (
8.1.2
). Let us write
\nu\leq_{2}\Lambda
for this relation between
\Lambda
and its refinement
\nu
. We see that interchanging the order of the
\Lambda
and
\nu
sums, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\Lambda\in\Pi_{p}}(-1)^{|\Lambda|-1}(|\Lambda|-1)!\prod_{B\in\Lambda}\frac{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\widetilde{\sigma}_{A}\prod_{j\in B}(\varepsilon_{w_{j}}+\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{w_{j}})\big\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{A}\widetilde{\sigma}_{A}\rangle_{0}}

\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{\nu\in\Pi_{p}}\prod_{B\in\nu}\frac{\big\langle\sigma_{A}\prod_{j\in B}\varepsilon_{w_{j}}\big\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{A}\rangle_{0}}\sum_{\Lambda:\nu\leq_{2}\Lambda}(-1)^{|\Lambda|-1}(|\Lambda|-1)!2^{|\Lambda|}.

To evaluate this last sum, let us split it according to the number of parts of
|\Lambda|
. Since
\Lambda
is obtained by pairwise merging some blocks of
\nu
, we must have
|\nu|/2\leq|\Lambda|\leq|\nu|
, so

\sum_{\Lambda:\nu\leq_{2}\Lambda}(-1)^{|\Lambda|-1}(|\Lambda|-1)!2^{|\Lambda|}=\sum_{|\nu|/2\leq k\leq|\nu|}(-1)^{k-1}(k-1)!2^{k}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Lambda\in\Pi_{p}:\\
|\Lambda|=k,\nu\leq_{2}\Lambda\end{subarray}}1.

To compute the
\Lambda
-sum, we note first that there must be
|\nu|-k
parts of
\nu
that get paired with another part of
\nu
. This means that there are
|\nu|-2(|\nu|-k)=2k-|\nu|
parts of
\nu
that are not paired. There are
\binom{|\nu|}{2k-|\nu|}
ways we can choose these parts that are not paired with anything, and out of the remaining
2(|\nu|-k)
parts, we can pair them in

\frac{(2(|\nu|-k))!}{(|\nu|-k)!2^{|\nu|-k}}

ways. This means that

\displaystyle\sum_{\Lambda:\nu\leq_{2}\Lambda}(-1)^{|\Lambda|-1}(|\Lambda|-1)!2^{|\Lambda|}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\frac{|\nu|}{2}\leq k\leq|\nu|}(-1)^{k-1}(k-1)!2^{k}\frac{|\nu|!}{(2(|\nu|-k))!(2k-|\nu|)!}\frac{(2(|\nu|-k))!}{(|\nu|-k)!2^{|\nu|-k}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\frac{|\nu|}{2}\leq k\leq|\nu|}(-1)^{k-1}(k-1)!2^{2k-|\nu|}\frac{|\nu|!}{(2k-|\nu|)!(|\nu|-k)!}

\displaystyle=\sum_{0\leq k\leq\frac{|\nu|}{2}}(-1)^{|\nu|-k-1}(|\nu|-k-1)!2^{|\nu|-2k}\frac{|\nu|!}{(|\nu|-2k)!k!}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|\nu|-1}(|\nu|-1)!\sum_{0\leq k\leq\frac{|\nu|}{2}}(-1)^{k}(|\nu|-k-1)!2^{|\nu|-2k}\frac{|\nu|-k+k}{(|\nu|-2k)!k!}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|\nu|-1}(|\nu|-1)!\sum_{0\leq k\leq\frac{|\nu|}{2}}(-1)^{k}2^{|\nu|-2k}\binom{|\nu|-k}{k}

\displaystyle\quad+(-1)^{|\nu|-1}(|\nu|-1)!\sum_{0\leq k\leq\frac{|\nu|}{2}}(-1)^{k}2^{|\nu|-2k}\binom{|\nu|-k-1}{k-1}.

Let us then introduce for
q\geq 0
the quantities

S_{q}^{(1)}=\sum_{0\leq k\leq\frac{q}{2}}(-1)^{k}2^{q-2k}\binom{q-k}{k}\qquad\text{and}\qquad S_{q}^{(2)}=\sum_{0\leq k\leq\frac{q}{2}}(-1)^{k}2^{q-2k}\binom{q-k-1}{k-1}

and compute their generating functions:

\displaystyle\sum_{q=0}^{\infty}x^{q}S_{q}^{(1)}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{k}2^{-2k}\sum_{q=2k}^{\infty}2^{q}x^{q}\binom{q-k}{k}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{k}2^{-k}x^{k}\sum_{q=k}^{\infty}2^{q}x^{q}\binom{q}{k},

now we use the fact that

\sum_{n=k}^{\infty}t^{n}\binom{n}{k}=\frac{t^{k}}{(1-t)^{k+1}}

to find that (for
|x|<1
)

\displaystyle\sum_{q=0}^{\infty}S_{q}^{(1)}x^{q}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{k}2^{-k}x^{k}\frac{(2x)^{k}}{(1-2x)^{k+1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1-2x}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\left(-\frac{x^{2}}{1-2x}\right)^{k}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1-2x}\frac{1}{1+\frac{x^{2}}{1-2x}}=\frac{1}{(1-x)^{2}}=\sum_{q=0}^{\infty}(q+1)x^{q},

implying that
S_{q}^{(1)}=q+1
. Similarly,

\displaystyle\sum_{q=0}^{\infty}x^{q}S_{q}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{k}2^{-2k}\sum_{q=2k}^{\infty}2^{q}x^{q}\binom{q-k-1}{k-1}

\displaystyle=1+\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{k+1}2^{-2(k+1)}\sum_{q=2(k+1)}^{\infty}2^{q}x^{q}\binom{q-k-2}{k}

\displaystyle=1+\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{k+1}2^{-2(k+1)}2^{k+2}x^{k+2}\sum_{q=k}^{\infty}2^{q}x^{q}\binom{q}{k}

\displaystyle=1+\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{k+1}2^{-2(k+1)}2^{k+2}x^{k+2}\frac{(2x)^{k}}{(1-2x)^{k+1}}

\displaystyle=1-x^{2}\frac{1}{1-2x}\frac{1}{1+\frac{x^{2}}{1-2x}}=1-\frac{x^{2}}{(1-x)^{2}}=1-\sum_{q=1}^{\infty}(q-1)x^{q},

so for
q\geq 1
‚
S_{q}^{(2)}=-(q-1)
. Since always
|\nu|\geq 1
, we conclude that

\sum_{\Lambda:\nu\leq_{2}\Lambda}(-1)^{|\Lambda|-1}(|\Lambda|-1)!2^{|\Lambda|}=(-1)^{|\nu|-1}(|\nu|-1)!(S_{|\nu|}^{(1)}+S_{|\nu|}^{(2)})=2(-1)^{|\nu|-1}(|\nu|-1)!.

Thus

\displaystyle\sum_{\Lambda\in\Pi_{p}}(-1)^{|\Lambda|-1}(|\Lambda|-1)!\prod_{B\in\pi}\frac{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\widetilde{\sigma}_{A}\prod_{k\in B}(\varepsilon_{u_{k}}+\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{u_{k}})\right\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\widetilde{\sigma}_{A}\right\rangle_{0}}

\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{\nu\in\Pi_{p}}\prod_{B\in\nu}\frac{\langle\sigma_{A}\prod_{j\in B}\varepsilon_{w_{j}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{A}\rangle_{0}}\sum_{\Lambda:\nu\leq_{2}\Lambda}(-1)^{|\Lambda|-1}(|\Lambda|-1)!2^{|\Lambda|}

\displaystyle\quad=2\sum_{\nu\in\Pi_{p}}(-1)^{|\nu|-1}(|\nu|-1)!\prod_{B\in\nu}\frac{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\prod_{k\in B}\varepsilon_{u_{k}}\right\rangle_{0}}{\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{0}}

which was our claim.

Clearly the combinatorics of the claim for
A=\emptyset
is identical. This concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Theorem 8.2
.

We have

(8.1.3)

\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{m\alpha}^{2}=\sum_{p=0}^{\infty}\left(-\frac{m}{\pi}\right)^{p}\frac{1}{p!}\int_{\Omega^{p}}d^{2p}u\prod_{j=1}^{p}\alpha(u_{j})\left\langle\sigma_{A}\widetilde{\sigma}_{A};\varepsilon_{u_{1}}+\tilde{\varepsilon}_{u_{1}};\dots;\varepsilon_{u_{p}}+\tilde{\varepsilon}_{u_{p}}\right\rangle_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}.

Before turning to the proof, we will need a simple identity for suitable correlation functions of bounded random variables.

Lemma 8.3
.

Let
X,Y_{1},\dots,Y_{p}
be bounded real-valued random variables with
\mathbb{E}(X)=1
. Then

(8.1.4)

\displaystyle\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{p}}\prod_{B\in\pi}\sum_{\sigma\in\Pi_{B}}(-1)^{|\sigma|-1}(|\sigma|-1)!\left(\prod_{C\in\sigma}\mathbb{E}\bigg[X\prod_{k\in C}Y_{k}\bigg]-\prod_{C\in\sigma}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\prod_{k\in C}Y_{k}\bigg]\right)

\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{[p]\cup\{\infty\}}}(-1)^{|\pi|-1}(|\pi|-1)!\prod_{B\in\pi}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\prod_{j\in B}Z_{j}\bigg],

where
Z_{\infty}=X
and
Z_{j}=Y_{j}
for
j\in[p]=\{1,\dots,p\}
.

Proof.

Let us start by looking at the function

(t_{1},\dots,t_{p})\mapsto\frac{\mathbb{E}(Xe^{\sum_{j=1}^{p}t_{j}Y_{j}})}{\mathbb{E}(e^{\sum_{j=1}^{p}t_{j}Y_{j}})}.

Since the random variables are bounded and
\mathbb{E}(X)\neq 0
, we see that this is an analytic function of
t_{1},\dots,t_{p}
in some neighborhood of the origin. Moreover, we know (say by Faà di Bruno) that

\partial_{t_{1}}\cdots\partial_{t_{p}}|_{t=0}\frac{\mathbb{E}(Xe^{\sum_{j=1}^{p}t_{j}Y_{j}})}{\mathbb{E}(e^{\sum_{j=1}^{p}t_{j}Y_{j}})}=\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{[p]\cup\{\infty\}}}(-1)^{|\pi|-1}(|\pi|-1)!\prod_{B\in\pi}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\prod_{j\in B}Z_{j}\bigg].

Thus it remains to show that this derivative also equals the left hand side.

To verify this, let us define

L(t_{1},\dots,t_{p})=\log\frac{\mathbb{E}(Xe^{\sum_{j=1}^{p}t_{j}Y_{j}})}{\mathbb{E}(e^{\sum_{j=1}^{p}t_{j}Y_{j}})}

which is also analytic in some neighborhood of the origin since
\mathbb{E}(X)=1
. We can thus compute

\displaystyle\partial_{t_{1}}\cdots\partial_{t_{p}}|_{t=0}\frac{\mathbb{E}(Xe^{\sum_{j=1}^{p}t_{j}Y_{j}})}{\mathbb{E}(e^{\sum_{j=1}^{p}t_{j}Y_{j}})}=\partial_{t_{1}}\cdots\partial_{t_{p}}|_{t=0}e^{L(t_{1},\dots,t_{p})}=\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{p}}\prod_{B\in\pi}\Bigg(\prod_{j\in B}\frac{\partial}{\partial t_{j}}\Bigg)\Bigg|_{t=0}L(t_{1},\dots,t_{p}).

It thus suffices to show that for each
B\subset[p]

\displaystyle\Bigg(\prod_{j\in B}\frac{\partial}{\partial t_{j}}\Bigg)\Bigg|_{t=0}L(t_{1},\dots,t_{p})=\sum_{\sigma\in\Pi_{B}}(-1)^{|\sigma|-1}(|\sigma|-1)!\left(\prod_{C\in\sigma}\mathbb{E}\bigg[X\prod_{k\in C}Y_{k}\bigg]-\prod_{C\in\sigma}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\prod_{k\in C}Y_{k}\bigg]\right).

In fact, it is even enough to prove that

\displaystyle\Bigg(\prod_{j\in B}\frac{\partial}{\partial t_{j}}\Bigg)\Bigg|_{t=0}\log\mathbb{E}\left(Xe^{\sum_{j=1}^{p}t_{j}Y_{j}}\right)=\sum_{\sigma\in\Pi_{B}}(-1)^{|\sigma|-1}(|\sigma|-1)!\prod_{C\in\sigma}\mathbb{E}\bigg[X\prod_{k\in C}Y_{k}\bigg]

since the second term is obtained by setting
X=1
. Note that for each
n\geq 1
and
x>0

\frac{d^{n}}{dx^{n}}\log x=(-1)^{n-1}(n-1)!x^{-n}.

Thus

\displaystyle\prod_{j\in B}\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial t_{j}}\right|_{t=0}\log\mathbb{E}\left[Xe^{\sum_{j=1}^{p}t_{j}Y_{j}}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\in\Pi_{B}}\hskip-3.0pt\bigg(\frac{\partial^{|\sigma|}}{\partial^{|\sigma|}x}\bigg|_{x=\mathbb{E}(X)}\log x\bigg)\bigg(\prod_{C\in\sigma}\prod_{j\in C}\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial t_{j}}\right|_{t=0}\bigg)\mathbb{E}\left[Xe^{\sum_{j=1}^{p}t_{j}Y_{j}}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\in\Pi_{B}}(-1)^{|\sigma|-1}(|\sigma|-1)!\prod_{C\in\sigma}\mathbb{E}\bigg[X\prod_{j\in C}Y_{j}\bigg],

which concludes the proof.
∎

Proof of Theorem
8.2
.

We begin by noting that combining Lemma
8.1
with Proposition
1.6
yields

\displaystyle\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{m\alpha}^{2}\hskip-2.0pt

\displaystyle=\hskip-2.0pt\bigg\langle\hskip-2.0pt\prod_{j=1}^{n}\hskip-1.0pt\big(\hskip-1.0pt\sqrt{2}\mathop{:}\cos\sqrt{\pi}\varphi(a_{j})\mathop{:}\hskip-1.0pt\big)\hskip-3.0pt\bigg\rangle_{\hskip-2.0pt\mathrm{GFF}}\hskip-2.0pt\exp\Bigg\{\hskip-2.0pt\sum_{p=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-m)^{p}}{\pi^{p}p!}\hskip-2.0pt\int_{\Omega^{p}}\hskip-3.0ptd^{2p}u\hskip-2.0pt\prod_{j=1}^{p}\hskip-2.0pt\alpha(u_{j})\hskip-5.0pt\sum_{\Lambda\in\Pi_{p}}\hskip-2.0pt(-1)^{|\Lambda|-1}(|\Lambda|-1)!

\displaystyle\qquad\times\Bigg[\prod_{B\in\Lambda}\frac{\big\langle\prod_{j=1}^{n}(\sqrt{2}\mathop{:}\cos\sqrt{\pi}\varphi(a_{j})\mathop{:})\prod_{k\in B}(2\mathop{:}\cos\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi(u_{k})\mathop{:})\big\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}}}{\big\langle\prod_{j=1}^{n}(\sqrt{2}\mathop{:}\cos\sqrt{\pi}\varphi(x_{j})\mathop{:})\big\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\prod_{B\in\Lambda}\bigg\langle\prod_{k\in B}\big(2\mathop{:}\cos\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi(u_{k})\mathop{:}\big)\bigg\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}}\Bigg]\Bigg\}.

We now expand the exponential as a series in
m
(using Faà di Bruno), and find

\left\langle\sigma_{A}\right\rangle_{m\alpha}^{2}=\left\langle\prod_{j=1}^{n}\big(\sqrt{2}\mathop{:}\cos\sqrt{\pi}\varphi(a_{j})\mathop{:}\big)\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}}\\
\qquad\qquad\times\sum_{p=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-m)^{p}}{\pi^{p}p!}\int_{\Omega^{p}}d^{2p}u\,\prod_{j=1}^{p}\alpha(u_{j})\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{p}}\prod_{B\in\pi}\sum_{\sigma\in\Pi_{B}}(-1)^{|\sigma|-1}(|\sigma|-1)!\\
\times\Bigg[\prod_{C\in\sigma}\frac{\big\langle\prod_{j=1}^{n}(\sqrt{2}\mathop{:}\cos\sqrt{\pi}\varphi(a_{j})\mathop{:})\prod_{k\in C}(2\mathop{:}\cos\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi(u_{k})\mathop{:})\big\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}}}{\big\langle\prod_{j=1}^{n}(\sqrt{2}\mathop{:}\cos\sqrt{\pi}\varphi(a_{j})\mathop{:})\big\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}}}\\
-\prod_{C\in\sigma}\bigg\langle\prod_{k\in C}(2\mathop{:}\cos\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi(u_{k})\mathop{:})\bigg\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}}\Bigg].

Note that the integrand is still integrable by construction and the series still converges absolutely (and uniformly when the points
a_{j}
are in a suitable compact set).

Using Lemma
8.3
with
X=\frac{\prod_{j=1}^{n}(\sqrt{2}\mathop{:}\cos\sqrt{\pi}\varphi(a_{j})\mathop{:})}{\langle\prod_{j=1}^{n}(\sqrt{2}\mathop{:}\cos\sqrt{\pi}\varphi(a_{j})\mathop{:})\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}}}
and
Y_{j}=2\mathop{:}\cos\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi(u_{j})\mathop{:}
would be our next step, the issue however is that these are not bounded random variables (or even random variables). However, we do know that these correlation functions are limits of regularized ones by Lemma
2.6
, and the regularized ones are bounded random variables so applying Lemma
8.3
with a limiting argument, we conclude that

\displaystyle\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{p}}\prod_{B\in\pi}\sum_{\sigma\in\Pi_{B}}(-1)^{|\sigma|-1}(|\sigma|-1)!\Bigg[\prod_{C\in\sigma}\frac{\big\langle\prod_{j=1}^{n}(\sqrt{2}\mathop{:}\cos\sqrt{\pi}\varphi(a_{j})\mathop{:})\prod_{k\in C}(2\mathop{:}\cos\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi(u_{k})\mathop{:})\big\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}}}{\big\langle\prod_{j=1}^{n}(\sqrt{2}\mathop{:}\cos\sqrt{\pi}\varphi(a_{j})\mathop{:})\big\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad-\prod_{C\in\sigma}\bigg\langle\prod_{k\in C}(2\mathop{:}\cos\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi(u_{k})\mathop{:})\bigg\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{[p]}\cup\{\infty\}}(-1)^{|\pi|-1}(|\pi|-1)!\prod_{B\in\pi}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\prod_{k\in B}Z_{k}\bigg],

with
Z_{\infty}=\frac{\prod_{j=1}^{n}(\sqrt{2}\mathop{:}\cos\sqrt{\pi}\varphi(a_{j})\mathop{:})}{\langle\prod_{j=1}^{n}(\sqrt{2}\mathop{:}\cos\sqrt{\pi}\varphi(a_{j})\mathop{:})\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}}}
and
Z_{k}=2\mathop{:}\cos\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi(u_{k})\mathop{:}
for
k\in[p]
.

Note that by definition of cumulants, this is simply

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\langle\prod_{j=1}^{n}(\sqrt{2}\mathop{:}\cos\sqrt{\pi}\varphi(a_{j})\mathop{:})\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}}}\left\langle\prod_{j=1}^{n}\big(\sqrt{2}\mathop{:}\cos\sqrt{\pi}\varphi(a_{j})\mathop{:}\big);\prod_{k=1}^{p}(2\mathop{:}\cos\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi(u_{k})\mathop{:})\right\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\mathsf{T}},

the factor in front cancels and using Proposition
1.6
again yields the desired expansion.
∎

8.2.
General spin weighted correlation functions

In this section, we insert fermion, disorder and energy fields to doubled spin-weighted correlations. We start with a lemma we need for this.

Lemma 8.4
.

For any
D\subset\{1,\dots,p\}
, we have

(8.2.1)

\displaystyle\sum_{\tau\in\Pi_{\{1,\dots,p\}\setminus D}}(-1)^{|\tau|}(|\tau|+1)!\prod_{B\in\tau}\frac{\langle\sigma_{A}\prod_{j\in B}\varepsilon_{w_{j}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{A}\rangle_{0}}

\displaystyle\qquad=\sum_{\tau\in\Pi_{\{1,\dots,p\}\setminus D}}(-1)^{|\tau|}|\tau|!\prod_{B\in\tau}\frac{\langle\sigma_{A}\widetilde{\sigma}_{A}\prod_{j\in B}(\varepsilon_{w_{j}}+\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{w_{j}})\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{A}\widetilde{\sigma}_{A}\rangle_{0}}.

Proof.

The proof is similar to an argument present in the proof of Lemma
8.1
. Let us begin by noting that

\displaystyle\frac{\langle\sigma_{A}\widetilde{\sigma}_{A}\prod_{j\in B}(\varepsilon_{w_{j}}+\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{w_{j}})\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{A}\widetilde{\sigma}_{A}\rangle_{0}}=\sum_{U\subset B}\frac{\langle\sigma_{A}\prod_{i\in U}\varepsilon_{w_{i}}\rangle_{0}\langle\sigma_{A}\prod_{j\in B\setminus U}\varepsilon_{w_{j}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{A}\rangle_{0}\langle\sigma_{A}\rangle_{0}}.

Note also that
U
and
U^{\mathsf{c}}
produce the same term. As in the proof of Lemma
8.1
, expanding the product over
B
, we see that we get
2^{|\tau|}
times a sum over all possible refinements
\nu
of
\tau
that are either obtained by splitting a block into two parts; or keeping the block. We again write
\nu\leq_{2}\tau
for this relation. We see that interchanging the order of the
\tau
and
\nu
sums, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\tau\in\Pi_{\{1,\dots,p\}\setminus D}}(-1)^{|\tau|}|\tau|!\prod_{B\in\tau}\frac{\langle\sigma_{A}\widetilde{\sigma}_{A}\prod_{j\in B}(\varepsilon_{w_{j}}+\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{w_{j}})\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{A}\widetilde{\sigma}_{A}\rangle_{0}}

\displaystyle\qquad=\sum_{\nu\in\Pi_{\{1,\dots,p\}\setminus D}}\prod_{B\in\nu}\frac{\langle\sigma_{A}\prod_{i\in B}\varepsilon_{w_{i}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{A}\rangle_{0}}\sum_{\tau:\nu\leq_{2}\tau}(-1)^{|\tau|}|\tau|!2^{|\tau|}.

To compute this sum, let us split it according to the size of
\tau
. As in the proof of Lemma
8.1
,

\displaystyle\sum_{\tau:\nu\leq_{2}\tau}(-1)^{|\tau|}|\tau|!2^{|\tau|}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\frac{|\nu|}{2}\leq k\leq|\nu|}(-1)^{k}k!2^{k}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau:|\tau|=k,\nu\leq_{2}\tau\end{subarray}}1

\displaystyle=\sum_{\frac{|\nu|}{2}\leq k\leq|\nu|}(-1)^{k}k!2^{k}\frac{|\nu|!}{(2(|\nu|-k))!(2k-|\nu|)!}\frac{(2(|\nu|-k))!}{(|\nu|-k)!2^{|\nu|-k}}

\displaystyle=(-2)^{|\nu|}|\nu|!\sum_{0\leq k\leq\frac{|\nu|}{2}}(-1)^{k}2^{-2k}\frac{(|\nu|-k)!}{k!(|\nu|-2k)!}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|\nu|}|\nu|!\sum_{0\leq k\leq\frac{|\nu|}{2}}(-1)^{k}2^{|\nu|-2k}\binom{|\nu|-k}{k}.

Notice that this
is exactly the same quantity as
S_{q}^{(1)}
from the proof of Lemma
8.1
, with
q=|\nu|
, which gives us
S_{|\nu|}^{(1)}=|\nu|+1
.
We conclude that

\sum_{\tau:\nu\leq_{2}\tau}(-1)^{|\tau|}|\tau|!2^{|\tau|}=(-1)^{|\nu|}(|\nu|+1)!

and

\displaystyle\sum_{\tau\in\Pi_{\{1,\dots,p\}\setminus D}}(-1)^{|\tau|}|\tau|!\prod_{B\in\tau}\frac{\langle\sigma_{A}\widetilde{\sigma}_{A}\prod_{j\in B}(\varepsilon_{w_{j}}+\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{w_{j}})\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{A}\widetilde{\sigma}_{A}\rangle_{0}}

\displaystyle\qquad=\sum_{\nu\in\Pi_{\{1,\dots,p\}\setminus D}}(-1)^{|\nu|}(|\nu|+1)!\prod_{B\in\nu}\frac{\langle\sigma_{A}\prod_{i\in B}\varepsilon_{w_{i}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{A}\rangle_{0}},

which was the claim.
∎

We now proceed to the main goal of this section.

Lemma 8.5
.

Around
m_{0}=0
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\langle\sigma_{V}\widetilde{\sigma}_{V}\mu_{U}\widetilde{\mu}_{U}\Psi_{Z}\widetilde{\Psi}_{Z}\prod_{l=1}^{r}(\varepsilon_{w_{l}}+\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{w_{l}})\rangle_{m\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{V}\widetilde{\sigma}_{V}\sigma_{U}\widetilde{\sigma}_{U}\rangle_{m\alpha}}=\sum_{p=0}^{\infty}\left(-\frac{m}{\pi}\right)^{p}\frac{1}{p!}\int_{\Omega^{p}}d^{2p}x\alpha(x_{1})\cdots\alpha(x_{p})

\displaystyle\quad\times\sum_{I\subset\{1,\dots,r\}}\sum_{\nu\in S_{I,[p]}}\sum_{C\subset\{1,\dots,p\}\setminus\nu(I)}\sum_{\Lambda\in\Pi_{\{1,\dots,p\}\setminus(\nu(I)\cup C)}}(-1)^{|\Lambda|}|\Lambda|!\prod_{i\in I}\left(-\langle\varepsilon_{w_{i}}+\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{w_{i}};\varepsilon_{x_{\nu(i)}}+\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{x_{\nu(i)}}\rangle_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\times\frac{\langle\sigma_{V}\widetilde{\sigma}_{V}\mu_{U}\widetilde{\mu}_{U}\Psi_{Z}\widetilde{\Psi}_{Z}\prod_{j\in\{1,\dots,r\}\setminus I}(\varepsilon_{w_{j}}+\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{w_{j}})\prod_{k\in C}(\varepsilon_{x_{k}}+\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{x_{k}})\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{V}\tilde{\sigma}_{V}\sigma_{U}\tilde{\sigma}_{U}\rangle_{0}}

\displaystyle\quad\times\prod_{B\in\Lambda}\frac{\langle\sigma_{V}\widetilde{\sigma}_{V}\sigma_{U}\widetilde{\sigma}_{U}\prod_{l\in B}(\varepsilon_{x_{l}}+\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{x_{l}})\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{V}\widetilde{\sigma}_{V}\sigma_{U}\widetilde{\sigma}_{U}\rangle_{0}}.

Proof.

Using Lemma
7.12
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\langle\sigma_{V}\widetilde{\sigma}_{V}\mu_{U}\widetilde{\mu}_{U}\Psi_{Z}\widetilde{\Psi}_{Z}\prod_{l=1}^{r}(\varepsilon_{w_{l}}+\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{w_{l}})\rangle_{m\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{V}\widetilde{\sigma}_{V}\sigma_{U}\widetilde{\sigma}_{U}\rangle_{m\alpha}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{L\subset\{1,\dots,r\}}\frac{\langle\sigma_{V}\mu_{U}\Psi_{Z}\prod_{l\in L}\varepsilon_{w_{l}}\rangle_{m\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{V}\sigma_{U}\rangle_{m\alpha}}\frac{\langle\sigma_{V}\mu_{U}\Psi_{Z}\prod_{l^{\prime}\notin L}\varepsilon_{w_{l^{\prime}}}\rangle_{m\alpha}}{\langle\sigma_{V}\sigma_{U}\rangle_{m\alpha}}

\displaystyle=\!\sum_{p,p^{\prime}=0}^{\infty}\!\left(-\frac{m}{\pi}\right)^{p+p^{\prime}}\!\frac{1}{p!(p^{\prime})!}\int_{\Omega^{p+p^{\prime}}}d^{2p}xd^{2p^{\prime}}y\prod_{j=1}^{p}\alpha(x_{j})\prod_{k=1}^{p^{\prime}}\alpha(y_{k})\!\sum_{L\subset\{1,\dots,r\}}\sum_{I\subset L,I^{\prime}\subset\{1,\dots,r\}\setminus L}\sum_{\nu\in S_{I,[p]}}

\displaystyle\kern 5.0pt\times\!\!\sum_{\nu^{\prime}\in S_{I^{\prime},[p^{\prime}]}}\sum_{C\subset\{1,\dots,p\}\setminus\nu(I)}\sum_{C^{\prime}\subset\{1,\dots,p^{\prime}\}\setminus\nu^{\prime}(I^{\prime})}\sum_{\Lambda\in\Pi_{\{1,\dots,p\}\setminus(\nu(I)\cup C)}}\sum_{\Lambda^{\prime}\in\Pi_{\{1,\dots,p^{\prime}\}\setminus(\nu^{\prime}(I^{\prime})\cup C^{\prime})}}\!\!(-1)^{|\Lambda|+|\Lambda^{\prime}|}|\Lambda|!|\Lambda^{\prime}|!

\displaystyle\kern 5.0pt\times\prod_{i\in I}\left(-\langle\varepsilon_{w_{i}};\varepsilon_{x_{\nu(i)}}\rangle_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)\prod_{i^{\prime}\in I^{\prime}}\left(-\langle\varepsilon_{w_{i^{\prime}}};\varepsilon_{y_{\nu^{\prime}(i^{\prime})}}\rangle_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)\frac{\langle\sigma_{V}\mu_{U}\Psi_{Z}\prod_{j\in L\setminus I}\varepsilon_{w_{j}}\prod_{k\in C}\varepsilon_{x_{k}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{V}\sigma_{U}\rangle_{0}}

\displaystyle\kern 5.0pt\times\!\frac{\langle\sigma_{V}\mu_{U}\Psi_{Z}\prod_{j^{\prime}\in(\{1,\dots,r\}\setminus L)\setminus I^{\prime}}\varepsilon_{w_{j^{\prime}}}\prod_{k^{\prime}\in C^{\prime}}\varepsilon_{y_{k^{\prime}}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{V}\sigma_{U}\rangle_{0}}\!\prod_{B\in\Lambda}\!\frac{\langle\sigma_{V}\sigma_{U}\prod_{l\in B}\varepsilon_{x_{l}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{V}\sigma_{U}\rangle_{0}}\!\!\prod_{B^{\prime}\in\Lambda^{\prime}}\!\frac{\langle\sigma_{V}\sigma_{U}\prod_{l^{\prime}\in B^{\prime}}\varepsilon_{x_{l^{\prime}}}\rangle_{0}}{\langle\sigma_{V}\sigma_{U}\rangle_{0}}.

Again, using the symmetry of the integrands, we can combine
x_{1},\ldots x_{p},y_{1},\ldots,y_{p^{\prime}}
into
x_{1},\ldots,x_{q}
for
q=p+p^{\prime}
. First, we replace the sum
\sum_{p,p^{\prime}=0}^{\infty}
with
\sum_{q=0}^{\infty}\sum_{p=0}^{q}
and rewrite
